Kubernetes and Cloud Native Security Associate (KCSA)

Complete Test Preparation — Pass the KCSA & Beyond. 200 MCQs across 6 domains · 44 sub-sections · 200+ diagrams. From Novice → Security-Aware Practitioner → Beyond. Built with the anihpj/jobpost Django application.

📊 Complete Document Overview & Implementation GuideOVERVIEW

🎯 Executive Summary

This document contains 200 multiple-choice questions covering ALL 6 official CNCF KCSA exam domains. Every question is anchored to the anihpj/jobpost Django application — a real-world job board with PostgreSQL, Redis, and multi-tier security. Built on the Template_test_Prep.html design system with dark theme, Prism.js syntax highlighting, interactive answer toggles, and rich ASCII-art diagrams. Target audience: Novice → Security-Aware Practitioner → Beyond.

🧠 Why 3.3× Coverage?

The KCSA exam has 60 questions. We provide 200 — that's over 3× the exam volume. This ensures you've seen every concept from multiple angles: definition, scenario, troubleshooting, and application. You won't just memorize — you'll understand.

🎯 Question Type Distribution
📘 Conceptual (What is X?) — ~35% ████████████████
🔧 Practical (How to configure?) — ~30% █████████████
🛡️ Scenario (Attack/Defense) — ~25% ███████████
📋 Compliance (Which framework?) — ~10% █████

🕸️ Domain Interconnection Map — How Concepts Build On Each Other

The 6 KCSA domains aren't isolated silos — they form a layered security stack. Understanding these relationships is key to passing the exam.

KCSA Domain Dependency & Knowledge Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                         KCSA SECURITY KNOWLEDGE STACK                                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  LAYER 6: 📋 DOMAIN 6 — COMPLIANCE & FRAMEWORKS (10%)                           │ │
  │  │  PCI-DSS · SOC 2 · GDPR · NIST · MITRE ATT&CK · SLSA · SSDF                    │ │
  │  │  └─► "HOW DO WE PROVE WE'RE SECURE?" — The evidence layer                       │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                   │ requires                                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────▼────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  LAYER 5: 🏗️ DOMAIN 5 — PLATFORM SECURITY (16%)                                  │ │
  │  │  Supply Chain · Image Signing · Observability · mTLS · PKI · Admission Control  │ │
  │  │  └─► "HOW DO WE BUILD & OPERATE SECURELY?" — The platform layer                 │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                   │ defends against                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────▼────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  LAYER 4: ⚠️ DOMAIN 4 — KUBERNETES THREAT MODEL (16%)                            │ │
  │  │  Trust Boundaries · Persistence · DoS · Malicious Code · Network Attacks · PrivEsc│ │
  │  │  └─► "WHAT ARE WE DEFENDING AGAINST?" — The threat intelligence layer            │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                   │ informs                                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────▼────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  LAYER 3: 🔑 DOMAIN 3 — SECURITY FUNDAMENTALS (22%)                               │ │
  │  │  PSA · PSA Admissions · AuthN · RBAC · Secrets · Isolation · Audit · NetPol      │ │
  │  │  └─► "HOW DO WE SECURE THE CLUSTER?" — The defense implementation layer          │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                   │ secures                                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────▼────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  LAYER 2: 🔐 DOMAIN 2 — CLUSTER COMPONENT SECURITY (22%)                          │ │
  │  │  API Server · etcd · Kubelet · Scheduler · Controller Manager · CRI · KubeProxy │ │
  │  │  └─► "WHAT ARE WE SECURING?" — The component hardening layer                     │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                   │ within                                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────▼────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  LAYER 1: ☁️ DOMAIN 1 — CLOUD NATIVE SECURITY OVERVIEW (14%)                      │ │
  │  │  4Cs Model · Cloud Provider · Frameworks · Isolation · Artifact Security         │ │
  │  │  └─► "WHY DO WE SECURE?" — The foundational mindset layer                        │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                                      │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  📌 STUDY ORDER: D1→D2→D3→D4→D5→D6  (bottom-up in this diagram)                  │ │
  │  │  💡 KEY INSIGHT: Domains 2+3 = 44% of exam score. Master these two first!       │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔗 4Cs → All Domains

The 4Cs model (Cloud→Cluster→Container→Code) is the mental framework that connects every other domain. Think of it as the "operating system" of KCSA thinking.

🔗 AuthN → AuthZ → Audit

The AAA triad spans D2 (API Server AuthN), D3 (RBAC AuthZ), and D3 (Audit Logging). You can't authorize without authentication; you can't audit without both.

🔗 Threat → Platform → Compliance

D4 identifies threats → D5 builds defenses → D6 proves effectiveness. This is the "detect→protect→certify" pipeline.

🏗️ The Learning Target — anihpj/jobpost Architecture

Every question in this document references the anihpj/jobpost Django application — a 3-tier web app with real security requirements that demonstrate every KCSA concept in practice.

anihpj/jobpost — 3-Tier Architecture with Security Controls
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                         anihpj/jobpost — Security Architecture                │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                              │
  │  INTERNET (HTTPS:443)                                                        │
  │      │  TLS terminated · WAF rules · Rate limiting · IP filtering            │
  │      ▼                                                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  TIER 1: WEB (nginx)                    Security:                     │   │
  │  │  ┌────────────────────────┐             • NetworkPolicy ingress only  │   │
  │  │  │ anihpj-web Deployment  │             • non-root (UID 1000)         │   │
  │  │  │ image: nginx:alpine    │             • readOnlyRootFilesystem      │   │
  │  │  │ ports: 80, 443         │             • drop ALL capabilities       │   │
  │  │  │ replicas: 3            │             • PSA: enforce=restricted     │   │
  │  │  └───────────┬────────────┘                                          │   │
  │  └──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                 │ :8000 (NP: web→api ONLY)                                     │
  │                 ▼                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  TIER 2: API (gunicorn + Django)         Security:                     │   │
  │  │  ┌────────────────────────┐             • SA: automountToken=false    │   │
  │  │  │ anihpj-api Deployment  │             • NetworkPolicy: api→db:5432  │   │
  │  │  │ image: distroless      │             •            api→redis:6379   │   │
  │  │  │ ports: 8000            │             • Django CSRF + XSS + CSP     │   │
  │  │  │ HPA: min 2, max 10     │             • Secrets via volume mount    │   │
  │  │  └─────┬──────────┬───────┘             • PSA: enforce=restricted     │   │
  │  └────────┼──────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │           │ :5432    │ :6379                                                   │
  │           ▼          ▼                                                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐                                   │
  │  │ TIER 3: DATABASE │  │ TIER 3: CACHE    │    Security:                      │
  │  │ ┌──────────────┐ │  │ ┌──────────────┐ │    • SA: automountToken=false     │
  │  │ │anihpj-db     │ │  │ │anihpj-redis  │ │    • NP egress: DNS only          │
  │  │ │StatefulSet   │ │  │ │Deployment    │ │    • EncryptionConfiguration      │
  │  │ │PostgreSQL 15 │ │  │ │Redis 7-alpine│ │    • PVC encrypted (KMS)          │
  │  │ │PVC: 10Gi EBS │ │  │ │password auth │ │    • PSA: enforce=restricted      │
  │  │ └──────────────┘ │  │ └──────────────┘ │                                   │
  │  └──────────────────┘  └──────────────────┘                                   │
  │                                                                              │
  │  CROSS-CUTTING: Falco (runtime) · OPA/Kyverno (admission) · Audit logs · RBAC│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

🔬 Document Anatomy — How Each MCQ Is Structured

Every one of the 200 MCQs follows a precise, repeatable pattern designed for maximum learning efficiency. Understanding this structure will help you extract more value from each question.

MCQ Block Anatomy — 8 Components Dissected
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  MCQ BLOCK ANATOMY (example: Q29 — API Server Security)                              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                                      │
  │  ① <!-- Q29 -->  ←── HTML comment for source navigation                            │
  │                                                                                      │
  │  ② <div class="mcq-block" id="q29" data-correct="B">  ←── Container + answer key   │
  │     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
  │     │  ③ <span class="domain-pill d2">🔐 Cluster Component Security</span>     │       │
  │     │     ↑ Domain color-coded: d1=#58a6ff(blue) d2=#3fb950(green)          │       │
  │     │     d3=#d2991d(gold) d4=#a371f7(purple) d5=#f85149(red) d6=#39d2c0   │       │
  │     │                                                                        │       │
  │     │  ④ QUESTION TEXT (14-16px):  "Which flag disables anonymous auth..."   │       │
  │     │     ├─ A) --anonymous-auth=false          ← 4 radio-style options      │       │
  │     │     ├─ B) --anonymous-auth=true  ✓         ← data-correct="B"          │       │
  │     │     ├─ C) --allow-anonymous=false                                    │       │
  │     │     └─ D) --insecure-port=0                                          │       │
  │     │                                                                        │       │
  │     │  ⑤ [✓ Show Answer]  ←── toggleAnswer button (JS onclick)              │       │
  │     │                                                                        │       │
  │     │  ⑥ ANSWER BOX (hidden until toggled):                                 │       │
  │     │     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │       │
  │     │     │  ✅ Correct Answer: B                                        │   │       │
  │     │     │  📝 Explanation:  "The --anonymous-auth flag defaults to     │   │       │
  │     │     │     true in older versions. Setting it to false ensures..."  │   │       │
  │     │     │                                                              │   │       │
  │     │     │  ⑦ DIAGRAM-CONTAINER:                                        │   │       │
  │     │     │     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │   │       │
  │     │     │     │  ASCII Art / Table / Flow / Comparison Matrix    │     │   │       │
  │     │     │     │  (Rich visual illustration of the concept)      │     │   │       │
  │     │     │     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │   │       │
  │     │     │                                                              │   │       │
  │     │     │  ⑧ anihpj/jobpost CONTEXT:                                  │   │       │
  │     │     │     "In the anihpj/jobpost application, the kube-apiserver  │   │       │
  │     │     │     manifest on cluster-1 (kubeadm) has --anonymous-auth    │   │       │
  │     │     │     set to false, preventing unauthenticated access to      │   │       │
  │     │     │     the jobpost API resources..."                            │   │       │
  │     │     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │       │
  │     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
  │                                                                                      │
  │  📌 KEY RULE: data-correct attribute determines the correct answer.                   │

🔧 How to Implement This Study Plan

This document is designed as a self-contained study system. Follow the roadmap below to progress from novice to exam-ready in 4 weeks.

📅
Week 1
Foundation
Q1-Q72
Cloud Overview +
Cluster Components
📅
Week 2
Deep Dive
Q73-Q116
Security Fundamentals
AuthN + AuthZ + Secrets
📅
Week 3
Practice
Q117-Q180
Threat Model +
Platform Security
📅
Week 4
Mastery
Q181-Q200
Compliance + Review
Mock Exam
📖

Study Mode

Answer 20-30 Qs per session. Read every explanation — even for correct answers. The diagrams and anihpj/jobpost context teach you WHY, not just WHAT.

✍️

Active Recall

Cover the options and try to answer from memory. Draw the diagrams yourself. Explain concepts to a peer. Active recall outperforms passive reading 3:1.

🧪

Lab Practice

Use your 6-cluster lab (ksw 1-6). Apply the concepts: create NetworkPolicies, configure RBAC, scan images with Trivy, deploy Falco rules.

🛠️ Document Design System & Interactive Features

🎨 Dark Theme

GitHub-dark inspired color palette. CSS custom properties for consistent styling. Prism.js syntax highlighting for code blocks. Responsive design with mobile breakpoints.

🔘 Interactive MCQs

Toggle answer buttons reveal explanations. Domain color-coded pills (d1-d6). Correct/wrong option highlighting. Detailed explanations with ASCII-art diagrams.

📐 Rich Illustrations

Every Q has a diagram-container with ASCII art, tables, flow charts, comparison matrices, attack/defense maps, and architecture diagrams.

🗂️ Sidebar Navigation

Collapsible sidebar with all 6 categories and 44 sub-sections. Quick-jump links to every chapter. Expand/collapse all controls.

🏗️ Real Application Context

Every Q ends with an anihpj/jobpost implementation paragraph. Learn security concepts applied to a real Django+PostgreSQL+Redis stack.

📈 Progressive Difficulty

🟢 Novice → 🟡 Intermediate → 🟣 Advanced → 🚀 Beyond. Each category builds on previous knowledge. Zero assumed security knowledge.

✅ Daily Implementation Checklist
  • Morning (30 min): Read 10-15 MCQs + review explanations. Focus on one security domain per day. Type out key commands from the diagrams — muscle memory matters.
  • Afternoon (45 min): Revisit MCQs you got wrong. Redraw the ASCII diagrams from memory. Explain the concept to an imaginary peer — if you can't explain it simply, you don't understand it yet.
  • Evening (60 min): Apply concepts on your 6-cluster lab. Create NetworkPolicies, configure RBAC roles, scan images with Trivy, deploy Falco rules, test Pod Security Standards. Type every command — don't copy-paste.
  • Weekend (2-3 hours): Full review of the week's domain. Run a 30-Q mini-mock exam. Focus on your weakest sub-sections from the heatmap. Connect concepts across domains.
  • Day before exam: Review the Domain Interconnection Map and 5-Stage Maturity Journey from the Grand Overview. Understand the 4 Cs, Zero Trust model, and RBAC basics from memory. Get a good night's sleep.
  • Exam day morning: Review the Exam Day Simulator checklist. Skim the 6 KCSA domain weights. Verify your knowledge of Pod Security Standards and NetworkPolicy basics. Trust your preparation. 🚀

🏆 Skills You'll Master — Before vs After This Guide

❌ BEFORE — Common Security Blind Spots

  • ❌ "What's a NetworkPolicy?" — No idea pods can talk to anything by default
  • ❌ "Secrets are encrypted, right?" — Didn't know base64 is NOT encryption
  • ❌ "I just use cluster-admin for everything" — No RBAC, maximum blast radius
  • ❌ "The container runs as root, what's the big deal?" — Container escape risk unknown
  • ❌ "We don't need image scanning, we trust Docker Hub" — Supply chain attack waiting to happen
  • ❌ "Audit logs? We'll check them if something goes wrong" — No proactive threat detection
  • ❌ "4 Cs? Zero Trust? Never heard of them" — No security mental model

✅ AFTER — Security Skills This Guide Builds

  • ✅ Apply the 4 Cs defense-in-depth model to any Kubernetes deployment
  • ✅ Secure every cluster component — API server, etcd, kubelet, kube-proxy
  • ✅ Implement RBAC with least privilege and audit all access
  • ✅ Harden pods with SecurityContexts, seccomp, and Pod Security Standards
  • ✅ Detect supply chain attacks with image signing (Cosign) + SBOM
  • ✅ Monitor runtime threats with Falco + audit logging for compliance
  • ✅ Think like an attacker — threat model, identify attack paths, minimize blast radius

⚔️ KCSA Exam Day Battle Plan — The First 5 Minutes & Beyond

⏱️ 0-2 min
Setup
alias k=kubectl
export KUBECONFIG=...
Verify context
⏱️ 2-5 min
Scan
Read ALL 17 tasks
Mark as Easy/Med/Hard
Identify highest-weight
⏱️ 5-90 min
Execute
Easy first (build confidence)
Then medium, then hard
Use --dry-run -o yaml
⏱️ 90-120 min
Verify
Re-check each answer
Test from debug pod
Leave nothing blank
🚨 Critical Do's & Don'ts During The Exam
  • ✅ DO: Use kubectl create ... --dry-run=client -o yaml to generate YAML in seconds — never type manifests from scratch.
  • ✅ DO: Run kubectl config use-context <given-context> as the FIRST command for every task. Wrong context = 0 points.
  • ✅ DO: Use kubectl explain <resource> --recursive when you forget a field name. It's faster than searching docs.
  • ✅ DO: Create a debug pod: kubectl run test --rm -it --image=busybox -- sh to test DNS, connectivity, and curl from inside the cluster.
  • ❌ DON'T: Spend more than 7 minutes on any single task. Flag it, move on, return with fresh eyes.
  • ❌ DON'T: Forget to set the namespace. Many tasks require -n flag. Use kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=<ns> to save typing.
  • ❌ DON'T: Leave any task blank. Even partial work (creating the namespace, writing partial YAML) may earn partial credit.
  • ❌ DON'T: Use kubectl delete pod on a static pod — you must delete the manifest file from /etc/kubernetes/manifests/.

📊 Exam Overview & StrategySTRATEGY

KCSA Exam Format & Preparation Strategy

Exam Duration: 90 minutes | Format: Multiple-choice (online, proctored) | Passing Score: TBD by CNCF | Cost: $250 | Certification Validity: 3 years

6 Official Domains: Cloud Native Security Overview (14%), Cluster Component Security (22%), Security Fundamentals (22%), Threat Model (16%), Platform Security (16%), Compliance & Frameworks (10%). Cluster Components + Security Fundamentals = 44% of your score — prioritize these!

Exam Strategy: Read all questions first (5 min) → Answer easy questions first → Flag and return to hard ones → Use process of elimination → Every question has exactly ONE correct answer. The KCSA tests your UNDERSTANDING of cloud native security concepts, not your ability to run kubectl commands (unlike CKA/CKS).

Your 6-cluster lab (below) is for hands-on practice. KCSA is a multiple-choice exam, but the best way to learn security is to DO it. Run the scenarios, break things, fix them, and understand WHY each security control matters.

🖥️ Your 6-Cluster Kubernetes LabLAB

🖥️ The 6-Cluster Lab — Where Theory Meets Practice

All 200 MCQs reference concepts you can test on your 6-cluster Kubernetes lab, managed via the ksw (Kubernetes Environment Switcher) script. This lab provides the hands-on environment to apply every security concept in this guide.

6-Cluster Lab Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                         YOUR 6-CLUSTER KUBERNETES LAB                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  ksw — Kubernetes Environment Switcher                                │   │
  │  │  ───────────────────────────────────                                  │   │
  │  │  1) kubeadm          ← Production-like (1 node, kubeadm-provisioned)  │   │
  │  │  2) kind-cka-lab     ← Multi-node (cp + wk1 + wk2, v1.30.0)          │   │
  │  │  3) kind-cl1         ← Isolated test cluster 1 (NetworkPolicy lab)    │   │
  │  │  4) kind-cl2         ← Isolated test cluster 2 (RBAC lab)             │   │
  │  │  5) kind-cl3         ← Isolated test cluster 3 (PSA lab)              │   │
  │  │  6) minikube         ← Local dev/test (quick experiments)             │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                              │
  │  SECURITY TOOLS AVAILABLE:                                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  🔍 Trivy      — Container image vulnerability scanner                │   │
  │  │  🛡️ Falco      — Runtime threat detection (syscall monitoring)        │   │
  │  │  📋 kube-bench — CIS Benchmark compliance checker                     │   │
  │  │  🔐 Cosign     — Container image signing & verification              │   │
  │  │  📦 Syft       — SBOM generation for supply chain visibility          │   │
  │  │  🚦 Kyverno    — Admission control & policy enforcement              │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                              │
  │  USE CASES:                                                                  │
  │  • kind-cl1: Test NetworkPolicies (default-deny → allow specific flows)      │
  │  • kind-cl2: Practice RBAC (ClusterRole → RoleBinding → test with can-i)    │
  │  • kind-cl3: Experiment with Pod Security Standards (privileged vs baseline) │
  │  • kubeadm: Run CIS benchmarks (kube-bench) against a production-like node  │
  │  • minikube: Quick iterations — test a single security control in isolation  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔧 kubeadm (Production-like)

Single control-plane node. Run kube-bench CIS scans, test kubelet hardening, verify API server flags. Closest to a real production cluster.

🐳 kind × 4 (Isolated Labs)

cka-lab (3-node), cl1, cl2, cl3 (1-node each). Perfect for destructive testing — break RBAC, test NetworkPolicies, experiment with PSA without affecting other clusters.

⚡ minikube (Quick Dev)

Fastest startup. Ideal for quick experiments: test a single SecurityContext, verify an RBAC role, or validate a NetworkPolicy before applying to kind clusters.

💡 Lab Tip: After studying a concept in the MCQs, switch to the relevant cluster with ksw and apply it. For NetworkPolicy questions (Q113-Q116), use kind-cl1. For RBAC questions (Q90-Q96), use kind-cl2. For PSA questions (Q73-Q83), use kind-cl3. Muscle memory from hands-on practice is 3× more effective than reading alone.

📚 Pre-Learning: Cloud Native Security FoundationsF.1–F.5

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Building Your Security Mental Model

F.1 What Is Cloud Native Security & Why Does It Matter? — The "4 Cs of Cloud Native Security": Cloud → Cluster → Container → Code. Real-world breaches (Tesla cryptojacking, SolarWinds). The Shared Responsibility Model. Why anihpj/jobpost is your security learning target.

F.2 Security Terminology — The Lexicon You MUST Know — CIA Triad, threat vs vulnerability vs risk, CVE/CVSS, Zero Trust, least privilege, defense-in-depth. OPA, Kyverno, Falco, Trivy, Cosign, Sigstore. Pod Security Standards: Privileged, Baseline, Restricted.

F.3 The Kubernetes Attack Surface — Where Threats Live — External attack vectors (API server:6443, kubelet:10250, etcd:2379). Internal attack vectors (compromised pod → lateral movement). Supply chain attacks. The "Crown Jewels" in anihpj/jobpost: PostgreSQL PII, Django SECRET_KEY, API tokens.

F.4 The anihpj/jobpost Application Under Attack — Full architecture with security controls at every layer. Django + PostgreSQL + Redis stack. NetworkPolicy design: default-deny → web→api:8000 → api→db:5432. Dockerfile: multi-stage, distroless, non-root user.

F.5 Security In Your 6-Cluster Lab — kubeadm (production-like), kind × 3 (multi-cluster security tests), minikube (dev). Security tools available: Falco, Trivy, kube-bench. ksw for safe cluster switching. NetworkPolicy testing on isolated kind clusters.

If you're new to Kubernetes security: Read through F.1–F.5 before diving into Part 1. These foundations will give you the security mental models needed to understand the MCQs, security issues, and lab scenarios. The entire guide uses the anihpj/jobpost Django application as its running example — a real application with a real attack surface.

📝 Part 1: 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Complete KCSA MCQ Bank — Organized by the 6 official CNCF Linux Foundation exam domains. Each question includes a detailed explanation, security diagrams, and real-world context using anihpj/jobpost.

200Questions
6Categories
200Explanations

☁️ Category 1: Overview of Cloud Native Security (14%)Q1-Q28

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Your Security Foundation

This category is your security foundation. Before you can lock down a cluster, you need to understand WHAT you're protecting, WHERE the threats come from, and WHY cloud native security is fundamentally different from traditional security. Think of it as Security 101 for Kubernetes — the 4 Cs (Cloud → Cluster → Container → Code), the Shared Responsibility Model, Zero Trust, least privilege, and understanding your attack surface. For anihpj/jobpost, this means knowing every layer where an attacker could strike — from the AWS VPC down to the Django code.

Q1-Q28 28 Questions 🟢 Beginner

1.1 The 4Cs of Cloud Native SecurityQ1–Q6

Cloud Native Overview Q1 of 200
What are the "4 Cs" of Cloud Native Security, and in what order do they build on each other?
A) Container → Cluster → Code → Cloud — starting from the application and moving outward
B) Cluster → Cloud → Container → Code — the cluster is always the most critical layer
C) Code → Container → Cloud → Cluster — security starts with writing secure code
D) Cloud → Cluster → Container → Code — each outer layer protects the inner layers
✅ Correct Answer: D

The 4 Cs of Cloud Native Security are: Cloud → Cluster → Container → Code. Each layer builds on the one below — you can't secure containers without first securing the cluster, and you can't secure the cluster without securing the cloud infrastructure underneath.

📝 Detailed Explanation
The 4 Cs Defense-in-Depth Model
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LAYER 1: CLOUD (Infrastructure Security)               │
  │  • AWS/GCP/Azure: IAM roles, VPC, KMS, CloudTrail       │
  │  • The foundation — if cloud is breached, all layers     │
  │    above are at risk                                    │
  │    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │    │  LAYER 2: CLUSTER (Kubernetes Security)          │  │
  │    │  • API Server auth, etcd encryption, RBAC,       │  │
  │    │    NetworkPolicy, Admission Controllers          │  │
  │    │  • Secures HOW workloads run and communicate     │  │
  │    │    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │
  │    │    │  LAYER 3: CONTAINER (Pod Security)       │  │  │
  │    │    │  • SecurityContext, seccomp, capabilities │  │  │
  │    │    │  • Image scanning, distroless, non-root   │  │  │
  │    │    │  • Restricts WHAT a container can do     │  │  │
  │    │    │    ┌───────────────────────────────────┐ │  │  │
  │    │    │    │  LAYER 4: CODE (App Security)      │ │  │  │
  │    │    │    │  • SAST, dependency scanning,      │ │  │  │
  │    │    │    │    input validation, secrets mgmt  │ │  │  │
  │    │    │    │  • The application logic itself    │ │  │  │
  │    │    │    └───────────────────────────────────┘ │  │  │
  │    │    └─────────────────────────────────────────┘  │  │
  │    └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Analogy: Building a secure house — you need a safe neighbourhood (Cloud), strong walls and locked doors (Cluster), secure individual rooms (Container), and a safe inside the room (Code). If any layer fails, the layers below still protect you. For anihpj/jobpost: Cloud = AWS VPC + IAM, Cluster = RBAC + NetworkPolicy, Container = non-root + readOnlyRootFS, Code = Django CSRF + SQL injection prevention.

Cloud Native Overview Q2 of 200
In a managed Kubernetes service (EKS, AKS, or GKE), where does the cloud provider's responsibility end and YOUR responsibility begin under the Shared Responsibility Model?
A) The provider secures the entire stack — you just deploy your application code
B) The provider manages the control plane (API server, etcd, scheduler); you manage worker nodes, pods, network policies, and data
C) The provider manages everything except your container images — you only need to scan those
D) The provider only manages the physical data center; you are responsible for everything else including the control plane
✅ Correct Answer: B

Under the Shared Responsibility Model, the cloud provider manages the control plane — API server, etcd, kube-scheduler, kube-controller-manager, and control plane TLS certificates. YOU are responsible for everything that runs ON the cluster: worker node OS patching, pod security, NetworkPolicy, RBAC, Secrets encryption, container images, and your application's data.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Shared Responsibility Model — Managed Kubernetes (EKS/AKS/GKE)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    CLOUD PROVIDER (AWS/Azure/GCP)             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  CONTROL PLANE (Provider-Managed)                      │  │
  │  │  • API Server (auth, TLS, scaling)                     │  │
  │  │  • etcd (RAFT consensus, backup, encryption)           │  │
  │  │  • kube-scheduler + kube-controller-manager            │  │
  │  │  • Control plane certificates & rotation               │  │
  │  │  • Kubernetes version upgrades                         │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                              │
  │  ── YOU ──────────────────────────────────────────────────   │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  WORKER NODES + WORKLOADS (YOUR Responsibility)        │  │
  │  │  • Worker node OS patching & hardening                 │  │
  │  │  • Pod Security Standards & SecurityContexts            │  │
  │  │  • NetworkPolicy (pod-to-pod firewall)                  │  │
  │  │  • RBAC (Roles, RoleBindings, ServiceAccounts)          │  │
  │  │  • Secrets encryption at rest (EncryptionConfiguration) │  │
  │  │  • Container images (scanning, signing, distroless)     │  │
  │  │  • Application data (PostgreSQL, Redis — encryption)    │  │
  │  │  • Audit logging configuration & monitoring             │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost running on EKS: AWS manages the API server and etcd — you never SSH into the control plane. But YOU must configure RBAC so anihpj-api pods can't access Secrets, set NetworkPolicy so anihpj-web can only reach anihpj-api:8000, and encrypt the PostgreSQL data at rest. The Shared Responsibility line is drawn at the worker node — everything below that line is yours.

Cloud Native Overview Q3 of 200
Which of the following is an example of a security control at the CLOUD layer of the 4 Cs model?
A) AWS IAM roles for node groups that control which cloud resources the cluster can access
B) Kubernetes RBAC roles that control which users can create pods in a namespace
C) A Container SecurityContext that sets runAsNonRoot: true
D) A SAST tool scanning Python code for SQL injection vulnerabilities
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Cloud layer is the outermost layer — it controls who can create, modify, or access the cluster infrastructure itself. AWS IAM roles for node groups determine what cloud resources (S3, KMS, ECR) your cluster can access. B is Cluster-layer (RBAC), C is Container-layer (SecurityContext), D is Code-layer (SAST).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cloud Layer Security Controls — Mapping to AWS/Azure/GCP
  CLOUD LAYER — Infrastructure Security (Your FIRST line of defense)

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AWS                        AZURE                GCP        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  IAM Roles & Policies       Managed Identity     IAM &      │
  │  (who can create EKS?)      (who can create AKS?) Workload  │
  │                             Identity                        │
  │  VPC + Security Groups      VNet + NSG           VPC +      │
  │  (network isolation)        (network isolation)  Firewall   │
  │                                                             │
  │  KMS (encryption keys)      Key Vault            Cloud KMS  │
  │  (etcd + EBS encryption)    (etcd + disk enc)    (CMEK)     │
  │                                                             │
  │  CloudTrail (audit)         Azure Monitor        Cloud Audit│
  │  (who did what in AWS?)     (who did what?)      Logs       │
  │                                                             │
  │  WAF + Shield (DDoS)        WAF + DDoS           Cloud      │
  │  (protect the API endpoint) (protect API)        Armor      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Key principle: If your cloud credentials are compromised,
  the attacker can CREATE, MODIFY, or DELETE your entire cluster.
  Cloud-layer security is the foundation — get this wrong and
  nothing else matters.

For anihpj/jobpost: The EKS node group has an IAM role that allows pulling images from ECR and writing logs to CloudWatch — and nothing else. If a pod is compromised, it cannot access S3 buckets or DynamoDB tables because the node's IAM role has least privilege scoped to only what the cluster infrastructure needs.

Cloud Native Overview Q4 of 200
Which of the following is a security control at the CLUSTER layer of the 4 Cs model?
A) A VPC security group that restricts inbound traffic to port 443 only
B) A NetworkPolicy that allows only anihpj-api pods to connect to anihpj-db on port 5432
C) A distroless container image that has no shell or package manager
D) Django's CSRF middleware that validates form submissions
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Cluster layer is about securing how workloads run and communicate inside Kubernetes. NetworkPolicy is a cluster-layer control because it operates at the Kubernetes API level — it defines rules for pod-to-pod communication. A is Cloud-layer (VPC security group), C is Container-layer (image hardening), D is Code-layer (application middleware).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cluster Layer Security Controls — Inside Kubernetes
  CLUSTER LAYER — Kubernetes-Native Security Controls

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONTROL                  WHAT IT SECURES                   │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🔐 API Server Auth       WHO can access the cluster        │
  │     • X.509 certs, OIDC, ServiceAccount tokens              │
  │     • --anonymous-auth=false (no credential = no access)    │
  │                                                             │
  │  📋 RBAC                  WHAT users/pods can DO            │
  │     • Roles & ClusterRoles (namespace vs cluster scope)     │
  │     • RoleBindings & ClusterRoleBindings                    │
  │     • Least privilege per ServiceAccount                    │
  │                                                             │
  │  🌐 NetworkPolicy         WHICH pods can TALK to which      │
  │     • Default-deny ingress (block all incoming traffic)     │
  │     • Default-deny egress (block all outgoing traffic)      │
  │     • podSelector + namespaceSelector + ipBlock rules       │
  │                                                             │
  │  📝 Audit Logging         WHO did WHAT and WHEN             │
  │     • Log all Secret access, RBAC changes, exec commands    │
  │     • Essential for incident investigation & compliance     │
  │                                                             │
  │  🚪 Admission Controllers SHOULD this request be allowed?   │
  │     • OPA/Gatekeeper, Kyverno — policy as code              │
  │     • Block :latest tag, enforce labels, validate images    │
  │                                                             │
  │  🔒 etcd Encryption       Protect Secrets AT REST           │
  │     • EncryptionConfiguration (AES-CBC, KMS)                │
  │     • Without it: anyone with etcd access reads ALL Secrets  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the cluster-layer controls are: RBAC so anihpj-api SA can't read Secrets, NetworkPolicy so anihpj-web can only reach anihpj-api:8000 (not the DB directly), audit logging to track all Secret access, and Kyverno policies that reject any pod without a SecurityContext. These controls operate inside Kubernetes — they're configured via kubectl, not via AWS console.

Cloud Native Overview Q5 of 200
Which security control belongs to the CONTAINER layer of the 4 Cs model?
A) An IAM policy that restricts which S3 buckets a node can access
B) A ClusterRoleBinding that grants pod-reader access to a ServiceAccount
C) A SecurityContext with runAsNonRoot: true, readOnlyRootFilesystem: true, and capabilities.drop: [ALL]
D) A pre-commit hook that runs Bandit to scan Python code for security issues
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Container layer is about restricting what a single container can do — its privileges, filesystem access, and syscall permissions. The SecurityContext (runAsNonRoot, readOnlyRootFilesystem, drop ALL capabilities) is the primary Container-layer control. A is Cloud-layer (IAM), B is Cluster-layer (RBAC), D is Code-layer (SAST in CI/CD).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container Layer — Hardening Individual Containers
  CONTAINER LAYER — What a Single Container Can (and Cannot) Do

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SECURITY CONTEXT (PodSpec → securityContext)               │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  runAsNonRoot: true          ❌ Cannot run as UID 0 (root)  │
  │  runAsUser: 1000             ✅ Runs as unprivileged user   │
  │  readOnlyRootFilesystem: true ❌ Cannot write to /, /bin,    │
  │                                /etc — malware can't install  │
  │  allowPrivilegeEscalation:   ❌ No setuid binaries work —    │
  │    false                       can't gain more privileges   │
  │  capabilities.drop: [ALL]    ❌ No special kernel powers —   │
  │                                even root can't mount disks   │
  │  capabilities.add:           ✅ Only add back what's needed  │
  │    [NET_BIND_SERVICE]          (e.g., bind to port 80)      │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  SECCOMP PROFILE (syscall filtering)                        │
  │  RuntimeDefault              ❌ Blocks ~40 dangerous syscalls│
  │                                (reboot, mount, kexec_load)   │
  │  If blocked syscall called → SIGSYS (signal 31) → killed    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  IMAGE HARDENING                                            │
  │  • Distroless base (no shell, no apt — attacker trapped)    │
  │  • Multi-stage build (build tools stay out of runtime)      │
  │  • Image scanning (Trivy — fail on CRITICAL CVEs)           │
  │  • Pinned by SHA256 digest (not :latest tag)                │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  RUNTIME PROTECTION                                         │
  │  • Falco: detect unexpected processes, network connections  │
  │  • Resource limits (CPU/memory) — prevent noisy neighbor    │
  │  • Sandboxed runtimes: gVisor / Kata for untrusted workloads│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the anihpj-api pod runs with: runAsNonRoot: true (UID 1000), readOnlyRootFilesystem: true (only /tmp is writable via emptyDir), capabilities.drop: [ALL] with only NET_BIND_SERVICE added back, and the distroless Python image (no bash, no apt — an attacker who gets code execution in the API pod cannot install tools or write malware to disk).

Cloud Native Overview Q6 of 200
Which security control belongs to the CODE layer of the 4 Cs model?
A) An AWS KMS key used to encrypt etcd secrets at rest
B) A Kubernetes NetworkPolicy that restricts pod-to-pod traffic by label
C) A seccomp profile that blocks the mount() and reboot() syscalls
D) A SAST (Static Application Security Testing) tool scanning source code for SQL injection and hardcoded secrets
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Code layer is the innermost layer — it's about the application logic itself. SAST tools scan source code for vulnerabilities (SQL injection, XSS, hardcoded secrets) BEFORE the code reaches production. A is Cloud-layer (KMS), B is Cluster-layer (NetworkPolicy), C is Container-layer (seccomp). The Code layer embodies "shift left" — catch security issues as early as possible in the development lifecycle.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Code Layer — "Shift Left" Security in the Development Lifecycle
  CODE LAYER — Application Security (The Innermost Layer)

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STAGE              TOOL / TECHNIQUE                        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  💻 Developer IDE   • IDE plugins (Snyk, SonarLint)         │
  │                     • Real-time security feedback as you type│
  │                                                             │
  │  📝 Pre-Commit      • git hooks: Bandit (Python), eslint    │
  │                     • Block commits with hardcoded secrets  │
  │                     • .gitignore: .env, *.pem, credentials  │
  │                                                             │
  │  🔀 Pull Request    • SAST: Semgrep, CodeQL, SonarQube      │
  │                     • SCA: dependency scanning (pip-audit)  │
  │                     • Secret scanning: detect AWS keys, JWT │
  │                                                             │
  │  🏗️ CI/CD Build     • Dockerfile linting (Hadolint)         │
  │                     • Image scanning (Trivy — CRITICAL CVEs)│
  │                     • Manifest validation (Kubesec)         │
  │                     • SBOM generation (Syft)                │
  │                                                             │
  │  ✍️ Code Signing    • Cosign: sign images with OIDC identity│
  │                     • Provenance: who built this, from what?│
  │                                                             │
  │  🚀 Deployment      • Admission controller: reject unsigned │
  │                     • GitOps: Argo CD — Git is source of    │
  │                       truth (no manual kubectl apply)       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Key principle: The EARLIER you catch a vulnerability, the CHEAPER
  it is to fix. Finding a CVE in production = emergency + customer
  impact. Finding it at the PR stage = simple code change.

For anihpj/jobpost, the Code layer includes: Bandit scanning the Django code for SQL injection and hardcoded secrets in CI/CD, pip-audit checking Python dependencies for known CVEs, Hadolint linting the Dockerfile to ensure non-root USER and COPY instead of ADD, and Cosign signing the anihpj-api image so the admission controller can verify it came from the trusted CI pipeline before deployment.

1.2 Cloud Provider and Infrastructure SecurityQ7–Q11

Cloud Native Overview Q7 of 200
In a self-managed kubeadm cluster (like your 6-cluster lab), who is responsible for securing etcd?
A) YOU — in self-managed clusters, you are responsible for the ENTIRE stack: etcd, API server, certificates, kubelet, node OS, everything
B) The cloud provider — they secure etcd as part of the managed service
C) etcd is self-securing — it automatically encrypts all data and manages its own certificates
D) The kubelet handles etcd security automatically — no manual configuration needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

In a self-managed cluster (kubeadm, kops, or manual install), YOU are responsible for securing every component — etcd TLS certificates, etcd encryption at rest (EncryptionConfiguration), etcd peer authentication, etcd backups, node OS hardening, API server authentication, and everything else. The cloud provider (if using VMs) only secures the physical data center. This is the OPPOSITE of managed Kubernetes (EKS/AKS/GKE) where the provider manages the control plane.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Self-Managed vs Managed Kubernetes — Responsibility Comparison
  RESPONSIBILITY COMPARISON: Self-Managed (kubeadm) vs Managed (EKS)

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  COMPONENT           SELF-MANAGED       MANAGED (EKS/AKS/GKE)│
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  etcd                YOU ✅             Provider ✅           │
  │  (TLS, encryption,   • --cert-file      • AWS manages etcd   │
  │   backup, quorum)    • --peer-cert-file • You never SSH to CP│
  │                      • EncryptionConfig • Auto-backup + HA   │
  │                      • snapshot save                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  API Server          YOU ✅             Provider ✅           │
  │  (auth, TLS,         • --anonymous-auth • --anonymous-auth   │
  │   admission)         • --oidc-issuer    set by AWS            │
  │                      • audit policy     • You configure OIDC │
  │                                                              │
  │  Controller Manager  YOU ✅             Provider ✅           │
  │  + Scheduler         • Static pod YAML  • AWS manages these   │
  │                                                              │
  │  Worker Nodes        YOU ✅             YOU ✅                │
  │  (OS, kubelet,       • OS patching      • OS patching        │
  │   container runtime) • kubelet config   • kubelet config     │
  │                      • containerd/CRI-O • containerd/CRI-O   │
  │                                                              │
  │  Workloads           YOU ✅             YOU ✅                │
  │  (pods, RBAC,        • NetworkPolicy    • NetworkPolicy      │
  │   secrets, images)   • SecurityContext  • SecurityContext    │
  │                      • Image scanning   • Image scanning     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KEY INSIGHT: In self-managed, you own the ENTIRE control plane.
  This gives you full control but also full responsibility.
  For your 6-cluster lab (kubeadm): YOU must secure etcd,
  rotate certificates, configure encryption, and take backups.

For your 6-cluster lab (kubeadm on node "anih"): You are 100% responsible for etcd security. You must configure --cert-file, --key-file, --peer-cert-file, --client-cert-auth=true, set up EncryptionConfiguration for Secrets, and take regular etcd snapshots. If your etcd is compromised, your ENTIRE cluster — including all anihpj/jobpost data — is compromised.

Cloud Native Overview Q8 of 200
How does IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) integrate cloud provider IAM with Kubernetes security?
A) It stores AWS access keys as Kubernetes Secrets that pods can mount as volumes
B) It maps a Kubernetes ServiceAccount to an AWS IAM Role via OIDC federation — pods get temporary, auto-rotating credentials without any static keys
C) It creates an IAM user for each pod and injects the credentials as environment variables
D) It requires the node's IAM role to be shared with all pods running on that node
✅ Correct Answer: B

IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) uses OIDC federation to give each Kubernetes ServiceAccount its own AWS IAM Role — WITHOUT storing any static AWS credentials in the cluster. When a pod needs AWS access, it gets temporary STS credentials that auto-rotate every hour. A is wrong (static keys in Secrets), C is wrong (no IAM users created), D is wrong (node-level IAM is shared — IRSA is per-pod).

📝 Detailed Explanation
IRSA Flow — How a Pod Gets Temporary AWS Credentials
  IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) — AWS Pod Identity

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① ServiceAccount annotated with IAM Role ARN               │
  │     metadata:                                               │
  │       annotations:                                          │
  │         eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn: arn:aws:iam::ROLE       │
  │                                                             │
  │  ② Pod mounts projected ServiceAccount token (JWT)          │
  │     /var/run/secrets/eks.amazonaws.com/serviceaccount/token │
  │                                                             │
  │  ③ AWS SDK in pod reads the JWT token                       │
  │     → Calls STS AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity                   │
  │     → Passes JWT + Role ARN                                 │
  │                                                             │
  │  ④ STS validates the JWT against the EKS OIDC provider      │
  │     → Checks: Is this JWT from cluster X?                   │
  │     → Checks: Is this ServiceAccount allowed to assume       │
  │       this IAM Role? (Trust Policy)                         │
  │                                                             │
  │  ⑤ STS returns TEMPORARY credentials                        │
  │     • AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID (short-lived)                       │
  │     • AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY (short-lived)                   │
  │     • AWS_SESSION_TOKEN (expires in 1 hour)                 │
  │                                                             │
  │  ✅ NO static keys stored anywhere                           │
  │  ✅ Credentials auto-rotate every hour                       │
  │  ✅ Per-pod, not per-node — least privilege at pod level     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Azure equivalent: Azure AD Workload Identity
  GCP equivalent:   GCP Workload Identity Federation

For anihpj/jobpost on EKS: The anihpj-api pod's ServiceAccount is annotated with an IAM role that allows s3:GetObject on the media bucket ONLY. The anihpj-db pod's SA has an IAM role for rds:Connect only. Two pods, two different IAM roles, zero static credentials. If the api pod is compromised, the attacker gets temporary S3 read access — NOT database access, NOT DynamoDB access, NOT the ability to create new IAM users.

Cloud Native Overview Q9 of 200
Why must you protect the cloud metadata endpoint (169.254.169.254) from pod access?
A) It exposes the Kubernetes API server credentials, allowing pods to authenticate as cluster-admin
B) It allows pods to modify the node's kernel parameters, potentially crashing the host
C) It exposes the NODE's IAM credentials — a compromised pod can curl this endpoint to get AWS credentials and access cloud resources with the node's permissions
D) It's a deprecated endpoint that slows down pod startup time and should be disabled for performance
✅ Correct Answer: C

The cloud metadata endpoint at 169.254.169.254 (AWS) or 169.254.169.254 (Azure/GCP) exposes the NODE's IAM credentials. A compromised pod can simply curl http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/ → get the node's IAM role credentials → access S3, DynamoDB, RDS, or any other AWS service the node can access. This is a critical privilege escalation vector.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cloud Metadata Endpoint Attack — How a Compromised Pod Escalates to Cloud Access
  THE ATTACK: Pod → 169.254.169.254 → Node's IAM Credentials → Cloud API

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Attacker compromises anihpj-web pod (RCE vulnerability)  │
  │                                                             │
  │  ② Attacker checks: "Can I reach the metadata endpoint?"    │
  │     $ curl -s http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/      │
  │     → Returns: iam/  local-ipv4  public-ipv4  ...          │
  │                                                             │
  │  ③ Attacker fetches the NODE's IAM role credentials:        │
  │     $ curl -s http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/      │
  │       iam/security-credentials/node-group-role              │
  │     → Returns:                                              │
  │     {                                                       │
  │       "AccessKeyId": "AKIA...",                             │
  │       "SecretAccessKey": "wJalrX...",                       │
  │       "Token": "FQoGZX...",                                 │
  │       "Expiration": "2026-06-12T15:00:00Z"                  │
  │     }                                                       │
  │                                                             │
  │  ④ Attacker now has the NODE'S AWS permissions:             │
  │     $ aws s3 ls                    ← lists all S3 buckets   │
  │     $ aws dynamodb list-tables     ← enumerates databases   │
  │     $ aws rds describe-db-instances ← finds RDS instances   │
  │                                                             │
  │  ⚠️ ATTACKER ESCALATED FROM POD → CLOUD ACCOUNT ACCESS       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  DEFENSES (layered):
  ① iptables rule: block 169.254.169.254 from pod network
  ② NetworkPolicy: deny egress to 169.254.169.254/32
  ③ IRSA instead of node-level IAM (per-pod, not per-node)
  ④ IMDSv2 (AWS): requires session token (PUT request first)
  ⑤ Hop limit = 1 (IMDSv2): blocks requests from containers

For anihpj/jobpost security: Always use IRSA (per-pod IAM) instead of node-level IAM roles. Apply a NetworkPolicy that blocks egress to 169.254.169.254/32. Enable IMDSv2 with hop limit = 1 on all EC2 instances. If the anihpj-web pod is compromised, the attacker cannot reach the metadata endpoint and cannot escalate to cloud-level access.

Cloud Native Overview Q10 of 200
In a managed Kubernetes service (EKS, AKS, or GKE), which of the following is the PROVIDER'S responsibility under the Shared Responsibility Model?
A) Managing the control plane components — API server, etcd, scheduler, and controller-manager — including TLS certificates and version upgrades
B) Configuring NetworkPolicy rules to restrict pod-to-pod communication within your namespaces
C) Scanning your container images for CVEs and blocking deployment of vulnerable images
D) Creating RBAC roles and RoleBindings for your application ServiceAccounts
✅ Correct Answer: A

In managed Kubernetes, the provider manages the control plane — API server, etcd, scheduler, controller-manager, TLS certificates, and version upgrades. You never SSH into control plane nodes (they don't exist in your account). B, C, and D are all YOUR responsibility — NetworkPolicy, image scanning, and RBAC are workload-level controls that you configure via kubectl.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Managed Kubernetes — Who Does What (EKS Example)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AWS (PROVIDER) RESPONSIBILITY                              │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  ✅ API Server         — HA deployment, TLS, auth config     │
  │  ✅ etcd               — RAFT quorum, encryption, backup     │
  │  ✅ Scheduler          — Pod placement logic                 │
  │  ✅ Controller Manager — Reconciliation loops               │
  │  ✅ Control Plane Certs— Rotation, renewal                   │
  │  ✅ K8s Version Upgrades — Control plane upgrades            │
  │  ✅ AWS Infrastructure— Physical security, hypervisor        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  YOU (CUSTOMER) RESPONSIBILITY                              │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  ✅ Worker Nodes       — AMI patching, kubelet config        │
  │  ✅ NetworkPolicy      — Pod-to-pod firewall rules           │
  │  ✅ RBAC               — Roles, RoleBindings, SAs            │
  │  ✅ Pod Security       — SecurityContext, PSS, seccomp       │
  │  ✅ Secrets Encryption — EncryptionConfiguration (KMS)       │
  │  ✅ Image Security     — Scanning (Trivy), signing (Cosign)  │
  │  ✅ Audit Logging      — Audit policy configuration          │
  │  ✅ Application Data   — PostgreSQL encryption, backups      │
  │  ✅ Ingress + TLS      — cert-manager, Let's Encrypt         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CRITICAL DISTINCTION: The provider secures the CONTROL PLANE.
  You secure everything that runs ON the cluster. If your pod
  is compromised because you didn't set runAsNonRoot, that's
  YOUR responsibility — not the provider's.

For anihpj/jobpost on EKS: AWS ensures the API server is available and etcd is backed up. But YOU must configure RBAC to prevent the anihpj-api ServiceAccount from reading Secrets, set NetworkPolicy so only anihpj-api can reach anihpj-db:5432, enforce Pod Security Standards on the anihpj namespace, and encrypt Secrets with a KMS key. The provider gives you a secure platform — you build a secure application on top of it.

Cloud Native Overview Q11 of 200
What is the "attack surface" of a Kubernetes cluster?
A) The total number of pods running in the cluster at any given time
B) Only the API server endpoint (port 6443) — that's the single entry point
C) The combined CPU and memory resources available across all worker nodes
D) The sum of ALL points where an attacker could enter or cause damage — API server, kubelet, etcd, exposed dashboards, container images, supply chain, weak NetworkPolicies, and leaked credentials
✅ Correct Answer: D

The attack surface is the SUM of ALL entry points an attacker could exploit — not just the API server. It includes every component, network path, credential, and image that could be targeted. A is a pod count (not security), B ignores kubelet/etcd/network paths, C is about resources (not attack points).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes Attack Surface — All Entry Points
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ATTACK VECTOR              PORT/PATH       RISK LEVEL      │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  API Server                 6443            🔴 CRITICAL     │
  │  (kubectl, controllers, CI/CD — single entry to everything) │
  │                                                             │
  │  etcd                       2379            🔴 CRITICAL     │
  │  (all cluster state + secrets — if unencrypted)             │
  │                                                             │
  │  Kubelet API                10250           🔴 HIGH         │
  │  (pod exec, logs, specs on EVERY node)                      │
  │                                                             │
  │  Exposed Dashboard          varies          🟡 MEDIUM       │
  │  (K8s dashboard, Lens, Rancher — often misconfigured)       │
  │                                                             │
  │  Container Images           N/A             🟡 MEDIUM       │
  │  (CVEs, backdoors, :latest tag — supply chain risk)         │
  │                                                             │
  │  NetworkPolicy Gaps         N/A             🟡 MEDIUM       │
  │  (default-allow = any pod can reach any pod)                │
  │                                                             │
  │  Leaked kubeconfig          N/A             🟢 LOWER        │
  │  (committed to Git, shared on Slack, lost laptop)           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The attack surface includes the EKS API endpoint (public or private?), the PostgreSQL port (is NetworkPolicy blocking non-api traffic?), the Django admin panel (is it behind MFA?), container images (are they scanned and signed?), and developer kubeconfig files (are they in Git?). Reducing attack surface means closing unnecessary ports, applying default-deny NetworkPolicy, using private clusters, and never committing credentials.

1.3 Controls and FrameworksQ12–Q16

Cloud Native Overview Q12 of 200
Why does defense-in-depth matter in cloud native security?
A) No single security control is 100% effective — multiple layers ensure that if one control fails, others still protect the system
B) It reduces the number of security tools you need to install and manage
C) It allows you to skip lower-layer security if upper layers are strong enough
D) It's a compliance requirement — you only implement it to pass audits
✅ Correct Answer: A

Defense-in-depth means layering multiple security controls so that if ONE fails, the NEXT one still protects you. In Kubernetes, this means combining Cloud (IAM, VPC), Cluster (RBAC, NetworkPolicy), Container (SecurityContext, seccomp), and Code (SAST, input validation) — an attacker must breach ALL layers, not just one.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Defense-in-Depth — The Castle Analogy
  🏰 DEFENSE-IN-DEPTH AT anihpj/jobpost

  LAYER 1: Cloud         → AWS IAM + VPC (who can even SEE the cluster?)
  LAYER 2: Cluster       → RBAC + NetworkPolicy (what can you do inside?)
  LAYER 3: Container     → non-root + readOnlyRootFS (what if they breach a pod?)
  LAYER 4: Code          → CSRF + SQL injection protection (what if they reach the app?)

  ATTACKER must breach ALL 4 layers to steal PostgreSQL data:
  Cloud IAM → Cluster RBAC → Container isolation → Django auth
  If ANY layer stops them → ATTACK FAILS

For anihpj/jobpost: Even if an attacker bypasses the WAF (Cloud), they face NetworkPolicy (Cluster). If they breach the web pod, readOnlyRootFilesystem prevents malware installation (Container). If they reach the API, Django CSRF protection blocks request forgery (Code). No single point of failure.

Cloud Native Overview Q13 of 200
What is the Zero Trust security model?
A) Trust all internal traffic by default — only verify traffic coming from outside the cluster
B) "Never trust, always verify" — every request must be authenticated, authorized, and encrypted, regardless of origin
C) A model where you trust pods in the same namespace but not pods in other namespaces
D) A security model that relies entirely on firewall rules at the network perimeter
✅ Correct Answer: B

Zero Trust means NO implicit trust based on network location. Being "inside the cluster" or "in the same namespace" does NOT mean you're trusted. Every request — even pod-to-pod within the same namespace — must be authenticated, authorized, and ideally encrypted (mTLS).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Traditional Perimeter vs Zero Trust
  TRADITIONAL (Perimeter Security):        ZERO TRUST:
  ┌──────────────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │  🔓 INSIDE = TRUSTED     │            │  🔒 INSIDE = UNTRUSTED   │
  │  Any pod can reach any   │            │  Every connection needs:  │
  │  pod because they're     │            │  ① Authentication        │
  │  "inside the cluster"    │            │  ② Authorization (RBAC)  │
  │                          │            │  ③ Encryption (mTLS)     │
  │  Firewall at edge only   │            │  Default-deny everywhere  │
  └──────────────────────────┘            └──────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Zero Trust means the anihpj-web pod is NOT trusted just because it's in the anihpj namespace. It must present its ServiceAccount token (auth), have RBAC permission (authz), and all traffic to anihpj-api is encrypted via mTLS. Default-deny NetworkPolicy enforces this — only explicitly allowed connections work.

Cloud Native Overview Q14 of 200
How does Zero Trust apply to pod-to-pod communication in Kubernetes?
A) Pods in the same namespace are automatically trusted and can communicate freely
B) All pod-to-pod traffic is blocked — even DNS — for maximum security
C) Default-deny NetworkPolicy allows ONLY explicitly permitted connections; all other traffic (even within the same namespace) is blocked
D) Pods trust each other based on their ServiceAccount — no NetworkPolicy is needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

Zero Trust networking in Kubernetes means default-deny — ALL traffic is blocked unless a NetworkPolicy explicitly allows it. This applies even to pods in the SAME namespace. You then add specific ALLOW rules for each required communication path (web→api:8000, api→db:5432).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Zero Trust NetworkPolicy for anihpj/jobpost
  WITHOUT Zero Trust (default):          WITH Zero Trust (default-deny):
  ┌─────────────────────────┐           ┌─────────────────────────┐
  │ anihpj-web              │           │ anihpj-web              │
  │   ↓ can reach anyone    │           │   ↓ ✅ api:8000 (allowed)│
  │ anihpj-api              │           │ anihpj-api              │
  │   ↓ can reach anyone    │           │   ↓ ✅ db:5432 (allowed)│
  │ anihpj-db               │           │ anihpj-db               │
  │   ↓ can reach anyone    │           │   ❌ BLOCKED everywhere  │
  │ anihpj-redis            │           │ anihpj-redis            │
  │   ↓ can reach anyone    │           │   ❌ BLOCKED everywhere  │
  └─────────────────────────┘           └─────────────────────────┘

  RESULT: Compromised web pod can        RESULT: Compromised web pod can
  directly access the database!          ONLY reach api:8000 — trapped!

For anihpj/jobpost: Apply a default-deny NetworkPolicy first, then add: allow web→api:8000, allow api→db:5432, allow api→redis:6379, allow all→DNS (UDP 53). Every other connection — including attacker's reverse shell — is BLOCKED.

Cloud Native Overview Q15 of 200
What is the "principle of least privilege" and how does it apply in Kubernetes?
A) Give every ServiceAccount cluster-admin so pods never encounter permission errors
B) Every subject (user, ServiceAccount, pod) should have ONLY the permissions needed for its job — and nothing more
C) Only the cluster administrator should have any permissions; all other users get read-only access
D) Least privilege only applies to human users, not to ServiceAccounts or pods
✅ Correct Answer: B

Least privilege means giving ONLY the permissions required — and no more. In Kubernetes, this applies at every level: ServiceAccounts (anihpj-db needs ZERO K8s API access), RBAC roles (developers get pod-reader, not cluster-admin), NetworkPolicy (api can only reach db:5432, nothing else).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Least Privilege Applied to anihpj/jobpost
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  IDENTITY             NEEDS                  GETS           │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  anihpj-web SA        Serve web pages        NO K8s access  │
  │                        (no API calls)        automount:false│
  │                                                             │
  │  anihpj-api SA        Serve REST API         NO K8s access  │
  │                        (no API calls)        automount:false│
  │                                                             │
  │  anihpj-db SA         Store data             NO K8s access  │
  │                        (no API calls)        automount:false│
  │                                                             │
  │  anihpj-redis SA      Cache data             NO K8s access  │
  │                        (no API calls)        automount:false│
  │                                                             │
  │  CI/CD SA              Deploy to anihpj ns   create/update  │
  │                                                deployments  │
  │                                                             │
  │  Developer             Debug dev namespace   get/list/watch │
  │                        (read-only)           in dev ns only │
  │                                                             │
  │  Admin                 Manage cluster        cluster-admin  │
  │                        (ONLY 1-2 people!)                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: If the anihpj-api pod is compromised, the attacker gets NO Kubernetes API access (automountServiceAccountToken: false). They can't list pods, read secrets, or deploy backdoors. Blast radius = the api pod only.

Cloud Native Overview Q16 of 200
How does the Kubernetes API server enforce security controls for every request?
A) It only checks the user's kubeconfig file — if it's valid, all requests are allowed
B) It authenticates the user, then skips authorization for read-only operations like GET
C) It authenticates once at login and trusts all subsequent requests from that session
D) Every request goes through 3 stages: ① Authentication (WHO?), ② Authorization — RBAC (CAN they?), ③ Admission Control (SHOULD we?) — all three must pass
✅ Correct Answer: D

The API server enforces a 3-stage pipeline for EVERY request: ① Authentication — validate the certificate/token (WHO is this?), ② Authorization — check RBAC (CAN they do this specific action?), ③ Admission Control — apply policies (SHOULD we allow this? e.g., reject :latest tag). If ANY stage fails, the request is DENIED.

📝 Detailed Explanation
API Server Request Pipeline — 3 Gates
  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml
       │
       ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GATE 1: AUTHENTICATION — "Who are you?"                │
  │  • Validate X.509 cert, JWT token, or OIDC identity     │
  │  • Extract username + groups from the credential        │
  │  • ❌ Invalid cert → 401 Unauthorized                    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  GATE 2: AUTHORIZATION (RBAC) — "Can you do this?"      │
  │  • Check ALL RoleBindings + ClusterRoleBindings         │
  │  • Does any Role allow this verb on this resource?      │
  │  • ❌ No matching rule → 403 Forbidden                   │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  GATE 3: ADMISSION CONTROL — "Should you do this?"      │
  │  • Mutating webhooks (add default securityContext)      │
  │  • Validating webhooks (reject :latest tag)             │
  │  • ❌ Policy violation → request DENIED                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  ✅ ALL 3 GATES PASSED → Write to etcd → Pod created    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When a developer runs kubectl delete pod anihpj-api-7d8f9 -n anihpj, the API server: ① Authenticates via OIDC (WHO is this developer?), ② Checks RBAC (does this developer have "delete pods" in the anihpj namespace?), ③ Runs admission (does Kyverno policy allow this?). If the developer only has "get pods" permission → 403 Forbidden. Three independent gates, zero bypass.

1.4 Isolation TechniquesQ17–Q20

Cloud Native Overview Q17 of 200
What are the isolation boundaries in Kubernetes, from weakest to strongest?
A) Namespace → Container (shared kernel) → Node → Pod (shared net/IPC) → gVisor/Kata (VM-level)
B) All pods are fully isolated from each other by default — no additional configuration needed
C) Container isolation is the only boundary that matters — namespaces and nodes provide no isolation
D) Kubernetes provides hardware-level isolation for all pods without any additional runtime
✅ Correct Answer: A

Kubernetes offers layers of isolation: Namespace (RBAC + NetworkPolicy separation), Container (Linux namespaces + cgroups, but SHARE the host kernel), Pod (containers in same pod share network/IPC), Node (taints/tolerations for dedicated nodes), Sandboxed runtimes (gVisor = user-space kernel, Kata = per-pod VM — STRONGEST isolation). B is wrong (pods are NOT fully isolated — they share the kernel), C is wrong (namespace/node isolation also matters).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes Isolation Spectrum — Weakest → Strongest
  ISOLATION SPECTRUM:

  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WEAKER      │  Namespace (RBAC + NetworkPolicy)             │
  │              │  → Different teams, different namespaces      │
  │              │  → Soft boundary (still share nodes)          │
  │              ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │              │  Container (Linux namespaces + cgroups)       │
  │              │  → PID, net, mnt, UTS, IPC, user namespaces  │
  │              │  → SHARES THE HOST KERNEL (⚠️ key risk)       │
  │              ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │              │  Node (Taints + Tolerations)                  │
  │              │  → Dedicated hardware for sensitive workloads │
  │              │  → Stronger, but more expensive               │
  │              ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  STRONGER    │  gVisor (User-space kernel — Sentry)          │
  │              │  → ~200 safe syscalls only                    │
  │              ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │              │  Kata Containers (per-pod lightweight VM)     │
  │              │  → Each pod gets its OWN kernel               │
  │              │  → VM-level isolation, highest security       │
  └──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Use namespace isolation (anihpj namespace with strict RBAC), container isolation (non-root + readOnlyRootFS), and consider gVisor for the web tier (exposed to internet = higher risk). The DB tier can use default runc (not internet-facing).

Cloud Native Overview Q18 of 200
In anihpj/jobpost, what Kubernetes API permissions does the anihpj-db (PostgreSQL) pod actually NEED?
A) get, list, watch pods — to discover other database replicas
B) create, update secrets — to rotate its own database password
C) get nodes — to monitor cluster health for the database
D) NONE — the database pod never calls the Kubernetes API; set automountServiceAccountToken: false
✅ Correct Answer: D

The PostgreSQL pod stores and retrieves data — it never needs to call the Kubernetes API. Setting automountServiceAccountToken: false means no token is mounted in the pod at all. Even if an attacker gets code execution in the DB container, they have ZERO Kubernetes API access — no kubectl, no listing pods, no reading secrets.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ServiceAccount Token — The Key to the Kingdom
  WHAT A SERVICEACCOUNT TOKEN ALLOWS:

  /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token  ← JWT token
  → Authenticate to the K8s API as this ServiceAccount
  → What you can DO depends on RBAC

  IF token is mounted + SA has cluster-admin:
  🔴 Read ALL secrets (including other apps' credentials)
  🔴 Create privileged pods on any node
  🔴 Delete any resource in any namespace
  🔴 Impersonate other users and ServiceAccounts

  IF automountServiceAccountToken: false:
  🟢 TOKEN DOES NOT EXIST in the pod
  🟢 Attacker CANNOT call the K8s API at all
  🟢 Blast radius = the pod only (can't escalate)

For anihpj/jobpost: anihpj-web, anihpj-api, anihpj-db, and anihpj-redis ALL have automountServiceAccountToken: false. None of them need Kubernetes API access. The CI/CD ServiceAccount is the ONLY one with API access — scoped to create/update deployments in the anihpj namespace only.

Cloud Native Overview Q19 of 200
Why shouldn't you use the "default" ServiceAccount for all pods in a namespace?
A) The default ServiceAccount doesn't have any permissions, so pods can't function
B) If all pods share one ServiceAccount, compromising ANY pod gives the attacker the SAME permissions across ALL pods — create dedicated SAs per workload with minimal RBAC
C) Using the default SA is a Kubernetes best practice — it simplifies permission management
D) The default SA expires after 24 hours, causing pods to lose API access unexpectedly
✅ Correct Answer: B

The default ServiceAccount is automatically mounted in every pod unless disabled. If all pods share it, a compromise of ANY pod (even the least critical one) gives the attacker the SAME permissions as every other pod. Dedicated SAs per workload mean: compromise of web SA ≠ access to db SA permissions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Shared vs Dedicated ServiceAccounts — Blast Radius
  SHARED DEFAULT SA (BAD):               DEDICATED SAs (GOOD):
  ┌─────────────────────────┐           ┌─────────────────────────┐
  │ default SA (shared)     │           │ anihpj-web SA           │
  │   ↕ anihpj-web          │           │   ↕ anihpj-web          │
  │   ↕ anihpj-api          │           │                         │
  │   ↕ anihpj-db           │           │ anihpj-api SA           │
  │   ↕ anihpj-redis        │           │   ↕ anihpj-api          │
  │                         │           │                         │
  │ Compromise web pod →    │           │ anihpj-db SA            │
  │ token works for ALL     │           │   ↕ (automount: false)  │
  │ 🔴 BLAST RADIUS = ALL   │           │                         │
  └─────────────────────────┘           │ Compromise web pod →    │
                                        │ only web SA compromised │
                                        │ 🟢 BLAST RADIUS = web   │
                                        └─────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Each tier gets its own ServiceAccount with minimal permissions. anihpj-web SA: no K8s API access. anihpj-api SA: no K8s API access. anihpj-db SA: no K8s API access. CI/CD SA: create/update deployments only. Compromise of the web pod = zero lateral movement within the cluster.

Cloud Native Overview Q20 of 200
What is the "blast radius" concept and how do you minimize it?
A) The physical distance between cluster nodes — minimized by placing nodes in the same rack
