B) The number of pods an attacker can delete — minimized by setting replicas to 1
C) The amount of damage a single compromise can cause — minimized through namespace isolation, least privilege RBAC, NetworkPolicy, and dedicated ServiceAccounts
D) The time it takes to detect a breach — minimized by running Falco with default rules
✅ Correct Answer: C

Blast radius = the total damage an attacker can cause after compromising one component. Minimize it by: namespace isolation (separate RBAC per ns), least privilege RBAC (SAs with minimal permissions), NetworkPolicy (restrict pod communication), and dedicated ServiceAccounts (one per workload).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Blast Radius Reduction — Before vs After
  BEFORE (Large Blast Radius):            AFTER (Small Blast Radius):
  ┌───────────────────────────┐          ┌───────────────────────────┐
  │ cluster-admin SA (shared) │          │ anihpj-web SA (no access) │
  │ anihpj-web ← compromised! │          │ anihpj-web ← compromised! │
  │   ↓ Can:                  │          │   ↓ Can:                  │
  │   🔴 List ALL pods        │          │   🟢 Nothing (no K8s API) │
  │   🔴 Read ALL secrets     │          │                           │
  │   🔴 Delete anything      │          │ NetworkPolicy blocks:     │
  │   🔴 Create backdoor pods │          │   ❌ web → db (only api)  │
  │                           │          │   ❌ web → redis          │
  │ BLAST RADIUS: 💥 ENTIRE   │          │   ❌ web → internet       │
  │              CLUSTER       │          │                           │
  └───────────────────────────┘          │ BLAST RADIUS: 🎯 web pod  │
                                         │              ONLY          │
                                         └───────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: If anihpj-web is compromised, blast radius is MINIMIZED: the web SA has no K8s API access, NetworkPolicy blocks web→db and web→redis, and RBAC scoping means the attacker can't list pods or read secrets in other namespaces. The attacker is TRAPPED in the web pod.

1.5 Artifact Repository and Image SecurityQ21–Q24

Cloud Native Overview Q21 of 200
What is a container image registry and what security controls apply?
A) A service that stores and distributes container images — with controls: private access, image scanning (Trivy), tag immutability, image signing (Cosign), and lifecycle policies
B) A Kubernetes controller that automatically scales pods based on registry traffic
C) A database that stores pod logs and metrics for monitoring purposes
D) A command-line tool for building container images from Dockerfiles
✅ Correct Answer: A

A container registry (Docker Hub, ECR, GCR, Harbor, ACR) is where images are stored and distributed. Security controls include: private access (authenticated pull only), image scanning (detect CVEs before deployment), tag immutability (prevent tag overwrite attacks), image signing (Cosign — verify who built it), and lifecycle policies (auto-delete old images).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container Registry Security Controls
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  REGISTRY SECURITY LAYERS                                   │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🔐 Authentication    Who can PULL images?                  │
  │     • IAM (ECR), Service Principal (ACR), robot accounts   │
  │     • imagePullSecrets in pod spec                          │
  │                                                             │
  │  🔍 Image Scanning    Are there KNOWN vulnerabilities?      │
  │     • Trivy, Grype — check OS + language packages          │
  │     • Block deployment if CRITICAL/HIGH CVEs found          │
  │                                                             │
  │  🔏 Tag Immutability  Can someone overwrite :v1.2.3?        │
  │     • Prevent tag mutation — force new tags for new versions│
  │     • Stops "the image you tested ≠ what's deployed"        │
  │                                                             │
  │  ✍️ Image Signing     WHO built this image?                 │
  │     • Cosign + Sigstore (keyless, OIDC-based)               │
  │     • Admission controller verifies before deployment       │
  │                                                             │
  │  🗑️ Lifecycle Policies How many old images to keep?         │
  │     • Auto-delete untagged or old images                    │
  │     • Reduce attack surface (fewer images = fewer targets)  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: All images are stored in a private ECR registry. Trivy scans every image on push — CRITICAL CVEs block the CI/CD pipeline. Tags are immutable (anihpj-api:v1.2.3 cannot be overwritten). Cosign signs every image with the developer's OIDC identity. The admission controller rejects unsigned images.

Cloud Native Overview Q22 of 200
What is a "supply chain attack" in the context of container images?
A) An attacker physically steals the hard drives from a data center
B) An attack compromising software BEFORE it reaches the cluster — poisoned base images, malicious dependencies (npm/pip), compromised build servers, tampered registries, or typosquatted image names
C) A DDoS attack that overwhelms the container registry with download requests
D) An attacker sending phishing emails to Kubernetes administrators to steal their passwords
✅ Correct Answer: B

A supply chain attack targets software BEFORE it reaches production — poisoned base images on Docker Hub, malicious pip/npm packages, compromised CI/CD build servers, tampered container registries, or typosquatted image names (e.g., "anihpj-api" instead of "anihpj-api"). The attacker's code runs WITH your application's privileges from the moment it starts.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Supply Chain Attack Vectors
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ATTACK VECTOR              EXAMPLE                         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🐳 Poisoned Base Image     Attacker publishes               │
  │     (Docker Hub)            "python:3.12-slim-backdoor"     │
  │                             Developer accidentally uses it   │
  │                                                             │
  │  📦 Malicious Dependency    event-stream npm incident —      │
  │     (npm, pip, gem)         attacker gained publish rights  │
  │                             Added crypto-stealing code      │
  │                                                             │
  │  🏗️ Compromised Build       SolarWinds attack — attacker     │
  │     Server (CI/CD)          injected code during build       │
  │                             Signed by legitimate pipeline   │
  │                                                             │
  │  🏷️ Typosquatted Image      "anihpj-api" (typo) on Docker    │
  │     Name                    Hub with backdoor                │
  │                             Developer pulls wrong image     │
  │                                                             │
  │  🔑 Stolen Signing Keys     Attacker signs malicious image  │
  │                             with YOUR private key            │
  │                             Admission controller: ✅ ALLOW   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Defense against supply chain attacks: ① Pin all base images by SHA256 digest (not tags), ② Scan all dependencies with pip-audit, ③ Sign images with Cosign (keyless, OIDC), ④ Allow only images from the private ECR registry (OPA policy), ⑤ Verify Cosign signatures at deployment time (admission controller).

Cloud Native Overview Q23 of 200
Why should you pin container images by SHA256 digest instead of using tags?
A) SHA256 digests are shorter and easier to type than image tags
B) Tags are deprecated in Kubernetes 1.30+ — only digests are supported
C) Tags are MUTABLE pointers — :latest today ≠ :latest tomorrow; SHA256 digest is CONTENT-ADDRESSABLE — the hash IS the identity, so if content changes, the hash changes
D) Using digests improves pod startup time because Kubernetes caches images by digest
✅ Correct Answer: C

Tags are MUTABLE — an attacker who gains access to your registry can push a malicious image with the same tag. SHA256 digests are CONTENT-ADDRESSABLE — the hash is mathematically derived from the image content. If a single byte changes, the hash changes completely. Pinning by digest guarantees you always deploy the EXACT image that was tested and approved.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Tags vs Digests — Why Digests Win
  TAGS (Mutable — Dangerous):             DIGESTS (Immutable — Safe):

  Mon 9AM: anihpj-api:v1.2.3              Mon 9AM: anihpj-api@sha256:8a3f4b9c...
  → Image: clean, scanned, tested         → Image: clean, scanned, tested

  Mon 2PM: Attacker pushes new             Mon 2PM: Attacker CAN'T overwrite
  image with SAME tag "v1.2.3"             the digest — content changes →
  → Image: BACKDOORED!                     sha256:7d2e1f8a... (completely
  → Your deployment still uses :v1.2.3     different hash!)
  → Pulls the BACKDOORED image            → Your deployment still uses
  → 🔴 ATTACK SUCCEEDS                     sha256:8a3f4b9c... (original)
                                           → 🟢 ATTACK FAILS

For anihpj/jobpost: All deployments reference images by digest: image: 123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj-api@sha256:8a3f4b9c.... Even if someone pushes a malicious image with the same tag, our pods continue using the pinned digest. Combined with tag immutability in ECR, this provides defense-in-depth for image integrity.

Cloud Native Overview Q24 of 200
What is the security difference between a public and private container registry?
A) Public registries automatically scan images for vulnerabilities; private ones don't
B) There is no difference — both store container images the same way
C) Private registries are slower because they require VPN access
D) Public registries allow anyone to pull images (risk: untested images, supply chain attacks); private registries require authentication — you control what images exist, scan all images, enforce signing, and audit who pulled what
✅ Correct Answer: D

Public registries (Docker Hub) allow anyone to pull images — convenient but risky. Private registries (ECR, Harbor, ACR) require authentication for every pull. Benefits: you control exactly which images exist, every image is scanned for CVEs, all images must be signed, and every pull is audited (who pulled what, when).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Best Practice: Private Registry for Production
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PUBLIC REGISTRY (Docker Hub)           PRIVATE (ECR/Harbor)│
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🔓 Anyone can pull                     🔐 IAM auth required│
  │  ⚠️ Rate limits (100 pulls/6hr free)    ✅ No rate limits   │
  │  ⚠️ No control over image content       ✅ You curate images │
  │  ⚠️ Can't enforce scanning              ✅ Scan on every push│
  │  ⚠️ Can't enforce signing               ✅ Cosign verification│
  │  ⚠️ No pull audit trail                 ✅ CloudTrail audit  │
  │                                         ✅ Image immutability│
  │  USE FOR: Base images, OSS              USE FOR: Production  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  BEST PRACTICE: Pull base images FROM Docker Hub, build YOUR
  images, scan + sign them, push TO private registry.
  Production clusters pull ONLY from private registry.

For anihpj/jobpost: The production cluster is configured to pull ONLY from the private ECR registry (enforced by OPA policy). Base images like python:3.12-slim are pulled once, built upon, scanned, signed, and pushed to ECR. No pod in production ever pulls directly from Docker Hub.

1.6 Workload and Application Code SecurityQ25–Q28

Cloud Native Overview Q25 of 200
An anihpj-api pod is compromised by an RCE vulnerability. Without least privilege RBAC, what can the attacker do?
A) Nothing — the attacker is contained within the pod and cannot access anything else
B) If the ServiceAccount has cluster-admin: the attacker owns the ENTIRE cluster — read all secrets, delete all pods, exfiltrate all data. With least privilege RBAC: the attacker can only see pod names — blast radius MINIMIZED
C) The attacker can only access the pod's own filesystem — RBAC doesn't affect what a compromised pod can do
D) The attacker automatically gains access to all pods in the same namespace regardless of RBAC
✅ Correct Answer: B

The difference between cluster-admin and least privilege is the difference between total cluster compromise and contained pod breach. With cluster-admin, the attacker can read all secrets (including DB passwords and API tokens), create privileged backdoor pods, delete everything, and exfiltrate all data. With least privilege (only get/list pods in anihpj ns), the attacker can only see pod names — the blast radius is the pod itself.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Compromised Pod — Blast Radius by RBAC Level
  IF SA = cluster-admin:                    IF SA = minimal RBAC:
  ┌────────────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ 🔴 kubectl get secrets -A  │           │ 🟢 kubectl get pods -n      │
  │ → ALL credentials exposed  │           │   anihpj → pod names only  │
  │                            │           │                            │
  │ 🔴 kubectl create -f       │           │ 🟢 kubectl get secrets →   │
  │   backdoor-pod.yaml        │           │   FORBIDDEN (no RBAC)      │
  │ → privileged backdoor pod  │           │                            │
  │                            │           │ 🟢 kubectl delete pods →   │
  │ 🔴 kubectl delete pods -A  │           │   FORBIDDEN (no RBAC)      │
  │ → destroy entire cluster   │           │                            │
  │                            │           │ 🟢 kubectl exec →          │
  │ BLAST RADIUS: 💥 ENTIRE    │           │   FORBIDDEN (no RBAC)      │
  │              CLUSTER        │           │                            │
  └────────────────────────────┘           │ BLAST RADIUS: 🎯 pod only  │
                                           └────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The anihpj-api pod's ServiceAccount has automountServiceAccountToken: false — no K8s API access AT ALL. Even with RCE in the API container, the attacker cannot call kubectl, list pods, read secrets, or deploy backdoors. The only attack path is through the application itself (network connections to db:5432 and redis:6379, which NetworkPolicy allows).

Cloud Native Overview Q26 of 200
What is the difference between SAST and DAST for application security?
A) SAST analyzes source code WITHOUT running it (finds SQL injection, XSS, hardcoded secrets at the PR stage); DAST tests the RUNNING application from outside (finds runtime misconfigurations, exposed endpoints, auth bypasses)
B) SAST is for Python applications; DAST is for JavaScript applications
C) SAST runs in production; DAST runs in development
D) They are the same thing — both scan source code for vulnerabilities
✅ Correct Answer: A

SAST (Static) = analyze source code without running it — finds SQL injection patterns, hardcoded secrets, and vulnerable function calls at the PR stage (shift left). DAST (Dynamic) = test the RUNNING application from the outside — finds runtime misconfigurations, exposed debug endpoints, and authentication bypasses that only appear when the app is live. Both are needed — SAST catches code-level issues early, DAST validates runtime security.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SAST vs DAST — Complementary Security Testing
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SAST (STATIC)                     DAST (DYNAMIC)           │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  🔬 Analyzes: Source code          🔬 Tests: Running app    │
  │  ⏱️ When: PR / CI/CD pipeline      ⏱️ When: Staging / Prod  │
  │  🎯 Finds:                         🎯 Finds:                │
  │    • SQL injection patterns           • Exposed /debug/     │
  │    • Hardcoded secrets (AKIA...)      • Missing auth on API │
  │    • XSS vulnerabilities              • Weak TLS config     │
  │    • Dangerous functions (eval)       • Open ports          │
  │  🛠️ Tools: Bandit, Semgrep,        🛠️ Tools: OWASP ZAP,     │
  │    SonarQube, CodeQL                 Burp Suite, Nikto      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: SAST via Bandit scans the Django code at every PR — blocks SQL injection patterns and hardcoded secrets before merge. DAST via OWASP ZAP scans the staging deployment — checks if /admin is exposed, if DEBUG=True in settings, and if API endpoints require authentication. SAST + DAST = defense-in-depth for application security.

Cloud Native Overview Q27 of 200
For anihpj/jobpost, what application-level security controls exist in the Django framework?
A) Django has no built-in security — all security must be implemented from scratch
B) Django only provides authentication — no protection against XSS, CSRF, or SQL injection
C) Django provides CSRF tokens (cross-site request forgery protection), XSS protection (auto-escaping templates), SQL injection prevention (ORM parameterized queries), secure session cookies, and HSTS/SSL redirect support
D) Django security is handled entirely by the Kubernetes NetworkPolicy — no application-level controls needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

Django provides defense-in-depth at the Code layer: CSRF tokens on all POST forms, auto-escaping of user input in templates (XSS prevention), ORM parameterized queries (SQL injection prevention), secure session cookies (HttpOnly, Secure flags), clickjacking protection (X-Frame-Options), and support for HSTS and SSL redirect. These are the innermost layer of the 4 Cs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Django Security Controls — The Code Layer in Action
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DJANGO SECURITY CONTROL        WHAT IT PROTECTS AGAINST    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  {% csrf_token %}               Cross-Site Request Forgery  │
  │  (every POST form)              Attacker's site can't       │
  │                                 submit forms as your user   │
  │                                                             │
  │  Auto-escaping templates        Cross-Site Scripting (XSS)  │
  │  {{ user_input }} → escaped        │
  │                                 becomes harmless text       │
  │                                                             │
  │  ORM parameterized queries      SQL Injection               │
  │  Job.objects.filter(id=user_in) "'; DROP TABLE users; --"   │
  │                                 is treated as VALUE, not SQL│
  │                                                             │
  │  SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE=True     Session hijacking            │
  │  SESSION_COOKIE_HTTPONLY=True   Cookie sent only via HTTPS   │
  │                                 JS can't read session cookie │
  │                                                             │
  │  SECURE_SSL_REDIRECT=True       Downgrade attacks            │
  │  SECURE_HSTS_SECONDS=31536000   Forces HTTPS for 1 year      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Django's built-in security is the Code layer of defense. Even if an attacker bypasses Cloud (AWS IAM), Cluster (RBAC), and Container (SecurityContext) — they still face CSRF tokens, SQL injection prevention, and XSS protection at the application level. Four layers must all fail for the attacker to succeed.

Cloud Native Overview Q28 of 200
What makes the anihpj-web pod's attack surface different from anihpj-api?
A) They have the same attack surface — both run Django behind gunicorn
B) anihpj-api has a larger attack surface because it connects to the database
C) anihpj-web is more secure because it uses nginx as a reverse proxy
D) anihpj-web faces North-South traffic from the internet via Ingress — exposed to potentially malicious actors; anihpj-api only receives East-West traffic from web (internal) — web has a LARGER attack surface requiring WAF, rate limiting, and CSP headers
✅ Correct Answer: D

anihpj-web is exposed to the INTERNET via Ingress — it faces malicious bots, script kiddies, and targeted attacks from anywhere in the world. anihpj-api only receives traffic from anihpj-web internally (East-West). The web pod has a LARGER attack surface and needs stronger perimeter defenses: WAF, rate limiting, IP filtering, CSP headers, and input validation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
North-South vs East-West Traffic — Security Implications
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  INTERNET (North-South)                                     │
  │  🌍 Anyone can reach this                                   │
  │     │                                                       │
  │     ▼                                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐  ← WAF, rate limiting, IP filtering   │
  │  │  Ingress NGINX   │  ← TLS termination                    │
  │  └────────┬─────────┘                                       │
  │           │                                                 │
  │           ▼                                                 │
  │  ┌────────────────┐  🔴 LARGER ATTACK SURFACE               │
  │  │  anihpj-web    │  • Exposed to internet                  │
  │  │  (nginx+static)│  • Needs: WAF, CSP, rate limiting       │
  │  └────────┬───────┘                                         │
  │           │ East-West (internal cluster traffic)            │
  │           ▼                                                 │
  │  ┌────────────────┐  🟡 SMALLER ATTACK SURFACE              │
  │  │  anihpj-api    │  • Only reached by web pod               │
  │  │  (gunicorn)    │  • NetworkPolicy: web→api:8000 only     │
  │  └────────┬───────┘  • Not internet-facing                  │
  │           │                                                 │
  │           ▼                                                 │
  │  ┌────────────────┐  🟢 MINIMAL ATTACK SURFACE              │
  │  │  anihpj-db     │  • Only reached by api pod               │
  │  │  (PostgreSQL)  │  • NetworkPolicy: api→db:5432 only      │
  │  └────────────────┘  • Never exposed to internet             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The web tier gets additional security controls: AWS WAF (SQL injection + XSS rules), rate limiting (100 req/min per IP), CSP headers (restrict which domains can load scripts), and a dedicated NetworkPolicy that only allows Ingress→web:80 and web→api:8000. The api and db tiers are protected by internal NetworkPolicy only — they don't need WAF because they're not internet-facing.

🔐 Category 2: Kubernetes Cluster Component Security (22%)Q29-Q72

🟡 Intermediate: Securing Every Cluster Component

This category is about securing EVERY component of the Kubernetes cluster — from the API server to the container runtime, from etcd to kubelet. Each component is a potential attack vector. If an attacker owns any one of them, they can compromise the entire cluster. Think of it like securing every door, window, and ventilation shaft of your fortress.

Q29-Q72 44 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

2.1 API ServerQ29–Q34

Cluster Components Q29 of 200
What happens when a request arrives at the Kubernetes API server?
A) The request is immediately written to etcd — authentication happens later
B) The API server only checks if the user's kubeconfig file exists
C) The API server forwards the request directly to the kubelet without any checks
D) ① TLS Termination → ② Authentication (Who?) → ③ Authorization — RBAC (Can they?) → ④ Mutating Admission → ⑤ Schema Validation → ⑥ Validating Admission → ⑦ etcd Write — every step is a security gate
✅ Correct Answer: D

The API server processes EVERY request through a 7-stage pipeline. Authentication verifies identity (X.509, JWT, OIDC), authorization checks RBAC, mutating admission can inject sidecars, schema validation ensures valid objects, validating admission can reject (e.g., block :latest), and finally etcd write with encryption. Any stage can DENY the request.

📝 Detailed Explanation
API Server Request Pipeline — All 7 Stages
  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml
       │
       ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ① TLS TERMINATION — Decrypt the HTTPS request               │
  │ ② AUTHENTICATION — "WHO is making this request?"            │
  │    X.509 cert / JWT SA token / OIDC (Google, Azure AD)      │
  │ ③ AUTHORIZATION (RBAC) — "CAN this user do this action?"    │
  │    Check RoleBindings + ClusterRoleBindings                 │
  │ ④ MUTATING ADMISSION — Modify request (inject sidecar)      │
  │ ⑤ SCHEMA VALIDATION — Is this a valid Kubernetes object?    │
  │ ⑥ VALIDATING ADMISSION — Reject :latest, require labels     │
  │ ⑦ etcd WRITE — Persist with encryption if configured        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When deploying a new version, the API server authenticates via OIDC, checks RBAC (can this dev update deployments in anihpj ns?), runs Kyverno policies (SecurityContext present? Image from ECR?), and only then writes to etcd. Seven independent gates — all must pass.

Cluster Components Q30 of 200
Why should you set --anonymous-auth=false on the API server?
A) Anonymous auth is faster — it skips the RBAC check entirely
B) With anonymous auth enabled, unauthenticated requests map to "system:anonymous" and STILL pass through RBAC — a misconfigured RoleBinding could expose the cluster. Setting false means "No credentials = No access. Period."
C) It's required for OIDC authentication to work
D) Anonymous auth only affects the deprecated read-only port 10255
✅ Correct Answer: B

With anonymous auth enabled, requests without credentials are mapped to system:anonymous (group system:unauthenticated) and STILL checked against RBAC. A single misconfigured RoleBinding granting permissions to system:unauthenticated exposes the entire cluster — no credentials needed. --anonymous-auth=false means the API server immediately rejects any request without credentials.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Anonymous Auth — Fail Closed, Not Open
  --anonymous-auth=true (DANGER):           --anonymous-auth=false (SAFE):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Request: No credentials    │           │ Request: No credentials    │
  │ → Maps to system:anonymous │           │ ❌ 401 Unauthorized         │
  │ → RBAC check: Does any     │           │    IMMEDIATELY REJECTED    │
  │   RoleBinding grant access │           │    (No RBAC check)         │
  │   to system:unauthent.?    │           └────────────────────────────┘
  │ → If YES: 🔴 ANONYMOUS     │
  │   ACCESS GRANTED!          │
  └────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: --anonymous-auth=false on the API server. Every request must present valid credentials. Even if RBAC is catastrophically misconfigured, anonymous users never reach the RBAC check. Fail closed, not open.

Cluster Components Q31 of 200
What are the three main authentication methods in Kubernetes?
A) X.509 Client Certificates (kubelet, control plane), Bearer Tokens / JWT ServiceAccount tokens (pods, time-bound), and OpenID Connect / OIDC (human users via Google, GitHub, Azure AD)
B) Username/password, SSH keys, and API tokens stored in ConfigMaps
C) LDAP, Active Directory, and SAML — same as traditional enterprise apps
D) Kubernetes only supports X.509 certificates — all others need plugins
✅ Correct Answer: A

X.509 certificates — used by kubelet and control plane, strong but hard to rotate. JWT ServiceAccount tokens — used by pods via projected volumes, time-bound (1h), auto-rotated. OIDC — delegates auth to Google/GitHub/Azure AD, best for humans (MFA, centralized lifecycle, no shared kubeconfig).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes Authentication Matrix
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  METHOD          WHO USES IT       PROS           CONS      │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  X.509 Certs     kubelet, CM,     Strong identity  Hard to │
  │  (CN=user,O=grp) scheduler, etcd  No token steal   rotate  │
  │                                                             │
  │  Bearer/JWT      Pods via SA      Time-bound (1h)  Expires │
  │  (SA tokens)     CI/CD pipelines  Auto-rotate       Refresh │
  │                                                             │
  │  OIDC            Human users      MFA support      Needs   │
  │  (Google,Azure)  Developers       Central lifecycle IdP     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Humans → OIDC (Azure AD + MFA). CI/CD → dedicated SA tokens. kubelet → X.509 certs. Three methods, zero shared credentials.

Cluster Components Q32 of 200
How does the API server protect against man-in-the-middle (MITM) attacks?
A) It uses a shared secret key that all clients must know
B) It only accepts connections from localhost
C) TLS encryption for ALL communications, client certificate validation, JWT tokens signed by the API server, and all certificates chained to the cluster CA established at kubeadm init
D) MITM is not a concern because Kubernetes runs on a private network
✅ Correct Answer: C

The API server uses mutual TLS — it presents its certificate AND requires clients to present valid certificates. ServiceAccount tokens are JWTs signed by the API server — the signature proves authenticity. All certificates chain to the cluster CA (established at kubeadm init). A MITM attacker cannot forge a certificate signed by the CA without the CA's private key.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Mutual TLS — Both Sides Verify
  STANDARD TLS:              MUTUAL TLS (KUBERNETES):
  ┌────────┐   ┌────────┐   ┌────────┐   ┌────────┐
  │ Client │→→→│ Server │   │ Client │→→→│ Server │
  │        │←cert│        │   │        │←cert│        │
  │ verify │   │        │   │ verify │   │ verify │
  │ server │   │        │   │ server │   │ client │
  │   ✓    │   │        │   │   ✓    │   │ cert ✓ │
  └────────┘   └────────┘   └────────┘   └────────┘
  Only server verified        BOTH sides verified

For anihpj/jobpost: The cluster CA is backed up securely off-cluster. All certificates are monitored for expiration (kubeadm certs check-expiration). If the CA key is lost, the entire cluster's trust chain is broken — CA backup is the #1 disaster recovery priority.

Cluster Components Q33 of 200
What happens if you lose the cluster's CA certificate and private key?
A) CATASTROPHE — the CA signs ALL certificates; without it, new certs can't be issued, expiring certs can't be renewed (cluster stops), and new SA tokens can't be created. Restore from backup or rebuild the cluster
B) You can regenerate the CA with "kubeadm init --regenerate-ca"
C) Only new nodes are affected — existing ones continue working
D) The CA is only used during initial setup — not needed day-to-day
✅ Correct Answer: A

Losing the CA is a catastrophic failure. The CA signs EVERY certificate — API server, kubelet, etcd peers, SA tokens. Without it: new nodes can't get valid certs to join, certificates expire and can't be renewed (cluster grinds to a halt), new SA tokens can't be signed (pods can't authenticate). Back up /etc/kubernetes/pki/ca.crt and ca.key off-cluster.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Certificate Chain — Everything Depends on the CA
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                  CLUSTER CA (ca.crt + ca.key)                │
  │         ┌───────────────┼───────────────┐                   │
  │         ▼               ▼               ▼                   │
  │  apiserver.crt    kubelet.crt      etcd-peer.crt            │
  │                                                             │
  │  ServiceAccount tokens also signed by the CA key             │
  │  ⚠️ BACK UP: /etc/kubernetes/pki/ca.crt + ca.key            │
  │  ⚠️ STORE: Off-cluster (S3, Vault, secure backup)           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: CA is backed up daily to an encrypted S3 bucket. Quarterly restoration tests verify the backup works. Without this, CA loss means rebuilding the entire cluster and redeploying all of anihpj/jobpost from scratch.

Cluster Components Q34 of 200
How does the NodeRestriction admission controller improve security?
A) It prevents nodes from running any pods
B) It limits a kubelet to ONLY modifying its own node's objects — a compromised kubelet on worker-1 cannot modify labels on worker-2, read secrets from pods on worker-2, or impersonate another kubelet
C) It automatically patches vulnerable kubelet versions
D) It restricts which container images a node can pull
✅ Correct Answer: B

NodeRestriction ensures a kubelet can ONLY modify objects related to its OWN node. A compromised kubelet on worker-1 CAN update its own Node status, but CANNOT modify labels/taints on worker-2, read secrets from pods on worker-2, or impersonate another kubelet. Works with the Node authorizer for defense-in-depth.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NodeRestriction — Limiting Kubelet Blast Radius
  WITHOUT NodeRestriction:                  WITH NodeRestriction:
  ┌────────────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Kubelet worker-1 (COMPRO)  │           │ Kubelet worker-1 (COMPRO)  │
  │ ✅ Update worker-1 status  │           │ ✅ Update worker-1 status  │
  │ 🔴 Update worker-2 labels  │           │ ❌ Update worker-2 labels  │
  │ 🔴 Read worker-2 secrets   │           │ ❌ Read worker-2 secrets   │
  │ BLAST: ALL NODES           │           │ BLAST: worker-1 ONLY       │
  └────────────────────────────┘           └────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: NodeRestriction + Node authorizer ensure that a compromised kubelet on a worker running anihpj-web cannot access secrets from pods running anihpj-db on a different worker. Defense-in-depth at the node level.

2.2 Controller ManagerQ35–Q37

Cluster Components Q35 of 200
What is the security role of the kube-controller-manager?
A) It only manages pod scheduling — security is handled elsewhere
B) It runs controllers that reconcile desired vs actual state — including the ServiceAccount token controller (creates tokens for every SA). A compromised controller-manager could create backdoor SAs with cluster-admin privileges
C) It acts as a firewall between pods, enforcing NetworkPolicy
D) It's a deprecated component — all functions moved to the API server
✅ Correct Answer: B

The controller-manager runs critical controllers including the SA token controller (creates JWT tokens for every SA) and certificate signing controller. A compromised controller-manager could create backdoor ServiceAccounts with cluster-admin tokens. It must run with minimal RBAC and all its actions must be audited.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Controller Manager — Key Controllers
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONTROLLER              SECURITY IMPACT                    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  SA Token Controller     Creates JWT tokens for ALL SAs     │
  │                          Compromised → backdoor SA token    │
  │  Certificate Signing     Approves kubelet cert requests     │
  │                          Compromised → MITM/impersonation   │
  │  Node Controller         Monitors node health               │
  │                          Compromised → falsely mark NotReady│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Controller-manager uses its own kubeconfig (not cluster-admin). Audit logs track all token creation. Falco alerts on unexpected SA token creation.

Cluster Components Q36 of 200
What security properties does the cloud-controller-manager add?
A) It manages cloud provider authentication for all pods
B) It encrypts all pod-to-pod traffic using cloud TLS certificates
C) It interacts with cloud provider APIs to create load balancers and manage volumes — must use dedicated IAM roles with least privilege to prevent cloud-level escalation
D) It replaces the kube-controller-manager in cloud environments
✅ Correct Answer: C

The cloud-controller-manager bridges K8s with cloud APIs — creating load balancers (could expose services), managing volumes, updating node metadata. Its IAM role must have LEAST PRIVILEGE: allow only ELB and EC2 operations needed, NEVER AdministratorAccess. A compromised cloud-controller-manager with broad IAM could create backdoor IAM users.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cloud Controller IAM — Least Privilege
  ✅ ALLOW: elasticloadbalancing:* (create/delete LBs)
  ✅ ALLOW: ec2:DescribeInstances (node metadata)
  ✅ ALLOW: ec2:AttachVolume, DetachVolume
  ❌ DENY:  iam:CreateUser (backdoor IAM user)
  ❌ DENY:  s3:ListBuckets (no storage access needed)
  ❌ DENY:  kms:Decrypt (no key access needed)

For anihpj/jobpost on EKS: The cloud-controller-manager IAM role allows ONLY ELB + EC2 operations. It CANNOT access S3, DynamoDB, or KMS. Compromised controller = limited to load balancer operations.

Cluster Components Q37 of 200
How does the controller-manager authenticate to the API server?
A) Via X.509 client certificate signed by the cluster CA, referenced by --kubeconfig=/etc/kubernetes/controller-manager.conf with restricted file permissions (root-only, chmod 600)
B) Using username/password stored in a Kubernetes Secret
C) It doesn't need to authenticate — it runs inside the API server process
D) Via OIDC — it logs in using the cluster administrator's Google account
✅ Correct Answer: A

The controller-manager uses an X.509 client certificate (/etc/kubernetes/controller-manager.conf) with chmod 600 (root-only). Its CN is system:kube-controller-manager, bound to a ClusterRole with precise permissions — no more than needed. Each control plane component gets its OWN certificate and kubeconfig.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Control Plane Component Authentication
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  COMPONENT           CONFIG FILE              CN (IDENTITY) │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  controller-manager  controller-manager.conf  system:kube-  │
  │                      (chmod 600, root-only)   controller-mgr│
  │  scheduler           scheduler.conf           system:kube-  │
  │                      (chmod 600, root-only)   scheduler     │
  │  kubelet (per node)  kubelet.conf             system:node:  │
  │                      (chmod 600, root-only)   <node-name>   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every component authenticates with its own certificate. No shared credentials. File permissions are strict. Compromised controller-manager pod → limited RBAC, not cluster-admin.

2.3 SchedulerQ38–Q40

Cluster Components Q38 of 200
What is the security role of the kube-scheduler?
A) The scheduler only assigns pods to nodes — no security implications
B) The scheduler decides WHERE pods run — compromised scheduler could place sensitive pods on attacker-controlled nodes, refuse to schedule security tools (Falco, OPA), or concentrate all pods on one node (DoS via resource exhaustion)
C) The scheduler enforces NetworkPolicy by programming iptables rules
D) The scheduler authenticates users before they can run kubectl
✅ Correct Answer: B

The scheduler uses a 3-phase algorithm: Filtering (eliminate nodes lacking resources or with taints), Scoring (rank by fitness), Binding (assign to highest score). A compromised scheduler could skip security tool nodes, direct pods to attacker nodes, or concentrate all pods on one node (DoS). Run with minimal RBAC, audit all bindings.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Scheduler — Healthy vs Compromised
  HEALTHY:                                  COMPROMISED:
  ① FILTER: Remove nodes lacking CPU/RAM     ① FILTER: Remove nodes running
     or with taints not tolerated                Falco/OPA! (blind security)
  ② SCORE: Rank by least requested           ② SCORE: Rank attacker's node
     (spread pods evenly)                        HIGHEST (all pods → one node)
  ③ BIND: Pod → highest-scoring node         ③ BIND: Pod → attacker's node

For anihpj/jobpost: Taints on control plane nodes repel app pods. Node affinity pins anihpj-db to SSD nodes. Pod anti-affinity spreads anihpj-api replicas across zones. All binding decisions audited.

Cluster Components Q39 of 200
How does the scheduler decide which node to place a pod on?
A) Randomly — any Ready node can receive any pod
B) Round-robin — distributed evenly in order
C) ① Filtering (eliminate nodes lacking resources or with taints), ② Scoring (rank by fitness — least requested, balanced), ③ Binding (assign to highest-scoring node)
D) It asks the API server which node to use — doesn't decide itself
✅ Correct Answer: C

The scheduler's 3-phase algorithm ensures pods land on suitable nodes. Filtering removes nodes without enough CPU/RAM or with un-tolerated taints. Scoring ranks remaining nodes by utilization (spread pods, balance resources). Binding creates a Binding object in the API server. Security: taints/tolerations prevent pods from landing on restricted nodes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Scheduler 3-Phase Algorithm
  Pod: anihpj-api (500m CPU, 256Mi RAM)

  FILTER: 3 nodes → 2 candidates
  Node-1: CPU 1/4, RAM 2/8, No taint → PASS ✅
  Node-2: CPU 4/4 (FULL) → FAIL ❌
  Node-3: CPU 2/4, RAM 4/8, No taint → PASS ✅

  SCORE: Node-1 (75% free) > Node-3 (50% free)
  BIND: anihpj-api → Node-1 ✅

For anihpj/jobpost: Scheduler uses node affinity (SSD for DB), pod anti-affinity (spread API across nodes), and tolerations (critical addons on control plane). Every scheduling decision logged.

Cluster Components Q40 of 200
How can taints and tolerations be used as a security control?
A) Taints encrypt pod-to-pod traffic
B) Taints are purely for resource management — no security function
C) Taints replace NetworkPolicy — they control pod communication
D) Taint critical nodes with NoSchedule to repel all pods except those with specific tolerations — "only the security DaemonSet can run on the control plane" prevents workload pods from running on infrastructure nodes
✅ Correct Answer: D

Tainting control plane nodes with node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane:NoSchedule ensures ONLY pods with matching tolerations (system components) can run there. Application pods — and any pod deployed by an attacker — are repelled. This is node-level access control: "this node is reserved for system workloads only."

📝 Detailed Explanation
Taints as Security Boundaries
  CONTROL PLANE (Tainted: NoSchedule):
  ✅ kube-apiserver, etcd, scheduler (have toleration)
  ✅ Falco DaemonSet (has toleration)
  ❌ anihpj-web (no toleration → REJECTED)
  ❌ Attacker's pod (no toleration → REJECTED) 🔒

  WORKER NODES (No taint):
  ✅ anihpj-web, api, db, redis — all accepted

For anihpj/jobpost: All control plane nodes tainted with NoSchedule. Only system-critical DaemonSets tolerate it. Even if an attacker deploys a privileged pod, it cannot schedule on the control plane.

2.4 KubeletQ41–Q45

Cluster Components Q41 of 200
Why is the kubelet considered a critical security boundary?
A) Runs on EVERY node with root-level access — starts/stops containers, reads pod specs including Secrets, accesses node filesystem. Compromised kubelet = compromised node
B) It only manages container logs — compromise exposes log data only
C) Kubelet runs in a container itself, isolated from the host
D) Being phased out — containerd handles all its functions now
✅ Correct Answer: A

The kubelet is the node agent running on EVERY node with root-equivalent access. It starts/stops containers (CRI), reads pod specs including mounted Secrets, accesses the node filesystem (hostPath), and reports node status. Port 10250 serves the Kubelet API — MUST use TLS + auth. A compromised kubelet can exec into any pod on that node and read all its Secrets.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubelet — What It Controls
  Port 10250: Kubelet API (MUST use TLS + auth!)
  • Pod specs — includes mounted Secrets (read credentials)
  • kubectl exec — run commands in ANY pod on this node
  • kubectl logs — read logs from ANY pod on this node

  Port 10255: Read-only (DEPRECATED — disable!)
  Port 10248: Health check (safe)

  Filesystem: /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ (static pods)
              /var/lib/kubelet/ (pod data, secrets, configs)

For anihpj/jobpost: Every kubelet hardened: --anonymous-auth=false, --authorization-mode=Webhook, TLS with auto-rotation, port 10250 firewalled to control plane only.

Cluster Components Q42 of 200
Why should you set --anonymous-auth=false on the kubelet?
A) Improves kubelet performance by skipping auth checks
B) With anonymous auth enabled, unauthenticated requests to port 10250 map to "system:anonymous" and still pass through authorization — if authz is misconfigured, anonymous users could exec into pods or read pod specs (containing Secrets)
C) Required for the kubelet to join the cluster
D) Only affects the deprecated read-only port 10255
✅ Correct Answer: B

Same pattern as the API server — anonymous auth maps unauthenticated requests to system:anonymous which then passes through authorization. A misconfigured RBAC could allow anonymous users to exec into pods or read pod specs. --anonymous-auth=false → no credentials → immediately rejected. Combined with --authorization-mode=Webhook for defense-in-depth.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubelet Anonymous Auth
  --anonymous-auth=true (DANGER):
  curl -k https://node:10250/pods (no credentials)
  → Maps to system:anonymous → Authorization check →
  → If "get pods" allowed for system:unauthenticated:
  → 🔴 ANONYMOUS ACCESS TO POD SPECS (INCLUDING SECRETS)

  --anonymous-auth=false (SAFE):
  curl -k https://node:10250/pods (no credentials)
  → ❌ 401 Unauthorized — IMMEDIATELY REJECTED

For anihpj/jobpost: Every kubelet: --anonymous-auth=false + --authorization-mode=Webhook. Even if RBAC misconfigured, anonymous requests never reach authorization.

Cluster Components Q43 of 200
How does the kubelet authenticate to the API server?
A) Via X.509 client certificate (kubelet.crt) signed by cluster CA — CN must be "system:node:<node-name>" and O must be "system:nodes", mapping to RBAC groups with precise kubelet permissions
B) Using a shared cluster-admin token for all kubelets
C) Via OIDC — each kubelet logs in with a Google service account
D) Kubelets are trusted implicitly — no authentication needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

Each kubelet has a unique X.509 certificate with CN=system:node:<node-name> and O=system:nodes. The Node authorizer further restricts each kubelet to ONLY its own node's resources. Combined with NodeRestriction admission controller, this provides three layers of kubelet access control.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubelet Certificate Authentication
  Certificate: CN=system:node:worker-1, O=system:nodes
  ↓
  RBAC: ClusterRoleBinding system:node
    Subjects: Group system:nodes ← O matches!
    RoleRef: ClusterRole system:node (precise kubelet permissions)
  ↓
  Node Authorizer: "ONLY access worker-1 objects"
  ↓
  NodeRestriction: "ONLY modify worker-1 objects"

For anihpj/jobpost: Each kubelet has unique cert. Worker-1's kubelet cannot impersonate worker-2. Three-layer access control: RBAC + Node authorizer + NodeRestriction.

Cluster Components Q44 of 200
What are static pods and what unique security risk do they pose?
A) Pods that never restart — "static" means they don't respond to health checks
B) Pods defined in a Deployment with replicas: 1
C) Pods defined by YAML in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ — kubelet creates them directly, BYPASSING the API server's authentication, RBAC, and admission control. Write access to this directory = instant cluster compromise
D) Pods that use static IP addresses instead of cluster IPs
✅ Correct Answer: C

Static pods bypass the ENTIRE API server security pipeline — no authentication, no RBAC, no admission control, no audit log. Writing a YAML to /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ creates a pod directly via the kubelet. This is how control plane components run, but it's also the ultimate backdoor. Protection: chmod 700, root-only, file integrity monitoring, restricted SSH.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Static Pod Threat
  NORMAL POD: kubectl apply → API Server → Auth → RBAC → Admission → etcd
  STATIC POD: Write YAML to /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ → kubelet creates DIRECTLY
              ⚠️ NO AUTH ⚠️ NO RBAC ⚠️ NO ADMISSION ⚠️ NO AUDIT LOG

  DEFENSE: chmod 700, root-only, file integrity monitoring (AIDE),
           restricted SSH via bastion + MFA, Falco rule on directory changes

For anihpj/jobpost: Only 2 senior engineers have control plane SSH access via bastion + MFA + session recording. Any file change in /etc/kubernetes/ triggers PagerDuty alert.

Cluster Components Q45 of 200
How do you verify a kubelet certificate hasn't expired and what's the auto-renewal mechanism?
A) Kubelet certs never expire — they're valid for the cluster's lifetime
B) Check pod logs — kubelet logs certificate status every hour
C) Only the API server manages certificates — kubelet has no cert
D) On the node: openssl x509 -in /var/lib/kubelet/pki/kubelet.crt -noout -dates. From CP: kubeadm certs check-expiration. Auto-renewal via CSR API if --rotate-certificates=true is set on the kubelet
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kubelet certificates expire (typically 1 year). Verify locally with openssl x509 -noout -dates or cluster-wide with kubeadm certs check-expiration. Auto-renewal: when --rotate-certificates=true, the kubelet automatically requests new certs via the CSR API before expiry. The Certificate Signing Request controller can auto-approve if configured.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubelet Certificate Lifecycle
  ① kubeadm init → CA creates kubelet cert (1 year validity)
  ② kubelet uses cert to authenticate to API server
  ③ 80% of validity → kubelet auto-requests new cert via CSR API
  ④ CSR controller auto-approves (if configured)
  ⑤ New cert issued, kubelet switches to new cert
  ⑥ kubeadm certs check-expiration — monitor ALL certs cluster-wide

For anihpj/jobpost: kubeadm certs check-expiration runs as a weekly CronJob. Alert if any cert has <30 days remaining. Auto-renewal via --rotate-certificates=true on all kubelets.

2.5 Container RuntimeQ46–Q49

Cluster Components Q46 of 200
What is a Container Runtime and why does its security matter?
A) It's the CLI tool for interacting with Kubernetes
B) The container runtime (containerd, CRI-O) actually runs containers on each node — a runtime CVE (e.g., CVE-2019-5736 runc escape) allows containers to break isolation and access the HOST. Regular updates are critical
C) It's a monitoring tool tracking container resource usage
D) It only manages image downloads — no security impact
✅ Correct Answer: B

The container runtime is the software that creates/runs containers using Linux namespaces and cgroups. Runtime CVEs are the most dangerous Kubernetes vulnerabilities — CVE-2019-5736 (runc) allowed overwriting the host runc binary from inside a container, leading to full host compromise on the next exec.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container Runtime Stack
  kubelet → CRI → containerd/CRI-O → runc → Linux Kernel
  ⚠️ CVE-2019-5736: Container overwrites /proc/self/exe →
  points to malicious host binary → next kubectl exec
  runs attacker code as ROOT on HOST

For anihpj/jobpost: Runtime updated within 24hrs of critical CVEs. Immutable AMIs rebuilt. Defense-in-depth: non-root + seccomp + readOnlyRootFS reduce escape impact.

Cluster Components Q47 of 200
What is the difference between runc, gVisor, and Kata Containers?
A) runc: containers share host kernel (fast, least isolated). gVisor: user-space kernel intercepts syscalls (~200 safe syscalls reach real kernel). Kata: each pod gets dedicated VM with own kernel (strongest, highest overhead)
B) Three names for the same thing — all are container runtimes
C) runc=Linux, gVisor=Windows, Kata=macOS
D) gVisor replaces Linux kernel entirely — no host kernel needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

runc (default): containers share ONE host kernel — fastest, least isolated. gVisor: user-space kernel (Sentry) written in Go — only ~200 safe syscalls reach the real kernel. Kata Containers: each pod gets dedicated lightweight VM (QEMU/Firecracker) with its own kernel — strongest isolation, higher memory/startup overhead. RuntimeClass selects per pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Runtime Isolation Spectrum
  ISOLATION: runc < gVisor < Kata Containers

  runc:     Container1,2,3 → SHARE host kernel (fast, least safe)
  gVisor:   Container1,2,3 → Sentry(Go) per container → host kernel
  Kata:     Container1,2,3 → Guest Kernel per pod → VMM → host kernel

  anihpj: web=gVisor (internet), api/db=runc (internal), payment=Kata (PCI)

For anihpj/jobpost: Web tier (internet-facing) → gVisor. API/DB (internal) → runc. If a CVE hits runc, only API/DB affected — web protected by gVisor. RuntimeClass in pod spec selects per workload.

Cluster Components Q48 of 200
What is RuntimeClass and how is it used for security?
A) Classifies pods by CPU/memory usage
B) Security policy defining which users can create pods
C) Allows selecting different container runtimes PER POD — internal workloads use runc (fast), untrusted workloads use gVisor (sandboxed), highly sensitive use Kata (VM-level isolation)
D) Deprecated — all pods now use same runtime
✅ Correct Answer: C

RuntimeClass enables mixed-trust deployment: create a RuntimeClass object ("gvisor", "kata"), then set runtimeClassName: gvisor in pod spec. Trusted internal pods use fast runc, untrusted customer-facing pods use sandboxed gVisor, highly sensitive financial pods use VM-level Kata. Defense-in-depth at the runtime layer.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RuntimeClass Mixed-Trust
  anihpj-web: runtimeClassName=gvisor (internet-facing → sandboxed)
  anihpj-api: runtimeClassName=runc (internal → fast)
  anihpj-db: runtimeClassName=runc (internal → fast)
  Payment Processor: runtimeClassName=kata (PCI-DSS → VM isolation)

For anihpj/jobpost: RuntimeClass enables defense-in-depth — different trust levels get different isolation strengths. Internet-facing pods get strongest isolation.

Cluster Components Q49 of 200
Why should you keep the container runtime updated?
A) Runtime updates only add features — no security impact
B) Runtime CVEs enable container escape (CVE-2019-5736, CVE-2024-21626) — the most critical Kubernetes vulnerability class. Update within 24hrs of critical announcements
C) Cloud provider handles runtime updates automatically
D) Outdated runtimes only cause performance issues
✅ Correct Answer: B

Runtime CVEs are the most critical: CVE-2019-5736 (CVSS 8.6) allowed overwriting host runc, CVE-2024-21626 (CVSS 8.6) allowed filesystem breakout. Update within 24hrs. Use immutable infrastructure: rebuild AMIs, cordon/drain/replace nodes. Never patch running nodes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Notable Runtime CVEs
  CVE-2019-5736 (runc) — CVSS 8.6: Overwrite host runc → ROOT on HOST
  CVE-2022-23648 (containerd) — CVSS 7.5: Path traversal to host files
  CVE-2024-21626 (runc) — CVSS 8.6: /proc/self/fd breakout

  anihpj: Immutable AMIs rebuilt within 24hrs. Nodes cordoned, drained, replaced.

For anihpj/jobpost: Automated node replacement pipeline. Critical CVE → new AMI built → cordon/drain old nodes → new nodes join → pods rescheduled. Zero downtime, zero unpatched nodes.

2.6 KubeProxyQ50–Q52

Cluster Components Q50 of 200
What is kube-proxy and what security implications does it have?
A) Implements Service abstraction: virtual ClusterIP → real pod IPs via iptables/IPVS on every node. iptables mode = sequential rules (DDoS risk with many services); IPVS mode = O(1) hash table (resilient)
B) A proxy server that routes external HTTP traffic to pods
C) A security tool that scans pod network traffic for threats
D) A deprecated component — Cilium replaces it entirely
✅ Correct Answer: A

kube-proxy runs on every node, programming iptables/IPVS rules so Service ClusterIPs resolve to real pod IPs. iptables mode: 5000 services → 25000+ rules → sequential traversal (DDoS amplifies). IPVS mode: kernel hash table → O(1) lookup regardless of count. Always prefer IPVS. Run kube-proxy with minimal RBAC.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kube-proxy: iptables vs IPVS
  iptables: 5000 services → 25000 rules → O(n) traversal → slow under load
  IPVS:     5000 services → hash table → O(1) lookup → resilient

  anihpj: --proxy-mode=ipvs, minimal RBAC, non-root since K8s 1.23

For anihpj/jobpost: kube-proxy runs in IPVS mode for DDoS resilience. Minimal RBAC (doesn't need cluster-admin). Metrics bound to localhost only.

Cluster Components Q51 of 200
What's the security difference between iptables and IPVS mode for kube-proxy?
A) iptables is encrypted; IPVS is plaintext
B) No difference — both use the same kernel subsystem
C) iptables: sequential rules → 5000 services = 25000+ rules traversed per packet (DDoS amplification). IPVS: kernel L4 LB with hash table → O(1) lookup regardless of service count
D) IPVS is legacy; iptables is the modern replacement
✅ Correct Answer: C

iptables processes rules sequentially — each packet traverses the entire chain. With 5000 services, that's 25000+ rules evaluated per packet. Under load (DDoS), rule processing overwhelms node CPU. IPVS uses a kernel-level hash table for O(1) lookup — constant time regardless of service count. Also supports scheduling algorithms (rr, lc, sh, dh).

📝 Detailed Explanation
iptables vs IPVS — Under Load
  iptables: Packet → Rule1→Rule2→...→Rule25000 → CPU spike, latency ↗
  IPVS:     Packet → Hash(IP:Port) → O(1) → pod IP → constant latency

  ALWAYS use IPVS (--proxy-mode=ipvs) for production clusters

For anihpj/jobpost: IPVS mode prevents service-related DoS. Even under heavy load, Service resolution stays fast. Combined with NetworkPolicy for defense-in-depth.

Cluster Components Q52 of 200
How should kube-proxy be configured securely in production?
A) Run as privileged with cluster-admin RBAC for full functionality
B) Expose metrics on 0.0.0.0:10249 for Prometheus monitoring
C) Use iptables mode — it's the default and most tested
D) IPVS mode (DDoS resilience), bind to localhost (--bind-address=127.0.0.1), minimal RBAC, metrics on localhost only (--metrics-bind-address=127.0.0.1:10249), run as non-root (supported since K8s 1.23)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Production kube-proxy hardening: --proxy-mode=ipvs (resilient), --bind-address=127.0.0.1 (no external exposure), minimal RBAC (not cluster-admin), --metrics-bind-address=127.0.0.1:10249 (metrics only via localhost), non-root operation (since 1.23). A privileged kube-proxy with cluster-admin is a massive security risk.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kube-proxy Secure Configuration
  ✅ --proxy-mode=ipvs                   (DDoS resilient)
  ✅ --bind-address=127.0.0.1            (no external exposure)
  ✅ Minimal RBAC                        (not cluster-admin!)
  ✅ --metrics-bind-address=127.0.0.1   (metrics localhost only)
  ✅ Run as non-root                    (K8s 1.23+)

For anihpj/jobpost: kube-proxy runs as a DaemonSet with IPVS mode, non-root, minimal RBAC. Metrics scraped via localhost by Prometheus node exporter — never exposed externally.

2.7 PodQ53–Q56

Cluster Components Q53 of 200
What security boundaries does a Pod provide?
A) Pod is the smallest deployable unit — containers inside share network namespace (same IP, localhost), IPC namespace (shared memory), and volumes. Containers in the SAME pod are NOT isolated from each other — sibling compromise = lateral access
B) Each container in a pod is fully isolated — they can't communicate at all
C) Pods provide VM-level isolation between containers
D) Pods are just a logical grouping — no isolation implications
✅ Correct Answer: A

Containers in the same pod share: network namespace (same IP, same localhost — can talk via localhost without NetworkPolicy), IPC namespace (shared memory), and volumes. If one container is compromised, sibling containers are DIRECTLY accessible via localhost. This is why sidecar patterns require trust between containers in a pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod Shared Namespaces
  POD: 10.244.1.5
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Container A (gunicorn)   Container B (envoy)│
  │   ↕ localhost:8000         ↕ localhost:15001│
  │   SHARE: network, IPC, volumes, PID (if on) │
  │ ⚠️ A compromised → B directly accessible     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: If using Istio sidecar, the Envoy proxy and gunicorn share a pod — compromise of gunicorn means the attacker can reach Envoy via localhost. Pod-level trust is explicit.

Cluster Components Q54 of 200
What is the difference between a regular Pod and a static Pod?
A) There is no difference — all pods are created the same way
B) Regular Pod: created via API server → etcd → scheduler → kubelet. Static Pod: kubelet watches /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ → creates pod DIRECTLY, bypassing API server/auth/RBAC/admission entirely
C) Static pods are faster because they skip the scheduler
D) Regular pods can be deleted; static pods are permanent
✅ Correct Answer: B

Static pods bypass the ENTIRE API server security pipeline. This is how control plane components run (API server, etcd, scheduler are all static pods). The kubelet creates them directly from YAML files in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ — no auth, no RBAC, no admission control, no audit log. This is both necessary (control plane needs to start before API server exists) and dangerous (write access = cluster compromise).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Regular vs Static Pods
  REGULAR: kubectl → API Server → Auth → RBAC → Admission → etcd → kubelet
  STATIC:  Write YAML → /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ → kubelet creates DIRECTLY
           ⚠️ NO AUTH ⚠️ NO RBAC ⚠️ NO ADMISSION ⚠️ NO AUDIT

For anihpj/jobpost: /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ is chmod 700 root-only. Any file change triggers PagerDuty alert via file integrity monitoring.

Cluster Components Q55 of 200
What are the security implications of hostNetwork, hostPID, and hostIPC?
A) These settings improve pod performance with no security impact
B) They enable pod-to-pod encryption using host certificates
C) hostNetwork: pod uses NODE's network namespace (bypasses NetworkPolicy, can sniff all traffic). hostPID: pod sees ALL host processes (can kill any). hostIPC: pod shares host IPC (access host shared memory). NEVER use for app pods
D) These are required for all production pods to function correctly
✅ Correct Answer: C

These three flags break pod isolation: hostNetwork — pod uses the node's network (bypasses NetworkPolicy, can sniff ALL node traffic). hostPID — pod sees all host processes (can kill/kubelet processes). hostIPC — pod shares host inter-process communication (can access host shared memory). NEVER set these for application pods — system daemons only.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Breaking Pod Isolation
  hostNetwork:true  → Pod uses NODE IP, bypasses NetworkPolicy, sniffs traffic
  hostPID:true      → Pod sees ALL host processes, can kill kubelet/container
  hostIPC:true      → Pod accesses host shared memory
  ⚠️ Pod Security Standards (Baseline) BLOCK all three

For anihpj/jobpost: Pod Security Standards enforce baseline policy — any pod with hostNetwork/hostPID/hostIPC is REJECTED in the anihpj namespace. Only kube-system pods get exceptions.

Cluster Components Q56 of 200
How does Pod Security Admission (PSA) enforce pod-level security?
A) PSA is a kubectl plugin that scans running pods for vulnerabilities
B) PSA replaces NetworkPolicy — it controls pod-to-pod traffic
C) PSA only applies to privileged pods — baseline pods are exempt
D) PSA labels on namespaces (enforce/audit/warn) validate every pod against Pod Security Standards — Privileged (no restrictions), Baseline (blocks hostPath/hostNetwork/privileged), Restricted (must run non-root, drop ALL caps, seccomp required)
✅ Correct Answer: D

PSA enforces Pod Security Standards at the namespace level via labels: pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted. Three modes: enforce (REJECT violating pods), audit (LOG violations), warn (SHOW message). Three levels: Privileged (no restrictions — system pods), Baseline (blocks known escalations like hostPath/hostNetwork), Restricted (hardened — non-root, drop ALL caps).

📝 Detailed Explanation
PSA Namespace Labels
  pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted    ← REJECT violating pods
  pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit: restricted      ← LOG violations
  pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn: restricted       ← SHOW warning to user

  anihpj: dev=baseline(enforce)+restricted(audit), prod=restricted(enforce)

For anihpj/jobpost: dev namespace: enforce=baseline, audit=restricted. prod namespace: enforce=restricted. Gradually tightening security as code moves through environments.

2.8 EtcdQ57–Q62

Cluster Components Q57 of 200
What data is stored in etcd and why is it called the "crown jewels"?
A) EVERYTHING — Pods, Deployments, Services, Secrets, ConfigMaps, RBAC, SA tokens, CRDs — the entire cluster state. etcd compromise = TOTAL cluster compromise. Secrets stored as base64 (NOT encrypted by default)
B) Only pod specifications — other data is in the API server memory
C) Just configuration data — Secrets are stored in a separate vault
D) Only cluster metadata — actual pod data is on worker nodes
✅ Correct Answer: A

etcd holds the ENTIRE cluster state — every Kubernetes object including Secrets. Without EncryptionConfiguration, Secrets are base64-encoded (NOT encrypted). Anyone with etcd access: etcdctl get /registry/secrets/ → ALL secrets in plaintext. etcd access = total cluster compromise. Encrypt with EncryptionConfiguration (AES-CBC or KMS).

📝 Detailed Explanation
What etcd Stores
  /registry/pods/anihpj/anihpj-api-7d8f9      ← Pod specs
  /registry/secrets/anihpj/db-password         ← Secret (base64!)
  /registry/deployments/anihpj/anihpj-api      ← Deployment config
  /registry/serviceaccounts/anihpj/default     ← SA + token
  ⚠️ WITHOUT EncryptionConfiguration: etcdctl get → plaintext secrets
  ✅ WITH EncryptionConfiguration: etcdctl get → encrypted blob

For anihpj/jobpost: EncryptionConfiguration with AES-CBC encrypts all Secrets before etcd write. etcd access is restricted to API server only via TLS + firewall.

Cluster Components Q58 of 200
How do you secure etcd communication?
A) etcd runs on localhost only — no network security needed
B) Peer TLS (--peer-cert-file, --peer-key-file between etcd members), Client TLS (--cert-file, --key-file for API server), --peer-client-cert-auth=true, --client-cert-auth=true
C) etcd uses HTTP by default — TLS is optional for performance
D) Only the API server needs TLS — etcd peers communicate via loopback
✅ Correct Answer: B

etcd requires TLS for BOTH peer communication (between etcd cluster members) AND client communication (API server → etcd). --peer-client-cert-auth=true requires ALL peers to present valid certs. --client-cert-auth=true requires ALL clients (API server) to present valid certs. All certs signed by the cluster CA.

📝 Detailed Explanation
etcd TLS Configuration
  PEER TLS (etcd ↔ etcd):             CLIENT TLS (API Server → etcd):
  --peer-cert-file=etcd-peer.crt      --cert-file=apiserver-etcd-client.crt
  --peer-key-file=etcd-peer.key       --key-file=apiserver-etcd-client.key
  --peer-client-cert-auth=true        --client-cert-auth=true
  --peer-trusted-ca-file=ca.crt       --trusted-ca-file=ca.crt

For anihpj/jobpost: etcd runs with mutual TLS for both peer and client. No unauthenticated connections. Certificates auto-renewed and monitored for expiration.

Cluster Components Q59 of 200
Why is etcd encryption at rest critical?
A) etcd automatically encrypts all data — no configuration needed
B) Encryption only matters for cloud deployments — on-prem is fine
C) By default, Secrets in etcd are base64 (NOT encrypted). Anyone with etcd access reads ALL secrets in plaintext. EncryptionConfiguration encrypts BEFORE etcd write — etcd stores only ciphertext
D) Encryption is only needed for PCI-DSS compliance — not general security
✅ Correct Answer: C

Default: Secrets in etcd = base64("myPassword") = "bXlQYXNzd29yZA==" — ONE command to decode. EncryptionConfiguration encrypts with AES-CBC before etcd write → etcd stores "k8s:enc:aescbc:v1:key1:<ciphertext>". Without it: etcd access = ALL secrets exposed. Providers: aescbc, aesgcm, secretbox, or external KMS (AWS KMS, Azure Key Vault).

📝 Detailed Explanation
etcd Encryption at Rest
  WITHOUT EncryptionConfiguration:
  kubectl create secret → API server → base64 → etcd (PLAINTEXT base64!)
  etcdctl get /registry/secrets/ → ALL secrets readable

  WITH EncryptionConfiguration:
  kubectl create secret → API server → ENCRYPT (AES-CBC) → etcd (CIPHERTEXT)
  etcdctl get /registry/secrets/ → encrypted blob (unreadable)

For anihpj/jobpost: EncryptionConfiguration with AES-CBC enabled. All Secrets encrypted at rest in etcd. Combined with etcd TLS + firewall for defense-in-depth.

Cluster Components Q60 of 200
How many etcd nodes for HA and WHY an odd number?
A) 3 or 5 (odd number!). RAFT quorum = N/2+1 → 3 nodes need 2 for quorum, 5 need 3. Odd prevents split-brain (tie votes). NEVER 2 nodes (quorum=2, tolerates 0 failures)
B) 2 nodes — one active, one standby. Even numbers are more stable
C) 4 nodes — provides the best balance of performance and reliability
D) 1 node is sufficient — etcd handles HA internally
✅ Correct Answer: A

etcd uses RAFT consensus. Quorum = N/2 + 1 (majority). 3 nodes: quorum=2, tolerates 1 failure. 5 nodes: quorum=3, tolerates 2 failures. Odd numbers prevent split-brain — with even numbers, a network partition could result in 2 vs 2 tie (no quorum = cluster unavailable). NEVER use 2 nodes (quorum=2, zero fault tolerance).

📝 Detailed Explanation
etcd RAFT Quorum
  3 NODES: quorum=2, tolerates 1 failure ✅ (RECOMMENDED)
  5 NODES: quorum=3, tolerates 2 failures ✅ (HIGHER RESILIENCE)
  2 NODES: quorum=2, tolerates 0 failures ❌ (WORSE THAN 1!)
  4 NODES: quorum=3, tolerates 1 failure ⚠️ (odd is better)

For anihpj/jobpost: 3-node etcd cluster for HA. Can survive 1 node failure while maintaining quorum. Daily automated snapshots to S3.

Cluster Components Q61 of 200
How often should you back up etcd and where?
A) Once at cluster creation — etcd is self-healing after that
B) Before/after every upgrade + daily automated snapshots (cron job). Store OFF-cluster (S3, GCS, Azure Blob) — if cluster destroyed, on-cluster backups die too. Command: ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl snapshot save /backup/etcd-$(date).db
C) Weekly is sufficient — etcd changes are infrequent
D) Backups are handled automatically by the cloud provider
✅ Correct Answer: B

etcd backup strategy: daily automated snapshots (cron job on control plane), before/after every cluster upgrade, before any destructive operation. Store backups OFF the cluster (S3, GCS, Azure Blob with versioning) — if the cluster is destroyed, local backups are destroyed too. Test restoration quarterly.

📝 Detailed Explanation
etcd Backup Strategy
  Daily: CronJob → ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl snapshot save → S3://backups/
  Pre-upgrade: Manual snapshot → verify → upgrade
  Post-upgrade: Snapshot → verify cluster healthy
  Quarterly: Restore from latest backup to test pipeline
  ⚠️ STORE OFF-CLUSTER — if cluster is destroyed, local backups die too

For anihpj/jobpost: Daily etcd snapshots to encrypted S3 bucket with versioning. Restoration tested quarterly. Before any upgrade: fresh snapshot taken and verified.

Cluster Components Q62 of 200
What happens during an etcd leader election?
A) All nodes restart simultaneously to elect a new leader
B) The API server appoints a new leader — no election needed
C) etcd becomes permanently read-only until an admin manually intervenes
D) Leader fails → nodes detect timeout (~1s heartbeat) → each increments term → requests votes → first to majority (quorum) becomes new leader. During election: cluster is READ-ONLY (no writes for 1-3 seconds). Frequent elections = network/disk problems
✅ Correct Answer: D

When the etcd leader fails: remaining nodes detect timeout (heartbeat ~1 second), each increments its RAFT term and requests votes from peers, the first to get majority (quorum) becomes the new leader and starts accepting writes. During election (1-3 seconds typically): cluster is READ-ONLY — no writes accepted. Frequent elections indicate network latency or disk performance issues.

📝 Detailed Explanation
etcd Leader Election
  ① Leader fails → heartbeat timeout (~1s)
  ② Remaining nodes: increment term, request votes
  ③ First to reach quorum → new leader
  ④ New leader starts accepting writes
  ⑤ Duration: 1-3 seconds (READ-ONLY during election)
  ⚠️ Frequent elections → check network latency + disk I/O

For anihpj/jobpost: etcd metrics monitored for leader changes. Frequent elections → alert via Prometheus. SSD storage for etcd to minimize disk latency.

2.9 Container NetworkingQ63–Q67

Cluster Components Q63 of 200
What is a CNI plugin and its security role?
A) A monitoring tool that tracks network bandwidth per pod
B) Implements the K8s networking model (every pod gets unique routable IP). The CNI ENFORCES NetworkPolicy — if CNI doesn't support it (bare Flannel), policies are stored but NEVER enforced. Popular: Calico (BGP+policy), Cilium (eBPF+encryption)
C) A load balancer that distributes external traffic to pods
D) A DNS service that resolves pod names to IP addresses
✅ Correct Answer: B

The CNI plugin is your network security enforcer. It implements pod networking AND enforces NetworkPolicy. If your CNI doesn't support NetworkPolicy (e.g., bare Flannel without Calico), your policies exist in etcd but are NEVER enforced — pods can communicate freely. Always verify: kubectl describe networkpolicy + test with busybox debug pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CNI — NetworkPolicy Enforcement
  Calico: iptables/eBPF rules enforcing NetworkPolicy
  Cilium: eBPF at kernel level + WireGuard encryption + Hubble observability
  Flannel: Simple overlay — NO NetworkPolicy support ⚠️
  Weave: Built-in encryption + NetworkPolicy support

  ALWAYS verify: kubectl describe networkpolicy → TEST with busybox

For anihpj/jobpost: Cilium CNI with eBPF enforcement + WireGuard encryption between nodes. NetworkPolicy verified quarterly with automated security tests.

Cluster Components Q64 of 200
In anihpj/jobpost, what NetworkPolicy rules secure the application tiers?
A) Default-deny all ingress+egress, then: web→api:8000, api→db:5432, api→redis:6379, all→DNS (UDP 53). Each tier can ONLY talk to its downstream dependency
B) Allow all traffic within the anihpj namespace — pods are trusted
C) Only block traffic from outside the cluster — internal is open
D) No NetworkPolicy needed — Kubernetes isolates pods by default
✅ Correct Answer: A

Zero Trust networking for anihpj: start with default-deny (block ALL traffic), then add explicit allows for each required path. web→api:8000 (frontend needs API), api→db:5432 (API needs database), api→redis:6379 (API needs cache), all→DNS (pods need name resolution). Result: compromised web pod CANNOT reach db or redis — trapped in web tier.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost NetworkPolicy Design
  DEFAULT-DENY (base policy):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ anihpj-web  → ✅ api:8000 (allowed)          │
  │             → ❌ db:5432 (blocked)            │
  │ anihpj-api  → ✅ db:5432 (allowed)           │
  │             → ✅ redis:6379 (allowed)         │
  │ anihpj-db   → ❌ everything blocked           │
  │ anihpj-redis→ ❌ everything blocked           │
  │ ALL         → ✅ DNS:53 (CoreDNS)            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Default-deny NetworkPolicy applied first. Specific allow rules for each tier. Compromised web pod = trapped in web tier. Cannot reach database or cache directly.

Cluster Components Q65 of 200
Why should the CNI configuration directory (/etc/cni/net.d/) be protected?
A) It contains pod logs — exposure means data leakage
B) It stores network performance metrics — not security-critical
C) CNI configs tell the runtime HOW to set up pod networking — attacker with write access can install malicious CNI plugin (redirect traffic), change IP ranges (break NetworkPolicy), or disable networking entirely (DoS)
D) This directory is automatically protected — no manual action needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

/etc/cni/net.d/ contains CNI configuration files. An attacker with write access can: install a malicious CNI plugin that redirects pod traffic to their server, change pod IP CIDR ranges (breaking existing NetworkPolicies), or remove configs entirely (denial of service — no pods can get network). Protection: chmod 700, root-only, file integrity monitoring.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Protecting /etc/cni/net.d/
  chmod 700 /etc/cni/net.d/           ← root-only write access
  File integrity monitoring (AIDE)    ← alert on ANY change
  Immutable infrastructure             ← rebuild, don't patch
  Falco rule: alert on writes to      ← runtime detection
    /etc/cni/net.d/ from containers

For anihpj/jobpost: /etc/cni/net.d/ protected with strict permissions + file integrity monitoring. Any change triggers PagerDuty alert.

Cluster Components Q66 of 200
How does Cilium improve security beyond traditional CNIs?
A) Cilium is just a CNI — no security benefits over Calico or Flannel
B) Cilium only provides faster networking — security is unchanged
C) Cilium replaces the need for NetworkPolicy entirely
D) eBPF kernel-level enforcement (no iptables), L7 policies (HTTP method/path), identity-based security (pod labels not IPs), WireGuard encryption between nodes, Hubble observability (real-time network flows with K8s metadata)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Cilium uses eBPF to enforce policies at the kernel level — no iptables traversal, line-rate performance regardless of rule count. L7 policies can filter by HTTP method, path, headers (not just port). Identity-based security uses pod labels instead of IPs (IPs change, labels don't). WireGuard provides transparent node-to-node encryption. Hubble gives real-time observability: which pod talked to which, DNS names, HTTP methods, labels.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cilium Security Features
  eBPF enforcement → kernel-level, no iptables, line-rate
  L7 policies → "Only GET /api/jobs, not POST /admin"
  Identity-based → "Only pods with label app=web" (IP-independent)
  WireGuard → Transparent encryption between ALL nodes
  Hubble → "pod anihpj-web → anihpj-api:8000, GET /api/jobs, 200 OK"

For anihpj/jobpost: Cilium with L7 policies restricting API access, WireGuard encrypting all node-to-node traffic, Hubble for real-time network visibility and forensics.

Cluster Components Q67 of 200
What does a default-deny NetworkPolicy look like and why use it?
A) podSelector: {} (all pods), policyTypes: [Ingress], no ingress rules → ALL incoming traffic DENIED. Then ADD specific policies. Zero Trust: deny everything, explicitly allow what's needed
B) Deny all traffic from outside the cluster — internal traffic is unaffected
C) Block pod egress only — ingress is always allowed
D) Default-deny is too restrictive — start with allow-all, then add denies
✅ Correct Answer: A
