Default-deny is Zero Trust networking: podSelector: {} selects ALL pods in the namespace, policyTypes: [Ingress] makes them "isolated" for incoming traffic, and no ingress rules means nothing is allowed. Then add specific NetworkPolicies for each legitimate communication path. This ensures only explicitly authorized traffic flows.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Default-Deny Pattern
  apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
  kind: NetworkPolicy
  metadata:
    name: default-deny-ingress
    namespace: anihpj
  spec:
    podSelector: {}          ← ALL pods in namespace
    policyTypes: [Ingress]   ← isolate for incoming traffic
    # No ingress rules = ALL incoming traffic DENIED

  THEN add: allow-web-to-api, allow-api-to-db, allow-dns...

For anihpj/jobpost: Default-deny applied FIRST to the anihpj namespace. Then specific allow policies for web→api, api→db, api→redis, DNS. Every other connection — including attacker reverse shells — BLOCKED.

2.10 Client SecurityQ68–Q70

Cluster Components Q68 of 200
What is a kubeconfig file and what security risks does it pose?
A) A log file tracking kubectl command history — no credentials inside
B) A configuration file for the kubelet — only used on worker nodes
C) Contains cluster endpoint, user credentials (cert/key or token), and context. If leaked: attacker has full access with that user's permissions. Protect: never commit to Git, use OIDC (no long-lived creds), rotate regularly
D) A deprecated file format — Kubernetes now uses API tokens instead
✅ Correct Answer: C

A kubeconfig (~/.kube/config) contains: cluster API server endpoint, user credentials (X.509 cert+key or Bearer token), and context (which cluster+user+namespace). If leaked (committed to Git, shared on Slack, stolen laptop): attacker gets the user's permissions. Use OIDC for human users — no long-lived credentials in kubeconfig files.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubeconfig Security
  ~/.kube/config:
  clusters:  [{name: prod, server: https://api.prod.com:6443}]
  users:     [{name: alice, user: {client-certificate: alice.crt,
                                   client-key: alice.key}}]
  contexts:  [{name: prod-alice, cluster: prod, user: alice,
               namespace: anihpj}]

  ⚠️ LEAKED kubeconfig = FULL ACCESS as that user
  ✅ OIDC: no long-lived credentials in kubeconfig
  ✅ .gitignore: always ignore kubeconfig files

For anihpj/jobpost: All human users authenticate via OIDC (Azure AD + MFA). No long-lived certificates in kubeconfig files. .gitignore blocks all kubeconfig patterns.

Cluster Components Q69 of 200
Why should human operators use OIDC instead of certificate-based auth?
A) OIDC is faster — certificates have high latency
B) Certificates are deprecated — only OIDC works in K8s 1.30+
C) OIDC requires no configuration — it works out of the box
D) MFA supported by IdP, centralized user lifecycle (revoke in one place), audit trail per human, no shared kubeconfig with embedded long-lived credentials, short-lived auto-refreshing tokens
✅ Correct Answer: D

OIDC is the best auth method for humans because: the identity provider (Google, Azure AD, Okta) handles MFA and user lifecycle — when someone leaves, revoke in ONE place; audit trail shows which HUMAN ran each command (not just which ServiceAccount); no long-lived certificates in kubeconfig files — tokens are short-lived and auto-refresh.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OIDC vs Certificates for Humans
  CERTIFICATES:                          OIDC:
  • Long-lived (1 year)                  • Short-lived tokens (1 hour)
  • No MFA                               • MFA via IdP (Google, Azure AD)
  • Revoke = reissue CA + all certs      • Revoke = disable in IdP (instant)
  • Stored in kubeconfig (stealable)     • Auto-refresh via kubectl plugin
  • No identity → kubectl mapping        • "alice@company.com ran delete"

For anihpj/jobpost: All developers and admins use OIDC via Azure AD with MFA. Audit logs show exactly which human ran each command. When someone leaves, access is revoked in Azure AD — instant across all clusters.

Cluster Components Q70 of 200
What is the "client" in Kubernetes security context?
A) Only kubectl users — machines don't count as clients
B) Any entity making API requests: kubectl users, CI/CD pipelines, controllers, operators, webhooks, monitoring tools. Each must authenticate, be authorized (RBAC), use TLS, and have actions audited
C) The web browser used to access the Kubernetes dashboard
D) Only external users — internal components are trusted implicitly
✅ Correct Answer: B

"Client" means ANY entity making API requests — human users (kubectl), CI/CD pipelines, controllers, operators, webhooks, monitoring tools. Every client must: ① Authenticate (prove identity via cert/token/OIDC), ② Be authorized (RBAC — what can this client do?), ③ Use TLS (encrypted communication), ④ Have actions audited (audit log). No client is trusted implicitly — not even internal components.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes Clients
  HUMAN:    kubectl → OIDC auth → RBAC check → audit log
  CI/CD:    GitHub Actions → SA token → RBAC (deployments only) → audit
  OPERATOR: cert-manager → SA token → RBAC (certificates + secrets) → audit
  MONITOR:  Prometheus → SA token → RBAC (get pods/nodes, read-only) → audit
  WEBHOOK:  Kyverno → SA token → RBAC (validate/mutate) → audit

  EVERY client: authenticate → authorize → encrypt (TLS) → audit

For anihpj/jobpost: Every client has a dedicated ServiceAccount with least privilege RBAC. CI/CD can only update deployments. Monitoring can only read metrics. No shared credentials. All actions audited.

2.11 StorageQ71–Q72

Cluster Components Q71 of 200
How does storage security relate to cluster component security?
A) CSI drivers run as privileged pods — compromised driver accesses ALL volumes on a node. hostPath mounts node filesystem (container escape risk). StorageClass provisioners need cloud API access — must use least privilege IAM. PVs store persistent data — encrypt at rest
B) Storage is unrelated to security — it's purely about data persistence
C) Kubernetes handles storage security automatically — no configuration needed
D) Storage security is only relevant for cloud deployments
✅ Correct Answer: A

Storage security connects to multiple components: CSI drivers (run as privileged pods — compromise = access to ALL node volumes), hostPath volumes (mount node filesystem into pod — container escape risk), StorageClass provisioners (need cloud API access — must use least privilege IAM), PersistentVolumes (store sensitive data — must encrypt at rest). Storage is not isolated from security — it's deeply connected.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Storage Security — Component Connections
  CSI Driver: Privileged pod → compromise = ALL volumes on node
  hostPath: Mount / → pod has FULL node filesystem access ⚠️
  StorageClass: Cloud API access → least privilege IAM role
  PV/PVC: Persistent data → encrypt at rest (EBS encryption, KMS)

For anihpj/jobpost: CSI driver runs with minimal RBAC. No hostPath volumes in application pods. EBS volumes encrypted with KMS. StorageClass IAM role scoped to EBS operations only.

Cluster Components Q72 of 200
What are the security implications of hostPath volumes?
A) hostPath is the safest volume type — data stays on the node
B) hostPath volumes are encrypted by default — no security risk
C) hostPath mounts the NODE's filesystem into a pod. Mounting / = full node access (container escape). Mounting /var/run/docker.sock = control host's container runtime. Mounting /etc/kubernetes = read cluster certificates. NEVER for app pods
D) hostPath is deprecated — Kubernetes blocks it by default
✅ Correct Answer: C

hostPath is the most dangerous volume type: it mounts the NODE's filesystem directly into the pod. Mounting / → pod has FULL node filesystem access (container escape). Mounting /var/run/docker.sock → pod controls host's container runtime (launch privileged containers). Mounting /etc/kubernetes → pod reads cluster certificates. Pod Security Standards (Baseline) BLOCK hostPath. Never use for application pods.

📝 Detailed Explanation
hostPath Dangers
  hostPath: / → chroot /host → FULL node filesystem access 🔴
  hostPath: /var/run/docker.sock → docker run --privileged → HOST ROOT 🔴
  hostPath: /etc/kubernetes/pki → steal CA cert+key → impersonate cluster 🔴
  hostPath: /proc → modify kernel parameters → crash host 🔴

  ⚠️ Pod Security Standards (Baseline) BLOCK all hostPath volumes
  ⚠️ Use PV/PVC with CSI drivers instead

For anihpj/jobpost: Pod Security Standards enforce baseline policy — all hostPath volumes REJECTED in application namespaces. Only kube-system DaemonSets (Falco, CNI) allowed limited hostPath mounts.

These are the controls you'll configure on EVERY Kubernetes cluster — the "how to lock down your workloads" knowledge.

Q73-Q116 44 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

3.1 Pod Security StandardsQ73–Q78

Security Fundamentals Q73 of 200
What are the three Pod Security Standard levels and when should you use each?
A) Development, Staging, and Production — they map directly to deployment environments
B) Privileged (no restrictions — for system/infra workloads that need host access like CNI plugins, CSI drivers), Baseline (minimal restrictive — blocks known privilege escalations like hostPath, hostNetwork, privileged containers, is the default for most production workloads), Restricted (heavily restricted — must run as non-root, drop ALL capabilities, seccomp required, for highly sensitive workloads)
C) Low, Medium, and High — they map to CVSS severity scores
D) None, Partial, and Full — they control the degree of container isolation
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Pod Security Standards (PSS) define three progressive security levels. Privileged is entirely unrestricted — for system components (CNI, CSI drivers, kube-proxy) that genuinely need host access. Baseline prevents known privilege escalation vectors: no hostPath volumes, no hostNetwork/hostPID/hostIPC, no privileged containers, no CAP_SYS_ADMIN, no hostPorts. It's the minimum standard for most production workloads. Restricted enforces pod hardening best practices: must run as non-root (UID ≠ 0), must drop ALL capabilities, seccomp profile required (not Unconfined), no privilege escalation allowed. This is the target for sensitive workloads.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod Security Standards — Progressive Hardening
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  PRIVILEGED              BASELINE               RESTRICTED      │
  │  (unrestricted)          (minimal hardening)     (maximum hardening)│
  │  ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐ │
  │  │ ✓ hostPath       │   │ ✗ hostPath       │   │ ✗ hostPath   │ │
  │  │ ✓ hostNetwork    │   │ ✗ hostNetwork    │   │ ✗ hostNetwork│ │
  │  │ ✓ hostPID/IPC    │   │ ✗ hostPID/IPC    │   │ ✗ hostPID/IPC│ │
  │  │ ✓ privileged     │   │ ✗ privileged     │   │ ✗ privileged │ │
  │  │ ✓ CAP_SYS_ADMIN  │   │ ✗ CAP_SYS_ADMIN  │   │ ✗ ALL caps   │ │
  │  │ ✓ any user       │   │ ✓ any user       │   │ ✗ UID 0      │ │
  │  │ ✓ any seccomp    │   │ ✓ any seccomp    │   │ ✗ Unconfined │ │
  │  │ ✓ any volume type│   │ ✗ hostPath vols  │   │ ✗ many types │ │
  │  │                  │   │                  │   │              │ │
  │  │ For: CNI, CSI,   │   │ For: most app    │   │ For: sensitive│ │
  │  │ kube-proxy,      │   │ workloads        │   │ data handling│ │
  │  │ logging agents   │   │ (default target) │   │ (PCI, PII)   │ │
  │  └──────────────────┘   └──────────────────┘   └──────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The prod namespace uses enforce=restricted — all pods must run as non-root, drop ALL capabilities, and use RuntimeDefault seccomp. The dev namespace uses enforce=baseline with audit=restricted (logs violations without blocking). kube-system uses privileged for infrastructure pods (Calico, CSI driver, kube-proxy) but those pods are tightly controlled with RBAC and Falco monitoring.

Security Fundamentals Q74 of 200
What exactly does the "Baseline" Pod Security Standard prevent?
A) hostProcess containers, hostNetwork, hostPID, hostIPC, hostPath volumes, privileged containers, CAP_SYS_ADMIN, CAP_NET_RAW, CAP_SYS_PTRACE, hostPorts, AppArmor "unconfined", SELinux "unconfined", and /proc mount type Unmasked. In plain English: "No sharing the node's network, processes, or filesystem. No privileged mode. No dangerous capabilities."
B) Only privileged containers — everything else is allowed
C) Baseline prevents nothing — it's just a recommendation
D) Only hostPath volumes and hostNetwork — capabilities are not restricted
✅ Correct Answer: A

Baseline is a "minimally restrictive" policy that blocks known privilege escalations. The philosophy: if an attacker escapes a Baseline-compliant container, they shouldn't be able to trivially escalate to host root. It blocks host filesystem access (hostPath), host network sharing (hostNetwork), host process visibility (hostPID), inter-process communication with host (hostIPC), privileged mode, and dangerous capabilities (CAP_SYS_ADMIN, CAP_NET_RAW, CAP_SYS_PTRACE). It does NOT require non-root user or seccomp — that's Restricted level. Baseline is the recommended minimum for ALL production namespaces.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Baseline — What It Blocks
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  BASELINE BLOCKS:                                                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Field/Setting              │ Why It's Blocked              │  │
  │  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ hostNetwork: true          │ Pod can sniff ALL node traffic│  │
  │  │ hostPID: true              │ Pod sees ALL host processes   │  │
  │  │ hostIPC: true              │ Pod accesses host shared mem  │  │
  │  │ hostPath volumes           │ Mount node filesystem → escape│  │
  │  │ privileged: true           │ ALL caps + devices → root     │  │
  │  │ CAP_SYS_ADMIN              │ mount, pivot_root → escape    │  │
  │  │ CAP_NET_RAW                │ Packet sniffing, ARP spoofing │  │
  │  │ CAP_SYS_PTRACE             │ Debug/inject other processes  │  │
  │  │ hostPorts                  │ Bypass NetworkPolicy on node  │  │
  │  │ AppArmor: unconfined       │ No AppArmor restrictions      │  │
  │  │ SELinux: unconfined        │ No SELinux restrictions       │  │
  │  │ /proc mount: Unmasked      │ See real /proc of host        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: All application namespaces (dev, staging, prod) enforce Baseline as the minimum. Even in dev, developers can't deploy pods with hostNetwork or privileged mode — those are blocked at admission time. This prevents "accidental" insecure configurations from reaching any environment.

Security Fundamentals Q75 of 200
What exactly does the "Restricted" Pod Security Standard enforce beyond Baseline?
A) Restricted is the same as Baseline — there's no difference
B) Restricted only adds image scanning requirements
C) Everything Baseline prevents PLUS: runAsNonRoot: true (CANNOT run as UID 0), seccompProfile must be RuntimeDefault or Localhost (not Unconfined), capabilities.drop must include ALL, allowPrivilegeEscalation: false, and volume types are further restricted. In plain English: "The pod runs as a regular user, has no special kernel powers, and can't break out of its container."
D) Restricted only applies to the kube-system namespace
✅ Correct Answer: C

Restricted takes Baseline and adds pod hardening best practices. The key additions: runAsNonRoot: true (pod cannot run as UID 0 — even if the image defaults to root, the pod fails to start), seccompProfile must be set (RuntimeDefault or Localhost — "Unconfined" is rejected), capabilities.drop: [ALL] is mandatory, and allowPrivilegeEscalation: false (prevents setuid binaries). Additional volume type restrictions beyond Baseline's hostPath ban. Restricted is the goal for sensitive workloads — it's the "you've done security right" level.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Restricted — Beyond Baseline
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  BASELINE (minimum)              RESTRICTED (hardened)           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐      ┌────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ✗ hostNetwork          │      │ ✗ hostNetwork (inherited)  │  │
  │  │ ✗ hostPath             │      │ ✗ hostPath (inherited)     │  │
  │  │ ✗ privileged           │      │ ✗ privileged (inherited)   │  │
  │  │ ✗ CAP_SYS_ADMIN        │      │ ✗ ALL capabilities dropped │  │
  │  │                        │      │   (not just dangerous ones)│  │
  │  │ ✓ any user allowed     │      │ ✗ UID 0 — runAsNonRoot    │  │
  │  │ ✓ any seccomp allowed  │      │ ✗ Unconfined seccomp       │  │
  │  │ ✓ privilege escalation │      │ ✗ allowPrivEscalation:false│  │
  │  │ ✓ default volume types │      │ ✗ additional volume types  │  │
  │  │                        │      │   (CSI ephemeral w/priv)   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────┘      └────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The prod namespace enforces Restricted. This means every pod must: run as UID 1000 (via PodSecurityContext), drop ALL capabilities (with NET_BIND_SERVICE explicitly added back for web/api), use RuntimeDefault seccomp, and set allowPrivilegeEscalation: false. If a developer accidentally sets runAsUser: 0, the pod is REJECTED at admission — it never reaches production.

Security Fundamentals Q76 of 200
How do you enforce Pod Security Standards on a namespace?
A) By installing a separate admission controller — PSS is not built into Kubernetes
B) By editing the kubelet configuration on every node
C) By creating a PodSecurityPolicy resource (deprecated since v1.21)
D) Using Pod Security Admission labels on the namespace: pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted (REJECT violations), pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit=restricted (LOG violations — useful for monitoring), pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn=restricted (SHOW warning to user). Three modes: enforce, audit, warn
✅ Correct Answer: D

Pod Security Admission (PSA) is a built-in admission controller (since Kubernetes 1.22, stable in 1.25) that enforces Pod Security Standards via namespace labels. Three modes: enforce rejects non-compliant pods (the hard block), audit logs violations without blocking (useful for monitoring what would break before enabling enforce), warn shows a warning to the user in kubectl output (like a compiler warning — "this pod violates Restricted: runAsUser=0"). The label format: pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted with optional version pinning via enforce-version: latest.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PSA — Namespace Labels Control Everything
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  NAMESPACE: anihpj-prod                                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Labels:                                                     │  │
  │  │   pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted           │  │
  │  │   pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-version: latest      │  │
  │  │   pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit: restricted             │  │
  │  │   pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn: restricted              │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  THREE MODES:                                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ enforce → REJECT: pod is NOT created                      │   │
  │  │          "Error: violates PodSecurity 'restricted:latest'"│   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │ audit   → LOG: pod IS created, violation logged           │   │
  │  │          (Useful: "what would break if we enable enforce?")│   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │ warn    → WARN: pod IS created, user sees warning         │   │
  │  │          "Warning: would violate PodSecurity 'restricted'"│   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Dev namespaces use enforce=baseline + audit=restricted (catches Restricted violations without blocking dev velocity). Staging uses enforce=baseline + warn=restricted (developers see warnings and fix before prod). Production uses enforce=restricted (hard block — no Restricted violation ever reaches production). This graduated approach prevents "security surprises" in production.

Security Fundamentals Q77 of 200
What's a good Pod Security Admission strategy for anihpj/jobpost across dev, staging, and prod?
A) dev namespace: enforce=baseline + audit=restricted (catches issues without blocking developers), staging: enforce=baseline + warn=restricted (developers see warnings and fix before going to prod), prod: enforce=restricted (hard block — no violations reach production). This graduated approach tightens security as code moves through environments
B) All namespaces should use enforce=restricted — security first, always
C) Dev should be privileged (unrestricted), staging should be baseline, prod should be restricted
D) PSA labels aren't needed if you have NetworkPolicy and RBAC configured
✅ Correct Answer: A

A graduated enforcement strategy balances security with developer productivity. The key insight: developers need to iterate fast in dev, but production must be locked down. The solution: use different enforcement modes per environment. Dev uses audit mode for Restricted violations — developers see what would fail in production without their deployments being blocked. Staging adds warnings — the CI/CD pipeline shows "this will fail in prod." Production hard-enforces — pods that violate Restricted cannot be deployed. This creates a "shift left" effect where security issues are caught early without breaking dev workflows.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Graduated PSA Strategy — Shift Left
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  DEV ──────────────► STAGING ──────────────► PRODUCTION         │
  │  ┌────────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐  │
  │  │ enforce:       │  │ enforce:       │  │ enforce:         │  │
  │  │   baseline     │  │   baseline     │  │   restricted     │  │
  │  │ audit:         │  │ warn:          │  │                  │  │
  │  │   restricted   │  │   restricted   │  │ (hard block)     │  │
  │  └────────────────┘  └────────────────┘  └──────────────────┘  │
  │        │                    │                     │              │
  │        ▼                    ▼                     ▼              │
  │  Pod with runAsUser:0  Pod with runAsUser:0  Pod with runAsUser:0│
  │  → DEPLOYED (Baseline   → DEPLOYED + WARNING  → REJECTED!        │
  │    allows it)             "Fix before prod"     "violates         │
  │  → AUDIT LOG: "pod X     → Dev fixes it         Restricted"       │
  │    would violate                                    │             │
  │    Restricted"                                      ▼             │
  │                                              Dev fixes early ✓   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: This graduated strategy is implemented via the k8s/15-pod-security.yaml manifest. Each namespace gets its own PSA labels. The CI/CD pipeline checks: "Does this pod pass enforce=restricted?" If not, the pipeline shows a warning. Developers fix security issues at PR time, not when the deployment fails in production.

Security Fundamentals Q78 of 200
Can you have different Pod Security Standard levels for different pods in the SAME namespace?
A) Yes — you can use pod-level annotations to override the namespace policy
B) No. PSA labels are per-NAMESPACE — they apply to ALL pods in that namespace uniformly. If you need different security levels for different pods, you MUST put them in different namespaces. This is intentional design — it encourages proper namespace isolation (dev/staging/prod should be separate namespaces anyway)
C) Yes — you can use a combination of namespace labels and pod annotations for mixed levels
D) Only the privileged level is namespace-wide; Baseline and Restricted can be per-pod
✅ Correct Answer: B

PSA labels are namespace-scoped only — there is no per-pod override. This is by design. It enforces the principle that different security levels should live in different namespaces, which naturally encourages proper isolation (separate RBAC, NetworkPolicy, ResourceQuota per namespace). If you need some pods to run privileged and others restricted, put them in different namespaces — e.g., kube-system for privileged infrastructure pods, anihpj-prod for restricted application pods. No mixing in the same namespace.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PSA — Namespace-Level Only (By Design)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  ❌ CANNOT DO THIS (mixed levels in one namespace):              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Namespace: anihpj-prod                                      │  │
  │  │ enforce: restricted                                         │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │ pod-A: restricted (OK)    pod-B: privileged (REJECTED!)    │  │
  │  │                              ↑                             │  │
  │  │              "violates PodSecurity 'restricted'"            │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ✅ DO THIS INSTEAD (separate namespaces per level):             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Namespace: anihpj-prod   │  │ Namespace: anihpj-monitoring │ │
  │  │ enforce: restricted      │  │ enforce: privileged           │ │
  │  │                          │  │                              │ │
  │  │ pod-A: restricted ✓     │  │ falco-daemon: privileged ✓  │ │
  │  │ pod-B: restricted ✓     │  │ Only monitoring pods here   │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘  └──────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: This is why we have separate namespaces: anihpj-prod (restricted), anihpj-staging (baseline), anihpj-dev (baseline + audit restricted), and kube-system (privileged for infrastructure). Each namespace has its own PSA level, RBAC, NetworkPolicy, and ResourceQuota. A pod that needs privileged access (like a monitoring agent) goes in kube-system, not in the application namespace — full isolation enforced.

3.2 Pod Security AdmissionsQ79–Q83

Security Fundamentals Q79 of 200
What happens if you label a namespace enforce=restricted but deploy a pod with runAsUser: 0 (root)?
A) The pod is deployed with a warning — Kubernetes will fix it automatically
B) The pod runs as root but is flagged for review
C) The pod is REJECTED at admission time — it's never created. The kubectl output shows: "Error from server (Forbidden): pods 'bad-pod' is forbidden: violates PodSecurity 'restricted:latest': runAsUser=0 (pod must not set runAsUser=0)." The pod YAML is valid Kubernetes syntax, but the admission controller blocks it before etcd write
D) The pod is created but immediately terminated by the kubelet
✅ Correct Answer: C

When enforce=restricted is active, the Pod Security Admission controller intercepts EVERY pod creation request. It validates the pod spec against the Restricted standard BEFORE writing to etcd. If the pod specifies runAsUser: 0 (or the image defaults to root without an override), the admission controller returns a detailed error and the pod is never created. This is a hard block — there's no "override" or "exception" at the pod level. The only way to deploy a root pod in a Restricted namespace is to change the namespace label (which you shouldn't do).

📝 Detailed Explanation
PSA Enforcement — Hard Block at Admission
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  $ kubectl apply -f root-pod.yaml -n anihpj-prod                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ API SERVER — Admission Control Pipeline                     │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │ ① Authenticate: user=devops@corp.com ✓                     │  │
  │  │ ② Authorize: RBAC allows create pods ✓                     │  │
  │  │ ③ Mutating Admission: no mutations needed                  │  │
  │  │ ④ Pod Security Admission:                                   │  │
  │  │    Check: runAsUser=0 allowed?                              │  │
  │  │    Policy: RESTRICTED → runAsUser MUST NOT be 0            │  │
  │  │    Result: VIOLATION → REJECT                             │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │  Error from server (Forbidden): pods "root-pod" is         │  │
  │  │  forbidden: violates PodSecurity "restricted:latest":      │  │
  │  │  runAsUser=0 (pod must not set runAsUser=0)                │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  RESULT: Pod was NEVER stored in etcd. Never scheduled.           │
  │          Never ran. Blocked at the admission gate.                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: This is tested in CI/CD — the pipeline runs a dry-run: kubectl apply -f pod.yaml --dry-run=server -n anihpj-prod. If PSA rejects it, the pipeline fails BEFORE merging to main. Developers see the exact error message and know exactly what to fix (set runAsNonRoot: true, use UID 1000). No root pod has ever reached the production namespace.

Security Fundamentals Q80 of 200
How do PSA labels work and what are the three enforcement modes?
A) PSA labels are set on pods, not namespaces — each pod controls its own security level
B) There are only two modes: On and Off
C) PSA labels are set in the kube-apiserver configuration — not on namespaces
D) Namespace labels: pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted (BLOCK violations — pod not created), pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit=restricted (LOG violations — pod IS created, violation recorded in audit log), pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn=restricted (WARN user — pod IS created, user sees warning in kubectl output). Three modes for progressive enforcement
✅ Correct Answer: D

The three PSA modes provide a graduated enforcement path. enforce is the hard block — the pod is rejected. audit is passive — the pod is created, but the violation is recorded in the audit log (useful for answering "what would break if I enable enforce?"). warn is user-facing — the pod is created, but the user sees a warning message (useful for educating developers without blocking them). The recommended strategy: start with audit+warn, monitor for violations, fix issues, then enable enforce.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PSA Modes — Graduated Enforcement
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  MODE       │ BEHAVIOR              │ USE CASE                  │
  │  ───────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────────│
  │  enforce    │ REJECTS pod           │ Production: hard block    │
  │             │ Pod NOT created       │ "No violations allowed"   │
  │             │ Returns error to user │                           │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │  audit      │ LOGS violation        │ Pre-production: monitoring│
  │             │ Pod IS created        │ "What would break?"       │
  │             │ Violation in audit log│                           │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │  warn       │ WARNS user            │ Education: teach devs     │
  │             │ Pod IS created        │ "Fix this before prod"    │
  │             │ Warning in kubectl    │                           │
  └─────────────┴───────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘
  │                                                                  │
  │  EXAMPLE LABEL SET:                                              │
  │  pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted                  │
  │  pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-version: latest              │
  │  pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit: restricted                    │
  │  pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn: restricted                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: All three modes are used simultaneously in most namespaces. The enforce mode is the "security gate", audit mode feeds the SIEM (security team monitors violations), and warn mode educates developers in real-time. A developer running kubectl apply -f pod.yaml -n anihpj-staging immediately sees: "Warning: would violate PodSecurity 'restricted': runAsUser=0" — they fix it before it becomes a blocking production issue.

Security Fundamentals Q81 of 200
Why should a CI/CD pipeline namespace (Jenkins, GitLab Runner) use "privileged" PSS level?
A) CI/CD runners often need to build container images (Docker-in-Docker or Kaniko with hostPath), which requires elevated privileges. But this is an EXCEPTION — CI/CD namespaces must be heavily locked down in OTHER ways: strict RBAC (only CI service account), NetworkPolicy (no egress except to registry + VCS), resource quotas (prevent runaway builds), and dedicated nodes (taints/tolerations to prevent other workloads from landing on CI nodes)
B) They shouldn't — CI/CD namespaces should always use Restricted
C) CI/CD pipelines don't need any special security — they can run in any namespace
D) Privileged mode is only for production — CI/CD should use it too for consistency
✅ Correct Answer: A

CI/CD pipelines are a necessary exception to the "everything should be Restricted" rule. Building container images typically requires elevated access: Docker-in-Docker needs the Docker socket, Kaniko needs hostPath for layer caching, BuildKit needs specific capabilities. However, this doesn't mean the CI/CD namespace gets a "free pass" on security. Instead, it gets compensating controls: dedicated nodes (no application pods on CI nodes), strict RBAC (only the CI service account can deploy), NetworkPolicy (CI pods can only reach the registry and VCS), ResourceQuota (prevents runaway builds from consuming all cluster resources), and Falco monitoring (detects if CI pods do anything unexpected like network scanning).

📝 Detailed Explanation
CI/CD Namespace — Privileged PSS + Compensating Controls
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  Namespace: ci-cd                                                │
  │  enforce: privileged              (builders need host access)    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  COMPENSATING CONTROLS (defense-in-depth):                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ✅ RBAC: Only ci-deployer SA can deploy                    │  │
  │  │ ✅ NetworkPolicy: egress ONLY to ECR + GitHub + PyPI      │  │
  │  │ ✅ ResourceQuota: max 4 CPU, 8Gi memory per namespace     │  │
  │  │ ✅ Node taint: ci-only=:NoSchedule → only CI pods         │  │
  │  │ ✅ Falco: alert on: unexpected process, outbound to internet│  │
  │  │ ✅ Audit: all pod exec, all Secret access logged          │  │
  │  │ ✅ Image: Kaniko (userspace builder — no Docker socket!)  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  RESULT: Builders get the access they need, but they're          │
  │  ISOLATED from the rest of the cluster. Compromise of a          │
  │  CI pod cannot spread to application namespaces.                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The CI/CD namespace uses enforce=privileged but runs on dedicated ci-worker nodes (tainted). NetworkPolicy restricts egress to ECR (image push), GitHub (source checkout), and PyPI (dependency download). The CI ServiceAccount has RBAC ONLY for the CI namespace — it cannot deploy to production. If a malicious dependency compromises the build, the attacker is trapped in the CI namespace with no path to production.

Security Fundamentals Q82 of 200
What's the relationship between Pod Security Admission (PSA) and the old PodSecurityPolicy (PSP)?
A) PSA and PSP are the same thing — just renamed in Kubernetes 1.25
B) PSP was deprecated in v1.21 and REMOVED in v1.25. PSA is its replacement, built directly into the API server (no separate admission controller needed). PSA is simpler: namespace labels instead of complex PSP resources, three standard levels instead of custom policies, and no need to bind PSPs to ServiceAccounts. If you're on Kubernetes 1.25+, use PSA — PSP is gone
C) PSA and PSP can be used together for defense-in-depth
D) PSP is still the recommended approach — PSA is experimental
✅ Correct Answer: B

PodSecurityPolicy (PSP) was the old way to enforce pod security. It was complex (required creating PSP resources + RBAC bindings), easy to misconfigure (forgetting to bind the PSP meant pods ran unrestricted), and was deprecated in Kubernetes 1.21 and completely removed in 1.25. Pod Security Admission (PSA) is the replacement: built into the API server, no separate admission controller to deploy, uses simple namespace labels, and provides three standard profiles instead of custom policies. If you're on Kubernetes 1.25+, PSP doesn't exist — use PSA.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PSP → PSA Migration — Simpler, Better, Built-in
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  PodSecurityPolicy (REMOVED in v1.25):                           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: policy/v1beta1                                 │  │
  │  │ kind: PodSecurityPolicy                                    │  │
  │  │ spec:                                                      │  │
  │  │   privileged: false                                        │  │
  │  │   runAsUser: MustRunAsNonRoot                             │  │
  │  │   requiredDropCapabilities: [ALL]                         │  │
  │  │   ...90+ lines of YAML...                                 │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │ THEN: Create Role + RoleBinding to grant USE of the PSP   │  │
  │  │ ⚠️ Forgetting the binding = pod runs unrestricted!        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Pod Security Admission (CURRENT — since v1.22, stable v1.25):   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Namespace labels ONLY:                                     │  │
  │  │   pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted           │  │
  │  │   pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-version: latest      │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │ That's it. No PSP YAML. No RBAC bindings.                 │  │
  │  │ ✅ Built-in. ✅ Simpler. ✅ Safer defaults.                 │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The cluster runs Kubernetes 1.30+ — PSPs don't exist. All pod security is managed through PSA namespace labels in k8s/15-pod-security.yaml. The migration from PSP (in the old cluster) to PSA took 15 minutes: label each namespace with the appropriate enforce/audit/warn level. No YAML, no RBAC bindings, no policy objects to maintain.

Security Fundamentals Q83 of 200
How do you audit which pods in your cluster would violate Restricted before enforcing it?
A) You can't — you must enable enforce and see what breaks
B) Run kubectl describe on every pod — it shows PSA compliance status
C) Set pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit=restricted on the namespace. This LOGS (doesn't block) every pod that would violate Restricted. Then check the audit logs to see which pods need fixing BEFORE you enable enforce. Also use warn=restricted to show warnings to developers in real-time
D) Use a third-party tool — Kubernetes has no built-in audit capability for PSA
✅ Correct Answer: C

The audit mode is the safe way to discover compliance gaps. Set pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit: restricted on the namespace. Every pod creation/update is checked against the Restricted standard — but instead of rejecting, the result is written to the audit log. You can then query: "Which pods in namespace X violated Restricted in the last 7 days?" Fix those pods, then enable enforce. The warn mode adds real-time feedback: developers see warnings in kubectl output. Together, audit+warn gives you a complete picture of compliance without breaking anything.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Audit Mode — Discover Before You Enforce
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  Step 1: Add audit label (NO blocking):                           │
  │  kubectl label ns anihpj-prod \                                  │
  │    pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit=restricted                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Step 2: Let the cluster run for 1-7 days. All pods work fine.   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Step 3: Query audit logs for violations:                         │
  │  $ cat audit.log | jq 'select(.annotations["pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit-violations"] != null)' │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Sample output:                                                   │
  │  {                                                               │
  │    "objectRef": {"name": "anihpj-api-7d4f8b9c-xyz"},            │
  │    "annotations": {                                              │
  │      "pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit-violations":             │
  │        "would violate 'restricted': runAsUser=0,                 │
  │         capabilities must drop ALL"                              │
  │    }                                                             │
  │  }                                                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Step 4: Fix violations → retest → enable enforce when clean     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Before enabling enforce=restricted on production, the security team ran audit=restricted for 2 weeks. They discovered 3 pods that would violate Restricted (all had CAP_NET_RAW from a copy-pasted securityContext). The teams fixed those, verified zero audit violations for 48 hours, then enabled enforce. Zero production impact — the audit mode caught everything before enforcement.

3.3 AuthenticationQ84–Q89

Security Fundamentals Q84 of 200
What is the difference between "authentication" and "authorization" in Kubernetes?
A) Authentication is for human users; authorization is for ServiceAccounts
B) They are the same process — Kubernetes combines them into one step
C) Authentication = "Who are you?" (validating certificates, tokens, OIDC claims — proving identity). Authorization = "What are you allowed to do?" (checking RBAC rules — does this identity have permission for this action?). Both are required — knowing someone's identity doesn't mean they should be allowed to do everything. Authentication happens FIRST, then authorization
D) Authorization happens first — Kubernetes checks what you can do before checking who you are
✅ Correct Answer: C

Authentication and authorization are sequential, independent checks in the API server pipeline. Authentication answers "Who are you?" by validating credentials (X.509 cert CN, JWT token signature, OIDC claims). If authentication fails → 401 Unauthorized. Authorization answers "Can you do this?" by checking RBAC rules for the authenticated identity. If authorization fails → 403 Forbidden. The distinction matters: a valid certificate proves WHO you are but doesn't grant any permissions — those come from RBAC separately.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AuthN vs AuthZ — Sequential, Independent
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  kubectl delete deployment anihpj-api -n anihpj                  │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ STEP 1: AUTHENTICATION ("Who are you?")                   │   │
  │  │ ─────────────────────────────────                        │   │
  │  │ Present X.509 cert with CN=alice, O=developers           │   │
  │  │ API server validates: cert signed by CA? Not expired?    │   │
  │  │                                                          │   │
  │  │ ✅ PASS → Identity: "alice", Groups: ["developers"]       │   │
  │  │ ❌ FAIL → 401 Unauthorized                                │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │       │ (identity established)                                   │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ STEP 2: AUTHORIZATION ("Can alice delete deployments?")   │   │
  │  │ ─────────────────────────────────                        │   │
  │  │ Check ALL RoleBindings in namespace "anihpj"              │   │
  │  │ Check ALL ClusterRoleBindings                             │   │
  │  │ Is "alice" OR group "developers" bound to a role that    │   │
  │  │ allows "delete" on "deployments" in "anihpj"?            │   │
  │  │                                                          │   │
  │  │ ✅ PASS (matching RBAC found) → 200 OK, deployment deleted│   │
  │  │ ❌ FAIL (no matching RBAC) → 403 Forbidden                │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: All human operators authenticate via OIDC (Azure AD). Their identity is established — "this is alice@corp.com". But what Alice can DO is controlled entirely by RBAC. Alice might be authenticated (valid token) but have zero permissions if no RoleBinding exists for her. Authentication proves identity; authorization grants power. They're independent and both are mandatory.

Security Fundamentals Q85 of 200
What are the three main authentication methods in Kubernetes?
A) Username/password, SSH keys, and API tokens
B) Basic auth, digest auth, and OAuth — same as web applications
C) Only X.509 certificates are supported — tokens are deprecated
D) ① X.509 Client Certificates (strong but hard to rotate — used by kubelet and control plane components), ② Bearer Tokens / JWT ServiceAccount tokens (used by pods via projected volumes, time-bound, auto-rotated by kubelet), ③ OpenID Connect / OIDC (delegate auth to external IdP — Google, GitHub, Azure AD, Okta — best for human users, supports MFA, short-lived tokens)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kubernetes supports three primary authentication strategies, each for different use cases. X.509 certificates are used for infrastructure components (kubelet, controller-manager, scheduler) — they're strong but have no built-in rotation mechanism. Bearer/JWT tokens (bound ServiceAccount tokens) are used by pods — they're short-lived (1 hour default), auto-rotated by the kubelet, and audience-bound. OIDC is the recommended method for human users — it delegates authentication to a trusted identity provider, supports MFA, and uses short-lived auto-refreshing tokens. No static passwords or long-lived shared secrets.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Three Authentication Methods — When to Use Each
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  METHOD             │ USED BY          │ STRENGTHS / WEAKNESSES │
  │  ──────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────│
  │  X.509 Client Certs │ kubelet,         │ ✅ Strong crypto        │
  │                     │ controller-mgr,  │ ✅ No external deps    │
  │                     │ scheduler        │ ❌ Manual rotation     │
  │                     │                  │ ❌ No MFA             │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │  Bearer/JWT Tokens  │ Pods (auto-      │ ✅ Auto-rotated        │
  │  (Bound SA Tokens)  │ mounted)         │ ✅ Short-lived (1h)    │
  │                     │                  │ ✅ Audience-bound      │
  │                     │                  │ ❌ Not for humans      │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │  OIDC (OpenID       │ Human operators  │ ✅ MFA support         │
  │  Connect)           │ (kubectl users)  │ ✅ Centralized user    │
  │                     │                  │   lifecycle            │
  │                     │                  │ ✅ Short-lived tokens  │
  │                     │                  │ ✅ Audit per human     │
  │                     │                  │ ❌ Requires external IdP│
  └─────────────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Control plane components authenticate via X.509 certs (kubeadm-managed). Application pods use bound ServiceAccount tokens (auto-mounted, 1-hour expiry). Human operators (developers, SREs, security team) authenticate via OIDC (Azure AD with MFA). No one uses static tokens or kubeconfig files with embedded long-lived credentials — those are security anti-patterns.

Security Fundamentals Q86 of 200
How does a pod authenticate to the Kubernetes API?
A) The projected ServiceAccount token volume auto-mounts three files at /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/: token (JWT — the credential), ca.crt (CA certificate — verifies the API server's TLS cert, prevents MITM), and namespace (the pod's namespace). The pod presents the token in the Authorization: Bearer header. The kubelet automatically rotates the token before expiry
B) Pods use a shared cluster password stored in a ConfigMap
C) Pods don't authenticate — they communicate with the API server without credentials
D) Each pod generates its own self-signed certificate at startup
✅ Correct Answer: A

Every pod that doesn't disable automountServiceAccountToken gets a projected volume mounted at /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/ containing three files. The token is a JWT signed by the API server — the pod sends it as an Authorization: Bearer <token> header. The ca.crt is the cluster's CA certificate — the pod uses it to verify the API server's TLS certificate (prevents man-in-the-middle attacks). The namespace file contains the pod's namespace name. The kubelet automatically rotates the token before it expires (default 1-hour lifetime).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod Authentication — Projected SA Token Volume
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  POD FILESYSTEM:                                                 │
  │  /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ token       ← JWT signed by API server (the credential)    │  │
  │  │             eyJhbGciOiJSUzI1NiIsImtpZCI6...               │  │
  │  │                                                           │  │
  │  │ ca.crt      ← Cluster CA certificate (verify API server)  │  │
  │  │             -----BEGIN CERTIFICATE-----                   │  │
  │  │                                                           │  │
  │  │ namespace   ← Pod's namespace (plain text)                │  │
  │  │             anihpj-prod                                   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  API CALL FROM POD:                                              │
  │  curl -k https://kubernetes.default.svc/api/v1/namespaces/...   │
  │    -H "Authorization: Bearer $(cat /var/run/secrets/.../token)"  │
  │    --cacert /var/run/secrets/.../ca.crt                          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  TOKEN PROPERTIES:                                               │
  │  • Lifetime: 1 hour (default), max 24 hours                      │
  │  • Audience: bound to API server (can't use for other services)  │
  │  • Rotation: kubelet rotates at 80% of lifetime                  │
  │  • Revocation: delete the pod → token invalid                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Most application pods (web, api, db, redis) set automountServiceAccountToken: false — they don't need API access and mount no token. Only pods that genuinely need to call the Kubernetes API (the cert-manager controller, External Secrets Operator, CI/CD deployer) mount SA tokens. This follows least privilege: if you don't need API access, don't give it.

Security Fundamentals Q87 of 200
What is the difference between legacy ServiceAccount tokens and projected (bound) tokens?
A) They are the same — just different names for the same token type
B) Legacy tokens: non-expiring, stored in a Secret object, valid FOREVER (never rotate). Bound (projected) tokens: time-bound (default 1h, max 24h), audience-bound (tied to the API server), auto-rotated by kubelet before expiry, NOT stored in Secrets. Since Kubernetes 1.21+, bound tokens are the DEFAULT. Always prefer bound tokens — they limit the damage window if stolen
C) Legacy tokens are more secure because they never expire — no rotation needed
D) Bound tokens are only available in managed Kubernetes services (EKS/AKS/GKE)
✅ Correct Answer: B

Legacy tokens (pre-1.21 default) were stored in Secret objects and had NO expiry — they lasted forever. If a legacy token leaked (e.g., from a compromised pod), the attacker had permanent API access until someone manually deleted the Secret. Bound (projected) tokens solve this: they're time-limited (1 hour default), tied to a specific pod (pod UID and audience), auto-rotated by the kubelet, and never stored in etcd as Secrets. If stolen, they expire within 1 hour. KCSA exam: understand that bound tokens are the modern, secure default.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Legacy vs Bound Tokens
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  LEGACY TOKEN (pre-1.21, still exists for backward compat):      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ • Stored as a Secret object in etcd                         │  │
  │  │ • NO expiration — valid FOREVER                             │  │
  │  │ • NOT audience-bound (can use for any purpose)              │  │
  │  │ • NO auto-rotation                                          │  │
  │  │ • ⚠️ If leaked: permanent access, must manually delete      │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  BOUND TOKEN (default since 1.21, projected volume):             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ • NOT stored in etcd — ephemeral, memory-only               │  │
  │  │ • EXPIRES: 1 hour (default), max 24 hours                   │  │
  │  │ • AUDIENCE-bound: tied to API server (can't reuse elsewhere)│  │
  │  │ • POD-bound: tied to specific pod UID                       │  │
  │  │ • AUTO-ROTATED: kubelet refreshes at 80% of lifetime       │  │
  │  │ • ✅ If leaked: expires within 1 hour, auto-rotated away    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: All pods use bound tokens (the Kubernetes default since 1.21). The token lifetime is 1 hour — if a pod is compromised and its token is stolen, the attacker has at most 1 hour of API access (and the kubelet rotates the token before that). No legacy Secret-based tokens exist in the cluster. This dramatically reduces the damage window from token compromise.

Security Fundamentals Q88 of 200
In the anihpj/jobpost cluster, which authentication method should human operators use and why?
A) X.509 client certificates — they're the most secure option
B) A shared kubeconfig file with a long-lived bearer token — it's the simplest
C) OIDC (e.g., GitHub SSO or Azure AD). Benefits: MFA supported by the identity provider, centralized user lifecycle (one place to revoke access), audit trail per human ("alice@corp.com deleted this deployment"), no shared kubeconfig files with embedded long-lived credentials, short-lived auto-refreshing tokens via kubectl oidc-login plugin
D) ServiceAccount tokens — they're designed for all Kubernetes users
✅ Correct Answer: C

OIDC is the gold standard for human Kubernetes users. It delegates authentication to a professional identity provider (Azure AD, Google Workspace, Okta, GitHub) that already has MFA, user lifecycle management, and audit trails. When Alice leaves the company, disabling her account in Azure AD instantly revokes ALL access — including Kubernetes. With certificate-based auth, you'd need to manually revoke her cert (and hope you found all copies). OIDC also enables per-user audit logs: "alice@corp.com ran kubectl delete deployment at 3 AM" vs "someone with the shared admin cert ran it."

📝 Detailed Explanation
OIDC — Why It's Best for Humans
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  OIDC FLOW (simplified):                                         │
  │  ┌─────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────┐              │
  │  │ kubectl │────►│ Azure AD     │────►│ API      │              │
  │  │ (user)  │     │ (IdP)        │     │ Server   │              │
  │  │         │     │ • MFA ✓      │     │ verifies  │              │
  │  │         │     │ • User db    │     │ JWT sig   │              │
  │  │         │     │ • Audit log  │     │ → allows  │              │
  │  └─────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────────┘              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  BENEFITS OVER CERTIFICATE-BASED AUTH:                           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ✅ MFA: Azure AD enforces MFA (certificates can't do MFA) │  │
  │  │ ✅ User lifecycle: disable in Azure AD → access revoked   │  │
  │  │ ✅ Audit per human: "alice@corp.com" vs "CN=admin"        │  │
  │  │ ✅ No shared kubeconfig: each user has their own           │  │
  │  │ ✅ Short-lived tokens: auto-refresh, no permanent creds   │  │
  │  │ ✅ No cert rotation: IdP handles token lifecycle          │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: All developers and SREs authenticate via Azure AD OIDC. The kubeconfig contains only the cluster endpoint and OIDC configuration — NO credentials. The kubectl oidc-login plugin handles the OIDC flow: opens browser → Azure AD login with MFA → short-lived token → kubectl uses it. Audit logs show individual identities: "alice@corp.com", not a shared "admin" cert. When a developer leaves, HR disables their Azure AD account — access is revoked within minutes.

Security Fundamentals Q89 of 200
Why is the cluster's CA (Certificate Authority) the most critical secret to protect?
A) The CA is only used during cluster initialization — it's not needed after that
B) The CA can be easily regenerated — losing it is not a security concern
C) The CA only affects new node joins — existing nodes continue to work fine
D) The CA signs ALL cluster certificates: API server, kubelet, etcd, ServiceAccount tokens. If the CA key is compromised, an attacker can forge ANY certificate — impersonate the API server (MITM all traffic), impersonate any kubelet (access all node secrets), create valid ServiceAccount tokens (full API access as any SA). If the CA key is lost, the cluster eventually stops (certificates expire and can't be renewed). Back up your CA key OFFLINE and encrypt it
✅ Correct Answer: D

The cluster CA (stored at /etc/kubernetes/pki/ca.crt and ca.key) is the root of all trust in the cluster. Every certificate in the cluster is signed by this CA: the API server's serving cert, every kubelet's client cert, etcd's peer certs, and the signing key for ServiceAccount tokens. If an attacker steals the CA private key: they can issue themselves a certificate with any CN (e.g., "system:admin" or "system:kube-controller-manager") and gain unrestricted cluster access. If the CA key is lost: certificates cannot be renewed when they expire (typically 1 year), and the cluster eventually becomes unusable. CA key backup is the #1 disaster recovery priority.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CA Compromise — Total Trust Collapse
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  CLUSTER CA (ca.key + ca.crt)                                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ SIGNS EVERYTHING:                                          │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │ ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐      │  │
  │  │ │ API Server   │  │ Kubelet      │  │ etcd Peer    │      │  │
  │  │ │ cert         │  │ certs        │  │ certs        │      │  │
  │  │ │ (serves TLS) │  │ (client auth)│  │ (raft comms) │      │  │
  │  │ └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘      │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │ ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐                        │  │
  │  │ │ SA Token     │  │ Front-Proxy  │                        │  │
  │  │ │ Signing Key  │  │ CA           │                        │  │
  │  │ │ (JWT issuer) │  │ (aggregation)│                        │  │
  │  │ └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘                        │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  CA KEY COMPROMISED → ATTACKER CAN:                              │
  │  • Issue cert for CN=system:admin → full cluster-admin access    │
  │  • Issue cert for CN=system:kube-controller-manager → control    │
  │  • Issue cert for CN=system:node:* → impersonate any kubelet     │
  │  • Sign forged SA tokens → access as any ServiceAccount          │
  │  • MITM: impersonate API server → intercept all traffic          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The CA key is stored in an offline HSM (Hardware Security Module) — it's never on a network-connected machine. The backup is encrypted and stored in a physical safe. Certificate expiry is monitored via kubeadm certs check-expiration with alerts at 30 days. The CA key is the "keys to the kingdom" — its security is treated accordingly.

3.4 AuthorizationQ90–Q96

Security Fundamentals Q90 of 200
What's the difference between a Role and a ClusterRole?
A) Role is for human users; ClusterRole is for ServiceAccounts
B) Role is NAMESPACE-SCOPED ("In namespace 'anihpj', you can get pods"). ClusterRole is CLUSTER-SCOPED ("In ANY namespace, you can get pods" OR "You can get nodes" — nodes aren't namespaced). Use Role for 90% of cases (most permissions should be namespace-scoped). Use ClusterRole for cross-namespace access or access to cluster-wide resources (nodes, PVs, namespaces themselves)
C) Role is deprecated — only ClusterRole should be used in modern Kubernetes
D) Role applies to pods; ClusterRole applies to the API server itself
✅ Correct Answer: B

Role is scoped to a single namespace — the permissions it grants only apply within that namespace. ClusterRole is cluster-wide — it can grant access to resources across ALL namespaces AND to cluster-scoped resources (nodes, PersistentVolumes, namespaces themselves, CSINodes). Best practice: default to Role (namespace-scoped, least privilege). Only use ClusterRole when you genuinely need cross-namespace access or access to cluster-wide resources. A ClusterRole bound via RoleBinding (not ClusterRoleBinding) is still scoped to a single namespace — this is a common pattern.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Role vs ClusterRole — Scope Matters
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  Role (namespace-scoped):                                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                   │  │
  │  │ kind: Role                                                 │  │
  │  │ metadata:                                                  │  │
  │  │   namespace: anihpj          ← ONLY works in this namespace│  │
  │  │ rules:                                                     │  │
  │  │ - apiGroups: [""]                                          │  │
  │  │   resources: ["pods"]                                      │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]                          │  │
  │  │ → Can only get/list/watch pods IN namespace "anihpj"       │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ClusterRole (cluster-scoped):                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                   │  │
  │  │ kind: ClusterRole                                          │  │
  │  │ metadata:                                                  │  │
  │  │   name: node-reader        ← NO namespace field!           │  │
  │  │ rules:                                                     │  │
  │  │ - apiGroups: [""]                                          │  │
  │  │   resources: ["nodes"]     ← Nodes are cluster-scoped      │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["get", "list"]                                   │  │
  │  │ → Can get/list nodes in the ENTIRE cluster                 │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The anihpj-api ServiceAccount has a Role (not ClusterRole) scoped to the anihpj namespace — it can only interact with resources in its own namespace. The monitoring system has a ClusterRole (to read node metrics cluster-wide). The CI/CD deployer has a ClusterRole (to deploy across dev/staging/prod namespaces). 90% of RBAC rules are namespace-scoped Roles — principle of least privilege.

Security Fundamentals Q91 of 200
How does RBAC authorization actually work when kubectl sends a request?
A) RBAC checks a single "allow/deny" list stored in a ConfigMap
B) RBAC only checks the user's group membership — individual users aren't checked
C) ① kubectl reads kubeconfig (cluster + user + namespace), ② Sends HTTPS request with credentials, ③ API server authenticates (validates cert/token → extracts user + groups), ④ Iterates ALL RoleBindings (in namespace) + ClusterRoleBindings (cluster-wide), ⑤ Finds matching subjects → checks Role/ClusterRole for matching verbs + resources, ⑥ ANY matching rule allows → ALLOW, ⑦ NO matching rule → DENY (default-deny for authorization)
D) RBAC is deny-by-default but allow-by-default for cluster-admin users
✅ Correct Answer: C

RBAC authorization is default-deny — if no RoleBinding/ClusterRoleBinding explicitly grants permission, the request is denied. The API server iterates through ALL RoleBindings in the target namespace AND ALL ClusterRoleBindings, looking for subjects that match the authenticated user/groups/SA. For each match, it checks if the referenced Role/ClusterRole allows the requested verb on the requested resource. If ANY single rule allows the action, the request is approved. If NO rule matches after checking everything → 403 Forbidden. There's no "deny" rule — you can only add "allow" rules.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RBAC Authorization — Default-Deny Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  REQUEST: alice wants to DELETE deployment "anihpj-api"          │
  │           in namespace "anihpj"                                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  API SERVER RBAC ENGINE:                                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │                                                           │  │
  │  │  CHECK 1: RoleBinding "dev-bind" in namespace "anihpj"    │  │
  │  │    Subject: alice ✓                                       │  │
  │  │    Role: "developer"                                      │  │
  │  │    Verbs: get, list, watch                                │  │
  │  │    → delete NOT in verbs → NO MATCH                       │  │
  │  │                                                           │  │
  │  │  CHECK 2: RoleBinding "admin-bind" in namespace "anihpj"  │  │
  │  │    Subject: group "admins"                                │  │
  │  │    → alice's groups: [developers] → NO MATCH              │  │
  │  │                                                           │  │
  │  │  CHECK 3: ClusterRoleBinding "cluster-admin-binding"      │  │
  │  │    Subject: system:masters                                │  │
  │  │    → NO MATCH                                             │  │
  │  │                                                           │  │
  │  │  ALL CHECKS DONE → NO MATCH → 403 FORBIDDEN               │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: RBAC is audited quarterly using kubectl auth can-i --list --as=<user> -n <ns> for every ServiceAccount and human user. The audit verifies: "Does the anihpj-web SA have more permissions than needed? Can the CI/CD SA deploy to production?" Any overly permissive binding is flagged and tightened.

Security Fundamentals Q92 of 200
How do you bind a Role to a user or ServiceAccount?
A) RoleBinding (namespace-scoped): binds subjects (User, Group, ServiceAccount) to a Role in ONE namespace. ClusterRoleBinding (cluster-scoped): binds subjects to a ClusterRole across ALL namespaces. Subjects reference: User via X.509 CN, Group via X.509 O (Organization), ServiceAccount via system:serviceaccount:<ns>:<name>
B) Add the user's name directly to the Role resource — no separate binding needed
C) Use kubectl create user command — Kubernetes manages users natively
D) Edit the kubeconfig file to include RBAC permissions for each user
✅ Correct Answer: A

RBAC uses a two-resource model: the Role/ClusterRole defines WHAT permissions exist, and the RoleBinding/ClusterRoleBinding defines WHO gets them. RoleBinding binds subjects to a Role in a specific namespace. ClusterRoleBinding binds subjects to a ClusterRole across the entire cluster. Subjects are referenced by: User name (from X.509 certificate CN), Group name (from X.509 certificate O field), or ServiceAccount (full name: system:serviceaccount:<namespace>:<name>). This separation means you can create ONE Role and bind it to multiple subjects.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RBAC Binding — The Two-Resource Model
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  Role ("WHAT permissions")           RoleBinding ("WHO gets them")│
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ kind: Role             │         │ kind: RoleBinding        │ │
  │  │ name: pod-reader       │◄────────│ roleRef:                 │ │
  │  │ namespace: anihpj      │  binds  │   name: pod-reader       │ │
  │  │ rules:                 │         │ subjects:                │ │
  │  │ - resources: [pods]    │         │ - kind: User             │ │
  │  │   verbs: [get, list]   │         │   name: alice            │ │
  │  └────────────────────────┘         │ - kind: ServiceAccount   │ │
  │                                     │   name: anihpj-api       │ │
  │                                     │   namespace: anihpj      │ │
  │                                     └──────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  SUBJECT TYPES:                                                  │
  │  • User: alice (from X.509 cert CN=alice)                       │
  │  • Group: developers (from X.509 cert O=developers)              │
  │  • ServiceAccount: system:serviceaccount:anihpj:anihpj-api       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Each tier has a dedicated Role + RoleBinding. The anihpj-api SA has Role "api-role" (get/configmaps, nothing else) bound via RoleBinding "api-binding" in the anihpj namespace. The CI/CD deployer has Role "deployer-role" (update deployments) bound in dev/staging/prod namespaces separately (three RoleBindings, one per namespace). This separation makes auditing clear: look at a RoleBinding → see exactly who has what access.

Security Fundamentals Q93 of 200
What is kubectl auth can-i and why is it the #1 RBAC debugging tool?
A) It's a plugin that automatically fixes RBAC misconfigurations
B) It's a deprecated command — RBAC debugging must be done through audit logs
C) Tests whether an action is allowed WITHOUT performing it: kubectl auth can-i create pods --as=alice -n anihpj → yes/no. Can also list ALL permissions: kubectl auth can-i --list --as=bob. Essential for: verifying RBAC before applying, debugging "why can't Alice do X?", and auditing "what permissions does this SA actually have?"
D) It only works for cluster-admin users — regular users can't use it
✅ Correct Answer: C

kubectl auth can-i is the RBAC dry-run tool. It asks the API server's authorization engine: "Would this action be allowed?" without actually performing the action. Use --as=<user> to impersonate another user/SA and check THEIR permissions. Use --list to enumerate ALL permissions a subject has. This is invaluable for: "I just created an RBAC rule — did it work?", "Why can't Alice delete pods?", and security auditing: "Does this ServiceAccount have more permissions than it should?"

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl auth can-i — RBAC Debugging Examples
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  CHECK A SPECIFIC ACTION:                                        │
  │  ────────────────────────                                        │
  │  $ kubectl auth can-i create deployments --as=alice -n anihpj   │
  │  yes                                                            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  $ kubectl auth can-i delete secrets --as=alice -n anihpj       │
  │  no                                                             │
  │  → Alice can create deployments but can't touch secrets ✓       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  CHECK SA PERMISSIONS:                                           │
  │  ─────────────────────                                           │
  │  $ kubectl auth can-i --list \                                   │
  │      --as=system:serviceaccount:anihpj:anihpj-api -n anihpj     │
  │  Resources          Non-Resource URLs   Resource Names   Verbs   │
  │  pods               []                  []              [get list]│
  │  configmaps         []                  []              [get]    │
  │  → anihpj-api SA can only get/list pods + get configmaps ✓      │
  │  → Audit result: no excessive permissions ✓                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  VERIFY BEFORE APPLY:                                            │
  │  ────────────────────                                            │
  │  $ kubectl auth can-i create -f backdoor-pod.yaml \              │
  │      --as=system:serviceaccount:anihpj:anihpj-api -n anihpj     │
  │  no                                                             │
  │  → Even if the SA token is stolen, backdoor pods can't be created│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The security team runs kubectl auth can-i --list --as=<SA> for every ServiceAccount as part of the quarterly RBAC audit. Any SA with more permissions than documented is flagged. The CI/CD pipeline runs can-i checks before deploying new RBAC rules (prevent accidental privilege escalation). Every developer knows: "If you're not sure about permissions, kubectl auth can-i is the answer."

Security Fundamentals Q94 of 200
What are RBAC aggregation rules and when would you use them?
A) Aggregation rules automatically delete unused Roles to keep RBAC clean
B) Aggregation rules combine multiple namespaces into one RBAC domain
C) Aggregation rules are deprecated — use individual RoleBindings instead
D) ClusterRoles can aggregate other ClusterRoles using labels like rbac.authorization.k8s.io/aggregate-to-view: "true". This ClusterRole auto-includes in the built-in "view" ClusterRole.
