  │ libc6                │ 2.31      │ deb          │ CVE-... │
  │ openssl              │ 1.1.1t    │ deb          │ none    │
  │ ca-certificates      │ 20230311  │ deb          │ none    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CVE ANNOUNCED: "openssl < 1.1.1u has CRITICAL vulnerability"
  → Query SBOM database: "Images with openssl < 1.1.1u?"
  → Result: NONE (we have 1.1.1t — not affected!) ✓

For anihpj/jobpost: Every CI/CD pipeline run generates an SBOM with Syft and uploads it to Dependency-Track. The SBOM is attached to the container image as an OCI artifact. When CVEs are announced, the security team queries Dependency-Track: "Show all projects affected by CVE-XXXX-YYYYY" → instant list of affected images → prioritized patching in hours, not weeks.

Platform Security Q156 of 200
What is SLSA provenance and why does it matter for security?
A) SLSA provenance is a vulnerability severity score like CVSS — it measures risk
B) It's a Kubernetes-specific RBAC extension for fine-grained authorization
C) It's a license management tool that ensures open-source compliance
D) Provenance answers: "Who built this image, from what source, using what build process, at what time?" It's a verifiable, cryptographically signed statement. Example: "This image was built from commit abc123 of github.com/anihpj/jobpost by GitHub Actions on 2026-06-12 at 14:30 UTC" — verifiable by anyone. It prevents "I don't know where this image came from" scenarios
✅ Correct Answer: D

SLSA provenance (Supply-chain Levels for Software Artifacts) is a machine-readable, cryptographically signed statement describing HOW an artifact was produced. It captures: the builder identity (which CI/CD platform), the source repository and exact commit, the build command/recipe, all input materials (base images, dependencies with their digests), and the build timestamp. This creates an auditable chain of custody: from source code → build process → container image. Anyone can verify the provenance signature to confirm the image hasn't been tampered with since build.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SLSA Provenance — From Source to Signature
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SLSA PROVENANCE CHAIN OF CUSTODY                                    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                      │
  │  SOURCE CODE ──► BUILD SERVICE ──► CONTAINER IMAGE ──► DEPLOYMENT   │
  │  ┌────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌───────────┐ │
  │  │ Git Repo   │  │ GitHub Actions│  │ ECR Registry │  │ Kyverno   │ │
  │  │ commit:abc │─►│ builder:      │─►│ anihpj-api   │─►│ verify:   │ │
  │  │            │  │   actions/run │  │ @sha256:xyz  │  │ ✅/❌     │ │
  │  └────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └─────┬─────┘ │
  │                                                            │        │
  │  PROVENANCE (signed):                                      │        │
  │  {                                                  ┌──────▼──────┐ │
  │    "builder": "github.com/actions/runner",           │ DEPLOY ✓   │ │
  │    "source": "github.com/anihpj/jobpost@abc123",     │ or          │ │
  │    "materials": ["django@4.2.0", ...],              │ REJECT ✗   │ │
  │    "timestamp": "2026-06-12T14:30:00Z"               └─────────────┘ │
  │  }                                                                    │
  │  ↑ Cosign-signed → Rekor log (immutable proof)                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every image build generates SLSA Level 2+ provenance: GitHub Actions (hosted build service), signed provenance attached to the image, all inputs recorded with digests. The Kyverno admission policy verifies provenance before deployment: "Was this image built by our trusted GitHub Actions from our repository?" If provenance is missing, invalid, or from an unknown builder → image REJECTED. This prevents "mystery images" from reaching production.

Platform Security Q157 of 200
How do you integrate security scanning into a CI/CD pipeline?
A) ① Checkout → ② SAST (Bandit for Python, hadolint for Dockerfile) → ③ Build image → ④ SCA (Trivy scan → fail on CRITICAL/HIGH CVEs) → ⑤ SBOM generation (Syft) → ⑥ Sign image (Cosign keyless) → ⑦ Push to private registry → ⑧ Upload SBOM to security dashboard. "Shift left": catch at steps ②-④ (minutes to fix, zero user impact) instead of in production (emergency, users affected)
B) Security scanning only happens after deployment — you can't scan before the image exists
C) Just run Trivy once before the first deployment — continuous scanning is overhead
D) CI/CD security scanning is the cloud provider's responsibility — no pipeline changes needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

The CI/CD pipeline is the security gate between code and production. The pipeline runs multiple security checks at different stages: SAST (static analysis) finds code-level issues before building, SCA (software composition analysis) finds CVE in dependencies after building, signing creates cryptographic proof of origin, and SBOM generation creates the ingredients list. The "shift left" philosophy: catch issues as early as possible — finding a CVE in CI/CD takes minutes to fix; finding it in production is an emergency.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Security in CI/CD — The Complete Pipeline
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  PR OPEN ──► ① SAST ──► ② BUILD ──► ③ SCA ──► ④ SBOM ──► ⑤ SIGN ──► DEPLOY │
  │  ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ │
  │  │Bandit  │ │docker  │ │Trivy   │ │Syft    │ │Cosign  │ │Kyverno │ │
  │  │hadolint│ │build   │ │scan    │ │generate│ │sign    │ │verify  │ │
  │  │pip-audit│ │        │ │FAIL on │ │SBOM    │ │keyless │ │signature│ │
  │  │        │ │        │ │CRITICAL│ │        │ │        │ │+ admit │ │
  │  └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘ │
  │                                                                      │
  │  SHIFT LEFT:                    SHIFT RIGHT:                        │
  │  Catch issues in pipeline       Assume breach → runtime detection   │
  │  ⏱️ Fix in minutes              Falco, audit logs, behavioral       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The .github/workflows/build-scan.yaml pipeline runs on every PR: Bandit (Python SAST) → hadolint (Dockerfile lint) → docker build → Trivy scan (fail on CRITICAL/HIGH) → Syft SBOM → Cosign sign → push to ECR → ArgoCD syncs → Kyverno verifies signature. If ANY step fails, the PR is blocked and the developer gets a detailed report of what to fix.

Platform Security Q158 of 200
What is the "allowed registries" pattern and how do you enforce it?
A) It's a team policy document — developers agree to only use approved registries
B) Only images from approved registries can be deployed. Enforced via OPA/Gatekeeper or Kyverno admission policy: validate every container's image field matches allowed patterns (e.g., only *.ecr.aws.amazon.com/anihpj/* and gcr.io/distroless/*). This prevents: pulling from public Docker Hub (CVE risk + supply chain), typosquatted images (anihpj-api vs anihpj-api), and malicious registries. Defense-in-depth: also restrict egress NetworkPolicy to block pods from reaching unapproved registries
C) Kubernetes enforces allowed registries by default — no configuration needed
D) Use NetworkPolicy only — it's the only way to restrict registry access
✅ Correct Answer: B

Registry allowlisting is a supply chain gate. Without it, any developer (or attacker with compromised credentials) can deploy images from any registry — including malicious images from Docker Hub that impersonate your applications. The primary enforcement is an admission controller policy (Kyverno ClusterPolicy or OPA Rego rule) that validates the image field against an approved pattern list. Defense-in-depth: also use egress NetworkPolicy to block pods from reaching unapproved registries at the network level — if admission control somehow fails, the kubelet can't pull from the blocked registry.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Registry Allowlist Policy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1                                       │
  │  kind: ClusterPolicy                                             │
  │  metadata:                                                       │
  │    name: allowed-registries                                      │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    validationFailureAction: Enforce                              │
  │    rules:                                                        │
  │    - name: validate-registries                                   │
  │      match:                                                      │
  │        resources:                                                │
  │          kinds: [Pod]                                            │
  │      validate:                                                   │
  │        pattern:                                                  │
  │          spec:                                                   │
  │            containers:                                           │
  │            - image: "*.dkr.ecr.*.amazonaws.com/anihpj/* |        │
  │                       gcr.io/distroless/*"                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  VALID: 123.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj/api:v1.2.3 ✓│
  │  VALID: gcr.io/distroless/python3-debian12:nonroot ✓            │
  │  REJECTED: docker.io/anihpj/api:v1.2.3 ✗                        │
  │  REJECTED: ghcr.io/attacker/anihpj-api:v1.2.3 ✗                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Kyverno ClusterPolicy enforces: all images must come from the private ECR registry (anihpj namespace) or the distroless GCR registry (base images only). Docker Hub, quay.io, ghcr.io, and all other registries are rejected at admission time. Attempting to deploy anihpj-api:latest from any non-approved registry → "image registry not in allowed list" error. The egress NetworkPolicy on worker nodes also blocks connections to non-approved registries at the network level.

Platform Security Q159 of 200
What is "continuous scanning" for container registries and why is it needed?
A) Scanning every image exactly once when it's first pushed to the registry
B) Continuously rebuilding images to ensure they have the latest patches
C) New CVEs are discovered DAILY. An image scanned as "clean" last week might have a CRITICAL CVE today. Continuous scanning rescans ALL images on a schedule (daily/weekly). If a new CRITICAL CVE is found → alert security team → identify running pods using affected image → patch or rollback. Admission controller also checks: is the LATEST scan clean? If not, block deployment even if the image was clean when built
D) Scanning images while they're running in production — live vulnerability detection
✅ Correct Answer: C

Continuous scanning acknowledges a fundamental truth: the vulnerability landscape changes daily. An image built and scanned as "clean" on Monday might have 3 CRITICAL CVEs by Friday — the CVE database has been updated, and vulnerabilities that were unknown on Monday are now documented. Registry-level continuous scanning (AWS ECR scanning, Trivy in cron mode, Harbor scanner) rescans every image on a schedule. When new CVEs are found, the security team is alerted and can immediately identify which running pods are affected — no need to rebuild and redeploy just to check.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Continuous Scanning — The Time-Bomb Problem
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CVEs ARE DISCOVERED DAILY — YOUR SCAN RESULTS AGE                    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                      │
  │  TIMELINE:                                                            │
  │  ────────                                                             │
  │  MON    │ Build + scan → 🟢 0 CRITICAL, 2 HIGH, 5 MEDIUM            │
  │  TUE    │ CVE-2026-XXXX published 📢 (CRITICAL, CVSS 9.8)           │
  │  WED    │ Registry rescan → 🔴 anihpj-api NOW has 1 CRITICAL!       │
  │         │ ⚡ Alert → identify running pods → patch → rolling update  │
  │         │                                                            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                      │
  │  WITHOUT CONTINUOUS SCANNING:          WITH CONTINUOUS SCANNING:     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────┐   ┌────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ "Scanned clean on Monday"      │   │ Rescan every 24 hours       │ │
  │  │ (3 months ago)                  │   │ CVE feed updated daily      │ │
  │  │                                 │   │ Alert on new CRITICAL       │ │
  │  │ Reality now: 12 CRITICAL CVEs   │   │ Block deploy if scan stale  │ │
  │  │ You don't know 😱               │   │ Always know your posture ✓  │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────┘   └────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: AWS ECR scanning is configured to rescan all images daily. Trivy runs as a weekly CronJob scanning all images in the cluster. The admission controller (Kyverno) checks: "Has this image been scanned in the last 24 hours? Are there 0 CRITICAL CVEs in the latest scan?" If either check fails, deployment is blocked. This prevents deploying images that were clean at build time but are now known-vulnerable.

5.3 ObservabilityQ160–Q164

Platform Security Q160 of 200
What is Falco and how does it detect threats at runtime?
A) Falco is a static code analyzer — it scans source code before deployment
B) A CNCF-graduated runtime security tool that monitors system calls (via eBPF or kernel module), Kubernetes audit events, and cloud provider APIs. Falco rules define "suspicious behavior" like "shell spawned in container" or "unexpected outbound connection" — when a rule matches, Falco generates an alert that can trigger automated response (webhook → PagerDuty, Slack, or kill the pod)
C) Falco is a Kubernetes-native firewall that replaces NetworkPolicy
D) Falco is a CI/CD tool that scans container images for CVEs before pushing to registry
✅ Correct Answer: B

Falco (CNCF Graduated) is the standard for Kubernetes runtime security. It hooks into the Linux kernel (via eBPF or kernel module) to monitor every system call from every container. Predefined rules detect known-bad patterns: shell spawned (someone exec'd into a container), unexpected network connection (reverse shell to attacker's IP), file writes to sensitive paths (modifying /etc/passwd), privilege escalation (gaining CAP_SYS_ADMIN). When a rule fires, Falco generates an alert that can be forwarded to Slack, PagerDuty, or trigger automated response (kill the pod, cordon the node).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Falco — Real-Time Syscall Monitoring
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  EVERY SYSTEM CALL FROM EVERY CONTAINER → FALCO INSPECTS              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                      │
  │  CONTAINER ──► LINUX KERNEL ──► FALCO eBPF PROBE ──► FALCO ENGINE    │
  │  ┌────────┐    ┌───────────┐   ┌──────────────┐   ┌─────────────┐   │
  │  │Pod A   │    │ syscalls  │   │ • attach to  │   │ • Rule eval │   │
  │  │ exec() │───►│ open()    │──►│   tracepoints│──►│ • Match?    │   │
  │  │ connect│    │ connect() │   │ • filter in- │   │ • Alert!    │   │
  │  │ write()│    │ exec()    │   │   kernel     │   │             │   │
  │  └────────┘    └───────────┘   └──────────────┘   └──────┬──────┘   │
  │                                                          │          │
  │  RULE EXAMPLE:                                           ▼          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ - rule: Terminal shell in container                          │  │
  │  │   condition: proc.name in (bash, sh, zsh) and container     │  │
  │  │   output: "Shell opened (user=%user.name)"                  │  │
  │  │   priority: WARNING                                          │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                          │          │
  │  ALERT DESTINATIONS:                                    ▼          │
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────────┐      │
  │  │  Slack   │  │PagerDuty │  │  Webhook │  │ Auto-Respond │      │
  │  │ (notify) │  │  (page)  │  │(kill pod)│  │  (serverless)│      │
  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────────┘      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Falco is deployed as a DaemonSet with eBPF probe (no kernel module needed — works on EKS/GKE/AKS). Custom rules watch for: "gunicorn worker exec's bash" (should never happen), "outbound connection to non-standard port from db pod" (PostgreSQL shouldn't connect to internet), and "file write to /app/static from api pod" (readOnlyRootFilesystem should prevent this — Falco catches if it somehow happens). Alerts go to Slack (WARNING) and PagerDuty (CRITICAL).

Platform Security Q161 of 200
What types of threats can Falco detect in a Kubernetes environment?
A) ① Unexpected process execution ("Why is curl running in the PostgreSQL pod?"), ② File system changes ("Who modified /etc/passwd?"), ③ Unexpected network connections ("Why is web pod connecting to a foreign IP on port 4444?" — reverse shell), ④ Privilege escalation ("Process just gained CAP_SYS_ADMIN!"), ⑤ Container escape attempts ("Process trying to mount host filesystem"), ⑥ Crypto mining ("Sustained 100% CPU + mining pool connections"), ⑦ Data exfiltration ("Unusually large outbound transfer at 3 AM")
B) Falco only detects network attacks — it can't see process-level activity
C) Falco only detects known CVEs — it's an image vulnerability scanner
D) Falco only monitors the Kubernetes API — it can't see inside containers
✅ Correct Answer: A

Falco monitors system calls — the lowest-level interface between programs and the kernel. This gives it visibility into EVERYTHING a process does: what programs it runs (execve), what files it opens/writes (open/write), what network connections it makes (connect/accept), and what privileges it gains (capset). Falco doesn't need to know about specific CVEs — it detects BEHAVIOR that's anomalous regardless of HOW the attacker got in. A reverse shell looks the same whether the attacker exploited CVE-2024-XXXX or used stolen credentials.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Falco Detection Categories — The Kill Chain View
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  KILL CHAIN STAGE        FALCO DETECTION                         │
  │  ──────────────────────  ─────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  Initial Access          "New container started from unknown     │
  │                          image outside allowed registries"       │
  │  Execution               "Shell (bash/sh) spawned in container   │
  │                          'anihpj-api' by user root"             │
  │  Persistence             "File written to /etc/crontab or        │
  │                          ~/.ssh/authorized_keys"                 │
  │  Privilege Escalation    "Process gained CAP_SYS_ADMIN or       │
  │                          setuid to root"                         │
  │  Defense Evasion         "Audit log files deleted or modified"   │
  │  Credential Access       "File /var/run/secrets/.../token read  │
  │                          by non-application process"             │
  │  Discovery               "Unexpected nmap, netstat, or hostname  │
  │                          command execution"                      │
  │  Lateral Movement        "SSH connection FROM container to       │
  │                          another pod in different namespace"     │
  │  Exfiltration            "Outbound connection on non-standard    │
  │                          port + large data transfer"             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Custom Falco rules are tuned for the specific application behavior: gunicorn workers should NEVER spawn shells, PostgreSQL should NEVER make HTTP connections, the api pod should ONLY connect to db:5432 and redis:6379. Any deviation from this baseline triggers an alert. The rules are in k8s/16-falco-rules.yaml and versioned in Git.

Platform Security Q162 of 200
How does Falco's eBPF probe work compared to the kernel module?
A) eBPF and kernel module are identical — they both load code into the kernel
B) eBPF is older and less secure — the kernel module is the modern approach
C) eBPF runs sandboxed programs in the Linux kernel WITHOUT loading kernel modules. Falco's eBPF probe attaches to syscall tracepoints, every syscall from every container passes through the eBPF filter, and only interesting events reach userspace (low overhead). No kernel module = no kernel crash risk, works on managed K8s services where kernel modules are blocked, and is safer (eBPF programs are verified before loading)
D) The kernel module is required for production — eBPF is experimental only
✅ Correct Answer: C

eBPF (extended Berkeley Packet Filter) is the modern, safer way to do kernel-level observability. Unlike kernel modules (which run unrestricted kernel code — a bug crashes the entire node), eBPF programs are sandboxed: they're verified by the kernel's eBPF verifier before loading (guarantees no infinite loops, no out-of-bounds memory access, limited instruction count). Falco's eBPF probe attaches to syscall tracepoints — every syscall passes through, but filtering happens in-kernel (only interesting events copied to userspace). This gives ~1-3% CPU overhead vs 5-10% for kernel module. And it works everywhere — including managed Kubernetes where custom kernel modules are blocked.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kernel Module vs eBPF — Safer, Faster, Portable
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  KERNEL MODULE (legacy):       eBPF PROBE (modern):             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Container            │     │ Container                    │  │
  │  │  ↓ syscall           │     │  ↓ syscall                   │  │
  │  │ Kernel Module        │     │ eBPF Program (sandboxed)     │  │
  │  │ (unrestricted C code)│     │  ↓ verified by kernel        │  │
  │  │  ↓ ALL events copied │     │  ↓ filter in-kernel          │  │
  │  │ Userspace Falco      │     │ Userspace Falco              │  │
  │  └──────────────────────┘     └──────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                │
  │  ⚠️ Bug = kernel crash!       ✅ Bug = program rejected        │
  │  ⚠️ Blocked on EKS/GKE/AKS    ✅ Works everywhere              │
  │  ⚠️ 5-10% CPU overhead        ✅ 1-3% CPU overhead             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Falco is deployed with the eBPF probe (default in modern Falco Helm chart). This works on all 6 clusters (kubeadm, kind × 4, minikube) without kernel module compatibility issues. The eBPF probe auto-detects the kernel version and loads the appropriate probe. CPU overhead is < 2% on production nodes — negligible for the security visibility gained.

Platform Security Q163 of 200
What is behavioral analytics in runtime security?
A) Analyzing past security incidents to write better documentation
B) Instead of signature-based detection (looking for KNOWN bad patterns), behavioral analytics establishes a BASELINE of normal behavior and alerts on ANOMALIES. Example: anihpj-api normally connects only to db:5432 and redis:6379 at ~5 req/s. Anomaly: suddenly connects to 185.220.101.x:4444 at 3 AM → ALERT (possible reverse shell or data exfiltration). Behavioral catches what signatures miss
C) Behavioral analytics is the same as image scanning — it looks for known CVEs
D) It's a user behavior tracking system for compliance auditing
✅ Correct Answer: B

Behavioral analytics is the security equivalent of "I know what normal looks like, so I can spot abnormal." Instead of maintaining a list of known-bad patterns (which attackers constantly evolve to avoid), you learn what's NORMAL for your application — typical processes, network destinations, file access patterns, CPU/memory usage — and alert on deviations. A new process in a distroless container (which shouldn't have shells) is anomalous. A database pod making an outbound HTTPS connection is anomalous. A 10× spike in outbound data transfer at 3 AM is anomalous. Behavioral analytics catches zero-days and novel attacks that signature-based detection misses.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Behavioral vs Signature Detection
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  SIGNATURE-BASED:                    BEHAVIORAL:                 │
  │  "I know what BAD looks like"        "I know what NORMAL looks   │
  │                                       like — everything else     │
  │  ✅ Fast, low false positives         is suspicious"              │
  │  ❌ Misses zero-days                                           │
  │  ❌ Requires constant updates          ✅ Catches zero-days       │
  │                                       ✅ No signature updates    │
  │  Example: Block known malware         ❌ Higher false positive   │
  │           hash "abc123"                rate initially             │
  │                                                                    │
  │                                       Example: anihpj-api baseline│
  │                                       • Connects to: db, redis    │
  │                                       • Processes: gunicorn only  │
  │                                       • Network: avg 5 req/s      │
  │                                       • Files: writes only /tmp   │
  │                                                                    │
  │                                       ANOMALY: connects to        │
  │                                       185.220.101.x:4444 at 3 AM  │
  │                                       → ALERT (unknown what       │
  │                                         this is, but it's NOT     │
  │                                         normal!)                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Falco rules combine signature AND behavioral approaches. Signature rules: "alert on bash in container" (known-bad). Behavioral rules: "alert if anihpj-api connects to any IP NOT in [db-cluster-ip, redis-cluster-ip]" (deviation from normal). The baseline is established from 2 weeks of production traffic and reviewed monthly. New microservices get a 1-week "learning period" where behavioral rules run in log-only mode before generating alerts.

Platform Security Q164 of 200
How do you RESPOND to a Falco alert automatically?
A) Falco alerts can only be viewed — manual response is always required
B) Just restart the pod — that fixes most security issues
C) Delete the entire namespace to ensure complete remediation
D) ① Falco alert → Falcosidekick forwards to Slack/PagerDuty/webhook, ② Automated response via serverless function: CRITICAL (container escape) → IMMEDIATELY cordon node + kill pod + forensic snapshot, HIGH (unexpected network) → apply emergency egress-deny NetworkPolicy, MEDIUM (shell spawned) → log event + notify team. Automation reduces MTTR from hours to seconds. BUT: automate carefully — false positives could kill production pods!
✅ Correct Answer: D

Falco's Falcosidekick component enables automated response by forwarding alerts to multiple destinations. The key is graduated automation: CRITICAL alerts (container escape, privilege escalation) trigger immediate automated containment — the damage from waiting 5 minutes for a human far exceeds the risk of a false positive. HIGH alerts (unexpected network connections) trigger semi-automated response — apply emergency NetworkPolicy but wait for human confirmation before killing pods. MEDIUM alerts (shell spawned) are notification-only — humans decide the response. This balances speed of response with safety.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Falco Alert → Automated Response Pipeline
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  FALCO ──► FALCOSIDEKICK ──► RESPONSE                           │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ PRIORITY    │ ACTION                                       │  │
  │  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ CRITICAL    │ IMMEDIATE auto-response:                     │  │
  │  │ (container  │ ① Cordon node (kubectl cordon)               │  │
  │  │  escape)    │ ② Kill compromised pod                       │  │
  │  │             │ ③ Forensic snapshot (etcd + logs)            │  │
  │  │             │ ④ Page on-call (PagerDuty)                   │  │
  │  ├─────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ HIGH        │ SEMI-AUTO response:                          │  │
  │  │ (unexpected │ ① Apply emergency NetworkPolicy (egress-deny)│  │
  │  │  network)   │ ② Notify Slack + PagerDuty                   │  │
  │  │             │ ③ Wait for human to decide: kill or monitor? │  │
  │  ├─────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ MEDIUM      │ NOTIFICATION only:                           │  │
  │  │ (shell      │ ① Log to SIEM                                 │  │
  │  │  spawned)   │ ② Notify Slack (security channel)            │  │
  │  │             │ ③ Human reviews and decides                   │  │
  │  └─────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Automated response is enabled for CRITICAL alerts only (container escape, privilege escalation to CAP_SYS_ADMIN, host filesystem mount). These are extremely rare and the cost of a false positive (one killed pod, auto-restarted by Deployment) is far lower than the cost of a real container escape. HIGH and MEDIUM alerts require human confirmation. The response playbooks are tested quarterly in game day exercises.

5.4 Service MeshQ165–Q168

Platform Security Q165 of 200
How does NetworkPolicy implement microsegmentation in Kubernetes?
A) Microsegmentation = fine-grained control over pod-to-pod traffic. Instead of "allow all traffic in namespace," you specify exactly: "anihpj-api pod can ONLY receive TCP on port 8000 FROM pods with label app=anihpj-web, and can ONLY send TCP on port 5432 TO pods with label app=anihpj-db." Each pod can ONLY talk to its explicit dependencies — nothing else. This is least privilege applied to the network
B) Microsegmentation separates the cluster into exactly two segments: internal and external
C) NetworkPolicy can only segment by namespace — pod-level segmentation requires a service mesh
D) Microsegmentation is a physical network concept — it doesn't apply to Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: A

Microsegmentation means defining the exact allowed communication paths between individual workloads, not just broad network zones. In Kubernetes, NetworkPolicy enables this through label-based pod selection: "pods with label X can talk to pods with label Y on port Z." This creates a least-privilege network model: the anihpj-web pod can reach the anihpj-api pod on port 8000, but NOT the anihpj-db pod on 5432 (it should go through the API). Without microsegmentation, the default "allow all pods to talk to all pods" means one compromised pod can reach EVERYTHING.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Microsegmentation — Pod-Level Network Control
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  WITHOUT MICROSEGMENTATION:       WITH MICROSEGMENTATION:        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ web ──► api ✓            │    │ web ──► api:8000 ✓           ││
  │  │ web ──► db ✓ (BAD!)     │    │ web ──► db:5432 ✗ (BLOCKED) ││
  │  │ web ──► redis ✓ (BAD!)  │    │ web ──► redis:6379 ✗ (BLOCKED)││
  │  │ web ──► internet ✓ (BAD!)│   │ web ──► internet ✗ (BLOCKED)  ││
  │  │                          │    │                              ││
  │  │ api ──► db ✓             │    │ api ──► db:5432 ✓            ││
  │  │ api ──► redis ✓          │    │ api ──► redis:6379 ✓         ││
  │  │ api ──► internet ✓ (BAD!)│    │ api ──► internet ✗ (BLOCKED) ││
  │  └──────────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                  │
  │  Each arrow = an explicit NetworkPolicy ingress/egress rule.     │
  │  No arrow = traffic DENIED by default-deny policy.               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Six NetworkPolicy objects implement microsegmentation: default-deny-all, web→api:8000, api→db:5432, api→redis:6379, allow-DNS, allow-monitoring. Each policy targets specific pod labels. The result: a compromised web pod can ONLY reach the API on port 8000 — it cannot bypass the API to directly access the database or cache. Every data access must go through the API's application logic (which has its own authentication and input validation).

Platform Security Q166 of 200
What is the "Zero Trust Network" model in Kubernetes?
A) Trust all internal traffic by default — only external traffic needs verification
B) A single firewall that protects the entire cluster perimeter
C) ① No implicit trust based on network location (being "inside the cluster" ≠ trusted), ② All traffic encrypted (mTLS via Istio/Linkerd/Cilium), ③ All traffic authenticated (both ends present valid certificates), ④ All traffic authorized (NetworkPolicy + AuthorizationPolicy), ⑤ Default-deny: all traffic blocked unless explicitly allowed. Result: even if attacker compromises a pod, they can't reach other services without explicit authorization
D) Zero Trust means disabling all network security — trusting nothing and no one
✅ Correct Answer: C

Zero Trust networking rejects the traditional "perimeter security" model where everything inside the firewall is trusted. In Kubernetes, this means: just because a pod is in the same namespace or same cluster doesn't mean it should be able to communicate with other pods. Every connection must be explicitly allowed (NetworkPolicy), authenticated (mTLS — both ends prove identity with certificates), and authorized (is this specific pod allowed to talk to that specific service?). The network becomes a hostile environment by design — default-deny, then carve out specific, authenticated, authorized paths.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Zero Trust — Never Trust, Always Verify
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  PERIMETER MODEL (❌):               ZERO TRUST (✅):            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │     FIREWALL         │           │  DEFAULT-DENY            │ │
  │  │  "Inside = trusted"  │           │  "Nothing is trusted"    │ │
  │  └──────┬───────────────┘           └──────────────────────────┘ │
  │         │                                                            │
  │  ┌──────▼──────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ INTERNAL (trusted by default)│   │ Every connection must:    │ │
  │  │ • web ↔ api (allow-all)     │   │ ① Be explicitly allowed   │ │
  │  │ • api → db (allow-all)      │   │   (NetworkPolicy)          │ │
  │  │ • Any pod → any pod ✓       │   │ ② Be authenticated (mTLS) │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────┘   │ ③ Be authorized (policy)  │ │
  │                                     └──────────────────────────┘ │
  │  One breach → total access                                        │
  │                                        web→api:8000 ✓ (mTLS)     │
  │                                        web→db:5432 ✗ (DENIED)    │
  │                                        web→redis ✗ (DENIED)      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Zero Trust is implemented through: default-deny NetworkPolicy on all namespaces, explicit per-tier allow rules, and mTLS between all services via Cilium (transparent WireGuard encryption between nodes). Even if an attacker compromises the Istio sidecar or CNI, they still need to bypass NetworkPolicy AND mTLS certificate validation to reach other services. Zero Trust means multiple independent security checks on every connection.

Platform Security Q167 of 200
What are the steps in a Kubernetes security incident response?
A) Delete everything immediately — rebuild from scratch
B) ① Detection (Falco/SIEM alert), ② Triage (real incident or false positive?), ③ Containment (isolate namespace/node via NetworkPolicy + cordon), ④ Investigation (audit logs, pod logs, Falco events → reconstruct timeline), ⑤ Eradication (remove foothold: delete compromised pods, revoke tokens, rotate secrets), ⑥ Recovery (restore from clean backup, verify no backdoors), ⑦ Post-mortem (what happened? how to prevent?)
C) Call the cloud provider — they handle all security incidents for managed Kubernetes
D) There's no standard process — each incident is unique and handled ad-hoc
✅ Correct Answer: B

Kubernetes incident response follows the standard NIST incident response lifecycle adapted for containers. The critical difference from traditional IR: containment must happen FAST — a compromised container can move laterally in seconds using the default "allow-all" network model. Also: preserve evidence BEFORE cleanup — containers are ephemeral; if you delete the compromised pod, you lose the forensic evidence (memory state, running processes, network connections). KCSA expects you to know the process flow, not specific tool commands.

📝 Detailed Explanation
K8s Incident Response — The 7-Step Lifecycle
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ① DETECT ──► ② TRIAGE ──► ③ CONTAIN ──► ④ INVESTIGATE             │
  │  ─────────    ─────────    ──────────    ──────────────              │
  │  Falco:       Is this      kubectl       etcd snapshot               │
  │  "Reverse     real?        cordon  Export audit                │
  │  shell!"      Check:       Apply egress- logs + pod                  │
  │  → PagerDuty  • pod?       deny NP →     logs → timeline             │
  │               • ns?        STOP EXFIL!   "What did                   │
  │               • context?                 attacker do?"               │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ⑤ ERADICATE ──► ⑥ RECOVER ──► ⑦ POST-MORTEM                       │
  │  ────────────    ──────────    ──────────────                         │
  │  Delete comp'd   Restore from  • What happened?                      │
  │  pods (after     clean backup  • How did we miss it?                 │
  │  evidence)       + verify no   • How to prevent?                     │
  │  Revoke tokens   backdoors     • Update runbooks                     │
  │  Rotate secrets  Rolling restart • Improve monitoring               │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ⏱️ MTTR TARGET: < 15 minutes from alert to containment              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The incident response runbook is rehearsed quarterly in game day exercises. Emergency NetworkPolicies are pre-staged as commented-out YAML files (just uncomment + apply). etcd snapshots run every 4 hours. The security on-call engineer has a laminated 7-step checklist. The MTTR (Mean Time to Respond) target is <15 minutes from alert to containment.

Platform Security Q168 of 200
What should you NEVER do during a Kubernetes security incident?
A) ① DON'T delete the compromised pod immediately — you lose forensic evidence (memory, processes, connections). Cordon first, capture logs, then delete. ② DON'T just "fix it and move on" — find ROOT CAUSE or attacker returns. ③ DON'T communicate on potentially compromised channels (same Slack/email infrastructure). ④ DON'T forget breach notification deadlines (GDPR: 72 hours if PII exposed)
B) Don't notify anyone until the incident is fully resolved — avoid panic
C) Don't take any action — wait for the security vendor to respond
D) Don't document anything — written records can be used against you in audits
✅ Correct Answer: A

There are four critical mistakes that turn a containable incident into a catastrophe. ① Deleting evidence: the compromised pod contains the attacker's tools, connections, and memory state — deleting it destroys forensic evidence. Cordon + snapshot first. ② Skipping root cause: if you just restart the pod without fixing HOW the attacker got in, they'll be back tomorrow through the same vulnerability. ③ Compromised communications: if the attacker has access to your Slack or email, they'll know you're onto them and accelerate their attack or cover their tracks. ④ Missing deadlines: GDPR requires breach notification within 72 hours — the clock starts at detection, not resolution.

📝 Detailed Explanation
4 Things NEVER to Do During an Incident
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  ❌ DON'T #1: kubectl delete pod compromised-pod                 │
  │  ───────────────────────────────────────────                     │
  │  Why: Loses forensic evidence (memory, active connections,       │
  │        running processes, attacker tools)                         │
  │  Instead: kubectl cordon <node> FIRST, then capture:            │
  │           • kubectl logs --timestamps > pod.log                  │
  │           • CSI volume snapshot                                   │
  │           • etcd snapshot                                        │
  │           • THEN delete the pod                                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ❌ DON'T #2: Fix the symptom, ignore the cause                  │
  │  Why: Attacker returns through same vulnerability tomorrow       │
  │  Instead: Full root cause analysis — how did they get in?        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ❌ DON'T #3: Discuss on Slack/Teams/email                       │
  │  Why: Attacker may have access → knows you're onto them          │
  │  Instead: Out-of-band communication (Signal, separate infra)     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ❌ DON'T #4: "We'll disclose next week"                         │
  │  Why: GDPR requires notification within 72 HOURS of detection    │
  │  Instead: Notify DPO + legal team immediately                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The incident response runbook has a "NEVER DO" section printed in RED on page 1. Every security on-call engineer is trained on these four rules during onboarding. The incident response game day exercises intentionally include scenarios where the "obvious" response (delete the pod) is the WRONG response — engineers learn to cordon + capture first.

5.5 PKIQ169–Q172

Platform Security Q169 of 200
How do you preserve forensic evidence during a container security incident?
A) Take a screenshot of the terminal — that's sufficient evidence
B) Delete and recreate everything — the Kubernetes audit log has all the evidence
C) ① etcd snapshot (preserves full cluster state at incident time), ② Export pod logs with timestamps, ③ Export audit logs for the timeframe (filter by namespace/user), ④ CSI volume snapshot (preserve container filesystem), ⑤ Capture network flows (Hubble/Cilium flow logs), ⑥ Save all Falco alerts for the timeframe, ⑦ DO NOT kubectl delete anything until evidence is captured. Cordon first, capture second, delete third
D) Forensic evidence isn't needed in cloud-native environments — containers are ephemeral
✅ Correct Answer: C

Forensic preservation in Kubernetes requires capturing MULTIPLE independent evidence sources before they disappear. Containers are ephemeral — once deleted, their state is gone forever. The evidence must answer: "What did the attacker do, in what order, to what resources?" An etcd snapshot captures the CLUSTER STATE (what pods/Secrets/RBAC existed). Pod logs capture APPLICATION-LEVEL activity. Audit logs capture API-LEVEL actions (who ran kubectl delete?). Network flow logs capture CONNECTIONS (what IPs did the pod talk to?). Falco events capture SYSCALLS (what programs ran, what files were accessed). All together = complete forensic timeline.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Forensic Evidence Collection — 6 Sources Must Be Preserved
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  EVIDENCE SOURCE          WHAT IT CAPTURES              COMMAND      │
  │  ───────────────────────  ───────────────────────────  ──────────── │
  │  ① etcd snapshot          Full cluster state at time   etcdctl snap │
  │  ② Pod logs (timestamps)  App output (attacker cmds?) kubectl logs │
  │  ③ Kubernetes audit logs  API-level actions (who del?) audit export │
  │  ④ CSI volume snapshot    Container filesystem (malware) CSI driver │
  │  ⑤ Network flow logs      Pod connections (C2 IPs)     Hubble/Cilium│
  │  ⑥ Falco events           System calls (processes run) Falco dump   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ⚠️ ORDER OF OPERATIONS:                                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ 1️⃣ CORDON node (stop new pods, preserve existing)              │ │
  │  │ 2️⃣ CAPTURE all 6 evidence sources (DON'T delete anything!)    │ │
  │  │ 3️⃣ DELETE compromised pods (only after evidence is secured)   │ │
  │  │ 4️⃣ RESTORE from clean backup + verify no backdoors            │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The incident response runbook includes a "Forensic Collection" checklist with exact commands for each evidence source. etcd snapshots are automated every 4 hours (so there's always a pre-incident snapshot for comparison). The security team practices forensic collection during quarterly tabletop exercises — they have 5 minutes to capture all 6 evidence sources from a simulated compromise.

Platform Security Q170 of 200
How do you build a "security-first culture" around Kubernetes?
A) Hire a dedicated security team and make security their sole responsibility
B) ① Security is EVERYONE'S responsibility, ② "Paved road" security: make the secure path the EASIEST path (secure base images as templates, admission controllers auto-add securityContext, CI/CD scans automatically), ③ Blameless post-mortems: "What in our PROCESS allowed this?" not "WHOSE fault?", ④ Security champions per team, ⑤ Gamification: leaderboards for fewest CRITICAL CVEs or fastest patch time
C) Send a company-wide email about security policies once a year
D) Security culture is irrelevant — just enforce policies through admission controllers
✅ Correct Answer: B

Security culture is about making security the DEFAULT, not an obstacle. The "paved road" approach means developers don't choose between "secure" and "fast" — the fastest path IS the secure path. Secure base images are provided as templates (no one starts from ubuntu:latest). Admission controllers auto-add securityContext (developers don't need to remember). CI/CD pipelines scan automatically (developers see results without running tools manually). Blameless post-mortems focus on PROCESS improvement, not individual blame. Security champions embed knowledge in each team. Gamification makes security engaging — teams compete to have the fewest CRITICAL CVEs, not the most features shipped.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Security-First Culture — The Paved Road vs The Obstacle Course
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ❌ OBSTACLE PATH (Security as gate):    ✅ PAVED ROAD (Security as   │
  │                                            default):                 │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Dev picks ubuntu:latest          │   │ Dev uses template:        │ │
  │  │ → 200 packages → 100 CVEs        │   │ → distroless: 10 pkgs    │ │
  │  │ Manual securityContext config    │   │ → securityContext auto-  │ │
  │  │ Security review BLOCKS deploy    │   │   generated ✓             │ │
  │  │ "Security is slowing me down!"   │   │ CI/CD scans pass ✓       │ │
  │  │                                  │   │ "That was easy!" 🚀      │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                      │
  │  KEY PRINCIPLES:                                                     │
  │  • SECURE DEFAULTS: Opt-out (not opt-in) for security features       │
  │  • AUTOMATION: Don't rely on humans remembering security steps        │
  │  • IMMEDIATE FEEDBACK: CI/CD tells developers what to fix NOW         │
  │  • BLAMELESS: "What in our PROCESS allowed this?" not "WHOSE fault?" │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The platform team provides "golden paths" — pre-configured Dockerfiles, Helm charts, and CI/CD pipeline templates that are secure by default. A developer starting a new service runs a single command that scaffolds a project with: distroless base image, restricted Pod Security context, default-deny NetworkPolicy, and CI/CD with Trivy scanning. The secure path is literally the easiest path — deviation requires extra effort.

Platform Security Q171 of 200
What is the relationship between DevSecOps and Kubernetes Platform Security?
A) DevSecOps is a separate team that handles security — developers don't need to worry about it
B) DevSecOps is the same as DevOps — there's no security-specific meaning
C) DevSecOps only applies to the CI/CD pipeline — it doesn't include runtime security
D) DevSecOps = integrating security into DevOps (not a separate phase). K8s platform security implements this through: "shift left" (SAST/SCA/image scanning in CI/CD — catch before production), "shift right" (Falco/audit logging — catch what slipped through), policy as code (OPA/Kyverno in Git → reviewed like code → GitOps deployment), immutable infrastructure (containers rebuilt, never patched), continuous compliance (automated CIS scans, SBOM, audit analysis)
✅ Correct Answer: D

DevSecOps means security is integrated into every phase of the DevOps lifecycle — not a separate step at the end. Kubernetes platform security is the practical implementation of DevSecOps principles: shift left catches issues before production (CI/CD scanning, admission control), shift right monitors production for what slipped through (Falco, audit logs), policy as code treats security policies like application code (versioned in Git, reviewed, tested, deployed via GitOps), and immutable infrastructure ensures containers are rebuilt from source (not patched in-place — guaranteeing consistency). KCSA is the theory foundation; CKS teaches the hands-on implementation.

DevSecOps — Security in Every Phase
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │

  │  └────┘  └──────┘ └───────┘ └───────┘ └─────────┘ └────────┘ └──────┘│
  │                                                                      │
  │  ← SHIFT LEFT (prevent) ──────► ← SHIFT RIGHT (detect + respond) ──► │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: DevSecOps is the engineering philosophy. The CI/CD pipeline, admission controllers, runtime monitoring, and compliance scanning are the implementation. The security team doesn't "review" deployments — the pipeline enforces policies automatically. Developers see security as part of their workflow, not as an external gate. KCSA teaches the WHAT and WHY; CKS will teach the HOW.

Platform Security Q172 of 200
How does Kubernetes help with PCI-DSS compliance?
A) PCI-DSS requirements map to specific Kubernetes controls: Req 1 (Firewalls) → NetworkPolicy default-deny, Req 2 (Secure Config) → CIS Benchmarks + kube-bench, Req 3 (Protect Cardholder Data) → EncryptionConfiguration for Secrets + TLS everywhere, Req 7 (Access Control) → RBAC least privilege, Req 10 (Audit) → K8s audit logs, Req 11 (Test Security) → Trivy + kube-bench + Falco

PCI-DSS (Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard) maps cleanly to Kubernetes security controls. Each PCI requirement has a corresponding Kubernetes implementation: firewalls become NetworkPolicy, secure configuration becomes CIS Benchmarks enforced by kube-bench, protecting cardholder data becomes EncryptionConfiguration (etcd) + TLS (transit), access control becomes RBAC with least privilege, audit logging becomes Kubernetes audit logs, and security testing becomes Trivy (image scanning) + kube-bench (config scanning) + Falco (runtime detection). The key for auditors: you must SHOW the mapping and provide EVIDENCE that the controls are implemented and working.

5.6 ConnectivityQ173–Q176

Platform Security Q173 of 200
How does Kubernetes help with SOC2 compliance?
A) SOC2 only applies to financial institutions — it's not relevant for Kubernetes
B) Kubernetes is automatically SOC2 compliant — cloud providers handle everything
C) SOC2's 5 Trust Service Criteria map to K8s: Security (NetworkPolicy, RBAC, TLS, audit logs), Availability (Resource limits, HPA, PDB, multi-AZ), Processing Integrity (Admission controllers, PSS), Confidentiality (EncryptionConfiguration, mTLS, private registry), Privacy (RBAC for PII access, audit logs as proof, data lifecycle management)
D) SOC2 only requires a firewall at the cluster perimeter
✅ Correct Answer: C

SOC2 (Service Organization Control 2) evaluates how well a service organization protects customer data across five Trust Service Criteria. Each maps to Kubernetes controls: Security = NetworkPolicy (firewall) + RBAC (who can do what) + TLS (encryption in transit) + audit logs (who accessed what when). Availability = resource limits + HPA (auto-scale) + PDB (minimum pods during disruption) + multi-AZ clusters. Processing Integrity = admission controllers validate inputs before processing, Pod Security Standards prevent unauthorized changes. Confidentiality = EncryptionConfiguration (secrets at rest) + mTLS (data in transit) + private registry. Privacy = RBAC restricts PII access, audit logs prove compliance, data lifecycle management (PVC reclaim policies, log retention).

📝 Detailed Explanation
SOC2 Trust Criteria → Kubernetes Implementation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  SOC2 CRITERION         KUBERNETES CONTROL            VERIFICATION   │
  │  ─────────────────────  ───────────────────────────  ────────────── │

For anihpj/jobpost: The SOC2 audit evidence package maps every Trust Service Criterion to specific Kubernetes controls, complete with YAML manifests, audit log excerpts, and kube-bench scan results. Platform Security Q174 of 200
How does Kubernetes help with GDPR compliance?
A) GDPR maps to K8s: Art 5 (Data Minimization) → separate PII from non-PII in different namespaces/DBs, Art 25 (Data Protection by Design) → PSS + RBAC + NetworkPolicy built in from day one, Art 30 (Records of Processing) → audit logs show WHO accessed WHAT data and WHEN, Art 32 (Security of Processing) → encryption at rest + transit, Art 33 (Breach Notification) → Falco + audit = rapid detection for 72-hour notification deadline, Art 17 (Right to Erasure) → PVC + snapshot lifecycle management
B) GDPR only applies to physical data centers — Kubernetes is exempt
C) GDPR compliance is handled entirely by cloud providers — you just need to enable it
D) Kubernetes can't help with GDPR — it requires legal/compliance teams, not technical controls
✅ Correct Answer: A

GDPR (General Data Protection Regulation) has specific articles that translate to Kubernetes controls. Article 25 (Data Protection by Design) means security must be built in from the start — Pod Security Standards, RBAC, and NetworkPolicy should be part of the initial deployment, not added later. Article 30 requires records of data processing — Kubernetes audit logs provide exactly this: a timestamped record of who accessed what data. Article 33 mandates breach notification within 72 hours of detection — Falco's runtime detection enables rapid discovery; if you don't detect the breach, you can't notify. Article 17 (Right to Erasure) means you must be able to delete ALL user data — including from backups. PVC snapshot management must support this.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GDPR Articles → Kubernetes Implementation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  GDPR ARTICLE          KUBERNETES CONTROL                        │
  │  ────────────────────  ───────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  Art 5 (Data           Separate namespaces for PII vs non-PII    │
  │   Minimization)        Only collect what's needed                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Art 25 (Data          Pod Security Standards (Restricted)       │
  │   Protection by        RBAC (least privilege from day 1)         │
  │   Design)              NetworkPolicy (default-deny from deploy)  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Art 30 (Records       Kubernetes audit logs                     │
  │   of Processing)       Timestamped: who accessed what data when  │
  │                                                                  │

  │   of Processing)       TLS for all communications                │
  │                        External vault for encryption keys        │
  │                                                                  │

  │   Notification)        + SIEM alerting → notify within 72 hours  │
  │                                                                  │

  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The GDPR compliance documentation maps each article to specific Kubernetes controls. User PII is stored in a dedicated PostgreSQL database with its own namespace, RBAC, and NetworkPolicy. Audit logs track every access to PII data. Falco detects unauthorized access attempts. PVC snapshots have a 30-day retention policy with secure deletion — if a user invokes Right to Erasure, all their data (including backup copies) is deleted within the 30-day window.

Platform Security Q175 of 200
What is the NIST Cybersecurity Framework and how does it apply to Kubernetes?
A) NIST CSF is a vulnerability database like CVE — it lists specific software vulnerabilities
B) NIST CSF has 5 core functions: IDENTIFY (know assets — SBOM, audit logs, kube-bench), PROTECT (implement safeguards — RBAC, NetworkPolicy, EncryptionConfiguration), DETECT (find breaches — Falco, SIEM, behavioral analytics), RESPOND (contain damage — cordon, emergency NP, revoke tokens), RECOVER (restore operations — etcd backup, GitOps reconciliation). It's a GOVERNANCE framework about PROCESS, not specific tools
C) NIST CSF is a Kubernetes-specific security standard created by CNCF
D) NIST CSF only applies to US government systems — not relevant for commercial Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: B

The NIST Cybersecurity Framework (CSF) is a governance framework organized around 5 core functions that form a continuous cycle. It's not about specific tools — it's about having PROCESSES for each function. For Kubernetes: IDENTIFY means knowing your assets (SBOM for software inventory, kube-bench for config inventory, audit logs for access inventory). PROTECT means implementing safeguards (RBAC, NetworkPolicy, encryption). DETECT means finding breaches quickly (Falco, SIEM). RESPOND means having an incident response plan. RECOVER means restoring normal operations (etcd backup, GitOps reconciliation). KCSA tests your understanding of the framework, not memorization of control numbers.

📝 Detailed Explanation

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │

  │         └──────────────────────────────────────┘                 │

  │         │          │ (know    │          │                       │
  │         │          │  assets) │          ▼                       │
  │         │          └──────────┘    ┌──────────┐                  │

  │  │ (restore)│                      │ (find    │                  │

  │                                    └──────────┘                  │
  │                                    cordon, revoke                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The security program aligns with NIST CSF. IDENTIFY: monthly SBOM audits + weekly kube-bench scans. PROTECT: RBAC least privilege + default-deny NetworkPolicy + EncryptionConfiguration. DETECT: Falco with eBPF + audit logs to SIEM. RESPOND: documented runbooks + quarterly game days. RECOVER: etcd snapshots every 4 hours + ArgoCD GitOps reconciliation. The NIST CSF provides the governance structure; Kubernetes tools provide the implementation.

Platform Security Q176 of 200
How do you PROVE compliance to an auditor for a Kubernetes deployment?
A) Tell the auditor "trust us" — Kubernetes is secure by default
B) Show the cloud provider's compliance certification — that covers everything
C) Provide a signed letter from the CTO — that's sufficient evidence
D) For each control requirement, show: the YAML manifest proving the control EXISTS, the admission controller logs proving it's ENFORCED, the audit logs proving it's WORKING, and the kube-bench/Trivy scan proving it's VERIFIED. Example: "How do you restrict database access?" → "NetworkPolicy anihpj-db-access (show YAML) is enforced by Calico (show CNI status) and verified by audit logs showing zero unauthorized connections in 90 days (show log excerpt)."
✅ Correct Answer: D

Auditors need EVIDENCE, not promises. For every security control, you must show four things: ① It EXISTS (the YAML manifest, the configuration file, the policy definition), ② It's ENFORCED (the admission controller is active, the CNI supports NetworkPolicy, RBAC is enabled), ③ It's WORKING (audit logs show it's being applied — e.g., "0 unauthorized connection attempts in last 90 days"), ④ It's VERIFIED (kube-bench, Trivy, or penetration test results confirm the control is effective). The days of "we have a firewall" are over — auditors want YAML files, log excerpts, and scan results.
